Harry Bland stood before the great oak doors of Castlewood, the heavy wood still warm from the summer sun, though a chill wind had already begun to stir the leaves of the surrounding forest. He was a man of twenty summers, his face open and unlined, save for a sharpness around the eyes that spoke of a mind constantly at work. To call him merely a gentleman of leisure would have been a grave injustice; he possessed a restless spirit, a hunger for knowledge and action that often made the quiet life of his family estate feel like a cage.

His mother, Mrs. Bland, watched him from the window with a mixture of pride and unease. "Go gently, Harry," she called out, her voice carrying over the rustle of the trees. "Do not let your curiosity outrun your judgment. You are leaving home, but you must never forget where your roots lie."

"I will not forget, Mother," Harry replied, turning back to face her. His tone was respectful, yet there was a steel beneath it. "But the world is vast, and if I am to understand it, I must step outside these walls. Father says the colonies need men who can think clearly, not just those who can inherit land."

Mrs. Bland sighed, a sound that seemed to carry the weight of years. She knew her son well enough to know that words were not enough to bind him here. "Then go," she said finally, stepping aside. "But remember, when you return, you bring something back with you. Something good."

Harry bowed slightly, a gesture of old-fashioned courtesy, and then turned to walk down the long drive. As he moved away, the silence of the estate settled over him, broken only by the occasional cry of a bird or the crackle of dry branches underfoot. It was a somber departure, yet there was a certain exhilaration in the air, the feeling of a new chapter beginning.

As he walked, Harry reflected on the lessons his father had taught him. Sir William Bland, a man of stern demeanor and deep convictions, had always emphasized duty above all else. "A man's place is where his honor demands it," his father had said. "And honor is not found in idleness, nor in the comfort of home alone." These words echoed in Harry's mind as he approached the edge of the woods, where the path grew rougher and less traveled.

He paused for a moment, looking back at the house. For now, it was just a memory, a point of origin rather than a destination. But soon, he would be far from it, venturing into lands unknown, seeking answers to questions he could not yet formulate. And somewhere along the way, he hoped to find not only truth but also friendship—a rare commodity in a world so often divided by class and circumstance.

With a final glance behind him, Harry stepped forward, disappearing into the shadows of the forest, ready to embrace whatever lay ahead.

Upon the wall of a distinguished study in Boston hangs a pair of crossed swords, relics of a father and son who, in the heat of the Great War of Independence, fought for opposing masters. One blade gleamed in the service of the Crown; the other served a Republic. Yet, today, they rest side by side as harmless trophies, borne by a man whose name is honored equally in the land of his birth and the soil of his adoption. Where genius finds peace, these weapons find honor.

The history they evoke mirrors their union. The protagonists of this tale, native-born Virginians and children of the Old Dominion, found themselves bound in chains of opposite allegiances. They clashed as brothers must, yet emerged from the storm not as enemies, but as kin, their love undimmed despite the fury of their division. The Colonel, clad in scarlet, and the General, arrayed in blue and buff, now hang side by side in the wainscot of the Warringtons' parlour in England. A descendant of the line displays them to me, alongside the letters they exchanged—the yellowed pages of a bygone era, some stained with the boyish tears of thwarted passion, others pierced by bullets or blotted with blood. To the Warringtons, these figures are simply "The Virginians," a title immortalized in their memoirs.

Both men spent their youth traversing Europe, living on the precipice of a world that drifts swiftly from memory. They knew varieties of men and fortunes unknown to later generations, encountering ghosts from history that breathe within the ink of their correspondence. These letters, preserved by the affectionate thrift of their mother, span a lifetime. They begin with Henry, the younger twin, sending love to his brother, who lay ill at Castlewood in Virginia, and thank their grandfather for a horse ridden with a tutor. They end with a final salutation, "from my beloved son," delivered mere hours before the mother's death.

Madam Esmond, ever after the burning of Castlewood, refused the name of Warrington, holding it in scant regard against her own lineage. She never ventured to Europe again, save once in the reign of George the Second. Her letters, sparse in detail, offer hints rather than full descriptions—outlines waiting to be filled with color. I have pored over them, striving to reconstruct the scenes, imagining the situations, the surrounding persons, the voices that still echo in the silence of the paper. Whether this revival succeeds, whether it brings profit or amusement, remains for the reader to judge.

***

It was a summer morning in 1756, under the reign of King George the Second. The *Young Rachel*, a Virginian vessel captained by Edward Franks, ascended the Avon River on her triumphant return from the annual voyage to the Potomac. She drifted toward Bristol, mooring near Trail's Wharf. Mr. Trail, a co-owner who watched the harbor from his counting-house windows, took a boat and hastened to the ship.

A large, grave man with hair as grey as his demeanor, Mr. Trail extended a welcoming hand to Captain Franks. After congratulating the captain on the swift and fortunate journey, he turned immediately to business, inquiring of the cargo and passengers.

Captain Franks, a man of cheerful disposition who relished a jest, grinned. "We have," he announced, "but yonder ugly negro boy carrying the trunks, and a passenger who claims the state cabin to himself."

Mr. Trail frowned, his face tightening. "Confound you, Franks, and your luck!" he muttered. "The *Duke William* arrived last week with fourteen, and she is not half your tonnage."

"And this passenger," the Captain pressed, "takes the whole cabin and pays not a penny. Swear now, it will do you good, Mr. Trail, truly it will. I have tried the medicine."

"A passenger takes the whole cabin and pays nothing?" Mr. Trail gasped. "Gracious mercy, are you a fool, Captain Franks?"

"Ask the passenger himself, for here he comes."

As the master spoke, a young man of nineteen stepped up the hatchway. Cloaked and armed with a sword beneath his arm, he was dressed in deep mourning. "Gumbo, you idiot, why don't you fetch the baggage out of the cabin?" he called down. Turning to the crew, he added, "Well, shipmates, our journey is ended. You shall see all the little ones tonight whom you have spoken of. Give my love to Polly, Betty, and Little Tommy; do not forget my duty to Mrs. Franks. I thought yesterday the voyage would never conclude, and now that it is done, I am almost sorry. That little berth in my cabin looks terribly comfortable now that I am leaving it."

Mr. Trail scowled at the unpaid passenger, offering but a curt nod. "Captain Franks," the merchant declared, "this gentleman is Mr. Trail, sir, whose name you have undoubtedly heard."

"It is well known in Bristol, sir," Mr. Trail intoned, standing with majestic dignity.

"And this is Mr. Warrington," the Captain interjected, "son of Madam Esmond Warrington, of Castlewood."

Instantly, the merchant removed his hat, bowing prodigiously as if before a crowned prince. "Gracious powers, Mr. Warrington! What a delight, indeed! What a crowning mercy that your voyage should have been so prosperous! You must accept my boat to go ashore. Let me cordially welcome you to England; let me shake your hand as the son of my benefactress and patroness, Mrs. Esmond Warrington, whose name is honored on Bristol Exchange, I warrant you. Is it not so, Franks?"

"There is no sweeter tobacco from Virginia," Captain Franks remarked, producing a great brass box from his pocket and stuffing a quid into his jolly mouth. "No better brand than the Three Castles. You don't know what a comfort it is, sir! You will take to it, bless you, as you grow older. Won't he, Mr. Trail? I wish you had ten shiploads of it instead of one. You might have ten shiploads; I have told Madam Esmond so. I have ridden over her plantation; she treats me like a lord when I go to the house. She doesn't grudge me the best wine, nor keep me cooling my heels in the counting-room as some folks do." He glanced sharply at Mr. Trail. "She is a real born lady, she is. Might have a thousand hogsheads as easily as her hundreds, if there were but hands enough."

"I have lately engaged in the Guinea trade," said Mr. Trail, bowing obsequiously. "I could supply her ladyship with any number of healthy young negroes before next fall."

"We are averse to purchasing negroes from Africa," the young gentleman replied, his voice cool and distant. "My grandfather and my mother have always objected to it. I do not like to think of selling or buying the poor wretches."

"It is for their good, my dear young sir! For their temporal and spiritual good!" cried Mr. Trail. "We purchase these poor creatures only for their benefit. Let me discuss this matter further at my own house. I can introduce you to a happy home, a Christian family, and the honest fare of a British merchant. Can't I, Captain Franks?"

"Can't say," growled the Captain. "Never asked me to take a bite or sup at your table. Asked me to psalm-singing once, to hear Mr. Ward preach. Don't care for them sort of entertainments."

Ignoring this aside, Mr. Trail lowered his voice. "Business is business, my dear young sir. I know 'tis only my duty, the duty of all of us, to cultivate the fruits of the earth in their season. As the heir of Lady Esmond's estate—for I speak, I believe, to the heir of that great property?—"

The young gentleman bowed politely.

"—I would urge upon you, at the earliest moment, the propriety, the necessity of increasing the ample means with which Heaven has blessed you. As an honest factor, I could not do otherwise; as a prudent man, should I scruple to speak of what tends to your profit and mine? No, my dear Mr. George."

"My name is not George; my name is Henry," the young man said, turning his head away, his eyes filling with unshed tears.

"Gracious powers! What do you mean, sir? Did you not say you were my lady's heir? And is not George Esmond Warrington, Esq.—"

"Hold your tongue, you fool!" cried Captain Franks, delivering a sharp blow to the merchant's flank as the young man turned away. "Do you not see the young gentleman swabbing his eyes? Note his black clothes?"

"What do you mean, Captain Franks, by laying your hand on your owners?" Mr. Trail demanded indignantly. "Mr. George is the heir; I know the Colonel's will perfectly well."

"Mr. George is there," the Captain pointed with his thumb toward the deck.

"Where?" cried the factor, straining forward.

"Mr. George is there!" the Captain reiterated, lifting a finger toward the topmast or the sky beyond. "He is dead a year, sir, come next the ninth of July. He would have gone out with General Braddock on that dreadful business to Belle Rivière. He and a thousand more never came back

For some time, Colonel Esmond's grandson stood ringing the bell at Castlewood, his summons ignored by the silent house. Finally, a servant emerged, indifferent to the news that the caller was a relation. With the family away, John cared little for kin and was eager to resume his card game with Thomas. Though the housekeeper was preoccupied preparing for the expected arrival of "my lord" and "my lady," she eventually yielded to strong entreaties, allowing Harry to inspect the drawing rooms. There, amidst portraits of his ancestors—a grandfather in periwig and breastplate, a grandmother in the attire of Charles II.'s reign—he found a visual connection to a lineage he loved. Yet, the stern housekeeper soon dismissed him, citing the imminent arrival of the aristocracy.

Defeated, Harry retreated to the hall. He took a slip of paper from his pocketbook and, with a heavy heart, wrote down his name and address: *Henry Esmond Warrington, of Castlewood, in Virginia.* Laying it on the table, he waited. The lackeys, rising from their games to claim their "wails," opened the door. Outside, Gumbo, the gatekeeper, accepted a guinea from Harry, hardly comprehending the gesture. Inside, however, the atmosphere was stark. Harry recalled that throughout his journey, the only face showing kindness was little Polly's; now, standing before the ancestral halls, he realized the depth of his disappointment. They ought to have welcomed their kin, yet here he was forced to seek a dish of bacon and eggs at a roadside alehouse.

After dinner, Harry sat on the bridge, watching the sun dip below the horizon as the rooks returned to the elms. His mind drifted to the tales of knights and cavaliers his mother and grandsire had told him, imagining scenes of chivalry near the ford. These reveries were shattered by a sharp pang of loss for his deceased brother, leaving him bowed with grief. Suddenly, two young men on horseback dashed across the bridge. One, swearing, insulted Harry, telling him to clear the way; the other, feigning innocence, spurred forward, urging his companion onward. Before Harry could recover from his shock and anger, they vanished up the hill. They were swiftly followed by livery servants who scowled with a distinctly British hostility, and finally, a heavy coach-and-six.

Three pale, handsome faces gazed out from the carriage as it crossed the bridge, offering no return salute despite Harry recognizing the family arms. Behind the carriage, a gentleman glared down at him. Feeling terribly isolated, Harry considered returning to Captain Franks, where the *Rachel* seemed like a cheerful haven compared to this stone fortress. The innkeepers confirmed the passengers' identities: the Countess, her stepdaughter Lady Maria, her daughter Lady Fanny, and the young gentleman in the grey frock, Mr. William, who had nearly thrown Harry into the ditch.

Harry had refused to unpack his trunks, hoping for an invitation to the great house, but hours passed in silence. Just as darkness fell, a second carriage arrived, carrying a stout, high-complexioned lady—the Baroness Bernstein—who stared intently at Mr. Warrington. Soon, the throng of carriages and postillions disappeared behind the castle gates, leaving Harry alone in the inn, overwhelmed by a crushing sense of neglect and loneliness. He could not sleep, disturbed by the raucous laughter and curses emanating from the bar downstairs.

Eventually, Gumbo's voice was heard outside, remonstrating that his master was asleep. But a shrill, drunken voice cut him down, cursing Gumbo as a "stupid negro woolly-pate." The door burst open, admitting a flood of oaths and a disheveled young gentleman.

"Beg your pardon, Cousin Warrington!" cried the intruder, Mr. William Esmond. "Are you asleep? I beg forgiveness for riding you over on the bridge. I thought it was a lawyer with a writ—dressed in black, you know. Gad! I thought it was Nathan coming to nab me." Laughing incoherently, it was evident he was intoxicated.

"You did me great honor to mistake me for a sheriff's officer, cousin," Harry replied, sitting up in his tall nightcap with grave dignity.

"Gad! I thought it was Nathan, and was going to send you souse into the river," William continued, oblivious to Harry's distress. "But I ask your pardon. You see, I had been drinking at the Bell at Hexton... Hullo! You, Davis! A bowl of punch!"

"I have had my share for tonight, cousin," Harry insisted, maintaining his composed tone.

"You want me to go, Cousin What's-your-name?" William demanded, his voice dropping to a serious register. "You want me to go, and they want me to come, and I didn't want to come. I said I'd see him hanged first—that's what I said. Why should I trouble myself to come down all alone to look after a fellow I don't care a pin for? Zackly what I said. Zackly what Castlewood said. Why the devil should he go down? Castlewood says, and so said my lady, but the Baroness would have you. It's all the Baroness's doing, and if she says a thing, it must be done; so you must just get up and come."

Mr. Esmond spoke with rapid, indistinct amiability, tacking about the room. But the young Virginian's wrath flamed. "I tell you what, cousin," Harry cried, "I won't move for the Countess, or for the Baroness, or for all the cousins in Castlewood."

Just then, the landlord entered with the bowl of punch Mr. William had ordered. The young gentleman leaped from bed, furious. "Sot, you little tobacconist! Sot, you Cherokee!" William screamed, brandishing imaginary weapons. "Jump out of bed, and I'll drive my sword through your body. Why didn't I do it to-day when I took you for a bailiff? A confounded pettyfogging bum-bailiff!" He continued his tirade of oaths until the entire staff rushed to lead him away. Afterward, Harry pulled his blankets tight around him, sulking in deep wrath, and eventually succumbed to sleep.

By the next morning, the landlord was obsequious, having learned Harry's name and status. Other messengers had arrived from the castle, bringing both young gentlemen home. Poor Mr. William had returned in a wheelbarrow, seemingly unaware of his nocturnal exploits, though the men claimed he beat them overnight when drunk. "He is of a real kind nature," the landlord vowed, adding that when sober, William was the very kindest of young gentlemen.

While Harry awaited recognition, a domestic council convened within Castlewood House. Having found the note left by Harry, the family debated his reception. My Lord Castlewood reasoned that since they hadn't drowned the man on the bridge, they must invite him. Lady Fanny suggested politeness required one of the brothers to visit, noting the rough greeting they had given. Lord Castlewood agreed to let his brother William go, only for William to vehemently refuse, declaring he would be hanged if he went. Meanwhile, Lady Maria, finding the American "pretty enough," argued for seeing him despite his vulgarity. Lady Castlewood, however, stalled, suggesting they wait for the Baroness's counsel. Thus, hospitality remained in abeyance.

Finally, the Baroness Bernstein's carriage arrived, sparking immediate enthusiasm among the family. The state chamber was prepared, the tables laden with silver and wine. For an hour or two, the entire household attended to their guest. The Countess pressed every dish onto the Baroness, who drank freely of champagne and punch. Full of spirits, the Baroness regaled them with wicked stories of European society, causing the usually demure Countess and the younger generation to laugh and giggle. She spoke of her father, pointing to a portrait of the military gentleman in the red coat, declaring him the finest of the Esmond line, unlike his successors who possessed "more brains than a goose."

Her excitement peaked when the subject of the recent visitor arose. "Who has been here to-day?" she cried, highly agitated. "Harry Esmond Warrington, of Virginia," Lord Castlewood answered, recounting how William had nearly pitched him into the river. "A lad whom I pressed my lady the Countess to invite."

"For shame!" shrieked the Baroness. "Do you mean that a grandson of Henry Esmond... has been here, and none of you have offered him hospitality?"

"She is staying at the Inn," William stammered, trying to excuse himself.

"That he is staying at the Inn, and you are sitting there!" the old lady roared, slamming her hand on the table so violently the glasses danced. "Get me my hood—I'll go to the boy myself."

Lord Castlewood rose in wrath. "Madame the Baroness of Bernstein," he declared, "your ladyship is welcome to go; but as for me, I don't choose to have such words as 'shameful' applied to my conduct." He refused to fetch the boy, asserting his independence and the family's wealth, sinking back into his chair.

The Baroness looked at the humiliated family, then at her nephew. Leaning close, she whispered in German, "I had unright when I said the Colonel was the only man of the family. Thou canst, if thou willest, Eugene." To this, Lord Castlewood merely bowed

Colonel Henry Esmond, a veteran who had worn the uniform of a regiment under Queen Anne, found himself, at the close of her reign, entangled in the turbulent schemes for restoring the Stuart line to the throne. While the nation wisely preferred the House of Hanover, a handful of loyalists fled across the seas. Among them was Colonel Esmond, advised by friends to seek exile abroad. Though he deeply regretted his political involvement, and though the new monarch, Prince George, proved a sovereign of remarkable patience, Esmond’s friends eventually brokered a reconciliation, allowing him to return to a quieter life.

It is recorded that Esmond hailed from the ancient English line of Castlewood, in Hampshire. Rumor had it that King James II and his son had once offered him the title of Marquis, along with an Irish peerage inherited by his family, but Esmond, weary of court intrigues and domestic strife, had declined to regularize the matter through formal channels. Instead, tired of the European struggle, he turned his gaze to Virginia, where he held a substantial estate originally granted by King Charles I to his ancestor. There, amidst the rolling fields of the New World, his daughter was born, followed by twin grandsons, while his wife passed away.

She had been the widow of the Colonel’s cousin, the unfortunate Viscount Castlewood, who met his end in a duel with Lord Mohun at the conclusion of King William's reign. Esmond named his American home "Castlewood," modeling every aspect of its life upon the customs of his homeland. It was a fiercely loyal colony, where the Virginians proudly claimed that King Charles II had ruled over their soil before he ever ascended the throne in England. Both the English Crown and the Church were revered with singular devotion. The local gentry, allied with the best bloodlines of Albion, stood head and shoulders above the Dutch merchants of New York and the Puritan traders of Pennsylvania. Indeed, the spirit of republicanism was nearly absent in this great province, which would later lead the charge against the British Crown.

Life on a Virginia plantation was conducted with a patriarchal grandeur. Each estate commanded a multitude of hands—purchased and indentured servants—who lived under the absolute sway of their masters. The land provided food, livestock, and game in abundance; the great rivers teemed with fish, offering a clear path home. Ships loaded tobacco from private wharves on the Potomac and James Rivers, carrying it to London or Bristol in exchange for manufactured goods. Hospitality was boundless; no stranger was ever turned away. The gentry traveled and received one another in a manner that bordered on the feudal.

Yet, the institution of slavery, though not yet fully articulated as a moral crisis, was accepted without hesitation. To own black servants caused no shock among the Virginian gentlemen, nor was the dominion they exercised typically described as savage. Provisions were plentiful, and the enslaved population, largely indolent yet content, rarely suffered. One might have preached abolition to Madam Esmond of Castlewood with the same futility as telling her to release her horses from their stalls; she viewed the lash and the corn-bag as necessities for both beasts and men.

Her father, however, possessed a skeptical mind. While he harbored doubts, he never voiced them in open defiance, appearing more disaffected than rebellious. Once, in his youth, he had sought glory in public life, but in later years, he seemed indifferent to its rewards. Some unseen tragedy had left a permanent melancholy shadow over his soul. He remained kind and affectionate to those around him, obsequious toward the women of his family, rarely contradicting them, yet his heart appeared bankrupt, and he never truly enjoyed living. He merely submitted to life, waiting for its final curtain.

Upon his wife's death, his daughter assumed control of the Colonel's affairs, to which he readily surrendered his books and peace. When guests arrived, he entertained them with cheerful sarcasm, deriving genuine pleasure from their eventual departure. "My love, I shall not be sorry to go myself," he once remarked to his daughter, "and you, though the most affectionate of daughters, will console yourself after a while. Why should I, who am so old, be romantic? You may, who are still a young creature." He spoke thus, knowing full well that his daughter, a pragmatic and matter-of-fact soul, possessed little capacity for romance.

Fifteen years after settling in Virginia, the estate prospered so greatly that the Colonel acquiesced to his daughter's ambitious plan to replace his plain wooden dwelling with a mansion of enduring grandeur, fitting for the nobility of their name. While neighbors like the Warringtons erected stately homes fueled by ambition, the Colonel himself cared little for architecture or lineage. Yet, his daughter, possessing a towering sense of her ancestry, pressed forward. Her father, now serene and good-natured in his twilight years, amused himself by accommodating her peculiarities. With his extensive antiquarian knowledge and skill as a painter, he assisted her in constructing a genealogical tree. Having studied extensively in Europe, Miss Esmond returned with volumes on heraldry and documents proving the Esmonds' descent from Norman warriors and native kings. Her father painted two magnificent trees: one tracing the line from Emperor Charlemagne, depicted in plate armor with an imperial diadem, and the other from Queen Boudicca, portrayed in light drapery with a golden crown and fur mantle, her skin beautifully tattooed with blue designs. From these illustrious roots, the family tree bloomed until it converged in the thirteenth century upon a fortunate ancestor who claimed both lines.

Regarding the Warrington family into which she had married, the virtuous Madam Rachel paid scant attention. She signed her letters "Esmond-Warrington" but was universally known as Madam Esmond of Castlewood. After her father's passing, she ruled the domain, though her temper was occasionally soured by disputes over precedence within colonial society. Although her father had burned a Marquis's patent from King James—which she disowned—she often acted as if it remained valid, considering the English Esmonds inferior to her own branch and asserting her supremacy over the entire colonial aristocracy. Consequently, her notes record frequent quarrels, angry words, and occasional scuffles at Governor's assemblies in Jamestown. Yet, why recall these petty squabbles? The participants are gone, and the Republic has since dismantled these social hierarchies. Before independence, Virginia was perhaps the most aristocratic region on earth, and its inhabitants, including the young twins of Castlewood, were bred to hold the institutions of home in the highest reverence.

When the boys' grandfather died, their mother proclaimed her eldest son, George, the sole heir, instructing Harry, his younger brother by half an hour, to show him respect. The entire household, from the large and happy family of negroes to the indentured servants from Europe, was taught to honor George. Only two figures remained defiant: Madam Mountain, a faithful friend to the mistress, and Harry's foster-mother, a devoted black woman who could not comprehend why her child, whom she deemed handsomer, stronger, and cleverer, should not be placed first despite the twins' physical similarity.

In temperament, however, the brothers were stark opposites. George was peaceful, studious, and silent; Harry was warlike and noisy. Harry learned quickly once started but struggled to begin tasks, whereas George aided his brother willingly. Harry possessed a strong military bent, drilling the little negroes on the estate and cane-handling them like a corporal, engaging in boxing matches without holding grudges. George, conversely, was gentle and sparing of blows. In accordance with custom, each boy had a personal attendant. It became notorious that George, finding his small black servant asleep on his bed, sat beside him to brush flies away with a feather fan. This act horrified Old Gumbo, the child's father, and enraged Madam Esmond, who ordered the boy to the overseer for a whipping.

George pleaded passionately, bursting into tears and begging for mercy. Despite his entreaties, his mother remained unmoved regarding the punishment. The little negro departed, imploring his young master not to weep. A fierce conflict erupted between mother and son. Refusing to be pacified, the boy declared the punishment a shameful affront, insisting that as master, he alone had authority over his servant, and no one—not even his mother—could lay a hand upon him. He vowed that upon reaching maturity, he would free young Gumbo. He visited the slave quarters and presented the boy with one of his own toys.

The young black child was an impudent, lazy, and saucy spirit who, the Colonel presumably believed, would benefit far more from a whipping than kindness. He acquiesced to the punishment when Madam Esmond insisted, only laughing in his benevolent fashion when his indignant grandson shouted, "You let Mamma rule you in everything, Grandpapa!"

"Why, so I do," admitted Grandpapa. "Rachel, my love, how obvious my petticoat-ridden state is that even this baby has discovered it."

"Then why don't you stand up like a man?" queried little Harry, eager to support his brother.

Grandpapa looked bewildered. "Because I like sitting down best, my dear," he explained gently. "I am an old gentleman, and standing fatigues me."

Due to a certain whimsical humor shared with the old man and a liking for his pursuits, the first-born twin became Grandpapa's favorite companion, pouring out his infant heart to the elderly gentleman, to whom the younger brother rarely spoke. George, a demure scholar, flourished in

If one possessed kind neighbors, a respectable lineage, ample wealth, a comfortable home, a loyal household of servants, robust health, affectionate offspring, and a competent staff, surely one ought to be deemed happily blessed? Madam Esmond-Warrington, later known as Madam Esmond, possessed every blessing save contentment. She recited them in prayer twice daily, yet remained perpetually dissatisfied. To the country gentry of Westmoreland County, Virginia, she appeared as "Princess Pocahontas," reigning from her castle of Castlewood with a whimsical despotism. While the local poor bowed to her charity and the lesser gentlemen lost games of backgammon to her wit, the reality behind the throne was far starker. Having lost her husband early, she ruled alone, dominating her household with a rigid, uncompromising will. She believed obedience was a virtue and resistance a sin, often engaging in fierce battles with anyone who dared question her authority. Though her father, the Colonel, had once amused himself with her caprices, his passing left her unchecked, transforming her into a tyrant over her own domain.

Her reign was marked by a shift from the modesty of her youth to a flamboyant assertion of status. She discarded her maiden name, embracing "Madam Esmond" with pride, frequently invoking the lost titles of her ancestors bestowed by King James. While her half-brother, Lord Castlewood, might have claimed precedence, she reserved it solely for Lord Fairfax, the colony's highest civilian authority, and yielded gracefully only to the wife of the Governor, representing the Sovereign. Her relations with the English relatives of her husband, the Warringtons, were strained to the point of rupture. A single encounter in London, where gossip regarding her treatment of her kin reached the ears of the English aristocracy, caused correspondence to cease entirely. Letters sent to the Warringtons and the Hampshire Esmonds lay unanswered, a silence that fueled her fury and further isolated her within Virginia society.

The internal fractures at Castlewood ran deep, particularly concerning her two sons, George and Harry. The first rift opened over the dismissal of their former tutor, Mr. Dempster. Madam Esmond, suspicious of anything intellectual, viewed him as a dangerous Jesuit in disguise. Where George stood firmly by his old master, Harry, who preferred sport and nature to books, maintained a distance. However, the true storm broke over the inheritance of six thousand pounds due to Harry upon the death of his uncle. As the sole beneficiary, Harry was entitled to the sum, yet Madam Esmond, convinced she was its rightful owner, refused to release it. She raged against the London lawyers, calling them insolent pettifoggers, and threatened to buy estates and slaves to provide for her younger son, despite the legal impossibility.

When pressed, George sided with reason and the law, refusing to violate his oath as executor, while Madam Esmond saw only betrayal. Tears, accusations, and bitter pleas tore through the household. She lamented Harry's "slender younger brother's pittance," accusing George of selfishness, though he offered no defense other than love. This tragedy shifted the family's focus; the construction of grand halls ceased, imports of luxury furniture halted, and the family carriage, once a symbol of pride, became a vehicle for sorrow. During Sunday rides, the scene was one of poignant division. Madam Esmond, seated opposite her sons, alternated between groans for Harry's lack of fortune and grim glances at George's apparent indifference. Her companion, Mrs. Mountain, a pragmatic widow, gently challenged the mother's obsession, noting that the estate's needs did not require such frantic hoarding.

"You will give me plenty while you live, and George will give me plenty when you die," Harry declared cheerfully, attempting to lighten the mood.

"Not unless he changes in spirit, my dear!" the mother snapped, her gaze hardening on George. "Unless Heaven softens his heart and teaches him charity!"

Mrs. Mountain interjected with a knowing chuckle, suggesting the mother was already disturbing herself unnecessarily. "Humph! I know you are always disturbing yourself..." she began, but the tension escalated. When George, overwhelmed by his mother's tears, begged for a blessing, Harry stepped forward, wrapping his arms around his older brother's neck and kissing him repeatedly. "Never mind, George. I know whether you are a good brother or not," Harry assured him. "Don't mind what she says. She doesn't mean it."

"And so it is, Harry," Mrs. Mountain agreed, shaking the boy's hand. "You never said a truer word."

Enraged, Madam Esmond accused her companion of setting her children against one another, threatening to turn them out. Mrs. Mountain, undeterred, retorted that she would pack immediately rather than endure false flattery, declaring she brought little to Castlewood and would take little away. Before the argument could escalate further, the church bells rang. The widow, softened by the sight of her children sitting close together during the sermon, attempted to compose herself, looking with profound affection at both boys.

Later, the narrator recounted these tales to an elderly listener, Madam Bernstein, who found infinite amusement in the stories. They conversed in a wainscoted parlor adorned with portraits of the Esmond line. Above Madam Bernstein's chair hung a vibrant painting by Sir Godfrey Kneller of a young lady from Queen Anne's time—a vision of beauty with auburn hair and fair skin.

"Are you my cousin?" the boy asked, noticing the resemblance.

"That picture is by Sir Godfrey," Madam Bernstein explained, pointing to the canvas. "He thought himself the greatest painter, but he was surpassed by Lely, who painted your grandmother—my Lady Castlewood—and by Van Dyck, who painted your great-grandfather. Some of us are painted darker than we are. Does that portrait remind you of anyone?"

"No, indeed," Harry replied.

"Ahem!" sighed the lady. "Here is a sermon! Harry, that face was mine once. My name was Beatrix Esmond. And your mother is my half-sister, child, and she has never even mentioned my name!"

As Harry Warrington recounted his tales of adventure to his newfound kin, Lady Bernstein—possessed of a sharp wit and a worldly wisdom—formed her judgments upon the characters and events described. If her verdict was not wholly favorable, it is a testament to the imperfection of mankind that human life is rarely entirely pleasant or profitable. The court-lady recoiled at the mere idea of her American cousin's rustic existence; such a life would prove wearisome to any bred in the city. Yet little Madam Warrington knew no better, content with her lot as she was with herself. While epicures disdain coarse fare, it does not follow that Hodge suffers misery over his bread and bacon. Madam Warrington led a life of duties, humdrum yet pleasing to her spirit. A brisk woman of business, she kept the affairs of her vast estate under her immediate gaze. No pie was baked at Castlewood without her little finger stirring the pot; she directed the maids, supervised the kitchen wenches, rode afield on her pony, and oversaw the overseers and enslaved hands in the tobacco and corn fields. If a slave fell ill, she visited his quarters in any weather, administering remedies with great resolve. She possessed a tome of old-fashioned receipts, a distillery for waters and elixirs, and a medicine chest that terrified her neighbors, who trembled at the prospect of her decoctions and pills.

A century prior, Virginia held scarce any towns; the gentry's establishments were little villages inhabited by themselves and their vassals. Rachel Esmond ruled Castlewood like a miniature queen, while neighboring "princes" governed their domains. Many were needy potentates, living plentifully yet roughly, with domestics whose liveries were often ragged. They kept open houses, welcoming every stranger, and were proud, idle, and fond of field sports becoming to gentlemen of noble lineage. The Widow of Castlewood was as hospitable as her neighbors and a better economist than most. Though many would have shared her life-interest in the estate to serve as fathers to her boys, where lay a man worthy of a ladyship of her exalted birth? Gossip spoke of installing the Duke of Cumberland as viceroy or even King of America. Madam Warrington's gossips laughed, suggesting she waited for him. With grave dignity, she remarked that persons of his royal blood had already offered alliance to the Esmond family.

Under her command served an officer's widow, previously named, who had been Madam Esmond's school companion, just as her late husband had been the regimental friend of the late Mr. Warrington. At the Kensington Academy, where Rachel Esmond received her education, English girls teased and tormented the little American stranger, mocking the princified airs she adopted from youth. Fanny Parker, however, defended and befriended her. Both married ensigns in Kingsley's Regiment and became tenderly attached, exchanging letters filled with "my Fanny" and "my Rachel." Tragically, Fanny's husband died in sad straits, leaving no provision for his widow or infant child. Later, during one of Captain Franks's annual voyages, he brought Mrs. Mountain aboard the *Young Rachel* to Virginia.

There was ample room at Castlewood House, and Mrs. Mountain served to enliven the place. She played cards with the mistress, possessed some musical knowledge to assist the eldest boy, laughed with guests, attended to strangers' chambers, and presided over the presses and linens. A kind, brisk, jolly-looking widow, more than one unmarried colonial gentleman sought to change her name for his own. Yet she chose to retain "Mountain," perhaps because it had brought her no fortune. "One marriage was enough," she declared, noting that Mr. Mountain had amiably spent her small fortune and his own. Her final trinkets paid for his funeral, and as long as Madam Warrington would keep her, she preferred a home without a husband to any offer made to her in Virginia. The two ladies quarreled plentifully yet loved one another deeply; they made up differences only to fall out again, reconciled once more. When either boy fell ill, each lady vied with the other in maternal tenderness. In his final days, Mrs. Mountain's cheerfulness and kindness were greatly appreciated by the Colonel, whose memory Madam Warrington cherished above all living persons. Thus, whenever Captain Franks asked if she intended to return on his next voyage, she declined, proposing to stay another year.

When suitors approached Madam Warrington, she received their compliments kindly, often inquiring if it was Mrs. Mountain they sought. She used her best offices for Mountain, declaring that Fanny—a girl of good English family—would make any gentleman happy. Should a suitor insist he courted her daughter rather than her dependent, she would curtsy gravely, confess her utter mistake, and remind him that the daughter of the Marquis of Esmond lived for her people and sons, showing no intent to alter her condition. Had we not read how Queen Elizabeth, a sensible woman of business, inspired both terror and love? So the little Virginian princess had her favorites, accepted their flatteries, grew weary of them, and treated them with cruelty or kindness suited to her wayward imperial humor. Compliments, however slight, she graciously received as her due. Her foibles were so well known that wags practiced upon them. Rattling Jack Firebrace of Henrico County held free quarters for months, a favorite because he versified stolen from pocket-books. Tom Humbold of Spotsylvania wagered fifty hogsheads against five that he would induce her to institute an Order of Knighthood, and he won.

The elder boy observed these freaks and chafed at them. From early days, he revolted against flattery, striving to expose it with juvenile satire. Consequently, his mother would say gravely, "The Esmonds were always of a jealous disposition, and my poor boy takes after my parents." George hated Jack Firebrace and Tom Humbold, whereas Harry sported with them, engaging in fowling, fishing, cock-fighting, and enjoying the rural fun.

One winter, after dismissing their first tutor, Madam Esmond took the boys to Williamsburg for education. There, it proved fortunate for the family to hear the famous Mr. Whitfield preach. Unlike many neighboring provinces, Virginia was a Church of England colony; clergymen were state-paid with glebes, yet lacking a bishop, colonists imported divines from the mother country. These arrivals were often noblemen's hangers-on or insolvent parsons seeking a living, bringing stained cassocks to the colony. It is no wonder Whitfield's great voice stirred those whom harmless Mr. Broadbent, the Williamsburg chaplain, could never awaken. Initially, the boys were as excited as their mother, singing hymns and listening with fervor. If Whitfield had stayed longer, Harry and George probably would have worn black coats instead of epaulettes. The simple boys communicated their experiences, constantly watching for the sacred "call," thrilled by the fervor sweeping Protestant England.

However, Mr. Whitfield could not remain with the small Williamsburg congregation forever. His mission was to enlighten the benighted church from east to west, trumpeting truth to slumbering sinners. Nevertheless, he comforted the widow with precious letters, promising a tutor capable of teaching not only profane learning but also strengthening the faith. Soon, a chosen vessel arrived from England. Young Mr. Ward possessed a voice as loud as Whitfield's and talked almost as readily and at length. Nights and evenings echoed with his exhortations; domestic negroes crept to doors to listen, darkening porch windows with their crisp heads. Most notably, Mr. Ward influenced the "black sheep" of the Castlewood flock. These woolly lambs were immensely affected by his words, and when he gave out a hymn, a negro chorus rang out across the Potomac—a sound unheard in the Colonel's time, for the worthy gentleman suspected all cassocks and refused controversy with clergymen save over backgammon. Where money was needed for charity, no man was more ready. The good Virginian clergyman, who loved backgammon, asserted that the Colonel's charity covered his shortcomings.

Ward was handsome, and his preaching pleased Madam Esmond immediately, satisfying her as much as Whitfield's. Women a hundred years ago were credulous, eager to admire and believe, imagining excellences in objects of their devotion. For weeks, nay months, Madam Esmond never tire of hearing Mr. Ward's glib voice and voluble commonplaces. As was her wont, she insisted neighbors attend and be converted. Her young favorite, Mr. Washington, she eagerly sought to influence, pressing him repeatedly to stay at Castlewood and benefit spiritually. Yet the young gentleman found particular business calling him home or away, ordering his horse every evening when Mr. Ward's exercises commenced. And what boys are just to their pedagogue? The twins quickly grew tired, even rebellious.

They found him a bad scholar, a dull fellow, and ill-bred. George knew more Latin and Greek than his master, catching him in perpetual blunders and false quantities. Harry, taking greater liberties, mimicked Ward's manner of eating and talking until Mrs. Mountain and Madam Esmond were forced to laugh, and little Fanny Mountain crowed with delight. Madam Esmond might have exposed him as a vulgar quack but for her sons' opposition, which she countered with indomitable will. "What matters whether he has more or less of profane learning?" she asked. "In that which is most precious, Mr. W. is

Following the departure of her discredited spiritual advisor, Madam Esmond and her son finally reached a truce. Yet, though George never voiced the grievance, the shadow of the quarrel haunted him deeply. Soon after these domestic upheavals, a fever seized the boy, driving him into a delirium wherein he screamed, "Broken! Broken! It never, never can be mended!" His mother sat vigil beside his tossing form, terrified by his ravings. Her remedies proved futile; the malady grew stronger despite the salves and potions she hoarded. Desperate, she sought out Mr. Dempster, riding through the snow on her pony to plead for his aid. With characteristic humility, Dempster promised to bury his resentment as she had her pride. He gathered his supplies and his sole enslaved attendant, returning to Castlewood with a renewed hope. For several days, it seemed likely that poor Harry would inherit the estate, but Dempster's skill prevailed. The fever broke, the spasms subsided, and George was restored to near-health.

A physician advised a voyage to England for a change of air, but the widow, contrite for her past haste in quarreling with her kin, decided against it. Instead, they resolved on a journey north and east. Accompanied by Mr. Dempster as tutor and two servants, the two youths sailed to New York and ascended the majestic Hudson River to Albany. There, they were welcomed by the province's elite before traveling deeper into French territory, where they received warm hospitality from the local gentry. While Harry spent his time camping with the Indigenous tribes, trading furs, and hunting bears, George, whose constitution remained fragile, became a darling of the French ladies. Fluent in the language, he mastered the *bel air*, dancing the minuet with grace and playing the violin with such proficiency that he could easily pass as a native Frenchman. He even cultivated a romantic entanglement with a young ensign of the Auvergne regiment, the Chevalier de la Jabotiere, dueling honorably with him and swearing an eternal friendship—a feat Harry, busy on a hunt, could only envy. Madame de Mouchy, the Superintendent's lady, wrote a glowing letter to Madam Esmond praising her son's conduct, a triumph that likely would have pained Mr. Whitfield.

Upon their return after ten delightful months, the transformation was striking. George had grown to match his younger brother in stature, the twins nearly indistinguishable save for their hair: George's raven tresses and Harry's light locks bound with ribbons. However, the traveler who had followed the lad's early history would note that George Esmond retained a jealous and suspicious nature, generous yet incapable of forgiveness, and unable to harbor goodwill toward an honorable gentleman whose name would later echo through the ages. Though Mr. Washington, then barely of age, possessed an aura of maturity and gravity beyond his years—trusted by the gentry as a manager of affairs since childhood—George returned from his travels unchanged in his low opinion of his mother's favorite friend.

Meanwhile, Mrs. Mountain, Madam Esmond's droll and jealous companion, delighted in speculating on matrimonial designs. She believed every unmarried man harbored intentions for the mistress of the mansion. She convinced herself that the odious Mr. Ward courted the widow, who reciprocated, and that the ambitious Mr. Washington, though fifteen years her junior, sought a wealthy bride. "Great tall men always marry little chits," she reasoned, dismissing the height disparity between the six-foot-two Washington and the five-foot-three widow. When she shared these "sage" theories with George, begging him to listen, he sternly interjected, "For Heaven's sake, hold your tongue." Mrs. Mountain protested that she could not keep her eyes shut, citing the Colonel's constant presence and the dedicated "room" at Castlewood reserved for him. "One day will come when he won't go away," she sighed.

Consequently, George adopted a frigid courtesy toward the Colonel, which chafed the honest gentleman, while Mrs. Mountain resorted to stinging sarcasm that George would have torn through like brambles. George retreated to his study, sulking amidst books of mathematics, French, and Latin, growing increasingly isolated.

While Harry was away with sporting companions, the Major visited Castlewood. Tender and kind to the housekeeper, he received a special cordiality that nearly ignited George's jealousy. Yet, this visit marked an adieu. Major Washington was departing for a perilous journey to the western Virginia frontier and beyond. News had reached them of French incursions into British territory; the Lords of Canada and Louisiana were aggressively expanding, driving settlers from their homes. Under the convenient legal fiction that coastal possession entitled one to all inland lands to the Pacific, the British charters failed to define eastern boundaries, leaving the colonies vulnerable. The French, meanwhile, aimed to connect their northern and southern colonies via the Mississippi and the Great Lakes. By 1753, the diplomatic pretense collapsed on the Ohio River, where British and French settlers clashed. Native Americans, the true proprietors of the soil, were ignored by the European logicians of St. James's and Versailles, their tomahawks merely tools in a dispute they deemed strictly European.

The Ohio Company, holding grants from Virginia, found their settlements invaded by French military detachments. Seeking protection, they appealed to Lieutenant-Governor Dinwiddie, who dispatched an embassy to demand the cessation of hostilities. Young Mr. Washington eagerly volunteered, leaving his rural pursuits to lead a guide, an interpreter, and a small retinue across the wilderness in the fall of 1753. Reaching Fort Le Boeuf, he delivered Governor Dinwiddie's message, only to receive a blunt refusal: the French held the place and intended to possess the entire Ohio region.

Returning through danger, across frozen rivers and lonely forests, Washington faced a different reality upon his arrival home. Harry, returning from a cockfight, lamented missing such noble sport, while the Colonel, now a hero of sorts, was lavishly praised by Madam Esmond. "Think of you," she exclaimed, contrasting Harry's frivolities with the Major's watchfulness over the frozen wilds. "I wish my eldest son were doing something in the service of his country!" George, however, offered a pragmatic plea: "I desire no better than to go home and seek for employment... You surely will not have me serve under Mr. Washington?" The widow refused, insisting an Esmond could only serve with a King's commission.

Washington, meanwhile, raised a regiment of Virginians, hoping to impose a peremptory veto on the French invaders. A smaller force under Colonel Trent had been sent ahead to fortify a site on the Pennsylvania border—the future location of Pittsburgh. There, Trent's forty men, vastly outnumbered by Canadians, were forced to withdraw. The French seized the fort, strengthened it, and named it Fort Duquesne in honor of their governor. War had not yet been declared; the guns were loaded, but no order had been given. It was a strange irony that a young Virginian officer, firing a single shot, would awaken a conflict destined to last sixty years, reshape continents, and ultimately birth a great republic, leaving the prize of fame to the man who struck the first blow.

Little did he know his fate. A simple gentleman eager to serve his King, he commanded a small body of provincial troops the following year. He encountered the French vanguard and, in a moment of impulse, fired upon them, killing their leader. But the odds were insurmountable; he was compelled to retreat and eventually capitulate. On July 4, 1754, Colonel Washington marched out from his hastily built Fort Necessity, surrendered to the conqueror, and departed.

Saddened and humbled, the young officer returned to Castlewood. Before his departure, he had boasted of victory, angering George with his perceived scorn. Now, standing before his old friend, he confessed his shame. "I was angry when I parted from you," he admitted, extending a hand that George grasped eagerly. "I thought you laughed at us. My ridicule made me angry, and I boasted too much."

"Nay, you have done your best, George," Harry replied, his jealousy forgotten in sympathy for his fallen comrade. "A hundred and fifty starving men with scarce ammunition could not face five times their number. Everybody knows Mr. Washington would do his duty."

"Oh, but that some of ours were here!" cried Madam Esmond, tossing her head. "I promise you a few good English regiments would make the white-coats run."

"You think nothing of the provincials," Washington said gloomily, hanging his head. "All those fine dreams are over. 'Tis kind of your ladyship to receive a poor beaten fellow."

George, moved by the Colonel's raw emotion, prepared to offer words of comfort, but his mother interrupted. "Kind of us to receive you, Colonel Washington!" she declared, performing a deep curtsy. "I never heard that when men were unhappy, our sex were less their friends."

The gesture, meant as kindness, instantly reignited George's jealousy, casting a new, darker shadow over the reunion.

Few causes exist to warrant admiration quite so strongly as the combination of courage, youth, beauty, and adversity. To Madame Esmond, the young soldier possessed a claim upon her affection that outweighed all others, even if she had borne twenty sons. Her praise for Mr. Washington grew so fervid that it echoed through every gathering she attended. Before her children, she contrasted his sterling virtues sharply with Harry's proclivity for pleasure—labeling him "the wild boy"—and George's melancholic immersion in books. Yet George remained unmoved by her extravagant eulogies. Instead, the jealousy festering within him curdled into a venomous malice, making him a torment to himself and his circle. He delivered barbs so biting and obscure that his simple mother sat bewildered, unable to decipher the depth of his sarcasm or comprehend his saturnine moods.

While domestic tensions simmered, the wider world shifted gears with terrifying momentum. The skirmishes between the French and English in North America, once merely provincial disputes, had swollen into a national conflict. Reinforcements poured from France into Canada, and English troops began to assemble in Virginia. From Quebec, Madame la Presidente de Mouchy wrote to George Warrington with a mixture of sorrow and pride. She lamented that fate seemed to conspire against them, seeing him abandon his beloved mother to throw himself into the arms of Bellona. She pictured him returning wounded from combat, hesitating to wish victory for the French "lilies" while he stood beneath the banners of the Leopard. Though enemies by nation, hearts remained at peace; everyone in Quebec saluted him, including the Chevalier de la Jabotière, who burned with rivalry, and Monsieur the Bear-hunter, George's stoic brother who preferred the hunt to feminine conversation. Even M. Du Quesne, her husband, sent his regards to the young friend.

Indeed, the Leopard flag snapped violently in the wind. A resolve hardened in the camps to reclaim every conquest the French had made upon British soil. Regiments were raised and paid locally, and a formidable fleet sailed from home under an experienced commander. By February 1755, Commodore Keppel, commanding the famous *Centurion*—the same vessel in which Anson had circumnavigated the globe—anchored in Hampton Roads. Beside him lay General Braddock, his staff, and a contingent of troops, appointed by the Duke of Cumberland. A flotilla of transports swiftly followed, laden with provisions, men, and funds.

General Braddock disembarked at Alexandria on the Potomac and journeyed to Annapolis, Maryland, where he convened the colonial governors. He urged them to mobilize their provinces for the common defense, a summons that rippled through the territories with immense excitement. Nowhere was the stir greater than at Castlewood. Harry immediately sought out the encamped troops at Alexandria. Their orderly lines and the stirring beat of fife and drum thrilled him. He quickly befriended the officers of both regiments, longing to join the expedition, and became a welcomed guest at their mess.

Madame Esmond viewed this exposure to fashionable English gentlemen with delight. She believed their company would refine her sons, marking a stark improvement over the local Virginian squires, whose pastimes involved horse racing and cockfighting, or the petty lawyers and schemers at the lieutenant-governor's table. She possessed a keen eye for flattery and despised the officious etiquette and squabbles for precedence at Williamsburg. However, her perception of the King's officers proved woefully naive. While Harry and George politely smiled at her compliments regarding their elegance, the reality behind the scenes was far grimmer. If Madame Esmond had heard the raucous jokes and bawdy songs sung over wine and punch, or witnessed the disheveled state of many soldiers dragged to their lodgings, she would have recoiled. These men swaggered through the countryside, frightening peaceful villagers with their riot. The General raged against their disorder and the "traitorous niggardliness" of the provincials. The soldiers treated the land as conquered territory, scorning the locals and insulting the wives of their Indian allies who had traveled to fight alongside them. Driven to madness by this conduct, the General forbade the presence of Indian women in his camp. Humiliated and outraged, their husbands withdrew, costing the General their invaluable support mere months later.

Yet, certain narratives were forbidden to Madame Esmond. Those officers who visited Castlewood upon her invitation were, in truth, models of courtesy. Soon, the General himself dispatched polite missives to Mistress of Castlewood. Citing his service under the Duke of Marlborough alongside her late father, Colonel Esmond, he requested the honor of calling upon her. If she had understood the motive behind this sudden politeness, her charm might have faded. At Alexandria, the Commander-in-Chief held levees, and among the visiting gentry were the twin sons of Castlewood, arriving on fine steeds in their finest London attire, accompanied by their black servants in smart liveries. The General, however, was in a foul temper with the provincial gentry and barely acknowledged the youths, casually querying his aide-de-camp over dinner who these "young Squire Gawkeys" in blue and gold waistcoats were.

Mr. Dinwiddie, the Lieutenant-Governor, and a gentleman from Pennsylvania happened to dine with his Excellency. Upon learning the identity of the visitors—"the sons of the Princess Pocahontas," Dinwiddie noted—the General erupted in a torrent of oaths, demanding to know who such a "deuce" a princess was. Dinwiddie, harboring a lifelong grudge against the imperious Madame Esmond, seized the opportunity to ridicule her pomposity and vast pretensions. He spun absurd anecdotes until the General succumbed to sleep. When the General awoke, Dinwiddie had departed, but the Philadelphia gentleman remained, deep in conversation with the officers. Resuming the topic, the General spoke of Madame Esmond in curt, disrespectful terms typical of soldiers of that era, again questioning the name of the "old fool" Dinwiddie had mentioned. He vented his contempt for the gentry and the country at large.

Then, Benjamin Franklin of Philadelphia interjected. He offered a diametrically opposed view of Madame Esmond, demonstrating a profound knowledge of her character, her father, and her extensive estates. He explained to the General that she possessed ample beef, horses, and stores, vital assets for the current juncture, and strongly advised conciliating her. The General, already impressed by Franklin's acumen, graciously invited the two young men to dinner the following day. True to their station, Harry and George conducted themselves with modesty and breeding, returning home delighted. Their mother, equally pleased by the civilities extended to her boys, promptly penned a gracious reply, inviting the General to visit Castlewood whenever it suited him.

News of the approaching campaign became the dominant topic at Castlewood. For Harry, the prospect of war was a dream realized; he dreamed only of battle, spent his days polishing the household firearms and swords, and drilled the negroes. Madame Esmond, possessing a gallant spirit herself, sensed the time had come for one of her sons to serve the Crown. She trembled as she awaited the decision, admiring the elder but loving the younger with a passion that threatened to overwhelm her reason.

Though eager, Harry dared not broach the subject directly, fearing the gravity of the moment. Twice he tried to mention it to George, whose countenance darkened ominously at the suggestion. Harry, bound by a feudal devotion to his brother, yielded to him in all matters, viewing George as the chief. Yet, to his terror, he observed George preoccupied with military strategy, surrounded by books on the campaigns of Eugene and Marlborough, and practicing fencing with Dempster, the old Scotsman who was a master of martial arts.

When Madame Esmond entrusted her boys with the General's letter, she attached substantial presents for the staff and officers, gifts so generous that the General repeatedly thanked Franklin for securing this alliance. "Would not one of the young gentlemen like to see the campaign?" the General asked. He noted that a friend of theirs, Mr. Washington—who had been unfortunate in the previous year's affair—had already promised to serve as aide-de-camp, and expressed a willingness to accept another Virginian gentleman. Harry's eyes lit up, his face flushing with joy. "With all my heart!" he cried. George, fixing a stern gaze upon his brother, countered that one would accompany the General, while the other's duty lay in caring for their mother. Harry, obedient to his senior's will despite his desperate desire to go, fell silent. On the ride home, they traversed miles in silence, each reading the other's mind, terrified to force the issue.

Upon reaching home, they revealed the General's offer to their mother. "I knew it must happen," she murmured. "At such a crisis, our family must stand forward. Have you decided?" She looked anxiously from one to the other, dreading the answer. "The youngest ought to go, Mother; of course I ought to go!" blurted Harry, turning crimson. "Of course he ought," agreed Mrs. Mountain, who had been listening. "There! Mountain says so! I told you so!" Harry exclaimed, casting a sidelong glance at George. "The head of the family ought to go, Mother," George said softly. "No! No! You are ill, and have never fully recovered from your fever. Should he go, Mountain?" "You would make the best soldier, I know, dearest Hal," Mrs. Mountain replied gently.

On the appointed day for Madame Esmond's entertainment to the General, the estate of Castlewood was draped in a splendor that bordered on the theatrical. Madame Esmond, arrayed in a gown far more magnificent than her custom, endeavored to cloak the inherent sadness of the occasion with a veneer of radiant hospitality. Her guest, the General's new aide-de-camp, arrived first. This young man, whom she received beneath the shelter of the covered gallery, was none other than Mr. Washington. In the colony, few equaled him in the mastery of horse and saddle; his mount was a creature of pure spirit, matching the vigor of its rider.

Before retiring indoors to remove his riding-boots, the Major and his hostess traversed the length of the gallery, engaged in a discourse that wove together personal news and public affairs. She sought his confirmation regarding his appointment to General Braddock's staff and expressed her anxiety concerning her son's imminent departure. As they spoke, the silence was punctuated by the rhythmic tread of negro servants carrying dishes for the impending feast. They moved from the gallery down to the rough lawn, pausing briefly in the shade before proceeding to the house. Mr. Washington announced the speedy arrival of His Excellency, accompanied by Mr. Franklin of Pennsylvania.

Mrs. Esmond recalled hearing that Mr. Franklin had begun life as a printer's boy. To her mind, it was a curious trajectory for a man to ride in the carriage of the Commander-in-Chief. Yet, Mr. Washington countered with a defense of the printer's character, describing him as a shrewd, sensible, and highly meritorious gentleman. Madame Esmond, however, remained convinced that the Major was too generously disposed toward a man of such humble origins. "At least," she declared, in a tone of stately resolve, "since my boy is to embark on this campaign, he will be surrounded not by tradesmen, but by gentlemen of honor and fashion."

Mr. Washington, having witnessed the vices of the courtly set, wisely refrained from exposing the flaws of "honorable" men to a young mind entering the world. Instead, he posited that Harry and George must accept the full spectrum of human experience, listening to both the wise and the foolish. The widow, trembling slightly, invoked the image of a veteran officer and friends of the Duke, pleading with her guest to ensure her son's safety. She emphasized the deep trust her father, Colonel Esmond, had placed in Mr. Washington, urging him to act as a surrogate father figure.

"You know I would have done anything to win Colonel Esmond's favor," the gentleman replied, bowing with a grace that belied his internal turmoil. "Madam, what venture would I not make to deserve the affections of your daughter?" The lady blushed, executing a curtsey of unrivaled elegance, and promised that so long as he cherished her children, he would hold her eternal gratitude. As they embraced, George emerged from within, meticulously powdered and attired in his finery. Unlike the modern youth who might swagger with a cigar in hand, George approached with low, respectful bows. Yet, despite his outward deference, his countenance betrayed a storm. A polite smile masked the watchfulness and wrath burning in his eyes.

"What manner of game?" the Colonel asked, seeking to lighten the mood. "None," George replied curtly, turning his gaze to the portrait of his father hanging in the parlor. The Colonel, usually taciturn, launched into a lengthy account of his morning hunt. He described the absurdity of General Braddock's heavy frame and massive boots as he floundered through the Virginian undergrowth, pursued by a motley pack of dogs and Negro hunters who barked with equal fervor. "Great God!" Braddock had exclaimed, panting, "what would Sir Robert say in Norfolk to see a man hunting with a fowling-piece in hand, chasing a turkey!"

Madame Esmond laughed gently, mistaking the anecdote for local trivia, unaware that the Colonel referred to Sir Robert Walpole's estates in England. Young Esmond, however, seized upon the moment with a sneer. "And yet," he scoffed, "there is good sport here."

"What sport?" the Colonel demanded, his temper rising.

"Why, surely you know," George interjected, feigning innocence, "without staring at me so fiercely, as if preparing to draw a foil. Are you not the premier sportsman of the countryside? Do you not command the fish of the stream, the beasts of the forest, and the fowl of the sky?" He listed the myriad creatures of the wild—shad, salmon, roe-deer, buffalo—with a listlessness that suggested he found the very concept repulsive. "I am no sportsman," he admitted. "I am a milksop, bred at my mother's knee. Behold these apron-strings!" He gestured to the ribbon pinned to his coat. "Who would not cherish such charms? Remember, they were once black—for my grandfather."

"And who would not mourn for a gentleman so tender-hearted?" the Colonel asked, though his expression remained stern.

"In truth, I wish my grandfather were here," George continued, his voice dropping to a whisper, "to rise from his tomb and restore my father, the Ensign."

"Oh, Harry!" Madame Esmond cried, her composure breaking as her younger son entered the room, dressed in a gold-corded frock and silver-hilted sword, a mirror of his elder brother. She flew to embrace him, tears streaming down her cheeks. Harry, confused by his mother's distress, asked what troubled her, turning to the Colonel for explanation. The Major, biting his lip, confessed his inability to decipher the source of her sorrow.

"A mere inquiry about pink ribbons," George explained, attempting to diffuse the tension. "Ribbons that seem to possess a magical power over our mother's heart, don't they, Colonel?"

"Speak for yourself, sir!" the Colonel snapped, his patience wearing thin. "Do you speak for yourself?"

"He speaks too much for himself," the widow wept, overwhelmed by the emotional turbulence of her household.

"I confess," George retorted, spinning on his scarlet heels, "I know not the source of these tears any better than the source of the Nile. If the portrait of my father began to weep, I would marvel at paternal sorrow as greatly as I do at hers." He picked up one of Madame Esmond's bright pink ribbons, treating it with a strange reverence. "I hate sport," he declared, "which you and the Colonel love. I would kill nothing alive—not a turkey, nor a titmouse, nor an ox. I care for nothing but roses."

The Colonel, taken aback by the sudden shift from aggression to effeminacy, retreated to the balcony, taking a modest draught from a bowl of apple-toddy to cool his agitation. Inside, the atmosphere shifted again. Almost reconciled, the mother offered a hand to each son. George placed his free hand on Harry's shoulder. "If you dislike sport, and prefer quietude, why not remain here?" Harry proposed, his face flushing. "Let me go forth with Colonel Washington and Mr. Braddock. Honneur oblige, after all; my name comes first."

"One must stay," George argued, "to tend to Mother. We cannot afford to be both scalped by Indians or fricasseed by the French."

"Fricasseed by the French!" Harry exclaimed, indignation rising. "They are the best troops in the world! Englishmen! I long to witness their defeat! What a thrashing you will deliver!"

As George sat at the harpsichord, playing and singing *"Malbrouk s'en va-t-en guerre"* with a mechanical precision, the gentleman from the balcony returned. "I play 'God Save the King' in tribute to the expedition," George announced, looking over his shoulder with a mischievous glint.

"The Colonel takes his leave," George continued, refusing to move. "He needs to prepare himself for the General's reception."

"Let me escort the Colonel to his chambers," Madame Esmond insisted, her voice sharp with irritation. She swept out of the room, followed closely by the bewildered Major, leaving George alone at the instrument. Harry, observing his brother's strange behavior, asked what the trouble was. "Why wouldn't he kiss her hand?" he wondered, recalling the previous scene.

"Common kindness!" George shrieked, brandishing a piece of paper he had hidden. "Look at this, Hal! Is that common kindness?" It was a fragment of a letter, yet its meaning was stark. "...is older than myself, but I, again, am older than my years... consider a sober person... Friend and guardian! Curse him!" George clenched his fists. "...The flattering offer which General Braddock hath made me... postpone this matter until after the campaign... return to repose under my own vine and fig-tree."

"He means Castlewood!" George shouted, pointing a shaking finger at the creeping ivy covering the walls outside the window. "'Under my own vine and fig-tree!' Where he hopes to present my dear brother to his new sister-in-law. She has a pretty Scripture name..."

"Rachel," George finished bitterly. "Rachel is not weeping for her children, and she longs to be comforted." Turning to Harry, he urged, "Upstairs at once, kneel down, and pray, 'Dear Papa, welcome to your house of Castlewood.'"

His Excellency the Commander-in-Chief departed for Madam Esmond's residence with a magnificence befitting the highest authority in His Majesty's North American dominions. A formidable escort of dragoons led the way from Williamsburg, thundered over by the raucous cheers of a devoted, predominantly African, populace. The General rode within a ponderous, heavily decorated coach. At its entrance stood Captain Talmadge, the Master of the Horse, who accompanied the vehicle throughout the journey. Seated inside, alongside the aide-de-camp Major Danvers, was Mr. Franklin, the diminutive postmaster from Philadelphia. Once merely a printer's apprentice, Franklin had evolved into a man of astonishing acumen, a fact acknowledged with respect by the General and his circle. "Extraordinary," observed His Excellency, tipping his hat gracefully, "how a man of such humble origins has acquired such breadth of knowledge and refinement!"

Franklin bowed politely. "It has been a fortunate chance, sir, to fall into the company of gentlemen like yourself. I have diligently studied their manners to adapt myself accordingly. Regarding formal education, I cannot boast; my father's means were narrow, and the schools in New England offered little. Yet, I have labored to the utmost of my ability, gathering whatever wisdom I could. I confess, however, I know nothing comparable to what you possess in England."

Braddock erupted in laughter. "Education! By George, there are gentlemen in the army who cannot decide whether 'bull' takes two 'b's or one! I've heard the Duke of Marlborough was hardly a proficient penman. Though I have not the honor of serving under such a noble commander—he predated my time—I have thoroughly thrashed the French, despite his lack of scholarship."

"I am fully aware of both facts," Franklin interjected calmly.

"Nor is my Duke a scholar," Braddock continued, feigning surprise at Franklin's knowing glance. "Aha, Mr. Postmaster, you heard that too—I see the wink in your eye." Instantly, Franklin withdrew the mischievous glint, replacing it with orbs innocent as a child's. "He lacks learning, but he stands as a match for any French general who has ever attempted to fry a frog in English style. He secured the Crown for the best of kings, Your Gracious Majesty, King George."

Franklin removed his hat, sending a cloud of powdered hair drifting from his voluminous wig. "He is the soldier's truest friend and the unyielding foe of those beggarly, red-shanked Scotch rebels and cunning Jesuits who seek to strip us of our liberties and faith. My gracious master, the Prince, shares this lack of academic polish, yet remains one of the finest gentlemen alive."

"I have seen His Royal Highness at a review of the Guards in Hyde Park," Franklin noted. "The Duke is indeed a magnificent rider."

"To your health today, Postmaster!" Braddock declared warmly. "He is the best of masters, the best of friends, the most dutiful son, and the finest gentleman to ever wear an epaulet."

"Epaulets are entirely out of my sphere, sir," Franklin chuckled. "I reside in a Quaker city."

"Certainly, every man to his trade," Braddock agreed, settling deeper into the swaying carriage. "You and your fellows belong to books; we to battle and governance. Look at these roads, and how this infernal coach plummets! Who are these two negroes in liveried livery?"

"That is Mr. Washington," Major Danvers replied.

"I'd wager him a corporal in the Horse Grenadiers," Braddock mused. "He carries himself well on horseback, and knows this terrain intimately, Mr. Franklin."

"Yes, indeed."

"And a remarkably genteel young man, considering his background. One would expect the polish of Europe."

"He does his best," Franklin observed, studying the stout chief with innocent eyes. Braddock sat slumped, his face flushed crimson beneath his coat, swearing at nearly every sentence. Ignorant of everything save the virtues of liquor and women, yet absurdly proud of his non-existent lineage, he was brave as a bulldog, savage, lustful, prodigal, yet generous. Gentle in soft moments, easily won, dull of wit, and utterly uneducated, he believed his nation supreme and himself equal to any gentleman. "He is mighty well suited for a provincial, truly. He was beaten at Fort What-d'ye-call-it last year, near the Thingamy River."

"The Lord knows the name, sir," Talmadge admitted, "and I suspect the Postmaster laughs at us both."

"Oh, Captain!"

"A classic trap," Franklin explained. "Militia and Indians alone. Good day, Mr. Washington. Fine mount, sir. Was that your maiden engagement last year?"

"At Fort Necessity?" The young gentleman saluted gravely as he approached, flanked by two sleek negro grooms in velvet caps. "I began poorly, sir, having never seen combat prior to that unfortunate day."

"You were all raw levies, my good fellow. I recall seeing our militia flee from the Scots, cursing them. You needed proper troops."

"It is my deepest passion to serve with them, Your Excellency," Washington stated earnestly.

"By George, we shall endeavor to grant you your wish!" Braddock boomed, using one of his customary oaths. As the heavy carriage rolled toward Castlewood, Washington requested permission to gallop ahead to announce the General's imminent arrival.
