The Commander-in-Chief's progress was so glacial that lesser dignitaries, invited to greet him, found themselves trailing in a dusty procession behind the great wheels. First came Mr. Dinwiddie, Lieutenant-Governor, accompanied by his servants and the jovial Parson Broadbent. Soon joined by Little Mr. Dempster, the schoolmaster, sporting a grand Ramillies wig reserved for state occasions, and Mr. Laws, the judge, riding a mule with Madam Laws perched behind him, her capbox carried by a negro man. The spectacle was so comical that Major Danvers and Mr. Franklin burst into laughter, though quietly enough not to disturb the sleeping General. They signaled the rear guard to proceed, leaving the Commander and his dragoons to follow at their leisure. Castlewood awaited them all with ample space.

Inside, provisions were abundant: meat, drink, and the finest tobacco for the soldiers; laughter and revelry for the negroes; and a lavish welcome for their masters. The honest General required assistance with nearly every dish and constantly raised his glass for another drink. He praised Nathan's sangaree highly, having consumed it liberally before dinner. The spread included cider, ale, brandy, and copious amounts of Bordeaux wine, some brought directly from England by Colonel Esmond itself, deemed fit for pontiffs' banquets according to Little Mr. Dempster. Parson Broadbent, a clergyman of questionable reputation who had spent time in Fleet Prison and various English gaming dens, returned a wink to Dempster and drank the vintage without concern for Latin phrases. Broadbent, a gambler and cock-fighter, had shared countless adventures with young George Esmond—hunting roe, hauling shad, slaughtering geese, and poaching ducks. Rumors circulated that Broadbent was the midnight poacher whom Washington had once caught and caned by the river at Mount Vernon; though Broadbent escaped, he returned two Sundays later with a black eye and a new collar. George and Broadbent played all the card games together, hunted every bird and beast, and fished every sea. Indeed, when the boys attended Mr. Dempster for lessons, I suspect Harry stayed behind to learn from the other professor, while George read his own books and told no tales of his younger brother.

Every bird of the Virginia air and every fish of the sea were laid before Madam Esmond's board to feed Her Excellency and the English and American gentlemen. Gumbo was declared perfection—a delicious discovery that earned the name of young George's black servant, found behind a door with his head buried in a bowl of the hotch-potch. The shad were rich and fresh; the stewed terrapins were worthy of London aldermen, a dish the General claimed the Duke himself would enjoy. Negro women possessed a genius for cookery, nurtured under the keen eye of the late and present Madam Esmond. Certain dishes, particularly sweets and flans, were prepared by Madam Esmond herself with remarkable dexterity. Carving principal pieces, rolling up the laced lappets of her sleeves, she revealed prettily rounded arms and slender wrists, performing an ancient rite of hospitality far superior to modern languid standards. The old law of the table dictated that the mistress press her guests with eager warmth, encouraging further enjoyment, mastering culinary anatomy, executing carving operations, and cheering her guests to fresh efforts. She whispered to her neighbor, Mr. Braddock, "I have kept for Your Excellency the jowl of this salmon—I take no denial!" She turned to Mr. Franklin, noting his water consumption despite a cellar of wholesome wines, and to Mr. Justice, asking if he favored woodcock pie.

"My dear lady," Mr. Laws responded with a profound bow, "we lack such a happy knack for pastry at home. I often tell my wife, 'My dear, I wish you had Madam Esmond's hand.'"

"It is a very pretty hand; surely others would admire it too," chimed the Postmaster of Boston, earning a half-smile from Mr. Esmond.

"So apt for

Once General Braddock and his distinguished guests had been settled into their respective lodgings, the two young Warringtons withdrew to their chamber. There, they poured out their heated opinions regarding the day's monumental event. They could not abide such a match—nay, it was unthinkable! Could the representative of the illustrious Marquis d'Esmond wed the younger son of a colonial family, a youth raised merely as a land surveyor? To consign Castlewood, and these very boys at nineteen, to the tender mercies of a stepfather barely twenty-three! "Oh, it is monstrous!" cried Harry. His impulse was immediate: he would rush to his mother's boudoir, where her black maids were stripping her of the simple jewels and fineries worn in honor of the feast. He intended to protest this odious union, declaring that they would depart, live upon their modest estate, and abandon her forever if this unnatural alliance proceeded.

George, however, advocated a different strategy, outlining his plan to his awestruck brother. "Our mother," he declared, "cannot marry a man with whom one or both of us have fought in the field—a foe who has wounded us or killed us, or whom we have wounded or killed. We must have him out, Harry."

Harry perceived the profound truth in George's assertion and marveled at his brother's sagacity. "No, George," he agreed, "you are right. Mother cannot wed our murderer; she will not stoop so low. And if we challenge him, he is finished. *Cadit quaestio*, as Mr. Dempster used to say. Shall I send my page with a challenge to Colonel Washington immediately?"

"My dear Harry," the elder replied, reflecting with some pride on his own affair of honor at Quebec, "you are not yet accustomed to matters of this gravity."

"No," admitted Harry, sighing with envy and admiration for his senior.

"We cannot insult a gentleman within our own walls," George continued, speaking with majestic authority. "The laws of honor forbid such inhospitable treatment. However, Sir, we may ride out with him, and the instant the park gates close, we shall make our intentions clear."

"That we can, by George!" cried Harry, seizing his brother's hand. "And that we will, too. Say, Georgy..." Suddenly, the lad's face turned crimson, and his brother asked what he wished to propose.

"This is my turn, brother," Harry pleaded. "If you undertake the campaign, I must have the other affair. Indeed, indeed, I ought." He prayed fervently for this promotion.

"Again, the head of the house must take the lead, my dear," George said with a superior air. "If I fall, Harry will avenge me. But I must fight George Washington, Hal—and 'tis best I do so, for indeed, I hate him the worst. Was it not he who counseled my mother to order that wretch, Ward, to seize me?"

"Oh, George," interjected the more pacific younger brother, "you ought to forget and forgive."

"Forget? Never, sir, so long as I remember. One cannot order remembrance out of a man's mind; and a wrong that exists today must exist tomorrow. I have never, to my knowledge, done harm to any man, and I never will suffer it, if I can help it. I think poorly of Mr. Ward, but not so poorly as to believe he will ever forgive that blow with the ruler. Colonel Washington is our enemy, mine especially. He has advised one injustice against me, and plots a greater. I tell you, brother, we must punish him."

The grandfathers' old Bordeaux had set George's ordinarily pale countenance ablaze. Harry, his brother's devoted worshipper, could not fail to admire George's haughty demeanor and rapid oratory. Prepared to follow his leader with his usual docility, the boys retired to their beds. Before sleep, the elder conveyed special injunctions to his junior: to remain civil to all the guests as long as they remained under the maternal roof on the morrow.

Good manners and a reluctance to spread gossip prevented us from naming which of Madame Esmond's guests first succumbed to the weight of her hospitality. The respectable descendants of Messrs. Talmadge and Danvers, aides-de-camp to his Excellency, might not care to hear how their ancestors were intoxicated a century prior; yet the gentlemen themselves took no shame in the fact, and there is little doubt they or their comrades were tipsy twice or thrice a week. Let us imagine them reeling to bed, supported by sympathetic negroes; and their vinous General, too stout a toper to surrender himself to mere six bottles of Bordeaux, conducted to his chamber by the young gentlemen of the house, swiftly falling into the slumber that friendly Bacchus grants. The good lady of Castlewood observed the state of her guests without surprise or horror. She rose early the next morning, providing cooling drinks for their parched throats, which the servants delivered to their respective rooms. At breakfast, one of the English officers rallied Mr. Franklin, who consumed no alcohol whatsoever, thereby refusing the morning draught of toddy. The officer showed how the Philadelphia gentleman lost two pleasures—the drink and the toddy itself. The young fellow retorted that the disease was pleasant and the remedy delicious, laughing as he proposed to repeat both. The General's new American aide-de-camp, Colonel Washington, sat quite sober and serene. The British officers vowed they must take him in hand and teach him the ways of the English army, but the Virginian gentleman gravely replied that he had no desire to learn that particular branch of English military education.

The widow, preoccupied with the cares of a great dinner followed by a magnificent breakfast, had scarce leisure to remark closely upon the behavior of her sons, though she noted that George was scrupulously polite to her favorite, Colonel Washington, as to all other guests.

Before Mr. Braddock departed, he held a private audience with Madame Esmond, wherein His Excellency formally offered to take her son into his family. When arrangements for George's departure were settled between his mother and future chief, Madame Esmond, though she felt the tremors, showed no squeamish terror regarding the dangers of the bottle, recognizing them as severe trials her son would inevitably face. She knew her boy must take his part in the world. "Mr. Braddock is a perfect fine gentleman in the morning," she said stoutly to her aide-de-camp, Mrs. Mountain. "And though my father did not drink, 'tis certain that many of the best company in England do." The jolly General, good-natured, shook hands with George, who presented himself to His Excellency after the maternal interview. Braddock welcomed him and bade him attend at Frederick in three days, shortly after which the expedition would set forth.

Now the great coach was again called into requisition. The General's escort pranced around it; the other guests and their servants mounted horse. The lady of Castlewood attended His Excellency to the steps of the verandah; the young gentlemen followed, standing flanking his coach door. The guard-trumpeter blew a shrill blast; the negroes shouted "Huzzay, and God save de King" as Mr. Braddock graciously took leave of his hospitable entertainers and rolled away on his road to headquarters.

As the boys ascended the steps, there the Colonel was once more taking leave of their mother. No doubt she had been once more recommending George to his namesake's care. Colonel Washington said, "With my life. You may depend on me," as the lads returned to their mother and the few guests remaining in the porch. The Colonel was booted and ready to depart. "Farewell, my dear Harry," he said. "With you, George, 'tis no adieu. We shall meet in three days at the camp."

Both young men were heading toward danger, perhaps toward death. Colonel Washington was departing, and she would see him no more before the campaign. No wonder the widow was deeply moved.

George Warrington watched his mother's emotion and interpreted it with a pang of malignant scorn. "Stay yet a moment, and console our mamma," he said with a steady countenance, "only the time to get ourselves booted, and my brother and I will ride with you a little way, George." George Warrington had already ordered his horses. The three young men were speedily underway, their negro grooms following behind. Mrs. Mountain, knowing she had wrought mischief between them and trembling for the result, felt a vast relief that Mr. Washington had departed without a quarrel with the brothers, without, at any rate, an open declaration of love to their mother.

No man could be more courteous in demeanor than George Warrington to his neighbor and namesake, the Colonel. The latter was pleased and surprised at his young friend's altered behavior. The community of danger, the necessity of future fellowship, the softening influence of the long friendship binding him to the Esmond family, and the tender adieux that had just passed between him and the mistress of Castlewood inclined the Colonel to forget the unpleasantness of recent days, making him more than usually friendly with his young companion. George was quite gay and easy; it was Harry who was now melancholy. He rode silently and wistfully by his brother, keeping away from Colonel Washington, to whose side he had always pressed eagerly before. If the honest Colonel remarked his young friend's conduct, he undoubtedly attributed it to Harry's known affection for his brother and his natural anxiety to be near George now that the day of parting drew near.

Within the confines of Benson's Tavern, the air grew heavy with the scent of impending violence and the sharp tang of unshed tears. In one chamber, Colonel Washington stood alone, his brow furrowed in a silence that spoke volumes, preparing his spirit for the ordeal ahead. Across the threshold, in a quieter room, young George Warrington engaged in a frantic, tear-stained ritual of his own. With his faithful brother Harry acting as his scribe, George dictated a final testament, a grandiloquent letter to his mother, bidding her a solemn farewell.

"My dear, dear George, don't say that!" cried Harry, his voice trembling as he held the quill. "Don't speak of falling!"

"'As probably will be the case,'" George insisted, his voice rising with a majestic, hollow conviction. "You know what a marksman Colonel Washington is, Harry. I am fair at a target myself, and it is likely that one or both of us shall drop. 'I scarcely suppose you will carry out the intentions you have at present in view.'" These final words were spoken with a bitterness that cut deeper than any prior grievance, leaving Harry weeping openly as he scrawled them down.

"You see, I say nothing," George continued, his composure cracking slightly, "Madame Esmond's name does not even appear in this quarrel. Do you not recall Grandfather's life? How Lord Castlewood fought Lord Mohun on a mere pretext of a card dispute, never once hinting at the lady who was the true catalyst? I took my cue from that, Harry. Our mother is not compromised in the—Why, child, what have you been writing, and who taught thee to spell?"

Harry had misspelled the final phrase "in view" as "in vew," and a fresh wave of salty tears had blurred the ink further.

"I can't think about the spelling now, Georgy," whimpered Harry, his eyes red-rimmed. "I'm too miserable for that. I begin to think, perhaps it's all nonsense... perhaps Colonel Washington never..."

"Never meant to take possession of Castlewood? Never gave himself airs? Never advised my mother to have me flogged? Never intended to marry her? Never insulted me before the King's officers? Never wrote to his brother claiming parental authority over us?" George cried out, slapping his breast pocket where the evidence lay hidden. "The paper is there, Harry Warrington! If anything happens to me, you will find it on my corpse!"

"Write yourself, Georgy, I can't hold the pen," Harry sobbed, burying his fists in his eyes until his elbows smeared the page with a chaotic mixture of bad spelling and grief.

Seizing another sheet, George sat at his brother's desk, channeling every ounce of his youth into a composition filled with the longest, most obscure words, the grandest Latin quotations, and the deepest satire his mind could conjure. He commanded that his enslaved boy, Sady, be set free immediately. He arranged for provisions for his tutor, Mr. Dempster, whose affection he cherished like a father figure. He directed that his silver fruit-knife, music books, and harpsichord be given to little Fanny Mountain. Finally, he requested that his brother take a lock of his hair, to be worn as a token of eternal devotion. With a seal bearing his grandfather's ancient arms, George signed the document, sealing a fate that seemed already written.

"The watch, of course, will be yours," George said, producing his grandfather's gold chronometer. He glanced at the face. "Two hours and a half have vanished! 'Tis time for Sady to return with the pistols. Take the watch, Harry dear."

"It's no good!" Harry screamed, throwing his arms around his brother in a desperate hug. "If he fights you, I'll fight him, too! If he kills my Georgy—" His voice broke into a string of oaths and curses, the kind that, legend held, caused the recording angels to swoon from their celestial ledgers.

Outside, the atmosphere shifted with the arrival of General Braddock's new aide-de-camp. With a resolute hand, he had penned five letters and sealed them with his wax. One went to his mother at Mount Vernon, another to his brother, a third to "M.C. only," and the fourth to the Major-General himself. "And one, young gentleman," the informant whispered, "is for your mother, Madam Esmond."

Another angelic observer, burdened by the weight of the moment, flew away at the sight of George Warrington's lips parting in a silent execration born of pure hatred, rage, and jealousy.

It was the tavern landlord who delivered the news to the brothers. Captain Waring, donning his old militia uniform to honor the gravity of the hour, announced that Colonel Washington was pacing the garden, awaiting them. The Regulars, mostly sober, waited under the porch. A plot of land near the Captain's log-house, cleared for a kitchen garden, served as the stage for this tragedy. There walked Colonel Washington, hands clasped behind his back, head bowed, a grave sorrow etched onto his handsome features. Negro servants pressed against the palings, peering over, while the officers beneath the porch stirred from their slumber. Captain Waring strode steadily along the balcony formed by the sloping roof, while Captain Grace lolled over the railing, his gaze vacant yet alert.

Benson's was notorious, a haven for cockfights, horse matches, boxing, and wrestling, drawing Virginians from every walk of life. It was a place where men arrived sound and departed with broken ribs or gouged eyes, where squires, farmers, and slaves alike indulged in the bloodsport.

Into this somber tableau stalked the tall young Colonel, lost in dismal meditation. The path forward was narrow and cruel, dictated by the harsh laws of honor and local custom. Goaded by a boy's impudence, he had used insulting words. The young man had demanded reparation. Shocked by the depth of George Warrington's jealousy and the length of time it had festered, the Colonel felt the weight of his mistake. He was bound to pay the forfeit.

Suddenly, a roar erupted—a wild, rhythmic hallooing typical of the enslaved population, who delighted in noise, especially when riding at full gallop. Heads, both woolly and powdered, snapped toward the sound. It came from the road the travelers had traversed hours prior. Soon, the clatter of hooves shattered the silence, and Mr. Sady appeared atop his foaming steed. Amidst a prodigious uproar from his fellow blacks, Sady fired a pistol into the air. Then another. His horse, trained to endure the chaos of the hunt, stood firm. Surrounded by a dozen bawling companions, Sady descended into the courtyard, kicking horses and shrieking pigs, his laughter mingling with the screams of the animals.

"Sady, sir, come here!" roared Master Harry.
"Sady, come here! Confound you!" shouted Master George.
"Comin', mas'r," Sady replied cheerfully, ignoring the orders, and resumed his chatter with the crowd. He grinned, drew the pistols from their holsters, snapped the locks, and pointed them first at a grunting hog that charged through the yard, then down the road whence he had come. "Everybody a-comin'," he declared, turning to point at the approaching dust clouds.

Sure enough, the thunder of hooves returned. First came Little Mr. Dempster, spurring his pony with frantic energy. Beside him, a rider in a riding habit on a small gray mare—could it be Madam Esmond? No, she was far too stout. It was Mrs. Mountain, riding Madam Esmond's mount.

"Oh Lor! O Golly! Hoop! Here dey come! Hurray!" a chorus of negroes rose in anticipation.
Dr. Dempster and Mrs. Mountain clattered into the yard, dismounted, and pushed through the throng of black servants, rushing straight into the house and bursting out onto the porch where the British officers sat in stunned amazement. They raced down the stairs and into the garden, where George and Harry stood facing the towering figure of the Colonel. Before George could sternly ask, "What do you do here, madam?" Mrs. Mountain threw her arms around his neck.

"Oh, George, my darling! It's a mistake! It's a mistake, and it's all my fault!"

"What's a mistake?" George asked, majestically pulling away from the embrace.

"What is it, Mounty?" cried Harry, trembling violently.

"That paper I took from his portfolio, that scrap I found where the Colonel said he was going to marry a widow with two children," Mrs. Mountain gasped, addressing the boys. "Who else could it be but you, children, and who else could it be but your mother?"

"Well?" Harry urged.

"Well, it's—not your mother. It's that little widow Custis whom the Colonel intends to marry. He always sought a wealthy wife; I knew he would. It's not Mrs. Rachel Warrington. He told Madam so today, just before departing, saying the wedding would follow the campaign. And—and your mother is furious, boys. When Sady came for the pistols and told the whole house you were fighting, I told him to fire them off in the yard! I galloped after him, and I've nearly broken my poor old bones getting here!"

"I have

It is easy to imagine that the separation between the brothers has been finalized, that George has stepped into his role within Mr. Braddock's command, and Harry has returned to Castlewood to fulfill his duties. Yet, his spirit remains tethered to the army; life at home offers him no solace. He cannot bear to voice the depth of his sorrow at being confined beneath the somber, quiet roof of Castlewood, now rendered more desolate by George's absence. Harry avoids passing through his brother's empty chambers, turns his face away, and takes George's seat at the head of the table, sighing deeply as he lifts his silver tankard.

Madam Warrington raises a hearty "To the King" daily, and on Sundays, as Harry reads the service and prays for travelers by land and sea, she intones, "We beseech Thee to hear us," with a gravity that unsettles the room. She insists on speaking of George constantly, yet with a cheerful, unshakable optimism, as if his return were a certainty written in stone. She strides into his vacant study, posture erect, masking any outward sign of emotion. She observes that his books, linens, and papers remain meticulously arranged, speaks of him with profound reverence, and appeals frequently to the elderly servants regarding tasks meant to be completed "when Mr. George returns." Mrs. Mountain trembles whenever George's name is spoken, causing Harry to wear a look of ghastly alarm, while his mother maintains a stoic, sedate composure. Though prone to blunders at piquet and backgammon, she rises before dawn, dressed and ready. Having summoned Mr. Dempster back to reside at Castlewood, she treats everyone with gentleness rather than her former severity, often drifting into tales of her father's campaigns—where he emerged unscathed—and expressing hope that her eldest son will share that fortune.

George sends frequent letters home, and now, with the army on the move, he has begun compiling a rough journal, forwarding it as opportunity allows. These documents are devoured with delight by Harry, who reads them aloud during the long summer evenings when the household gathers. Madam Esmond sits upright at her tea-table, refusing to lean back; little Fanny Mountain sews diligently beside her; Mr. Dempster and Mrs. Mountain play their cards; and the ancient, silent servants of the house move quietly through the gloaming, listening intently to the words of their young master.

Hearken to Harry Warrington reading his brother's letter! As we gaze at the slender script on the yellow pages, carefully preserved, we might almost believe the writer and reader were truly alive. And yet, alas, they seem as though they never existed—their likenesses faint ghosts in frames of tarnished gold. Did they truly live and breathe? Did they love one another as faithful brothers and loyal gentlemen? Can we still hear their voices from the past? I am certain of Harry's voice, sitting there in the warm summer air, reading his younger brother's simple chronicle:

"It must be admitted that the colonies treat His Majesty, King George II., with scant courtesy, and his representative here burns with indignation. Virginia is difficult, Maryland is poor, but Pennsylvania is the worst of all. We begged them to send troops from home to fight the French, promising to support them upon arrival. Not only did we break this promise, failing to provide adequate provisions, but our populace demands exorbitant prices for cattle and stores, effectively cheating the soldiers who have come to fight *their* battles. No wonder the General swears, and the troops are sullen. Delays have been interminable. Due to the failure of the provinces to supply promised stores and transport, weeks and months have passed. During this time, undoubtedly, the French have fortified themselves on our frontiers and reclaimed the forts we lost.

"There will be no further suit concerning the quarrel at Benson's Tavern, nor any rematch between Colonel Washington and the anonymous young gentleman. Captain Waring sought to pursue the matter upon entering camp, delivering a message from Captain Grace, which your friend—who is as bold as Hector—was eager to accept, employing Brother Aide-de-Camp Colonel Wingfield on his side. However, when Wingfield learned the circumstances—that the altercation arose from Grace's drunkenness, fanned by Waring's own tipsiness, and involved two regulars insulting a militia officer—he swore that Colonel Washington would not engage the 44th men. Instead, he would carry the matter directly to His Excellency, who would convene a court-martial for the captains for brawling with the militia, drunkenness, and indecent conduct. Thus, the captains were forced to lower their toasting irons and swallow their wrath. They were sufficiently good-natured outside their cups, eating their humble pie with relish at a reconciliation dinner hosted by Colonel Washington, where he conducted himself with a perfection akin to Prince Prettyman. Hang him! He has no faults, and that is precisely why I dislike him. When he marries that widow—ah me! what a dreary existence awaits her."

"I wonder at the palate of some men and the audacity of some women!" Madam Esmond exclaimed, setting down her teacup. "I marvel at any woman who, having been married once, forgets herself enough to wed again! Do you not agree, Mountain?"

"Monstrous!" cried Mountain, with a peculiar expression.

Mr. Dempster kept his gaze steadily fixed on his glass of punch. Harry appeared as though he were choking with laughter or some other concealed emotion, until his mother interjected, "Continue, Harry! Proceed with your brother's journal. He writes well; but, ah, will he ever be able to write like my father?"

Harry resumed: "We maintain the strictest discipline here in camp. Orders against drunkenness and misconduct among the men are severe. The roll of each company is called at morning, noon, and night, with a return of absentees and disorderly elements submitted to the regimental commander for punishment. The men are duly punished, and the drummers are constantly at work. Oh, Harry, it made one sick to behold the first blood drawn from a great, strong, white back, and to hear the pitiful cry of the poor fellow."

"Oh, horrid!" gasped Madam Esmond.

"I believe I would have murdered Ward if he had flogged me. Thank heavens he escaped with nothing but a crack of the ruler! The men are cared for adequately, I say. I wish the officers were treated similarly. The Indians have just broken camp and retreated in anger, for the young officers were constantly drinking with the squaws—and... and... hum... ha." Here, Mr. Harry paused, unwilling to continue the narrative in the presence of little Fanny, who sat primly by her mother's side, stitching her sampler.

"Skip the mention of those odious tipsy creatures," commanded Madam. And Harry began anew, in a louder tone, with a far more satisfactory account: "Each regiment performs Divine Service at the head of its colors every Sunday. The General does everything in the power of mortal man to prevent plundering and encourages the surrounding populace to bring in provisions. He has decreed that any soldier daring to interrupt or molest market-goers shall be shot. He has ordered the price of provisions raised by a penny a pound and lent money from his own pocket to sustain the camp. Altogether, he is a strange composite, this General. He flogs his men without mercy, yet gives freely without stint. He swears terrible oaths in conversation and tells stories that would shock Mrs. Mountain..."

"Why me?" asked Mountain. "What business do I have with the General's foolish stories?"

"Never mind the stories, and continue, Harry," cried the mistress of the house.

"...would shock Mrs. Mountain after dinner, but he never misses service. He adores his Great Duke, whose name is constantly on his lips. Our two regiments both served in Scotland, where I dare say Mr. Dempster knows the color of their facings."

"We saw the tails of their coats, as well as their facings," grumbled the little Jacobite tutor.

"Colonel Washington has caught the fever sharply and has barely been well enough to keep pace with the march. Would it not be better for him to return home and be nursed by his widow? When either of us falls ill, we are almost as close as ever. But I feel somehow as if I cannot forgive him for having slighted me. Good Powers! How I have hated him for these past months! Oh, Harry! I was in a fury at the tavern the other day because Mountain arrived so promptly, ending our disagreement. We ought to have burned a small quantity of gunpowder between us and cleared the air. But though I do not love him as you do, I know he is a good soldier, a good officer, and a brave, honest man; and, at any rate, I shall love him none the less for not wishing to be our stepfather."

"A stepfather, indeed!" cried Harry's mother. "Jealousy and prejudice have completely unhinged the poor child! Do you suppose the Marquis of Esmond's daughter and heiress could not have found other stepfathers for her sons than a mere provincial surveyor? If there are any further allusions to such follies and blunders in George's journal, I implore you to skip them, Harry, my dear. Enough talk has already occurred regarding this piece of folly."

"'Tis a pleasing sight," Harry continued, reading from his brother's journal, "to observe a long line of redcoats threading through the woods or establishing positions after

With every mile Harry Warrington traveled north toward Pennsylvania, the tales of the British rout grew louder, confirmed by a chorus of shaken voices. Two legendary regiments, veterans of Scotland and the Continent, had dissolved before an enemy that seemed to vanish into the mist. Their iron discipline, their famed valor, had crumbled against a handful of "savages" and scattered French infantry. The General, Braddock, had fought with a courage that bordered on the suicidal; four times his horse fell beneath him, twice he bled from wounds, and finally, a mortal blow ended his life three days later.

Those who spoke to Harry painted a harrowing scene: the crossing of the swollen river, the long, weary march through the wilderness, the futile frontal assault, and the artillery failing to clear the path. Then, the ambush. Fire erupted from behind every bush and tree, a murderous fusillade that tore the expedition apart, cutting down half the force before they could even regroup. Yet, Harry learned, not everyone perished. An aide-de-camp, a Virginia gentleman, lay at Dunbar's camp, ravaged by fever and exhaustion.

"But which one?" Harry asked, rushing toward the encampment, his heart pounding. "Is it George?"

It was not George. Stretched in a canvas tent, pale and burning with fever, was George Washington. The sight of Harry brought a sharp pain to Washington's brow, deeper than the malady itself. He shook his head, unable to offer news of his friend. He could not speak the full truth. For three days following the battle, his duty had bound him to the General. On that fatal ninth of July, he had seen George stride forward with orders from the chief, never to return. Only after Braddock's death had the aide managed to retrace his steps to the battlefield. There, amidst the horrors of the aftermath, the bodies lay stripped and mutilated. Washington had buried what he believed to be George's corpse, a task that had deepened his sickness with the agonizing weight of uncertainty.

"Ach, George!" Harry cried, his voice breaking. "If you truly loved him, you would find him, dead or living!"

Nothing satisfied the boy but the physical act of searching. With money borrowed, he hired guides and crossed the river at the point where the army had passed. He traversed the dreadful field, no longer haunted by the redcoats but teeming with vultures feasting on mangled flesh. It was here Harry saw the face of Death for the first time—not in the calm repose of his grandfather, sleeping with a sweet smile, but in the grotesque reality of rotting bodies. The horror of the mutilation turned his stomach; he could glean no sign of his brother from the silent woods or the festering remains.

Driven by desperation, Harry considered approaching the French fort alone, bearing a white flag. But his guides refused to accompany him past the river. "Keep your hair for your lady mother, my young gentleman," the guide warned grimly. "'Tis enough that she loses one son in this campaign." The warning hung heavy in the air: the French might show mercy, but the Indians would not.

When Harry returned to Dunbar's encampment, the fever claimed him. Delirium seized the boy, and he lay for days in a tent, then on a bed from which Washington had just risen convalescent. For several days, he drifted in and out of consciousness, unaware of his watchers. Poor Dempster, who had nursed him through previous illnesses, feared the worst—that the widow would lose both her children. Yet, slowly, the fever abated. The boy rallied, mounted his horse, and prepared to journey home. Both Mr. Washington and Dempster escorted him back to Castlewood.

Their arrival was met with a servant ahead to announce their presence. First came Mrs. Mountain and her small daughter, Fanny. They welcomed Harry with tears and embraces, but Mrs. Mountain offered not a nod to Mr. Washington. Little Fanny, however, caused the young officer to turn deadly pale. She approached him, hands clasped behind her back, and asked, "Why have you not brought George back too?"

Harry, overwhelmed by his sister-in-law's sobs and the warm caresses of his nurse, failed to hear the child's question. But as the carriage settled, the tension in the air became unbearable. Dempster was treated with warmth, but the atmosphere shifted when the topic of food arose. Mr. Washington, having ridden hard and counting on the hospitality of Castlewood, politely declined a meal, stating he needed only water for his horse and a drink for himself before departing.

"My mother won't see him, child," Mrs. Mountain whispered urgently to Harry. "She left this room when the Colonel's servant announced your coming. She said she felt she could not see Mr. Washington."

"Not see George? Why, he is like a son of the house!" Harry protested, confused. "He gave me his bed when I was ill!"

"She had best not see him," Mrs. Mountain insisted, her composure cracking. "I don't meddle in family matters, but... I cannot look upon him. Will you go to her?"

Harry took his friend's arm, apologized to the Colonel, and ascended the stairs. He moved quickly, averting his gaze from the door of his brother's empty room, which he dreaded to open. Instead, he found Madam Esmond waiting for him. She pulled him into a crushing embrace, leading him into the room where George had lain. A settee sat by the bed, and a book of Psalms lay open on the covers. Everything remained exactly as it had been when George departed.

"My poor child! How thin you've grown," she sobbed, smoothing his hair. "Never mind. A mother's care will heal you. 'Twas nobly done to seek your brother through sickness and danger. If others had been as faithful, he might be here now." Her voice rose, filled with a fierce, almost manic conviction. "Our hero will return! I know it! He is not dead. One so good, so brave, so gentle—he cannot be lost to us entirely."

When Harry pressed her for the basis of her faith, she revealed the source of her torment and hope: she had seen her father in dreams for two nights running. In visions, he had told her that her boy was a prisoner among the Indians.

Madam Esmond's grief, unlike Harry's, had not paralyzed her. It had animated her, lending her eyes a frantic eagerness and her countenance an air of righteous anger. Harry marveled at her transformation. When he urged her to descend and welcome George Washington, who had accompanied him, her excitement spiked dangerously.

"I should shudder at touching his hand!" she declared, recoiling as if from poison. "He took my son from me! I cannot sleep under the same roof with him!"

"He gave me his bed when I was ill, Mother!" Harry pleaded, bewildered. "If George lives, how does George Washington bear responsibility for his death? Please God, let it be only as you say."

"If your brother returns, it will not be through Mr. Washington's aid," she snapped. "He did not defend him on the field, nor did he bring him out."

"But he tended me!" Harry interjected, defending his friend. "He was still ill himself when he yielded his bed. Any other man would have thought only of self."

"A friend!" Madam Esmond sneered, the word dripping with venom. "Of all His Excellency's aides-de-camp, mine is the only one who returns unwounded. The brave and noble fall, but *he* survives! I entrusted my boy to him, the pride of my life, whom he would defend with his own blood—and he leaves my George in the forest, bringing me back himself! Oh, what a welcome I shall give!"

"No gentleman," Harry cried, standing his ground, "ever refused shelter under my grandfather's roof."

"Oh, no gentleman!" the little widow exclaimed, composing herself. "Let us go down, if you wish, son, and pay our respects to this one. Will you give me your arm?" She took hold of his slender wrist, supporting herself, and they descended the broad stairs together.

In the parlor, where the Colonel sat, Mrs. Mountain made a formal curtsy and placed her small, trembling hand in his. "I wish that our meeting had been happier, Colonel Washington," she said softly.

"You do not grieve more than I do that it is otherwise, madam," Washington replied, his voice steady despite the turmoil around him.

"I might have wished for the meeting to be spared," the widow continued, her black eyes piercing and unreadable. "That I might not have kept you from friends you are naturally anxious to see. Home, his nurse, and his mother, along with our kind Doctor Dempster, will soon restore him. 'Twas scarce necessary, Colonel, that you, burdened with so many military and domestic affairs, should turn physician as well."

"Harry was ill and weak, and I thought it my duty to ride by him," Washington stammered slightly.

"You yourself, sir, have endured the fatigues and dangers of this campaign in a most wonderful manner," Mrs. Mountain observed, curtsying again. "I wish to Heaven, madam, some one else had come back in my place!"

"Nay, sir," the widow countered gently, though her tone held steel. "You have ties that make your life invaluable, and duties to which you must return. In our current state of doubt and distress, Castlewood

When Queen Dido had bade the wandering Trojans recount their labors and terrors, she gave ear with a rapt attention that turned her royal heart to stone for the sorrow of others. Now, little Harry Warrington sat before his elderly Aunt Beatrix, telling his tale with a simplicity that lacked the tragic grandeur of Aeneas, yet possessed a warm, artless charm that pierced the hard shell of his listener. Mrs. Bernstein, a woman whose life had been a vast, arid plain of solitude, found her heart beating with a strange, unfamiliar rhythm as the boy spoke. To her, his stories were not merely entertainment; they were the sweet, refreshing waters of a fountain she had forgotten existed in her desert existence. She looked at him with a tenderness she had never known, a soft, flushed joy rising in her cheeks that she quickly tried to mask with a stern countenance. Yet, within, a dark voice whispered warnings: *Wait, Beatrix,* it scoffed. *You will tire of this boy as you have tired of everything else. He may perish, and you will find no tear for him.* And yet, she answered the whisper with a smile, deciding to seize the shade under the tree while it lasted, knowing that tomorrow would demand another march into the wilderness.

As Harry continued, weaving tales of his mother and her past, Aunt Beatrix listened with a mixture of amusement and maternal pride. She noted the echoes of the Esmond bloodline in his words—the gentle courtesy inherited from Colonel Washington, whom Harry described not with the fiery passion some expected, but with a quiet, steady friendship. "My mother vowed a thousand times she held no such feelings," Harry admitted, a blush creeping to his face as he recalled his own youthful, absurd jealousy of the Colonel. "But I fear my half-sister will never forgive him," Beatrix mused, her eyes twinkling. "And surely, sir, you do not blame women for finding affection for a younger kin?" she teased gently. "Do I not dote upon you? And do not the ladies of Castlewood all envy me for this favor?"

Despite the underlying currents of jealousy within the household, the surface remained smooth as glass. Within days of his arrival, the formal "Cousin" became simply "Harry" to the young and old alike. The Countess of Castlewood, in particular, showered him with gracious attention, praising his manners and marveling at how a boy raised in the wilds of Virginia could possess such polish. To the Countess, this elegance was proof of the Colonel's enduring influence; to the Baroness, it was evidence of a noble upbringing passed down through the blood. They exchanged glances filled with unspoken rivalry and mutual admiration, a silent dance of wit and flattery where compliments served as the sharpest weapons and the deepest insults were hidden behind smiles. "How exquisite!" the Countess would declare regarding Harry's grace, while the Baroness countered with praises of his natural warmth, each seeking to prove that their version of truth was the superior one.

Yet, behind the scenes, a different reality unfolded. Through the gossip of servants and the casual eavesdropping of a maid, the Baroness learned the true nature of the Countess's behavior. Later, when the young people gathered in the drawing room, the Countess entered, her demeanor shifting instantly from public benevolence to private venom. As Harry and Lady Fanny danced a minuet, guided by the latter's enthusiastic instruction, the Countess watched with a cruel delight. When William Esmond entered, boots still caked with mud from the kennels, the atmosphere curdled. "Is he teaching Fanny the war-dance?" William sneered, his temper flaring. "Does she intend to try her tricks upon this brute?" His mother's glance, sharp and stinging, confirmed his intent.

Fanny, caught between the pressure of her mother's disdain and her own fascination with Harry, curtsied deeply, causing Harry to stumble slightly in his step and turn crimson. The music faltered as Lady Maria stepped away from the harpsichord, leaving the pair to stand in an awkward silence. The Countess, feigning concern, seated herself again at the instrument, playing a lively tune to cover the tension. "Do not let me spoil the sport," she cooed, urging Fanny to continue. "Teach him properly, dearest!"

"I—I know not how to dance," Harry stammered, his shame evident. "Perhaps when I have learned the steps, I may ask my cousin for a walk."

"That shall happen soon, Cousin Warrington," the Countess assured, her voice dripping with honeyed malice.

Outside the room, the Countess's voice dropped to a harsh, cutting register as she dragged her daughter aside into the tapestry-lined corridor. "You booby! You double fool!" she hissed, shaking Fanny violently. "What mischief are you hatching with that Huron? Do you wish to marry a swine, a squaw, and end up a wife in a wigwam?" She mocked the idea of Harry's lineage, reducing his prospects to a swamp, a cabin, and a barrel of tobacco. "Remember your rank, Frances Esmond!" she screamed, forcing her daughter to recall the weight of her birthright. "Before the world, you must act civil enough to keep appearances, but in private, I forbid you to set foot near him or encourage him!"

"But he is our cousin!" Fanny pleaded, her voice trembling. "And Madame de Bernstein says so!"

"Bah!" the Countess dismissed. "Let her like what she will. She takes up a thing today and discards it tomorrow. In private, you obey me. If I cannot bring you to reason, your brother shall."

The conversation ended abruptly, the informants silenced, but the seeds of discord were sown. Only later did the Baroness reveal fragments of this secret struggle to Harry. For now, he perceived only the outward civility of his hosts. Lord Castlewood remained cool and reserved, indistinguishable from his treatment of others, while William and the ladies offered drinks, rides, and companionship. He accepted their kindness as the natural affection of kindred spirits, blind to the fact that he was being courted not out of genuine love, but as a strategic pawn in a web of familial intrigue and ancient grievances. He drank from their cups and walked beside their hands, unaware that he was walking a path paved with traps, serving schemes he could not yet see and creating angers he did not understand.

The second day following Harry's arrival at Castlewood proved to be a Sunday. The chapel attached to the castle served double duty as the village church. A private door from the mansion opened directly onto a grand state pew, reserved for the family, where they took their appointed places in strict order. Below them sat a rather large congregation drawn from the village. High above, a few ancient, dust-choked banners hung from the rafters; Harry indulged himself in the pleasant fantasy that these had been carried by retainers of his lineage during the Commonwealth Wars—a conflict in which his ancestors, as he knew well, had fought with loyalty and distinction.

Within the altar rails stood the effigy of the Esmond who lived in the time of King James the First, the common forefather of everyone gathered in the family pew. Madame de Bernstein, in her capacity as a Bishop's widow, never missed mass. She conducted her devotions with a gravity nearly equal to that of the ancestor seated nearby, who wore a square beard and a red gown, forever kneeling on his stone hassock before his great marble desk and book beneath his emblazoned coat of arms.

The clergyman, a tall, high-complexioned, handsome young man, read the service in a lively, agreeable voice, lending the biblical chapters a distinct dramatic flair. The music was excellent, performed by one of the young ladies of the family at the organ, though it might have been superior but for an interruption. Something akin to a burst of laughter erupted from the servants' pew, triggered by Mr. Warrington's lacqueman, Gumbo. Knowing the tune assigned for the Psalm, Gumbo launched into it with a voice so exceedingly loud and sweet that the entire congregation swiveled toward him. Even the parson covered his mouth with his handkerchief, and the liveried gentlemen from London were thrown into a state of profound impropriety. Perhaps pleased with the sensation he had generated, Gumbo continued his performance until it became virtually a solo, silencing the voice of the clerk himself. Indeed, though Gumbo held the book alongside the portress's daughter, Molly—who had been the first to welcome the strangers to Castlewood—he sang and recited entirely by ear, unable to read a single syllable of the verses printed before him.

Once this choral interlude concluded, a brief sermon duly followed, which Harry found rather too short. In a lively, familiar, and striking discourse, the clergyman recounted a scene he had witnessed the previous week: the execution of a horse-thief after the Assizes. He painted a picture of the man, detailing his formerly good character, his loving family, and his agony at separation. He depicted the execution itself in a manner that was startling, terrible, and picturesque. Unlike the somewhat Calvinistic preachers Harry's mother favored, this divine spoke not in Scripture phraseology but as a man of the world addressing other sinful folk who might profit from sound advice.

The unfortunate man, once a farmer with promising prospects, had succumbed to the vices of the age—drinking, card-playing, horse-racing, and cock-fighting. Against these, the young clergyman expressed generous indignation. The man eventually drifted into poaching and horse-stealing, crimes for which he now suffered. The divine rapidly sketched striking and fearful images of these rustic offenses. He startled his hearers by illustrating that the "Eye of the Law" watched the poacher at midnight and set traps to ensnare criminals. He galloped the stolen horse over highways and commons, from county to county, yet showed Retribution always hot on its heels, seizing the malefactor at a country fair, dragging him before the justice, and never unlocking his manacles until he laid them down at the foot of the gallows. "Heaven be pitiful to the sinner!" the clergyman intoned, acting out the scene. He whispered words in the criminal's ear at the cart; he dropped his handkerchief onto the clerk's head. Harry recoiled as the cloth fell. The clergyman had been speaking for over twenty minutes, yet Harry felt as though he had not been listening for five.

The gentlefolk in the great pew were thoroughly enlivened by the discourse. Occasionally, Harry, glancing toward the servants' pew, noted how attentively they listened. Especially Gumbo, his own man, sat in an attitude of intense consternation. Yet the smock frocks seemed to take no notice, clamping out of the church quite unconcerned. Gaffer Brown and Gammer Jones accepted matters as they came, while the rosy-cheeked, red-cloaked village lasses sat beneath their broad hats, entirely unmoved. From his pew, Lord Castlewood nodded slightly to the clergyman as the divine's head and wig surged up from the cushion.

"Sampson has been strong today," said his lordship. "He has assaulted the Philistines in great force."

"Beautiful, beautiful!" exclaimed Harry.

"Bet five to four it was his Assize sermon. He has been over to Winton to preach and to see those dogs," cried William.

Having played the small congregation out into the sunshine, the organist left the church. Only Sir Francis Esmond, *temp.* Jac. I., still knelt on his marble hassock before his stone prayer-book. Mr. Sampson emerged from his vestry in his cassock, nodding to the gentlemen still lingering in the great pew.

"Come up, and tell us about those dogs," said Mr. William, and the divine nodded with a laughing assent.

The gentlemen passed from the church into the gallery connecting it to the house. Mr. Sampson made his way through the courtyard and soon joined them. Introduced by Lord Castlewood to the Virginian cousin of the family, Mr. Warrington, the chaplain bowed profoundly, hoping Mr. Warrington would benefit from the virtuous example of his European kinsmen. Was he related to Sir Miles Warrington of Norfolk? Sir Miles was Mr. Warrington's father's elder brother. What a pity he had a son! It was a pretty estate, and Mr. Warrington looked as if he would secure a baronetcy and a magnificent holding in Norfolk.

"Tell me about my uncle," cried Virginian Harry.

"Tell us about those dogs!" said English Will, breathless.

"Two more jolly dogs, two more drunken dogs, saving your presence, Mr. Warrington, than Sir Miles and his son, I never saw. Sir Miles was a staunch friend and neighbor of Sir Robert's. He can drink down any man in the county, save his son and a few others. As for the other dogs regarding which Mr. William is so anxious—for Heaven hath made him a prey to dogs and all kinds of birds, like the Greeks in the *Iliad*—"

"I know that line in the *Iliad*," said Harry, blushing. "I only know five more, but I know that one." And his head fell. He was thinking, "Ah, my dear brother George knew all the *Iliad* and all the *Odyssey*, and almost every book that was ever written besides!"

"What on earth" (mentioning only a place under the earth) "are you talking about now?" asked Will of his reverence.

The chaplain returned to the dogs and their exploits. He opined that Mr. William's dogs were certainly a match for theirs. From dogs, the conversation drifted to horses. Mr. William was very eager regarding the Six Year Old Plate at Huntingdon. "Have you brought any news of it, Parson?"

"The odds are five to four on Brilliant against the field," said the parson, gravely. "But, mind you, Jason is a good horse."

"Whose horse?" asked my lord.

"Duke of Ancaster's. By Cartouche out of Miss Langley," said the divine. "Have you horse-races in Virginia, Mr. Warrington?"

"Haven't we!" cried Harry. "But oh! I long to see a good English race!"

"Do you... do you... bet a little?" continued his reverence.

"I have done such a thing," replied Harry with a smile.

"I'll take Brilliant even against the field, for ponies with you, cousin!" shouted Mr. William.

"I'll give or take three to one against Jason!" said the clergyman.

"I don't bet on horses I don't know," said Harry, wondering at hearing the chaplain speak thus, remembering his sermon half an hour prior.

"Hadn't you better write home and ask your mother?" suggested Mr. William, with a sneer.

"Will, Will!" called out my lord. "Our cousin Warrington is free to bet, or not, as he pleases. Take care how you venture on either of them, Harry Warrington. Will is an old file, in spite of his smooth face; and as for Parson Sampson, I defy our ghostly enemy to get the better of him."

"Him and all his works, my lord!" said Mr. Sampson, bowing.

Harry was highly indignant at this allusion to his mother. "I'll tell you what, cousin Will," he said. "I am in the habit of managing my own affairs in my own way, without asking any lady to arrange them for me. And I'm used to making my own bets upon my own judgment, and don't need any relations to select them for me, thank you. But as I am your guest, and, no doubt, you want to show me hospitality, I'll take your

Having secured substantial winnings from his cousin and the reverend chaplain, our young Virginian felt a solemn obligation to afford them a chance at redemption. I fear his mother and other voices of serious morality would have disapproved of his conduct. Indeed, he indulged far too freely in card games. Beyond the daily rounds of whist or quadrille, which commenced shortly after the three o'clock dinner and lingered until supper, there arose stakes of considerable magnitude. Into these high-stakes diversions, wherein all the gentlemen, including Lord Castlewood himself, participated, the young man was drawn. Following their Sunday conversations, his lordship had grown notably more open and confiding toward his kinsman, betting with him affably and engaging him in backgammon and piquet. Both Mr. William and the pious chaplain relished a touch of hazard; however, this pastime was conducted clandestinely, hidden from the ladies of the house, who had repeatedly extracted vows from Cousin Will that he would not entice the Virginian into mischief and that he would himself abstain. Thus, Will promised as much as his aunt or mother could demand—a pledge of total abstinence, repeated with fervor—and when the family retired, Mr. William would stroll over to Cousin Harry's chambers armed with a dice-box and a bottle of rum, where he, along with Hal and the reverend gentleman, would gamble until the break of dawn.

When Harry offered those glowing accounts of his maternal estate in America to Lord Castlewood, he harbored no intention to deceive, boast, or lie, for the lad possessed a nature of profound honesty. Yet, life at home had acquainted him with a motley crew of companions—horse-jockeys, tavern loafers, gamblers, and sportsmen, many of whom populated his native colony. An aristocracy of land, surrounded by a workforce of negroes tilling fields of tobacco and corn, found little amusement save in the hunt or at the card table and punch bowl. The hospitality of the province was boundless; every man's house was his neighbor's. Idle gentlemen rode from mansion to mansion, finding in each the same sport, welcome, and rough plenty. The Virginian squire often sat upon a barefooted valet and a cobbled saddle, yet within the crumbling fences and behind the cracked windows of the hall, there abounded corn for the horses, venison for the master, and limitless drink. Harry had slept on countless straw mattresses and engaged in endless, jovial night bouts over claret and punch in cracked bowls until morning, when it was time to follow the hounds. His poorer brother, conversely, was of a much more sober temperament, a fact the lad admitted with contrition. "Thus does Nature fashion folk," the narrator observed, "for some love books and tea, while others crave Burgundy and a gallop across the countryside." These youthful tastes were quickly revealed to his friends in England. None of them favored the rigid Puritan discipline, nor did they dislike Harry for lacking the humility of a milk-drinking lamb. Manners, one sees, were far looser a century prior; tongues were vastly freer, and names and deeds spoken of then, which we would now shriek to hear mentioned. "Yes, madam," the voice of history seems to whisper, "we are not as our ancestors were. Should we not thank the Fates for improving our morals so prodigiously?"

Maintaining a sharp, calculating eye on those around him, and correctly surmising that his cousins sought to probe his affairs, Harry Warrington wisely kept his own counsel regarding his personal matters. In games of chance or sport, he proved quite a match for the three gentlemen into whose company he had fallen. Even in the noble game of billiards, he could hold his own after a few days of play with his cousins and their revered pastor. His grandfather, a lover of the game, had imported from Europe one of the very few tables existing in His Majesty's province of Virginia. Though Mr. William could defeat him at the outset, Harry refused to yield undue odds to the young gambler. After their first wager, Harry remained vigilant against Mr. William, and Cousin William, speaking with respect, acknowledged that the American was his equal in most things and superior in many. While Harry played so well that he could best the parson and soon rival William—who, of course, could best the girls—it remained a mystery why, in contests with them, particularly with one of them, Mr. Warrington frequently emerged second. He was profoundly courteous to every soul wearing a petticoat; nor has that traditional politeness yet departed from his country. Every woman of the Castlewood establishment adored the young gentleman. The stern housekeeper was mollified by him; the portly cook greeted him with boisterous smiles; the ladies' maids, whether French or English, smirked and giggled in his favor; and the porter's daughter at the lodge invariably returned a kind word. Madame de Bernstein noted all these things, saying nothing aloud, but watching carefully the boy's disposition and behavior.

Who can truly say how old Lady Maria Esmond was? Books of the Peerage were scarce in those days compared to our blessed modern times, and I cannot determine her age to within a few years, or even a lustre or two. When Will claimed she was thirty-five, he spoke abusively and was prone to exaggeration. Maria was Will's half-sister, the child of the late Lord Castlewood's first wife, a German lady whom 'tis known my lord wed during the reign of Queen Anne. Baron Bernstein, who married Maria's Aunt Beatrix (Bishop Tusher's widow), was also a German, a Hanoverian nobleman and relative of the first Lady Castlewood. If Lady Maria was born under George I., and if His Majesty George II. had reigned for thirty additional years, how could she possibly be twenty-seven, as she told Harry Warrington? "I am old, child," she used to say, addressing him with a familiarity reserved for intimates. "I am a hundred years old. I am twenty-seven. I might be your mother almost." To which Harry would retort, "Your ladyship might be the mother of all the cupids, I am sure. You don't look twenty, on my word you do not!"

Lady Maria appeared any age one wished. She was a fair beauty with a dazzling complexion of white and red, an abundance of fair hair flowing over her shoulders, and beautiful, round arms which displayed to uncommon advantage when she played billiards with Cousin Harry. When she stretched across the table to execute a stroke, that youth caught glimpses of a dainty ankle, a laced stocking, and a little black satin slipper with a tiny red heel, filling him with unutterable rapture. He swore there had never been such a foot, ankle, stockinged limb, or satin slipper in the world. And yet, oh, foolish Harry! Your mother's foot was far more slender and half an inch shorter than Lady Maria's. But somehow, boys do not gaze with rapture upon their mothers' slippers and ankles.

No doubt Lady Maria was very kind to Harry when they were alone. Before her sister, aunt, stepmother, or in public, she treated him lightly, calling him a simpleton, a chit, and various trivial names. Behind his back, and even before his face, she mimicked his accent, which bore the distinct mark of his province. Harry blushed and corrected his faulty intonation under the tutelage of his English monitresses. His aunt pronounced that they would soon make him a fine gentleman.

Lord Castlewood, as we have said, became daily more familiar and friendly with his guest and relative. Until the crops were gathered, there was little sport save for an occasional cock-match at Winchester and bull-baiting at Hexton Fair. Harry and Will rode to many jolly fairs and races about the region, where the young Virginian was introduced to several county families—the Henleys of the Grange, the Crawleys of Queen's Crawley, the Redmaynes of Lionsden, and so forth. Neighbors arrived in their great heavy coaches, staying two or three days in country fashion. Fewer might have come had not the entire Castlewood family feared offending Madame de Bernstein. She disliked country company; the rustic society and conversation annoyed her. "We shall be merrier when my aunt leaves us," the young folks declared. "We have cause, as you may imagine, to be very civil to her. You know what a favorite she was with our father? And with reason. She procured his earldom, being very well indeed at Court at that time with the King and Queen. She commands here naturally, perhaps a trifle too much. We are all afraid of her: even my elder brother stands in awe of her, and my stepmother is much more obedient to her than she ever was to my father, whom she ruled with a rod of iron. But Castlewood is merrier when our aunt is absent. At least, we have much more company. You will come to us in our gay days, Harry, won't you? Of course you will: this is your home, sir. I was so pleased—oh, so pleased—when my brother said he considered it your home!"

A soft hand was extended following this pretty speech, a pair of well-preserved blue eyes looking exceedingly friendly. Harry grasped his cousin's hand with ardour. I know not what privileges of kinship he would not have claimed, had he not been so timid. They called the English selfish and cold. He initially thought his relatives so; how mistaken he was! How kind and

Young Harry Warrington's sudden rebellion caught Madame de Bernstein completely off guard, leaving her with no alternative response but the prompt, fiery eruption of anger that marked the end of the previous scene. As she stormed from the room, darting two piercing glares at Lady Fanny and her mother, Lady Maria remained oblivious, her attention fixed entirely on her tambour-frame. She barely lifted her head from the embroidery to watch her aunt retreat or to register the sharp, silent exchanges passing between the mother-in-law and her daughter.

"So, despite all warnings, you have yielded, madam?" the maternal gaze seemed to inquire.

"Yield to what?" asked Lady Fanny, her eyes wide with feigned confusion. Yet, innocence was a mask she wore poorly. She lacked the practiced composure of Maria, who, if guilty, would have known exactly how to craft a visage capable of deflecting blame. Whatever truth lay behind Fanny's eyes, her mother stared at her with a glare intense enough to peel flesh, wishing she could pluck them from their sockets.

However, Lady Castlewood possessed no immediate means to extract them, unlike the barbarous monsters described in *King Lear*. When her ladyship intended such violence, she first embraced her daughter with a simpering affection, only to deliver the blow in privacy.

"So you have decided against accompanying the old lady to Tunbridge Wells?" Lady Castlewood asked, masking her displeasure with a perfectly bred smile.

"And who could blame our cousin?" interjected My Lord, breaking the silence. "A young man requires more than idle holidays. He must blend utility with pleasure; perhaps visit the fiddlers and pump-rooms of Tunbridge or Bath later. Mr. Warrington must learn to manage a great American estate; let him observe how ours are conducted here. Will has already shown him the kennels and stables, and the customary games—I believe you play them well enough, cousin. After the harvest, we shall introduce him to English fowling and shooting; in winter, we will take him hunting. Although relations with our kinswoman in Virginia have grown cold, the blood remains the same. Before sending our cousin back to his mother, let us demonstrate what constitutes the life of an English gentleman at home. I have pressed him to stay recently, partly to sport with him, but chiefly to read with him."

Lord Castlewood spoke with such unfeigned candor that his wife, half-brother, and sister stood bewildered, unsure of his intent. These three often conspired in quiet corners, murmuring against the head of the household. When he adopted this open tone, however, his meaning became a riddle unsolvable for months. It was the unwritten convention that Lord Castlewood harbored a noble desire to cultivate domestic affections and educate his young relative, taking a genuine fancy to the lad and wishing Harry to remain under his roof for some time.

"What scheme does Castlewood pursue now?" William whispered to his mother and sister as they slipped down the corridor. "Wait! By George, I have it!"

"What, William?"

"He intends to lure him into gambling, to win back the Virginia estate."

"But the lad possesses no debt to pay if he loses," remarked the mother.

"If my brother has no design in view, may I..."

"Hush! Of course he has a plan. What is it?"

"It cannot be Maria—she is nearly as old as Harry's mother," mused Mr. William.

"Nonsense! With her aged features, sandy hair, and freckled complexion! Impossible!" cried Lady Fanny, exhaling a sigh.

"Of course, your ladyship fancied the Iroquois, too!" laughed the mother.

"I trust I understand my station and duties better than that, madam. Even if I had felt a spark for him, that would not compel me to wed him. You have taught me as much."

"My Lady Fanny!"

"I am certain you married our father without loving him. You have confessed this a thousand times!"

"And if you did not love him prior to the wedding, you certainly did not fall in love afterward," broke in Mr. William, laughing. "Fan and I recall how our honored parents used to quarrel. Did we not, Fan? And little Esmond kept the peace."

"Do not dredge up those dreadful, vulgar scenes, William!" cried the mother. "When your father drank too freely, he became a madman; his conduct must serve as a warning to you, sir, who share this terrible habit."

"I am sure, madam, you found little happiness in marrying the man you did not love, and your ladyship's title has brought scant fortune compared to many trade widows." Lady Fanny whined. "Consider the grocer's widow from London Bridge who moved into our square at Kensington. Her daughters possess three gowns where I have one. While they are attended by merely a man and two maids, I know they feast and drink a thousand times better than we do, subsisting on scraps of cold meat and flanked by our great, flagrant, insolent, lazy lackeys!"

"He! He! Glad I dine at the Palace rather than at home!" chuckled Mr. Will. Through his aunt's influence with Count Puffendorff, Groom of the Royal Powder-Closet, he held a minor court position as Deputy Powder.

"Why should I not find contentment without a title save my own?" continued Lady Frances. "Many do. I dare say they are even happier in America."

"Yes—with a mother-in-law who is a perfect Turk and Tartar, as I hear, with Indian war-whoops echoing around you, and the constant peril of losing your scalp or being devoured by a wild beast whenever you attend church."

"I would not step foot in church," declared Lady Fanny.

"You would go with anyone who asked you, Fan!" roared Mr. Will. "And so would old Maria, and so would any woman, that is the fact." He laughed at his own wit.

"Pray, good friends, what causes this merriment?" asked Madame Bernstein, peering into the gallery from the tapestried door.

Will explained that his mother and sister were embroiled in a dispute—a novelty only to outsiders, given Madame Bernstein's familiarity with their bickering—because Fanny desired to marry their cousin, the "wild Indian," and the Countess forbade it. Fanny immediately protested this fabrication. Since her mother first ordered her to avoid speaking to the young gentleman, she had exchanged fewer than two words with him. She knew her place and feared neither scalping nor being eaten by bears.

Madame Bernstein appeared perplexed. "If he is not staying for you, for whom is he staying?" she asked. "In the homes to which he has been led, you ensured no woman was presented to him unless she was a fright or confined to a nursery. I suspect the boy is too proud to fall in love with a dairymaid, Will."

"Hmph! That is a matter of taste, ma'am," shrugged Mr. William.

"The taste of Mr. William Esmond, as you say; but not of yonder boy. An Esmond, nurtured by his grandfather, would not seek a courtesan in the kitchen."

"Well, ma'am, every man to his taste. A fellow might fare worse than my brother's servants' hall. Besides Fan, there is only the maidens or old Maria to choose from."

"Maria! Impossible!" Yet, as the words left her lips, a sudden thought struck Madame Bernstein. This elderly Calypso might have captivated her young Telemachus. She recalled instances of young men infatuated by older women. She remembered Harry Warrington's recent, unexplained absences, which she had attributed to his love for field sports, and how often Maria Esmond vanished simultaneously. Whispers in garden temples, casual hand-squeezes in twilight corridors, and lingering glances on the stairs suggested a lively romance, an intimate knowledge of the world, and perhaps considerable personal experience. These possibilities danced in her mind, contradicting her initial dismissal of the idea as impossible.

"Impossible, ma'am! I don't know," Will persisted. "My mother warned Fan off."

"Oh, your mother did warn Fanny?"

"Certainly, my dear Baroness!"

"Did she not? Did she not pinch Fanny's arm black-and-blue? Did they not fight over it?"

"Nonsense, William! For shame!" cried both implicated ladies in unison.

"Now, since we have heard how rich he is, perhaps it is sour grapes. And since he has been barred from the young bird, perhaps he is hunting the old one."

"William, how can you speak of Lady Suffolk to your aunt?"

A grin spread across the young gentleman's face. "Because Lady Suffolk was a special favorite at Court? Well, others have succeeded her."

"Sir!" cried Madame Bernstein, sensing offense.

"They have, I say. Who, pray, is Lady Yarmouth now? Did not old Lady Suffolk fall in love with George Berkeley and marry him when she was advanced in years? Nay, ma'am, if I remember aright—and we hear much town-talk at our table—Harry Estridge went mad over you when you were rising twenty; he would have changed your name a third time had you permitted it."

This allusion to a notorious affair from her later years did not anger Madame Bernstein as her previous remark about her favor at George II's court might have. Instead, it put her in a cheerful mood.

As Lady Castlewood, her son, and her daughter filed past the saloon door where they had sat, Lord Castlewood slipped quietly out by another exit. Behind them, the demure eyes lifted from the tambour-frame, having exhausted their examination of innocent violets and jonquils, to fix upon Harry Warrington. He stood beneath an ancestral portrait by the great fireplace, his honest countenance adorned with a great heap of blushes—flowers that bloom so rarely after a gentleman's springtime—that they decorated his cheeks, his forehead, and even his youthful ears.

"Why did you refuse to go with our aunt, cousin?" asked the lady of the tambour-frame.

"Because your ladyship bade me stay," answered the lad.

"I bid you stay! La! child! What one says in fun, you take in earnest!" cried the spinster, feigning indignation. "Are all you Virginian gentlemen so obsequious as to fancy every idle word a lady says is a command? Virginia must be a pleasant country for our sex if it be so!"

"You said—" Harry stammered, his voice trembling with sudden emotion, "—when we walked in the terrace two nights since..."

"Oh, that sweet night, cousin!" interrupted the Tambour-frame eagerly.

"When you gave me this rose from your own neck," roared Harry, producing a crumpled and decaying vegetable from his waistcoat with surprising force, "which I will never part with—not by heavens, whilst this heart continues to beat! You said, 'Harry, if your aunt asks you to go away, you will go, and if you go, you will forget me.' Didn't you say so?"

"All men forget!" sighed the Virginian.

"In this cold, selfish country they may, cousin—but not in ours," Harry continued, his spirit soaring again. "I had rather have lost an arm almost than refused the old lady. It went to my heart to say no to her; she is so kind, and she was the means of introducing me to..." He paused, overwhelmed. "O heaven!"

(A kick sent an intervening spaniel yelping from before the fire, followed by a rapid advance toward the tambour-frame.) "Look here, cousin!" cried Harry, seizing her hand. "If you were to bid me jump out of yonder window, I should do it; or murder—I should do it."

"La! but you need not squeeze one's hand so, you silly child!" remarked Maria.

"I can't help it—we are so in the South," Harry protested, his grip unyielding. "Where my heart is, I can't help speaking my mind out, cousin—and you know where that heart is! Ever since that evening—that O heaven! I tell you I have hardly slept since. I want to do something—to distinguish myself—to be ever so great. I wish there were giants, Maria, as I have read of in books, that I could go and fight 'em. I wish you were in distress, that I might help you, somehow. I wish you wanted my blood, that I might spend every drop of it for you. And when you told me not to go with Madame Bernstein..."

"I tell thee, child, *never*."

"I thought you told me," Harry insisted, his eyes shining with feverish devotion. "You said you knew I preferred my aunt to my cousin. And I said then what I say now: 'Incomparable Maria! I prefer thee to all the women in the world and all the angels in Paradise—and I would go anywhere, were it to dungeons, if you ordered me!' And do you think I would not stay anywhere, when you only desired that I should be near you?" he added after a moment's pause.

"Men always talk in that way—that is, that is what I have heard so," said the spinster, correcting herself quickly. "For what should a country-bred woman know about you creatures? When you are near us, they say you are all raptures and flames and promises and I don't know what; when you are away, you forget all about us."

"But I think I never want to go away as long as I live," groaned the young man. "I have tired of many things; not books and that, I never cared for study much, but games and sports which I used to be fond of when I was a boy. Before I saw you, it was to be a soldier I most desired; I tore my hair with rage when my poor dear brother went away instead of me on that expedition in which we lost him. But now, I only care for one thing in the world, and you know what that is."

"You silly child! don't you know I am almost old enough to be..."

"I know—I know!" Harry interrupted, ignoring the implication. "But what is that to me? Haven't your brothers—or well, never mind who—told me stories against you? Didn't they show me the Family Bible, where all your names are down, and the dates of your birth?"

"The cowards! Who did that?" cried out Lady Maria, her composure cracking. "Dear Harry, tell me who did that? Was it my mother-in-law, the grasping, odious, abandoned, brazen harpy? Do you know all about her? How she married my father in his cups—the horrid hussey!--and..."

"It wasn't Lady Castlewood," interposed the wondering Harry.

"Then it was my aunt," continued the infuriate lady, her anger rising again. "A pretty moralist, indeed! A bishop's widow, forsooth, and I should like to know whose widow before and afterwards. Why, Harry, she intrigued with the Pretender, and with the Court of Hanover, and, I dare say, would with the Court of Rome and the Sultan of Turkey if she had had the means. Do you know who her second husband was? A creature who..."

"But our aunt never spoke a word against you," broke in Harry, growing more and more amazed at the nymph's vehemence.
