She checked her anger. In the inquisitive countenance opposite to her, she thought she read some alarm regarding the temper she was exhibiting.

"Well, well! I am a fool," she said, smoothing her dress. "I want thee to think well of me, Harry!"

A hand was put out and seized, and no doubt kissed by the rapturous youth. "Angel!" he cried, looking into her face with his eager, honest eyes.

Two fish-pools irradiated by a pair of stars would not kindle to greater warmth than did those elderly orbs into which Harry poured his gaze. Nevertheless, he plunged into their blue depths, and fancied he saw heaven in their calm brightness. So that silly dog (of whom Aesop or the Spelling-book used to tell us in youth) beheld a beef-bone in the pond, and snapped at it, and lost the beef-bone he was carrying. O absurd cur! He saw the beefbone in his own mouth reflected in the treacherous pool, which dimpled, I dare say, with ever so many smiles, coolly sucked up the meat, and returned to its usual placidity. Ah! what a heap of wreck lie beneath some of those quiet surfaces! What treasures we have dropped into them! What chased golden dishes, what precious jewels of love, what bones after bones, and sweetest heart's flesh! Do not some very faithful and unlucky dogs jump in bodily, when they are swallowed up heads and tails entirely? When some women come to be dragged, it is a marvel what will be found in the depths of them. *Cavete, canes!* Have a care how ye lap that water. What do they want with us, the mischievous siren sluts? A green-eyed Naiad never rests until she has inveigled a fellow under the water; she sings after him, she dances after him; she winds round him, glittering tortuously; she warbles and whispers dainty secrets at his cheek, she kisses his feet, she leers at him from out of her rushes: all her beds sigh out, "Come, sweet youth! Hither, hither, rosy Hylas!" *Pop* goes Hylas. (Surely the fable is renewed for ever and ever?) Has his captivator any pleasure? Doth she take any account of him? No more than a fisherman landing at Brighton does of one out of a hundred thousand herrings.... The last time. Ulysses rowed by the Sirens' bank, he and his men did not care though a whole shoal of them were singing and combing their longest locks. Young Telemachus was for jumping overboard: but the tough old crew held the silly, bawling lad. They were deaf, and could not hear his bawling nor the sea-nymphs' singing. They were dim of sight, and did not see how lovely the witches were. The stale, old, leering witches! Away with ye! I dare say you have painted your cheeks by this time; your wretched old songs are as out of fashion as Mozart, and it is all false hair you are combing!

In the last sentence you see Lector Benevolus and Scriptor Doctissimus figure as tough old Ulysses and his tough old Boatswain, who do not care a quid of tobacco for any Siren at Sirens' Point; but Harry Warrington is green Telemachus, who, be sure, was very unlike the soft youth in the good Bishop of Cambray's tw

At the following meal, when the household gathered, there was not a shadow of displeasure upon Madame de Bernstein's countenance. Her behavior toward the entire company, Harry included, was marked by a kindness and cordiality that seemed almost unnatural given the morning's storm. She lavished praise upon the cook, declaring the fricassee exquisite and noting that no eels in the world could compare to those swimming in the Castlewood moats. She staunchly defended the vintage, refusing to accept the notion that the wine was corked or that it would be an extravagance to open a fresh bottle for an "useless old woman" like herself.

When the new wine was served, she raised a toast to Madam Esmond Warrington of Virginia, expressing a hope that Harry had secured his mother's permission to bring back an English wife with him. Harry did not recall his grandmother, yet the Baroness spoke of her with boundless affection—describing her beauty, her goodness, and her profound happiness despite the significant age difference between her and the elderly Colonel Esmond. She recounted tales of their union, describing it as a model of devotion celebrated throughout the countryside, rendering any discussion of marital disparity utterly vain. While Lady Castlewood and her two children sat in respectful silence, Madame Bernstein prattled on, her spirits infectious. Lord Castlewood, though puzzled by this sudden metamorphosis in his aunt's demeanor, carefully concealed any sign of solicitude or doubt, wearing instead the mask of perfect satisfaction.

The Baroness's good humor permeated the entire gathering; no one at table escaped a gracious word from her. When the artless Mr. Will expressed his surprise at her transformation, she frankly admitted her fault. "Complimentary, my dear!" she exclaimed. "Of course I am. I wish to make amends for having been exceedingly rude to everyone this morning." She recalled her childhood in this very room, at this very table, centuries prior. "If I had been naughty in the morning, I used to try to coax my parents at night," she confessed, her eyes twinkling. "I remember in this place, oh, ever so many hundred years ago, how I coaxed my father, my mother, and your grandfather, Harry Warrington. It was that same dish of collared eels which brought the memory back to me. I was as wayward then at seven years old as I am today at seventy. So I confess my sins and ask forgiveness, like a good girl."

"I absolve your ladyship!" cried the chaplain, joining the festivities.

"But your reverence knows not how cross and ill-tempered I truly was," she continued, her voice softening. "I scolded my sister, Castlewood; I scolded her children; I boxed Harry Warrington's ears—all because he would not go with me to Tunbridge Wells."

"But I will go, madam!" cried Mr. Warrington eagerly. "I will ride with you with all the pleasure in life."

"You see, Mr. Chaplain, what good, dutiful children they all are," the Baroness observed, turning her gaze to the others. "'Twas I alone who was cross and peevish. Oh, it was cruel of me to treat them so! Maria, I ask your pardon, my dear."

"Sure, madam, you have done me no wrong," Maria replied humbly to her humble suppliant.

"Indeed I have, a very great wrong, child!" the Baroness insisted. "Because I was weary of myself, I told you that your company would be wearisome to me. Yet you offered to accompany me to Tunbridge, and I rudely refused you."

"Nay, ma'am, if you were sick..." Maria began timidly.

"But it will not annoy me!" interrupted the Baroness, her hands clasped imploringly. "You were most kind to say you would come. I do, of all things, beg, pray, entreat, implore, command that you will come."

Lord Castlewood filled a glass and sipped it slowly, his expression utterly unconscious of the comedy that had unfolded. This, then, was the meaning of the previous farce.

"Anything which can give my aunt pleasure, I am sure, will delight me," Maria said, striving to look as happy as possible.

"You must come and stay with me, my dear," the Baroness promised, vowing to be good-humoured. "And you, my dear lord, will spare your sister to me?"

"Lady Maria Esmond is quite of age to judge for herself regarding such matters," Lord Castlewood replied with a formal bow, though the sentence, when interpreted, likely carried a subtext of, "Plague take the old woman! She is taking Maria away to separate her from this young Virginian."

"Oh, Tunbridge will be delightful!" sighed Lady Maria.

"And Mr. Sampson will go to see Goody Jones for you," Lord Castlewood noted.

While the adults arranged the itinerary, Harry Warrington traced patterns on the tablecloth with his finger. His mind raced with fantasies of delights: endless walks, spirited rides, interminable conversations, delicious shrubberies, and sweet summer houses. He imagined poring over music books, bathed in moonlight, engaged in billing and cooing. The day was approaching. Everyone else was departing—Lady Castlewood to her friends, Madame Bernstein to her waters—and he was to be left alone with his divine charmer. Alone with her and unutterable rapture! The thought of the impending pleasure was maddening. These people were leaving, and he was to be left to enjoy this heaven, to sit at the feet of that angel and kiss the hem of her white robe. *O Gods!* It was a bliss too great to be real. "I knew it couldn't be," thought poor Harry. "I knew something would happen to take her from me."

"But you will ride with us to Tunbridge, Nephew Warrington, and keep us from the highwaymen?" Madame de Bernstein asked.

Harry hoped the company did not see how red his cheeks grew. He forced his voice to remain calm and free of tremor. "Yes," he assured them, "I will ride with your ladyships. I am sure there is no danger." Indeed, Harry felt he would prefer danger to anything else. He vowed mentally that he would slay ten thousand highwaymen if they dared approach his mistress's coach. At the very least, he would ride beside it, catching glimpses of her eyes at the window. He might not speak to her, but he would be near. He would press her blessed hand at the inn at night and feel it resting on his as he led her to the carriage at dawn. With two whole days' journey ahead, perhaps he could stay at Tunbridge for a day or two. Was not the wretch condemned to Newgate grateful for even a brief respite?

One need not choose to describe in length the condition of Mr. Harry Warrington, nor the utter depth of imbecility into which the poor young man had now fallen. Some boys suffer from love gently and favorably; others, when struck by the fever, become sick unto death, carrying the scars of the malady to their graves or into remotest old age. It is not fair to transcribe a young fellow's ravings when he is raging in such a delirium. Suppose he loves a woman twice his age; have we not all read of the young gentleman who committed suicide due to his fatal passion for Mademoiselle Ninon de l'Enclos, who turned out to be his grandmother? Perhaps you, young Harry Warrington of Virginia, are making an ass of yourself. Are there not people in England who bray too loudly? Kick and abuse him, you who have never brayed yourselves; but bear with him, all honest fellow-cardophagi, long-eared messmates, for we recognize a brother-donkey!

"You will stay with us for a day or two at the Wells," Madame Bernstein continued. "You will see us settle into our lodgings. Then you may return to Castlewood and the partridge-shooting, and all the fine pursuits you and my lord are to study together."

Harry bowed in acquiescence. A whole week of heaven! Life was not altogether a blank, then.

"As there is surely to be plenty of company at the Wells," the lady added graciously, "I shall be able to present you."

"Company! Ah! I shan't need company," Harry sighed, though his tone betrayed his eagerness. "I mean that I shall be quite contented in the company of you two ladies." No doubt Mr. Will wondered at his cousin's peculiar tastes.

Since this was to be the last night of cousin Harry's visit to Castlewood, cousin Will suggested that he, the chaplain, and Warrington meet at Harry's quarters to settle accounts. Harry, having emerged as a considerable winner in his recent play transactions with the two gentlemen, had no objection. Accordingly, when the ladies retired for the night and Lord Castlewood withdrew, as was his custom, to his own apartments, the three gentlemen found themselves assembled in Mr. Harry's small chamber before the punch-bowl, which was Will's usual midnight companion.

However, Will's method of settling accounts involved producing a couple of fresh packs of cards and offering to subject Harry's debt to the process of being doubled or acquitted. The poor chaplain possessed no more ready cash than Lord Castlewood's younger brother, and Harry Warrington wished to win the money of neither. Would he cause pain to the brother of his ad

While the virtuous Bishop of Cambrai, in his recent romance, lamented Calypso's desolation at Ulysses' departure, I recall not whether he noted the sorrow of her handmaiden leaving Odysseus's own gentleman. Yet surely the servants must have wept in the kitchens while their masters embraced wildly in the drawing-room; they must have clung to one another in the forecastle as their principals shattered their hearts in the salon. When the final bell tolled and the mate roared, "Now! Any one for shore!" Calypso and her attendant stepped onto the same rotting plank, hearts pounding, eyes streaming. They waved handkerchiefs—of vastly different value and weave—to friends on the quay, while crowds on land and crew in the bows shouted "Hip, hip, hurrah!" in some ancient tongue. The truth remains: if Calypso could not console herself, her maid could not either. They traversed the same plank of grief, feeling the identical sting of separation. Upon returning home, their handkerchiefs might differ in texture, but the tears shed in marble halls and the servants' quarters alike were equally salty and abundant.

Harry Warrington departed Castlewood a captive of love, yet so did Gumbo, fleeing the same premises driven by a delightful passion. Though Gumbo's wit, accomplishments, humor, and skills in dance, cooking, and music endeared him to the entire female domestic circle, jealousy among the men was a distant concern. In those days, no such objections existed against black men as plague modern whites. Their condition was not equal, yet they possessed a sufferance and a grotesque sympathy from all, particularly from women, whose kindness toward them was boundless and generous. When Ledyard and Parke faced persecution in Blackmansland, did they not find black women pitiful and kind? Women, it seems, always cherish our sex. What mental negroes do they not adore? What moral hunchbacks, lepers, idiots, or misshapen monsters do they not fondle and cuddle? Gumbo was treated with the same gentleness afforded to many far superior beings; it was only the men in the servants' hall who rejoiced at the Virginian lad's departure. I long to witness his farewell: I picture Molly, the housemaid, slipping to the terrace gardens at grey dawn to gather a wistful bouquet, and Betty, the kitchen maid, snipping a thick lock of chestnut ringlets to trade for a woolly token from young Gumbo's head. Naturally, he claimed descent from Ashantee royalty (*regum progenies*), boasting of ancestors who once ruled vast realms in Caffraria, Connaught, and elsewhere.

At the appointed hour for Madame de Bernstein's departure, the numerous domestics of Castlewood swarmed the doors and passages. Some sought a final glimpse of her ladyship's men and the captivating Gumbo; others to bid farewell to her maid; all to intercept the Baroness and her nephew for parting fees, a customary largesse distributed that day. Generously, both bestowed gifts upon the liveried staff—the gentlemen in black and ruffles, the swarm of female attendants. Castlewood was the Baroness's youth, and as for her honest Harry, who had lived at free charge, won horses, and secured promises of money from his cousin and the unlucky chaplain, he was naturally generous. He reasoned that his mother, knowing the full extent of his generosity, would expect him to treat the retainers of the Esmond family liberally. Thus, he scattered gold pieces right and left, acting as if he were as wealthy as Gumbo proclaimed. From the major-domo to the shoeblack, Harry offered a peace-offering to all. To the grim housekeeper in her still-room, the feeble old porter in his lodge, he distributed tokens of remembrance. When a man loves one woman in a family, he strangely becomes fond of every soul connected to it. He ingratiates himself with maids, is bland with the butler, interests himself in footmen, runs errands for daughters, advises and lends to the young son at college, pats dogs he would otherwise kick, smiles at old stories he would otherwise yawn through, drinks sweet port he would otherwise curse, endures the cantankerous old maiden aunt, beats time for little Fanny's piano recitals, and smiles when wicked little Bobby spills coffee over his shirt.

For a brief time, Harry Warrington, following the customs of that age, had bestowed these artless testimonies of affection upon the Castlewood family, having recently declared and gained acceptance from one of them. Cousin Will should have reclaimed his money and welcome, or at least some of Harry's own, yet the lad, though a lover, remained shrewd, keen, and fond of sport and fair play—a true judge of a good horse. Having gambled away all of Will's money, alongside numerous autographs from Mr. Esmond, Harry was delighted to secure Will's brown horse—the same quadruped that had nearly thrown him into the water on his first evening at Castlewood. Having gazed upon the stars shining over his mistress's window and extinguished her candle, he retired to his dormitory. There, undoubtedly, he ruminated on his Maria and his horse with youthful satisfaction, dreaming of making the tour of the island on such a splendid animal with a pair of white arms encircling his waist. He fell asleep blessing his Maria a million times, anticipating at least a week more of her company.

In the early morning, poor Chaplain Sampson dispatched his little black mare via his groom, footman, and gardener, who wept and kissed the beast's white nose as they handed her over to Gumbo. Both Gumbo and his master were moved by the fellow's sensibility; the negro servant wept openly, while Harry produced two guineas to astonish and swiftly comfort the chaplain's boy. Then Gumbo and the former groom led the beast to the stable, receiving orders to return with Mr. William's horse after breakfast, at the hour the Baroness's carriages were scheduled.

So courteous was Harry to his aunt, or so grateful for her departure, that the master of the house appeared at the morning meal to bid farewell to his guests. The ladies and the chaplain were present; the only absentee was Will, who left a note stating, in exceedingly poor spelling, that he was obliged to depart for Salisbury Races that morning but had left the horse Harry had won the previous night. Tom, Mr. Will's groom, would deliver it to Mr. Warrington's servant. Will's absence did not hinder the remaining party from drinking tea amicably. Soon, the carriages rolled into the courtyard, servants packed them with the Baroness's multiplied luggage, and the moment of departure arrived.

A large open landau carried the stout Baroness and her niece, flanked by two men-servants on the box with pistols and blunderbusses ready for highwaymen. Another carriage held their ladyships' maids, while a third guarded the trunks, vast and numerous as they were, dwarfed only by the enormous baggage-train accompanying a lady of the present time. Mr. Warrington's modest valises were placed in the second carriage under the maid's guardianship, and Mr. Gumbo proposed to ride by the window for most of the journey.

My Lord, with his stepmother and Lady Fanny, accompanied their kinswoman to the carriage steps, bidding farewell with many dutiful embraces. Her Lady Maria followed in a riding dress, which Harry considered the most becoming costume in the world. A host of servants stood around, begging Heaven to bless her ladyship. The Baroness's departure was known throughout the village, and scores of folk waited under the trees outside the gates, cheering and waving hats as the ponderous vehicles rolled away.

Gumbo had gone for Mr. Warrington's horses while My Lord, with his arm under his young guest's, paced the court. "I hear you carry away some of our horses out of Castlewood?" My Lord asked.

Harry blushed. "A gentleman cannot refuse a fair game at the cards," he replied. "I never wanted to play, nor would have played for money had not my cousin William forced me. As for the chaplain, it went to my heart to win from him, but he was as eager as my cousin."

"I know—I know! There is no blame to you, my boy. At Rome you can't help doing as Rome does. I am very glad you have been able to give Will a lesson. He is mad about play—he would gamble his coat off his back—and I and the family have had to pay his debts many times. May I ask how much you have won of him?"

"Well, some eighteen pieces the first day or two, and his note for a hundred and twenty more, and the brown horse, sixty—that makes nigh upon two hundred. But, you know, cousin, all was fair, and it was even against my will that we played at all. Will ain't a match for me, my lord—that is the fact. Indeed he is not."

"He is a match for most people, though," said My Lord. "His brown horse, I think you said?"

"Yes. His brown horse—Prince William, out of Constitution. You don't suppose I would set him sixty against his bay, my lord?"

"Oh, I didn't know. I saw Will riding out this morning; most likely I did not remark what horse he was on. And you won the black mare from the parson?"

"For fourteen. He will mount Gumbo very well. Why does not the rascal come round with the horses?" Harry's mind drifted to lovely

Let no gentle soul tremble for Mr. Harry Warrington's fate, supposing his shattered steed had borne him swiftly to the end of his earthly journey. Rest assured, at the outset of this chapter, that no tragedy of consequence has befallen our hero. To dispatch a protagonist, still in the bloom of youth, merely because he tumbled from a horse would be a cruelty unworthy of a tale wherein the age of manhood has not yet fully dawned. Indeed, we mourn already for one Virginian who met his end across the Atlantic; surely, we need not extinguish the spirit of the other within the bounds of England? Heroes do not vanish without cause, nor does the chronicle itself meet its grave unless necessity demands it. Had a gentleman perished with every stumble of his mount, the author of this history would long since have joined the silent majority beneath the earth, whereas the unfortunate youth sprawls safely outside, destined to rise anew once delivered into the sanctuary of Madame de Bernstein's household.

To prove that this youngest of the Virginians draws breath, consider this genuine correspondence from the lady who received him after his fall from Master Will's unruly beast, and in whose kindness he found refuge:

"At Her House at Richmond, in Virginia

"If Mrs. Esmond Warrington of Virginia can recall twenty-three years past, when Miss Rachel Esmond attended Kensington Boarding School, she may perhaps remember Miss Molly Benson, her classmate. Any petty squabbles they shared—of which Miss Molly was frequently at fault—are now forgotten, save for the memory of the generous, high-spirited, vivacious Miss Esmond, the Princess Pocahontas, to whom so many of our schoolfellows paid homage.

"Dear Madam! I cannot erase the fact that you were dear Rachel once, as I was your dearest Molly. Though we parted not on terms of perfect friendship when you returned to Virginia, you know how fond we were. I still cherish the gold etui your father gifted me upon his visit to our speech-day at Kensington; we once acted out the quarrel of Brutus and Cassius from Shakespeare. It was but yesterday morning I dreamed we were summoned before the stern Miss Hardwood to recite our lessons, and that, as usual, Miss Rachel Esmond stood above me. How vividly those days remain! How youthful we seem when recalling them! I remember our strolls and drills, our beloved King and Queen walking through Kensington Gardens with their retinue, while the pupils of Miss Hardwood curtsied in a single file. I can still recount our weekly dinners and point to the spot where your garden flourished, ever superior to mine. Your chest of drawers was a model of neatness, whereas mine were a mess. Do you recall the tales we spun in the dormitory, interrupted by Madame Hibou, the French governess, emerging from bed with her hoots? Have you forgotten the dancing master, who claimed to be ambushed by assassins, only to be dispatched by your brother's footmen? My dear, your cousin, Lady Maria Esmond (whose father was, I believe, Viscount Castlewood at that time), has recently visited this house. I spared her mention of those sorrowful times, about which I am now garrulous to Mrs. Esmond.

"Her Ladyship has stayed here, along with another relative of yours, the Baroness of Bernstein. Both ladies have departed for Tunbridge Wells; however, a dearer relative remains in my house, sound asleep, I trust, in the very next chamber. His name is Mr. Henry Esmond Warrington. Now, do you comprehend how you receive this news? Be not alarmed, dear Madam! I perceive you thinking, 'My boy is ill; therefore Miss Molly Benson writes.' Nay, my dear; Mr. Warrington was unwell yesterday, but today he is quite comfortable. Our physician, no less a person than my dear husband, Colonel Lambert, has bled him, reduced his dislocated shoulder, and declares that in two days more, Mr. Warrington shall be fit to travel.

"I fear my girls and I shall regret his swift recovery. Yesterday evening, a loud ringing at our gate disturbed the peace, a sound rare in this quiet retreat unless a wandering beggar sought alms. The servants ran forth and returned with tidings that a young gentleman, having fallen from his horse, lay seemingly lifeless upon the road, encircled by his traveling companions. At this, my Colonel—who is surely the most Samaritan of men!—hurried forth to render aid to the fallen traveler. Soon thereafter, aided by the servants and followed by two ladies, he brought into the house such a pale, lifeless, beautiful youth! Ah, my dear, how I rejoice that your child has found shelter and succor under my roof! That my husband has spared him from agony and fever, and served as the instrument of restoring him to you and health! We shall be friends again, shall we not? I fell grievously ill last year, nearly meeting my end. In those moments, I often thought of you and our estrangement born of anger so many years prior. I began a foolish note to you, which sickness prevented me from finishing, wishing to declare that if death were to claim me, I should depart the world in charity toward every soul I had ever known.

"The Right Honourable Lady Maria Esmond displayed profound maternal tenderness and concern for her young kinsman following his accident. I am certain she possesses a kind heart. The Baroness de Bernstein, being of advanced years, could not be expected to feel as acutely as the younger generation; yet she remained deeply moved and interested until Mr. Warrington regained consciousness, whereupon she expressed anxiety to proceed to Tunbridge, fearing to linger where medical assistance was absent. My Aesculapius laughed gently, stating he would not undertake the care of a lady of quality, yet he vouched for his young patient. Indeed, the Colonel, through his campaigns, has ample experience with such accidents, and I am convinced that were the entire faculty within twenty miles summoned, Mr. Warrington could receive no better treatment. Thus, leaving the young gentleman to the care of me and my daughters, the Baroness and her Ladyship took their leave, the latter reluctant to depart. When he is strong enough, my Colonel will ride with him as far as Westerham, but on his own horses, to an army comrade of Mr. Lambert's residence. Since this letter shall not reach Falmouth via the post until, by God's grace, your son is well and perfectly restored, you need harbor no alarm for him while under the roof of, Madam, your affectionate and humble servant, MARY LAMBERT.

"P.S. Thursday.

"I am glad to learn (Mr. Warrington's colored gentleman informed our staff of this gratifying turn of events) that Providence has blessed Mrs. Esmond with such immense wealth and an heir poised to honor it. Our current means suffice, yet will dwindle when divided among our survivors. Ah, dear Madam! I have heard of your calamity from last year. Though the Colonel and I have raised many children (five), we have lost two, and a mother's heart feels for yours! I confess, my heart yearned for your boy today when, in a manner inexpressibly moving to me and Mr. Lambert, he spoke of his dear brother. 'Tis impossible to behold your son and not to love and esteem him. I am grateful that it has been our lot to succor him in his distress, and that in welcoming a stranger within our gates, we extend hospitality to the son of an old friend.'"

Nature inscribes a letter of credit upon the visage of certain men, honored almost universally where presented. Harry Warrington's countenance bore this stamp in his youth. His eyes burned with brightness, his cheek flushed with health, his gaze so frank and open that almost all who beheld him—even those who sought to deceive him—trusted him implicitly. Yet, as hinted, the lad was not the artless stripling he appeared. He possessed a knowingness commensurate with his blushing cheeks; perhaps more wily and wary than he became in later ages. Surely, a shrewd and generous man, who leads an upright life and harbors no secret blushes for his conscience, grows simpler with age, arriving at the summation of right through more rapid calculations, learning to discard false arguments with ease, and striking the mark of truth with less preliminary aim and mental disturbance. Or is it merely a senile delusion that our vanities cure themselves with the years, rendering us more just in our perceptions of our own and neighbors' shortcomings? I would humbly suggest that young people, though they possess fairer features, larger eyes, and fewer wrinkles around their lids, are often as artful as some of their elders. What little monsters of cunning are your frank schoolboys! With what dexterity they cheat their mothers, hoodwink their fathers, bamboozle the housekeeper, and cringe to the senior boy for whom they toil at school! What a protracted falsehood and five years of hypocrisy and flattery constitute their conduct toward Dr. Birch! And what of the little boys' sisters? Are they any better, or do the little darlings learn a trick or two only after entering the world?

From the aforementioned letter of Mrs. Lambert, it is evident that she, like all virtuous women (and indeed, almost all wicked ones), was a sentimental soul. As she regarded Harry Warrington reclining in her finest bed, following the Colonel's bleeding and setting of his shoulder, and holding her husband

The gentle cadence of the evening faded, and the young patient sank into a slumber of sweet oblivion. When consciousness returned, the heavy lids lifted to reveal a shaft of summer sunlight pouring across the floorboards. Beside his bed, the kindly host and hostess smiled, their faces warm with genuine concern. Hunger, sharp and immediate, seized him; yet the doctor, observing his state, gave permission for a bowl of chicken and rice. The doctor's wife mentioned, with a touch of pride, that the meal had been prepared by the hands of one of her own daughters.

A sudden, vivid image pierced the haze of his recovery. Two figures, youthful and vibrant, with flushed cheeks and dark, flowing hair, had hovered around his couch, offering comfort, only to vanish the moment he opened his eyes. Then, the reality of the present intruded—a vision of a woman, undeniably lovely yet considerably older, accompanied by a shadow of a scandal that brought a flush of horror and deep remorse to the young man's brow. He writhed slightly in his sheets, the weight of time pressing heavily upon him. An abyss yawned between his current self and the innocent boy he had been. How long, indeed, since he had believed those pearls to be real, or those golden tresses natural? That innocence belonged to a distant childhood; now, he was a man, aged and weary, plagued by a lingering fever and the bitter taste of deception.

"What troubles you, my dear?" cooed Mrs. Lambert, sensing his start.

"Nothing, madam," Harry murmured, trying to steady his voice. "Only a twinge in my shoulder. I speak to my hosts? You have been most kind."

"We are old friends, Mr. Warrington," replied the Colonel, his voice thick with history. "My wife and your mother were schoolmates at Kensington. To us, you were never a stranger, however distant your circumstances."

"And they?" Harry asked, glancing at the empty chairs. "Are Aunt and Cousin here?"

"They rested at Tunbridge Wells last night," the Colonel explained gently. "A messenger came from Reigate seeking news."

"I remember," Harry said, touching his bandaged arm. "Thank you both."

"Do not trouble yourself," said the Colonel, rising. "I have healed you sufficiently. From this moment, Mrs. Lambert and the cook shall attend to your needs."

"Nay!" interjected Mrs. Lambert quickly. "Theo prepared the chicken and rice, Mr. Lambert. Will Mr. Warrington rise after breakfast? We shall send your valet."

"If loyalty is measured by howling, your man must be a creature of profound devotion," the Colonel laughed.

"He did allow your luggage to depart in your aunt's carriage," Mrs. Lambert added softly. "You will wear my husband's linen until your own returns. I trust it is not inferior to yours."

"Pish!" cried the Colonel, arching an eyebrow. "These shirts serve any Christian."

"They are the work of Theo and Hester," Mrs. Lambert insisted, glancing at her husband with a knowing smile. "And Theo has stitched the sleeve to accommodate your injured shoulder."

Turning his gaze to a porcelain vase brimming with crimson blooms on the dressing table, Harry exclaimed, "What beautiful roses!"

"My daughter Theo cut them this morning," the Colonel said, chuckling. "She is a gardener as well as a nurse."

Harry nodded, distracted by the scent. "Last night, I thought I heard someone singing the Evening Hymn so sweetly..."

"Theo again!" the Colonel laughed. "Our servants claim your former servant sang it with the power of an organ."

"Our people sing it at home, sir," Harry said, his voice softening. "My grandfather loved it. His wife's father was a close friend of Bishop Ken, the author. And... my brother loved it, too." The words slipped out, heavy with a grief he had tried to suppress.

For a fleeting moment, Mrs. Lambert felt compelled to press a kiss to his forehead. The boy's suffering, followed by the warmth of their care, had thawed the icy shell Harry had built around his heart. For nearly six weeks, he had breathed an air tainted by vanity and self-interest, but here, amidst the quiet kindness of the Lamberts, he drew breath that smelled of purity. Fate, or perhaps a shift in his own nature, had placed him in the circle of wise and virtuous souls, whose lamps burned bright and whose hearts beat with modesty.

Leaving Harry to enjoy Miss Theo's nourishing fare, the Colonel settled beside the bed. Gratitude mingled with physical satisfaction, leaving Harry in a buoyant frame of mind. Usually taciturn and guarded, the Virginian now found his tongue loosened. He was no scholar of literature, his knowledge limited to the practical matters of life—dogs, horses, and the mechanics of the world. Yet, on these subjects, he was a keen critic, eager to converse with anyone who shared his interests.

Soon, the Colonel, a man of sharp wit and worldly experience, gauged his guest. Harry learned of the Colonel's bond with his mother and the shared military history of their families dating back to Queen Anne's reign. For the first time, Harry felt the comforting certainty of being in a friend's country, surrounded by honest, cordial men.

"My sons are away," the Colonel noted. "Thus, you shall find little company save for my wife and her daughters. Mrs. Lambert has spoken of Theo, our eldest—the soup-maker, the tailor, the singer who lulled you to rest. She is a sensible housekeeper, though perhaps not the miracle her mother paints her to be."

"It is very kind of Miss Lambert," Harry said gratefully.

"She does no more than duty demands," the Colonel replied, a smile playing on his lips. "I jest with her praises, yet I admit, God has gifted us with dutiful children, and I need no disguise to express my thanks. Do you have a sister, Mr. Warrington?"

"No, sir. I am alone."

"Ay, I apologize for my thoughtlessness. Your man spoke of last year's affair. I served with General Braddock in Scotland. I trust he recovered before passing." The Colonel leaned forward. "A wild soul, yes, but with a core of truth beneath the rough exterior. Your Negro servant speaks freely of you. He claims you rescued him from the Indians. Is that true?"

"Rescued me?" Harry sat up, surprise coloring his tone.

"On that very expedition, you saved him from a party of redskins," the Colonel confirmed. "Molly and I never expected to host a man of such apparent wealth. He swears half of Virginia is yours. Even if North America itself belonged to you, we would offer only our best."

"These negro boys," Harry corrected sharply, "lie with the fluency of the Father of Lies. They imagine it honors us to portray us as fabulously rich. My mother holds a vast estate in England, and we live as comfortably as most neighbors. All the splendor exists only in Gumbo's delusion. He never rescued me from an Indian; indeed, he would have fled at the sight of one, just as my brother's boy did on that fatal day."

"The bravest man runs at unlucky times," the Colonel observed calmly. "I saw the finest regiments rout at Preston before a ragged mob of Highlanders."

"That was because the Highlanders fought for a righteous cause, sir," Harry countered.

"Do you think the French and their Indian allies possessed a good cause in last year's war?" the Colonel asked.

"The scoundrels!" Harry clenched his fist. "I would trade scalps for the honor of cutting down every murderer among them! They robbed and invaded British lands. But the Highlanders fought for their King."

"So did we," the Colonel agreed, his laughter ringing clear. "And we won the battle."

"Oh!" Harry cried, his eyes lighting up with fervor. "If His Royal Highness the Prince had not retreated at Derby, your King and mine would now sit upon the throne as King James the Third!"

"Who instilled such Tory sentiments in you, Mr. Warrington?" the Colonel asked.

"Nay, sir, the Esmonds have always been loyal!" Harry answered earnestly. "Had we lived at home, twenty years prior, my brother and I would have wagered our heads on the King's side."

"Fortunate for you, Mr. Warrington," the Colonel said gravely. "Your royalist opinions remain safely upon your shoulders rather than impaled on a pole at Temple Bar. I have seen such sights, and they hold no charm."

"I shall doff my hat and salute them whenever I pass the gate," Harry declared, "if the King and the Court stand there!"

"I suspect your relations, Lord Castlewood and the Baroness of Bernstein, are less steadfast supporters of the Crown abroad," the Colonel mused. "Though the Baroness may have harbored old partialities, she has repented. She has rallied to our side, secured positions for her nephews in the Household, and seeks suitable matches for her nieces. If you harbor Tory views, Mr. Warrington, take an old soldier's counsel: keep them to yourself."

"Why, sir, you cannot betray me!" Harry protested.

"Not I, but others might. You spoke thus freely at Castlewood, did you not?"

"I was safe amongst my kin, surely!"

"Doubtless. Yet even kin can play slippery tricks. Lacqueys possess ears as

Mrs. Lambert's fleeting reverie evaporated as quickly as it had formed. Both Miss Theo and her mother were forced to admit, in the privacy of their hearts, that their idealized hero was merely a fallible human being. They exchanged few words on the matter, yet they understood each other's thoughts with the intuitive bond of deep affection. With an extra tenderness and a specially caressing demeanor toward her daughter, the mother sought to soothe her disappointment. "Never mind, my dear," she whispered, pressing a maternal kiss to her filial cheek. "Our hero has proven to be but an ordinary mortal, and surely no such common man is worthy of my Theo. Thou shalt find a true husband ere long, if fortune permits in England. Why, I was scarce fifteen when your father first beheld me at the Bury Assembly, yet while I was still at school, I vowed I would accept no other suitor. If Heaven grants me such a husband—the finest man in the entire kingdom—surely it will bless my child equally, for she deserves a king if she dares to imagine one!" Indeed, it is possible that Mrs. Lambert, who undoubtedly knew the Prince of Wales's age and recognized his handsome, virtuous nature, harbored a secret fantasy: that he too would ride past her gate, tumble from his steed, be ushered into the house, be cured, and thereby earn a regiment for Martin Lambert from his royal grandfather, all while falling hopelessly in love with Theo.

For his part, the Colonel, along with his second daughter, Miss Hetty, sat firmly on the side of laughter, scorn, and disbelief. Mamma was perpetually engaged in matchmaking. Mrs. Lambert was notably addicted to novels, often reducing her eyes to tears with great diligence over their contents. Not a single coach passed the gate without her expecting a husband to alight, ring the bell, and commence courtship. As for Miss Hetty, her tongue wagged with a sauciness rarely seen; she peppered their conversations with a hundred sly allusions to their unexpected guest. She introduced Prussia and Persia into their discourse with abominable pertness and frequency. She inquired whether the current King of Prussia was known as the Shaw or the Sophy, calculated the distance from Ispahan to Saxony—which His Majesty was supposedly invading—and questioned why Papa was so preoccupied with maps and newspapers regarding such wars? She retrieved the *Persian Tales* from her mother's closet and laid them slyly upon the parlor table where the family sat. "I would not marry a Persian prince for myself," she declared, preferring a gentleman who possessed only one wife at a time. She dubbed our young Virginian, Theo's companion, "Theo's prince." She then asked her mother if she, Hetty, wished to claim the other visitor, the "black prince," for herself. Thus, she relentlessly rallied her sister and mother against their sentimentalities, refusing to cease until she had provoked them into anger; only then would she burst into tears, kiss them violently one after another, and coax them back into good humor.

Simple Harry Warrington, meanwhile, remained oblivious to the jokes, tears, quarrels, reconciliations, and matrimonial schemes for which he served as the innocent catalyst. A hundred allusions to Prussians and Persians were hurled at him, yet those Parthian arrows failed to penetrate his skin at all. Was he a Shaw? A Sophy? He likely believed a Sophy to be a lady and would have deemed it the height of absurdity that a man with a magnificent black beard should bear such a name. We stumble into the midst of a quiet family, dropping like a stone into a still pool; we are perfectly compact and cool, unaware of the flutter and excitement we generate, disturbing the fish, frightening the ducks, and agitating the entire surface of the water. How could Harry know the profound effect his sudden appearance produced in this little, quiet, sentimental circle? He thought quite well of himself on many points, yet he was diffident regarding women, being of that tender age when young gentlemen require encouragement and advancement, having been raised in very modest and primitive relations with the opposite sex. Consequently, Miss Hetty's jokes circled around the lad like summer gnats, which he minded no more. It was not that he was stupid, as she certainly assumed; he was simply simple, too engrossed with himself and his own honest affairs to concern himself with others. Why, what tragedies, comedies, interludes, intrigues, farces, and pantomimes unfold beneath our noses in friends' drawing-rooms where we visit daily, leaving us utterly ignorant, self-satisfied, and blind!

As these sisters sat combing their flowing ringlets of night or conversed within the great bed—a custom of the day wherein they lay together—how could Harry know that he held such a significant share in their thoughts, jokes, and conversation? Three days after his arrival, his new and hospitable friends walked with him through Lord Wrotham's magnificent park, where they were free to roam. Upon reaching a piece of water, they encountered some swans, which the young ladies were accustomed to feeding with bread. As the birds approached the young women, Hetty cast a queer look at her mother and sister, followed by a glance at her father, who stood nearby, honest and happy in a red waistcoat. Then Hetty spoke: "Mamma's swans are something like these, papa."

"What swans, my dear?" asked mamma.

"Something like, but not quite," continued Miss Hetty. "They have shorter necks than these, and there are scores of them on our common. I saw Betty plucking one in the kitchen this morning. We shall have it for dinner, with apple-sauce and—"

"Don't be a little goose!" chided Miss Theo.

"And sage and onions. Do you love swan, Mr. Warrington?"

"I shot three last winter on our river," replied the Virginian gentleman. "Ours are not such white birds as these—they eat very well, though."

The simple youth had the slightest notion that he himself was an allegory at that precise moment, and that Miss Hetty was narrating a fable concerning him. In some exceedingly obscure Latin work I have read, it is noted that long before Virginia was discovered, other peoples were equally dull of comprehension.

Thus, it was a premature sentiment on the part of Miss Theo—that little, tender flutter of the bosom we have acknowledged she felt upon first beholding the Virginian, so handsome, pale, and bleeding. This was not the great passion which she knew her heart was capable of feeling. Like the birds, it had awakened and begun to sing at a false dawn. "Hop back to thy perch, and cover thy head with thy wing, thou tremulous little fluttering creature!" For it is not yet light, and roosting is preferable to singing. Soon, however, morning will arrive, the whole sky will redden, and thou shalt soar up into it to salute the sun with thy music.

One little phrase, some thirty-three lines back, perhaps the fair and suspicious reader noticed: "Three days after his arrival, Harry was walking with," etc. If he could walk—as it appeared he did perfectly well—what business had he to be walking with anyone but Lady Maria Esmond on the Pantiles at Tunbridge Wells? His shoulder was set, his health entirely restored; he lacked even a change of coats, owing to the Colonel's generosity, and was obliged to wear borrowed raiment. Surely a young man in such a condition had no right to linger at Oakhurst and was bound by every tie of duty, convenience, love, and relationship, alongside a gentle heart waiting for him and a washerwoman's final entreaty, to depart for Tunbridge. Why did he remain behind, unless he was in love with either of the young ladies (and we assert he was not)? Could it be that he did not wish to go? Hath the gracious reader understood the meaning of the mystic 'S' with which the last chapter commenced, in which the designer feebly endeavored to depict the notorious Sinbad the Sailor, surmounted by that odious old man of the sea? What if Harry Warrington should be that sailor, and his fate that choking, deadening, inevitable old man? What if, for the past two days, he has felt those knees throttling him round the neck? If his fell aunt's purpose is answered, and if his late love is killed as dead by her poisonous communications as fair Rosamond was by her royal and legitimate rival? Is Hero then lighting the lamp and preparing supper, whilst Leander sits comfortably with some other party, never thinking of taking to the water? Ever since that coward's blow was struck in Lady Maria's back by her own relative, surely kind hearts must pity her ladyship. I know she has faults—yes, and wears false hair and false jewels—but a woman in distress, shall we not pity her? A lady of a certain age, are we to laugh at her because of her years? Between her old aunt and her unhappy delusion, be sure my Lady Maria Esmond is having no very pleasant time at Tunbridge Wells. There is no one to protect her. Madam Beatrix has her all to herself. Lady Maria is poor, hoping for money from her aunt. Lady Maria possesses secrets which the old woman knows and brandishes over her. I, for one, am quite melted and grown soft-hearted as I think of her. Imagine her alone, a victim to that old woman! Paint to yourself that antique Andromeda (if you please, we will allow that rich, flowing head of hair to fall over her shoulders

Mrs. Lambert stood by the roadside, her lily-white handkerchief pressed to her lips, watching the travelers pace down the path. Her daughters lingered beside her, their eyes fixed on the retreating figures until a cluster of lime trees obscured them from view. For this good matron, those green trunks served as a veil between the domestic sphere and the wider world. Through them, she had long since bid farewell to husbands marching to war, sons bound for school, and brothers venturing into unknown lands, trusting that Heaven's will would guide their return with joy and love. The quiet rhythm of home life—filled with the empty spaces where beloved ones had slept, the lingering scent of a boy's carpet bag, and the ghostly echo of his voice at evening prayer—had taught her patience and deepened her affection. Now, separated by the rustling leaves, she fell silent, a thoughtful figure amidst the garden. Beside her, Miss Theo, gifted with a poetic spirit, composed verses for the trees, which her delighted mother later sent to a periodical, while the Colonel, embarking on his journey, offered a final salute.

"I know my wife watches from her window until we round this bend," Colonel Lambert declared, tipping his hat to the departing gentlemen. "I trust, Mr. Warrington, you will find pleasure in visiting us again, especially with the boys returned."

"And I," replied Mr. Warrington, "shall count it a supreme happiness to leave friends so dear."

"The host for tonight has a son who is likewise an old friend of ours," the Colonel continued, his tone lightening. "My wife, a tireless matchmaker, would have paired him with one of my daughters, but fate intervened. Colonel James Wolfe chose another."

"Ah!" sighed Mr. Warrington.

"There were valiant souls before Agamemnon," the Colonel remarked with a twinkle in his eye. "Though I suspect you meant to ask if his name was Aga..."

"No, sir, James Wolfe," corrected the Colonel, laughing gently. "He is scarcely thirty, yet holds the rank of Lieutenant-Colonel, surpassing nearly all the nobility who hold commission before us. I have witnessed him leading captains twice his senior, men of greater age and experience, yet lacking his brilliance and fortune. Though enviable, his superiority is unresented; he is beloved by every soul in our old regiment and a scholar as profound as he is a soldier."

Harry Warrington bowed his head, a mixture of shame and humility coloring his features. "Sir, I confess I have squandered my youth. I return to England an ignoramus. If my brother George had lived, he would have upheld our name far better than I. He was a scholar, a musician, conversant with the wisest minds of our realm. In comparison, I feel but a hollow shell."

"If you recognize your ignorance, you have gained wisdom already," the Colonel smiled. "Do not despair. Here, the gentry may know little beyond hounds and horses, yet I assure you, a gentleman need not be a scholar to be worthy. Look around: His Royal Highness commands, Martin Lambert leads as Colonel, and Jack Hunt, who follows behind, was once a private soldier and now stands as a loyal groom. Does it matter if one wears epaulets or a currycomb? I once dreamed of glory and honor, but now I cherish duty, the quiet companionship of family, and the simple joys of home. Come, let us hasten; night falls quickly."

Their journey led them to Westerham, where they were greeted by a stately household comprising a veteran soldier, his wife, and their son—the aforementioned Lieutenant-Colonel James Wolfe. Though physically unattractive, with a gaunt frame, pale complexion, and sharp features, Wolfe possessed a courtesy so radiant and a conversational warmth so engaging that listeners forgot his appearance and marveled at his spirit.

Supper ensued, marked by lively jests. The host's wife, overhearing whispers between her son-in-law and the young Colonel, teased them about an impending alliance with a lady of the illustrious Lowther family.

"If she were the daughter of Charlemagne," Lambert quipped, "she would still be beneath James Wolfe."

"Nay, Mother," Wolfe protested, "if she knew her stature, she would hesitate."

"She is a priceless gem," the mother insisted, "while you are... well, you are just James."

The old Colonel chuckled. "We tend our children through fever and plague, sending them to distant wars with heavy hearts, only to welcome them back for fleeting weeks. Yet, in the end, a stranger arrives with bright eyes, and our boys, who cared not for us, depart forever."

"How did you meet James's father?" the younger Colonel asked curiously.

"His father was gout-ridden and required care," the lady explained simply, "more than I cared for him personally."

As dawn broke, Colonel Lambert released Harry Warrington into the custody of Mr. Wolfe, turning his steed toward home. Wolfe, eager for Tunbridge Wells, spurred forward, while Harry, his heart heavy with longing, trailed slightly behind. Despite his delay, Harry spoke highly of the Lamberts, describing them as paragons of virtue. Wolfe, however, seemed preoccupied with a vision of domestic tranquility.

"Glorious deeds and honors mean little now," Wolfe mused. "My dreams have shifted. Having served since adolescence, traversed garrison towns, and fulfilled nearly every duty of arms, I now seek peace, books, a loving wife, and children to dandle upon my knee. True love outweighs glory; a quiet fireside with the woman of one's heart is the highest gift the gods can offer."

Harry nodded, though his countenance betrayed a lack of genuine enthusiasm.

"You agree so readily," Wolfe observed, noting Harry's somber expression, "yet a fireside and a sweetheart seem to hold little appeal for a young man like yourself."

"Why, Colonel," Harry replied, "one cannot deny the allure of adventure. Having known but a fraction of life, I dream of campaigns, distinction, and fame. Only after witnessing the world's breadth might I consider retirement."

Wolfe, his mind elsewhere, launched into a passionate eulogy of marital bliss, recounting the virtues of his mistress with evident fervor—a subject that stirred him deeply, though it failed to resonate fully with Harry, whose outlook on marriage remained tinged with melancholy.

Their ride concluded uneventfully near Tunbridge Wells, interrupted only by a misunderstanding involving two mounted men who appeared to be highwaymen. Wolfe, amused, reassured Harry that the riders were merely agents for local inns. Gumbo, Harry's mount, bolted in a panic, requiring considerable effort to rein in before the pair reached their destination and separated to seek out their respective acquaintances.

In Tunbridge, Harry discovered his Aunt installed in grand lodgings, attended by a retinue of lacquers. Upon his arrival, she received him with boundless kindness. However, the atmosphere shifted dramatically with the entrance of a fashionable beau and two dowagers adorned in voluminous hoops and heavy rouge. Introducing Harry as her nephew, the "Young Croesus of Virginia," the Baroness launched into a torrent of exaggerations. She described his estate as larger than Kent itself, teeming with gold mines, spices, and diamond fields, and claimed his mother ruled a vast dominion akin to a kingdom. She spoke of his immense wealth and noble bearing, delighting the visitors with tales of his generosity and virtue.

"You could not have arrived at a finer moment, my dear," the Baroness confided to Harry once the guests departed. "These three have the sharpest tongues in the town. They will trumpet your virtues across every circle. I have introduced you to hundreds and spun a web of lies about Virginia's geography to elevate your status. I fear I have overstated the size of your estate, filling it with wonders that may not exist, and portrayed your mother as a sovereign princess. Yet, the world believes, and belief is power. A London newspaper has already heralded your arrival, and tomorrow, the entire congregation will turn from their prayers to gaze upon you as a golden idol."

"But how does this reputation benefit me?" Harry asked, bewildered.

"To enter the world, the key must be gold, real or imagined," she advised wisely. "To be thought rich is nearly as potent as being rich. Mothers will smile, daughters will curtsy, and doors will swing wide. Do not let vanity cloud your judgment. Remember, I saved you from the clutches of Lord Castlewood, a gambler who ruined himself and nearly destroyed my niece. Gaming is the madness of our family; I, too, am not entirely cured. Beware the card tables, my dear nephew. The world is cruel, and it thrives on appearances."

Harry listened, his cheeks flushing as he reflected on the stark contrast between the virtuous simplicity of Oakhurst and the gilded, deceptive opulence of Tunbridge. The old lady's words about gambling and her own "cures" struck a discordant chord, reminding him of the moral decay he had witnessed among his relatives. Yet, as his aunt patted his cheek, whispering of her protective instincts, Harry felt a pang of guilt. He could not bear to see the kind villagers of Oakhurst spoken of lightly by these worldly figures, nor could he reconcile the gap between the truth of his humble beginnings and the fabricated grandeur his aunt sold to the town. With a sense of unease, he realized that the world outside awaited him with a mask of falsehood, a mask he would soon be forced to wear.

Cousin Maria appeared, flanked by two gardener's boys carrying baskets brimming with fresh blooms. Their mission was clear: to transform Madame de Bernstein's drawing-room into a fitting stage for the arrival of her ladyship's distinguished guests. Behind them followed three footmen in liveries gorgeously laced with worsted, setting out twice as many card tables as needed. A major-domo, dressed in black with fine-laced ruffles and a heavy bag, trailed behind the lacqueys. He carried fasces of wax candles, placing a pair upon each card table and lighting the silver sconces that lined the wainscoted walls. As the sun's slanting rays gilded the interior, they also illuminated the prospect beyond—a green common dotted with rocks, tree-clumps, and distant houses twinkling in the sunlight. Outside, groups of figures in hoops, powder, and brocade sauntered across the lawn, dappling the plain with shifting shadows. Across the street lay the Pantiles, where a perpetual fair buzzed with the clamor of company. A band played music for the benefit of the visitors to the Wells.

While Madame Bernstein's chief sitting-room might not suit a recluse or a scholar, it was perfect for those who relished bustle, gaiety, bright light, and a view of the lively scene unfolding around them. Once the windows were lit, pedestrians walking below knew instantly that her ladyship was hosting a card assembly, accessible to anyone willing to seek an introduction. Speaking of the past, the night-life of society a century prior was undeniably darker. There was scarcely one candle for every ten seen in modern drawing rooms, let alone gaslight or electric illumination. Horrible guttering tallow smoked and stank through the passages; the candle-snuffer was a notorious officer in the theater. One need only look at Hogarth's pictures to see the darkness, the feasts begrimed with dripping fat. In "Marriage à la Mode," within Lord Viscount Squanderfield's grand saloons, where he and his wife yawned before a horror-stricken steward after their party, there were but eight candles—one per card table and half a dozen in a brass chandelier. If Jack Briefless had convoked friends to oysters and beer in his chambers at Pump Court, he would surely have employed double the number. Yet, we may comfort ourselves by recalling that Louis XIV held his revels in shadow, and blessing the likes of Mr. Price and other luminous benefactors who banished the abominable gloom of our ancestors' youth.

Maria, herself the fairest flower among them, arranged roses, sweet-williams, and other blossoms into vases scattered throughout the apartment, adorning the space with the best of her art. She lingered fondly over this bowl and that dragon-jar, casting only sly, timid glances at young cousin Harry. Her own blush would have become any young woman, and one might have surmised she intended to outstay her aunt indefinitely. However, the Baroness, seated in her armchair with a crooked tortoiseshell stick in hand, pointed imperiously to the servants, rating them soundly. She scolded Tom for a darn in his stocking and John for having greased his locks too profusely from the candle-box, maintaining a stern domination over the household. Another remark concerns the plight of James a hundred years ago: he slept two to a bed, four to a room—likely a cellar—and washed in a trough rarely seen outside the barracks of Her Majesty's Foot Guards today.

If Maria hoped for a present interview, her fond heart was disappointed. "Where are you going to dine, Harry?" asked Madame de Bernstein. "My niece Maria and I shall have a chicken in the little parlour—I think you should go to the best ordinary. There is one at the White Horse at three; we shall hear his bell in a minute or two. And you must understand, sir, that you ought not to spare expense, but behave like Princess Pocahontas's son. Your trunks have been taken over to the lodging I have engaged for you. It is not good for a lad to be always hanging about the aprons of two old women, is it, Maria?"

"No," said her ladyship, dropping her meek eyes, whilst the other lady glared in triumph. I think Andromeda had been exposed to the Dragon significantly during the last five or six days; if Perseus had cut off the latter's cruel head, he would have committed no unjustifiable monsticide. But he bore no sword or shield; he merely looked mechanically at the lacqueys in tawny and blue as they creaked about the room.

"And there are good mercers and tailors from London always here to wait on the company at the Wells. You had better see them, my dear, for your suit is not of the very last fashion—a little lace..."

"I can't go out of mourning, ma'am," said the young man, looking down at his sables.

"Ho, sir!" cried the lady, rustling up from her chair and rising on her cane. "Wear black for your brother till you are as old as Methuselah, if you like. I am sure I don't want to prevent you. I only want you to dress, and to do like other people, and make a figure worthy of your name."

"Madam," said Mr. Warrington with great state, "I have not done anything to disgrace it that I know."

Why did the old woman stop and give a little start as if struck? Let bygones be bygones. She and the boy shared a score of little passages where swords were crossed and thrusts rapidly dealt or parried. She liked Harry none the worse for his courage in facing her. "Sure, a little finer linen than that shirt you wear will not be a disgrace to you, sir," she said with a rather forced laugh.

Harry bowed and blushed. It was one of the homely gifts of his Oakhurst friends. He felt pleased somehow to think he wore it, thought of the new friends—who were so good, so pure, so simple, so kindly—with immense tenderness. While invested in this garment, he felt as if evil could not touch him. He said he would go to his lodging, making a point of returning arrayed in the best linen he possessed.

"Come back here, sir," said Madame Bernstein, "and if our company has not arrived, Maria and I will find some ruffles for you!" And under a footman's guidance, the young fellow walked off to his new lodgings.

Harry found not only handsome and spacious apartments but a groom in attendance waiting to be engaged by his honor, and a second valet, should he wish to hire one to wait upon Mr. Gumbo. Ere many minutes had passed in his rooms, emissaries from a London tailor and bootmaker awaited him with cards and compliments from their employers, Messrs. Regnier and Tull. The best articles in his modest wardrobe were laid out by Gumbo alongside the finest linen his thrifty Virginian mother had provided. Visions of the snow-surronded home in his own country, of crackling logs and trim, quiet ladies working by the fire, rose before him. For the first time, a small thought crossed his mind that the homely clothes were not quite smart enough, that the home-worked linen lacked the finesse it might possess. That he should feel ashamed of anything belonging to him or to Castlewood! That was strange. The simple folk there were only too well satisfied with all things done, said, or produced at Castlewood; and Madam Esmond, when sending her son forth on his travels, thought no young nobleman needed better provision. The clothes might have fitted better and been of a later fashion, to be sure—but still, the young fellow presented a comely figure when he issued from his apartments, his toilet complete. Gumbo called a chair, marched beside it, until they reached the ordinary where the young gentleman was to dine.

Here, he expected to find the beau whose acquaintance he had made a few hours before at his aunt's lodging, who had indicated that the White Horse was the most modish place for dining at the Wells. He mentioned his friend's name to the host, but the landlord and waiters, leading him into the room with many smiles and bows, assured his honor that he needed no other introduction than his own. They helped him hang up his coat and sword on a peg, asked whether he would drink Burgundy, Pontac, or champagne to his dinner, and led him to a table.

Though the most fashionable ordinary in the village, the White Horse did not happen to be crowded on this day. Monsieur Barbeau, the landlord, informed Harry that a great entertainment at Summer Hill had taken away most of the company. Indeed, when Harry entered the room, there were but four other gentlemen present. Two were drinking wine and had finished their dinner; the other two were young men in the midst of their meal. As the landlord passed, he must have whispered the newcomer's name to them, for they looked at him with some appearance of interest and made a slight bow across the table as the smiling host bustled away for Harry's dinner.

Mr. Warrington returned the salute of the two gentlemen, who bade him welcome to Tunbridge and expressed hope that he would like the place upon better acquaintance. Then they smiled and exchanged waggish looks with each other, of which Harry did not understand the meaning, nor why they cast knowing glances at the two other guests over their wine.

One of these persons wore a somewhat tarnished velvet coat with a huge queue and bag

Before the windows of the tavern's eating-room lay a bowling-green, dotted with tables where guests sipped punch or tea. Having concluded their meal simultaneously, Mr. Morris proposed they adjourn to the green to drink a cool bottle. "Jack Morris would adjourn to the Dust Hole as a pretext for a fresh drink," remarked my lord. To this, Jack replied that surely each gentleman possessed his own favored method of proceeding to the deuce, admitting openly that his weakness was a bottle.

"My Lord Chesterfield's deuce is deuce-ace," observed my Lord March. "His lordship cannot keep away from cards or dice."

"My Lord March possesses not one devil, but several," countered Jack. "He loves gambling, horse-racing, betting, drinking, eating, money, and women. You have fallen into bad company, Mr. Warrington, by lighting upon his lordship. He will play you for every acre you hold in Virginia."

"With the greatest pleasure in life!" interjected my lord eagerly. "And for all your tobacco, spices, slaves, oxen, and asses, and everything else that is yours."

"Shall we begin now?" Jack asked, noting that Mr. Warrington was never without a dice-box or bottle-screw. "I will set Mr. Warrington for whatever he pleases."

"Alas, my lord," Harry protested, "the tobacco, slaves, asses, and oxen are not yet mine. I am just of age, and my mother, scarcely twenty years older, has as good a chance of long life as I."

"I will bet you that you survive her," my lord offered. "I will pay you a sum now against four times the sum payable at her death. I will set a fair stake over this table against the reversion of your estate in Virginia upon her departure. What do you call your place?"

"Castlewood."

"A principality, I hear," my lord mused. "I will bet its value has been exaggerated tenfold by the gossip-mongers here. How came you by the name of Castlewood? Are you related to my lord? Ah, I know—my lady, your mother, descends from the true head of the house. He took the losing side in '15. I have heard this story a dozen times from my old Duchess. She knew your grandfather; he was a friend of Addison, Steele, Pope, and Milton, I dare say, and the great figures of his time. It is a pity he did not stay home and transplant the other branch of the family to the plantations."

"I have just been staying at Castlewood with my cousin there," Harry noted.

"Hm! Did you play with him? He is fond of pasteboard and bones."

"Never, save for sixpences and a pool of commerce with the ladies."

"So much the better for both parties. But did you play with Will Esmond if he was at home? I will lay ten to one you did."

Harry blushed and admitted that evenings past, he and his cousin had played a few hands at cards.

"And Tom Sampson, the chaplain?" cried Jack Morris. "I wager Tom made a third, and may the Lord deliver you from Tom and Will Esmond together!"

"Nay, the truth is, I won of both of them," Harry declared proudly.

"And they paid you? Well, miracles will never cease!"

"I did not speak of miracles," Harry smiled over his wine.

"And you do not tell tales out of school—the *volto sciolto*—hey, Mr. Warrington?" asked my lord.

"I beg your pardon," said straightforward Harry. "French is the only language besides my own of which I know aught."

"My Lord March has learned Italian at the Opera, and a pretty penny his lessons have cost," Jack Morris remarked. "We must show him the Opera—must we not, March?"

"Must we, Morris?" my lord asked, seeming to accept the invitation only half-heartedly.

Both gentlemen wore identical attire: small scratch-wigs unpowdered, blue frocks with plate buttons, buckskins, riding-boots, and little hats adorned with narrow cords of lace, devoid of fashionable markers.

"I care little for the Opera, my lord," Harry said, warming to his Burgundy, "but I should like to go to Newmarket and behold a good English hunting-field."

"We will show you Newmarket and the hunt, sir. Can you ride well?"

"I believe I can," Harry answered. "I can shoot fairly well and jump some."

"What is your weight? I bet we weigh even, or I weigh most. I bet Jack Morris beats you at bird or mark, at twenty-five paces. I bet I leap farther than you on level ground, right here on this green."

"I know not Mr. Morris's shooting—I have never seen either gentleman before—but I take your bets, my lord, at whatever you please," cried Harry, now thoroughly heated by the wine.

"Ponies on each!" shouted my lord.

"Done and done!" echoed my lord and Harry in unison. The young man felt it was for the honor of his country not to be ashamed of any wager made.

"Now we may try the last bet, if your feet are steady," my lord said, rising, stretching his limbs, and surveying the crisp, dry grass. He removed his boots, followed by his coat and waistcoat, buckling his belt tight around his waist before casting his garments onto the ground.

Harry held greater respect for his garments, which were his finest suit. Removing the velvet coat and waistcoat, he folded them carefully. Seeing that the nearby tables were sloshed with drink, he carried them to a table within the eating-room, whose windows stood open.

Then a new guest appeared: none other than Mr. Wolfe, soberly consuming a chicken and salad with a modest pint of wine. Harry, in high spirits, told the Colonel he had a wager with my Lord March and asked if the Colonel would stand him halves. The Colonel, being too poor to bet, nonetheless agreed to witness fair play. He set down his glass and stalked through the open window after his young friend.

"Who is that tallow-faced Put with carroty hair?" Jack Morris asked, the effects of Burgundy fully manifest.

Mr. Warrington explained this was Lieutenant-Colonel Wolfe of the 20th Regiment.

"Your humble servant, gentlemen!" the Colonel bowed rigidly.

"Never saw such a figure in my life!" cried Jack Morris. "Did you, March?"

"I beg your pardon, I think you said March?" the Colonel looked surprised.

"I am the Earl of March, sir, at Colonel Wolfe's service," the nobleman bowed. "My friend, Mr. Morris, is so intimate with me that, after dinner, we are quite like brothers."

"Why is not all Tunbridge Wells present to hear this?" thought Morris. Delighted, he shouted, "Two to one on my lord!"

"Done!" called Mr. Warrington, forcing the enthusiastic Jack to cry "Done!" as well.

"Take him, Colonel," Harry whispered.

"But the Colonel said he could not afford to lose, and therefore could not hope to win," Harry noted later.

"I see you have won one of our bets already, Mr. Warrington," my Lord March observed. "I am taller than you by an inch or two, but you are broader round the shoulders."

"Pooh, my dear Will! I bet you you weigh twice as much as he does!" cried Jack Morris.

"Done, Jack!" said my lord, laughing. "The bets are all ponies. Will you take him, Mr. Warrington?"

"No, my dear fellow—one's enough," said Jack.

"Very good, my dear fellow," said my lord. "Now we will settle the other wager."

Already arrayed in his best silk stockings, black satin-net breeches, and neatest pumps, Harry refused to remove his shoes, as his antagonist's heavy riding-boots and spurs were ill-suited for leaping. Before them stretched a fine, even green turf of about thirty yards, sufficient for a run and a jump. A gravel walk circled this space, beyond which stood a wall and gate sign—a field azure bearing the Hanoverian White Horse rampant between two skittles proper, with the motto naming the landlord and the beast.

My lord's friend placed a handkerchief on the ground as the mark from which the leapers were to start, while Mr. Wolfe stood at the far end of the grass plat to measure where each landed.

"My lord went first," Mr. Warrington wrote in a letter to his mother, Mrs. Mountain, at Castlewood, Virginia, which remains extant. "He wished for me to take the lead; but, recalling the story of the Battle of Fontenoy which my dearest George used to tell, I said, 'Monseigneur le Comte, tirez le premier, s'il vous plaît.' Thus he took his run in stocking-feet, and for the honor of Old Virginia, I had the gratification of beating his lordship by more than two feet—namely, two feet nine inches. I jumped twenty-one feet three inches, by the drawer's measured tape, whereas his lordship leaped only eighteen-six. I had won from him about my weight before (which I knew the moment I set my eye upon him). So he and Mr. Jack paid me these two bets. And with my best duty to

"Tell me about those children, Harry—the ones I saw darting about the house where they sheltered you after your terrible fall?" Maria asked, pacing alongside him along the common. "Oh, that fall! I felt as though I'd perish seeing it. Please, stop squeezing my arm so tightly. It seems you've forgotten I exist, haven't you?"

"The people are the kindest souls I've ever encountered," Mr. Warrington exclaimed, his voice brimming with emotion. "Mrs. Lambert was a childhood friend of my mother's, and Colonel Lambert is a man of immense distinction, a veteran of Scotland, Flanders, and Minorca. To call them natural parents feels an understatement; their tenderness knows no bounds. How do I repay such grace? I simply must gift them something." Harry shoved his hand deep into his pocket, grasping the crisp, heavy folds of cash earned from his recent gambling sprees with Morris and March.

"We could visit the toy shop, my dear, and purchase dolls for the children," Lady Maria suggested gently. "Offering coin for their charity would surely insult their generosity."

"Dolls for Hester and Theo!" Harry scoffed, a flicker of irritation crossing his features. "Do you truly believe a woman ceases to be a woman at forty? Miss Hester is hardly a child, and Miss Theo is years older. They speak multiple languages fluently. Their libraries are vast, filled with volumes upon volumes. They master the harpsichord and sing with exquisite harmony, with Theo composing her own melodies."

"I admit, I barely noticed them. I assumed they were mere children. I had no notion they possessed such refinement or were anything less than ordinary."

"That is precisely why women are so tedious!" Harry declared, laughing. "Back home, if George or I praise a woman, Mrs. Esmond or Mountain will immediately find a flaw."

"I would never fault anyone who treats you with such kindness, Mr. Warrington," Maria sighed, her tone softening. "Though I envy them deeply—for they cared for you when you were broken and ill, while I..." She trailed off, looking away. "...while I had to abandon you."

"You dear, good Maria!" he said, reaching out to comfort her.

"No, Harry! Stop it. You need not be so persistent. Look!" She pointed toward the street. "There goes your black man, striding among a dozen others in liveried servitude. These wretched creatures are heading to the tea-garden to become as drunk as their masters. That dreadful Mr. Morris was already staggering when I left you, frightening you half to death."

"I had just secured massive wagers against both of them," Harry announced, his mood instantly lifting. "What shall I buy you, my dear cousin?" He recounted his recent triumphs, his laughter ringing out, a hint of boastfulness coloring his speech. "For the honor of Virginia, I resolved to demonstrate what true leaping entails. With a bit of practice, I imagine I could clear two feet further."

Maria, delighted by her young champion's success, leaned in. "But beware, child. Be cautious about play. It has ruined our entire family line. My brother Castlewood, Will, our poor father, even Lady Castlewood herself—all fell victim to it. And as for my Lord March, he is the most voracious gambler and the most successful player of all the nobility."

"I have no intention of fearing him, nor his companion, Jack Morris," Harry replied, thumbing the gold in his palm. "What games do you enjoy at Aunt Bernstein's? Cribbage, all-fours, brag, whist, commerce, piquet, quadrille? I am proficient at any of them. Does that clock strike seven?"

"And you wish to commence immediately?" Maria's voice held a note of gentle reproach. "You seem to have forgotten your poor cousin. You used to enjoy strolling with me not long ago."

"Hah! Youth *is* youth, cousin!" Harry tossed his head back, strutting confidently beside her. "A young man must have his fling!" He moved with an air of absolute certainty, buoyant and eager.
