Just recently, he had relished walking with her; the day prior, he had preferred the company of Theo, Hester, and Mrs. Lambert. Yet, the allure of pleasure, life, gaiety, the burning desire to shine and conquer, exerted a powerful pull on the lad. Like so many boys, he seized the cup without a thought for the migraine awaiting him at dawn.

While they conversed, the violins from the open orchestra on the Parade began a raucous tuning, a series of screeches preparing for the evening's concert. Maria sensed her aunt had awakened again; she knew she must return to her duties. Harry never inquired about this "slavery," though he likely understood its nature if he had paused to reflect. He offered no pity for his cousin. His mind was entirely elsewhere. Yet, when disaster had struck him, she had suffered a wound far deeper and more cruel. She had scarcely lost sight of him, a fact she buried beneath layers of smiling hypocrisy, a necessary armor for a woman of her station. *I know,* thought the narrator, addressing the reader, *that you consider her an old fool?* Indeed, do you suppose fools' caps fail to conceal gray hair as effectively as jet or auburn? Treat our elderly *fredaines* gently, O Minerva of a woman! Perhaps you are so wise you avoid novels entirely. But observe this: late crops of wild oats grow as readily as early harvests, and from personal experience, I posit that *avena fatua* reaches maturity right up until the final days of the year.

Much like worldly parents eager to shed a troublesome child to attend their own social engagements, Madame Bernstein and her staff had prematurely laid the sun to sleep, drawing the curtains shut while daylight lingered. They busied themselves with cards, candles, tea, and negus. Ladies arrived one by one, seated in chairs that landed them at the Baroness's door, adorned in paint, patches, and brocade. Gentlemen followed in gala attire. Mr. Poellnitz boasted the largest star and the most embroidered coat of the assembly.

Lord March and Ruglen, however, were transformed. Gone was the disheveled figure Harry had met at the White Horse; now he wore a neatly curled feather top, powdered hair, and a sumptuous French suit, replacing his jockey's garb. Jack Morris mirrored this transformation with a similar wig and a suit of fine stuff that closely resembled his lordship's. Mr. Wolfe attended his beautiful mistress, Miss Lowther, alongside her aunt, a card-loving matron beloved by all.

When Lady Maria Esmond entered, her appearance defied Madame Bernstein's previous descriptions. Her form was striking, her dress revealing much of her figure. Nature had gifted her with an exceeding fair complexion, accentuated by a dark, frilled ribbon jeweled around her neck, enhancing its snow-like whiteness. Her cheeks lacked the flush common to the other ladies, whose roses were openly purchased from perfumeries. Artful patches, it was whispered, augmented her charm. Her hoops were modest, no larger than the iron contrivances fashionable today; while absurd in some aspects, the costume was, in general, pleasing.

As for the Baroness de Bernstein, despite her grand toilette, she appeared magnificent yet melancholy, bordering on terrifying. One reads the past in her aged visage. The fire had gone out of her eyes, replaced by crow's-feet that puckered around them. They flashed no longer with scorn, anger, or love; they merely gazed, unmelting, undazzling. To the young reader who finds herself outnumbered at the ball by peers like the peerless Lindamira, console yourself: at fifty, you will look as kind and pleasant as she appears now at eighteen. You need not relinquish your carriage of beauty nor endure the depreciation of your smiles. You will not see fashion abandon your quarter, leaving your saloons dusty and your windows plastered with "to Let" signs. You may lack grandeur, but you will avoid bankruptcy.

"Our hostess," Lord Chesterfield whispered confidentially to a friend, unaware of the volume of his words, "reminds me of Covent Garden in my youth. Then it was the court end of town, inhabited by high fashion. Now, a nobleman's house is nothing more than a gaming-house, or you may enter with a friend to order a bottle."

"A bottle and a tavern are virtues in their own right," Lord March shrugged. "I was not born before the Georges ascended, yet I intend to live to a hundred. I knew the Bernstein only as an old woman; if she ever possessed beauty, hang me if I know how she squandered it."

"How did she spend it?" Jack Morris laughed.

"Here is a table! Sit down and play. Do not let the Frenchman join; he will not pay. Mr. Warrington, will you take a card?" Mr. Warrington and Lord Chesterfield paired against Morris and the Earl of March. "You have arrived too late, Baron," the elder nobleman remarked to the approaching guest. "We have set our game. Have you forgotten Mr. Warrington of Virginia, the young gentleman you met in London?"

"The youth I encountered at Arthur's Chocolate House had black hair, a slightly crooked nose, and was by no means so fortunate in his looks as Mr. Warrington," the Baron replied, displaying considerable presence of mind. "Warrington, Dorrington

For three days following his arrival at Tunbridge Wells, the young Virginian emerged as the undisputed center of attention in that bustling watering-place. Though no local nobleman commanded quite the same measure of curiosity, the Bishop of Salisbury himself received scantier reverence than Harry Warrington. As the youth traversed the streets, eyes swiveled to track his passage, and country folk paused in their market duties to stare with open admiration. Inside the assembly rooms, matrons warmly urged him to join them, ingeniously arranging circumstances to leave him in private moments with their daughters, nearly all of whom returned his smile.

Every soul within the town seemed to possess an intimate knowledge of Harry's estate, down to the precise acreage and shilling-for-shilling calculation of his income. At every tea table, his gambling winnings were dissected and recalculated with feverish precision. "Wonderful indeed," the narrator observed, "is the knowledge neighbors hold regarding our affairs!" The curiosity surrounding Harry was so profound that even his servant, Gumbo, became a subject of inquiry. Villagers approached the boy with ale and cold meats, seeking news of their master. Gumbo, plumpened by this sudden diet, ascended to the ranks of a leading valet in the district, lying with a gusto that bordered on the legendary.

No social gathering felt complete without the presence of Mr. Warrington. The lad, though amused and slightly astonished by this rapid elevation, carried himself with a dignified composure. His upbringing at home had instilled a robust self-regard, and his current fortune only served to validate it. Yet, he did not succumb to vanity. Engaging in games with challengers, he adhered to a peculiar code of honor; he felt compelled to answer every summons, whether for a race, a drinking bout, or a card match, acting as an ambassador for Old Virginia. Consequently, he proved a formidable rival to the locals, possessing a keen mind, a dexterous hand, a steady seat, and nerves of iron. In his spirited competition with the mother country, the representative of Virginia acquitted himself with distinction.

Madame de Bernstein, who had seemingly recovered from her previous losses through nightly gaming, was thoroughly delighted by her nephew's triumphs and reputation. He had bested Jack Morris at shooting, outpaced Mr. Scamper in a riding match, and held his own remarkably well against Captain Batts at tennis, despite having never touched a racket prior to his arrival. Furthermore, he had faced the celebrated gamblers, the Lords of Chesterfield and March, earning praise from both for his coolness, gallantry, and breeding. While his mathematical skills at the books were not dazzling, his handwriting was impeccable, and his naive ignorance charmed the old lady. A lover of literature, humor, and the works of Molière and Fielding, yet preferring the living world to the library, she supervised his attire with maternal delight, sourcing exquisite laces for his ruffles and securing a delicate diamond brooch for his cravat. Soon, he became the prime favorite among all her nephews and kin.

Lady Maria, however, could not help but feel a sting of jealousy at her brother's successes, fearing that her own prospects with their wealthy aunt were eclipsed. Indeed, the other gentlemen must have trembled with envy as they witnessed Mr. Warrington's meteoric rise in the Baroness's favor. For two weeks, Harry became a figure of immense importance, navigating the circles of the "good" company while inevitably stumbling upon the "bad." Was it his fault for accepting the world as he found it, drinking from the sweet, sparkling cup of pleasure that had been filled to the brim for him? Aunt Bernstein, neither a rigorous moralist nor a prudent guide for youth, merely bid him pursue his enjoyments. If letters of affection arrived from the mysterious Cattarina, the Baroness likely would have encouraged acceptance; yet Harry retained a stock of modesty inherited from his colonial home, where libertine vices were unknown. He blushed at the bold talk of his new European associates, finding even his aunt's wit and jokes startling.

Despite his innocence, the world readily credited him with the vices of others. How could he know better than to avoid the saucy Cattarina? Having seen Lord March drive her in his carriage, Harry deemed nothing improper in offering her his arm on the public promenade. When she covetously eyed a trinket at a toy shop, he delighted in presenting it, followed soon after by a piece of lace, and then another fad. However, when her requests began to escalate, the young gentleman gently deflected them with laughter, refusing to purchase diamond drops for her ears. He possessed a shrewdness beneath his generosity and showed no sign of recklessness.

Yet, who would credit his modesty? Old Bernstein persisted in believing her nephew was playing the role of Don Juan, supplanting Lord March. This belief intensified whenever poor Maria was present, delighting the aunt in stinging the spinster's tender heart with tales of her niece's piteous silence and discomfiture. "Boys will be boys," the Baroness declared, insisting Harry should not be the first "milksop" in his family—a sentiment that often left Maria choking on the bread she ate at her aunt's expense.

Meanwhile, Colonel Wolfe, ever devoted to the lady of his love, visited frequently, leaving little room for Harry's company. Yet, the officer's conversation stood in stark contrast to the ribald chatter surrounding Harry. Wolfe spoke of no cards, horse pedigrees, hunting exploits, or female favors; he avoided retailing the era's endless scandals. The age itself was dissolute, marked by a king with open mistresses and a nobility driven mad by pleasure, a looseness of tongue and deed that required careful navigation. Harry, however, remained unaware of how his actions were interpreted. He failed to notice Colonel Wolfe's increasingly cold demeanor or the stories circulating about him. Who crafted these tales? Who believed them? Poor Harry, content with his rides and games, was passing among respectable circles as the most abandoned and profligate youth of the moment.

Tragically, the gentle lad, so simple and easily pleased, was whispered about as a hardened gambler, a spendthrift, and a frequenter of abandoned women. These rumors reached Colonel Lambert at Oakhurst, filling the good man's heart with grief and care. First came one tale, then another, until crowds of malicious whispers convinced the family of a catastrophe. Mrs. Lambert, unable to resist her husband's anxiety, pressed for answers until Lambert confessed the truth. The shock rippled through the household; Theo and Hester, initially defiant, eventually succumbed to the weight of the scandal. "O, the serpent they had nurtured!" lamented the kind-hearted ladies, imagining Harry parading his shame alongside that horrid Frenchwoman, distributing diamonds, and exposing himself to the gaze of the entire town at the Wells. Even the parish priest, preaching against the human propensity to believe the worst of neighbors, found his sermons futile against the tide of gossip.

While Harry remained blissfully unaware of the storm brewing around his name, indulging in an idle and pleasant existence, the reality was far grimmer. Emerging from a tennis match with Captain Batts, Harry encountered Colonel Wolfe, who had just finished a visit to his beloved. Harry extended his hand, but the Colonel's grip was icy, his salute perfunctory. The Captain looked disconcerted as he departed, leaving Harry to ponder the strange chill between them.

"I have not seen you for many days," Harry remarked.

"You have sought other companions," Wolfe replied curtly.

"But I prefer your company to theirs," Harry protested.

"Indeed, I might offer better counsel than some of them," Wolfe suggested grimly. "Do you mean Captain Batts?"

"He teaches me tennis, yes. But he is hand-in-glove with my Lord and the fashionable set."

"I am not a man of fashion," Wolfe stated flatly. "And if you continue to live with such, trust me, you will find many humble folk cannot afford their company. I am courting an honorable lady. Yesterday, I saw you walking openly with a French ballet-dancer, removing your hat. I must tell you plainly: I had rather you did not remove your hat in such company."

"Sir," Harry cried, turning crimson, "do you suggest I forgo the honor of your acquaintance?"

"I certainly request it when you keep her company," Wolfe answered angrily, using a word unsuitable for print, reminiscent of Othello.

"Heavens! What shame to speak thus of any woman!" Harry exclaimed. "How dare anyone call her dishonest?"

"You ought to know best, sir," Wolfe countered, surprised by Harry's defense. "Or the world lies greatly about you."

"What ought I to know?" Harry demanded. "I see a poor little dancer ordered by doctors to drink the waters. Is it not true that a gentleman of my rank rarely keeps such company? Are you so squeamish? I can speak a little French, and she is welcome to it. If Colonel Wolfe chooses not to bow, let him do so."

"Do you truly not know the woman's character?" Wolfe asked sharply.

"Of course, she is a dancer. But if she were a duchess, I could not respect her more."

"And do you not recall winning her at dice from Lord March?" Wolfe pressed. "Everyone knows the story. I heard it myself from the worthy Mr. Richardson. They speak of you as a colonial Lovelace."

"What else have they said?" Harry asked, bracing himself.

Wolfe recounted the most alarming accusations: a corrupter of virtue

Madame de Bernstein, despite the abundance of card players awaiting her at her lodgings and the assembly rooms, found a peculiar contentment remaining at the Wells. Her days were filled with the dual tasks of chiding her niece and engaging in her rubber. For a man of Harry's age, almost every location becomes a paradise if it offers lively company, fresh air, and a share of sport and diversion. Indeed, at twenty, all pleasure seems exquisite. One rushes forth to meet it with eager anticipation, speculating on its arrival, and when the invitation comes, counting the days until reunion. Yet, how gently and calmly one regards these joys near life's closing season! Does not Lady Merton recall her first ball? Does not her mind wander to that golden past as she sits adorning the walls while her daughters dance? I, too, remember a time when walking three miles through the countryside to dine with Old Captain Jones seemed a delight. To imagine enjoying such a trek today, merely to share Jones's half-pay port! Perhaps it was purchased from a local merchant for a modest sum, yet it arrived wrapped in a kind welcome, flavored by a youth that neither aged wine nor mature men can replicate now. *Viximus nuper.*

I am far less inclined to judge young Harry's conduct and idleness with the stern severity of his friend, the Colonel of the Twentieth Regiment. Oh, blessed idleness! Divine lazy nymph! Reach me a novel as I recline in my dressing gown at three in the afternoon; mix a sherry-cobbler, and bring me a cigar! You, slatternly and smiling enchantress! Though they may assail you with vile names, swear away your character, and brand you the Mother of Evil, you remain the finest company in the world.

Lord March departed for the North, and Lord Chesterfield, finding the Tunbridge waters failed to cure his deafness, returned to his solitude at Blackheath. However, other gentlemen lingered to revel and indulge, and Mr. Warrington quickly discovered ample companionship at his usual haunt, the White Horse. He swiftly mastered the art of ordering a French dinner with the sophistication of any fashionable gentleman from St. James's. He conversed with Monsieur Barbeau in his native tongue with a fluency surpassing many others, cultivated an elegant and discerning taste for wines, and could distinguish between Clos Vougeot and Romande with remarkable acumen. He became the youthful monarch of the Wells, a realm frequented by easygoing men of the world who, rather than being shocked by Harry's reputation for gallantry and extravagance—which had so terrified Mr. Wolfe—embraced it with open arms.

Although our Virginian mingled among the revellers and swam in the same waters as the loose-finned, the boy possessed a natural shrewdness and honesty that shielded him from the common snares laid for the unwary. He rarely entered foolish wagers with the jolly idlers surrounding him, and the seasoned hands found it difficult to deceive him. He participated in games, both indoor and outdoor, driven by a natural aptitude, proving capable of holding his own against any fair competitor. He was scrupulous, playing only with gentlemen he trusted and invariably settling his debts immediately. While he might have fared poorly at a college examination, he possessed prudence, fidelity, keen perception, generous spirit, and dauntless courage.

He frequently found occasion to demonstrate the quality of his make. Consider, for instance, the unfortunate Cattarina, whose entreaties had pushed past the limits of Harry's generosity. With perfect composure and skill, he withdrew from the advances of the Opera-House siren, leaving her free to charm some more gullible soul. In vain did the mermaid's hysterical mother pursue Harry, claiming that a cruel bailiff had seized all her daughter's possessions for debt and that her venerable father lay languishing in a London gaol. Harry firmly declared that no dealings existed between himself and the bailiff. Since he had only gained the fortune of acquainting himself with Mademoiselle Cattarina and satisfying her whims with various trifles and knick-knacks for which she fancied them, he was under no obligation to pay her family's past debts. He refused to act as bail for her father or settle her outstanding accounts at Tunbridge. First, the old woman labeled him a monster and ingrate; then, with a veteran smirk, she questioned why he did not accept payment for the services he had rendered to the young lady. Initially, Mr. Warrington could not comprehend the nature of such compensation. But when the old woman explained, the honest lad rose in horror at the thought of a woman trafficking in her child's dishonor. He told her he hailed from a land where even the savages would recoil from such a bargain. Having bowed the old lady ceremoniously to the door, he ordered Gumbo to note her well and never admit her to his lodgings again. Undoubtedly, she retired breathing vengeance against the Iroquois; indeed, she declared no Turk or Persian would treat a lady thus. Soon after, she and her daughter retreated to London as soon as their anxious landlord permitted.

Then followed Harry's perils of gaming, alongside his dangers regarding gallantry. A man who "plays at bowls" must expect rubbers. After dinner at the ordinary, having declined to play piquet further with Captain Batts, and being roughly asked for his reasons, Harry frankly told the Captain that he played only with gentlemen who honored their debts, like himself. Yet, expressing his readiness to settle any outstanding account with Mr. Batts, the Captain declared himself satisfied beforehand and promptly left the Wells without paying Harry or any other creditor. Furthermore, he demonstrated his spirit by beating a chairman who had been rude to old Miss Whiffler one evening as she headed to the assembly. Upon learning that the calumny regarding himself and the unfortunate opera dancer was being repeated by Mr. Hector Buckler, one of the Wells' fiercest frequenters, Mr. Warrington approached Buckler in the pump room. There, the latter was regaling a crowd of water drinkers with the very slander, and Mr. Warrington publicly informed him that the story was false, warning that he would hold anyone accountable who spread it again. Thus, although our friend, being at Rome, certainly did as Rome did, he proved himself a valorous and worthy Roman. Hurling with the wolves, he was acknowledged by Mr. Wolfe himself to be as brave as the best of them.

Had that officer revealed the stories that caused Colonel Lambert so much pain, we might be certain that when Mr. Wolfe discovered his young friend's innocence, he would have taken the first opportunity to retract the odious charges. Joy rippled through the Lamberts upon the lad's acquittal—a pleasure akin to that felt by higher natures at the recovery of sinners. Never before had the little family been so happy—not even when news arrived of Brother Tom winning his scholarship—as when Colonel Wolfe rode over bearing the account of his conversation with Harry Warrington. "Hadst thou brought me a regiment, James, I think I should not have been better pleased," said Mr. Lambert. Mrs. Lambert summoned her daughters, who were in the garden, kissing them both when they entered, and shouted the good news to them. Hetty leaped for joy, and Theo performed some remarkably brilliant operations upon the harpsichord that night. When Dr. Boyle entered for his game of backgammon, he could not initially account for the illumination in their faces until the three ladies, singing in a happy chorus, told him how right he had been in his sermon and how dreadfully they had wronged that poor, dear, good young Mr. Warrington.

"What shall we do, my dear?" asked the Colonel of his wife. "The hay is in, the corn will not be cut for another fortnight—the horses have nothing to do. Suppose we..." Here he leaned over the table and whispered in her ear.

"My dearest Martin! The very thing!" cried Mrs. Lambert, taking her husband's hand and pressing it.

"What is the very thing, mother?" cried young Charley, home for his Bartholomewtide holidays.

"The very thing is to go to supper. Come, Doctor! We will have a bottle of wine tonight and drink repentance to all who think evil."

"Amen," said the Doctor, "with all my heart!" And with this, the worthy family proceeded to their supper.

One afternoon, Mr. Harry Warrington repaired to his customary lunch at the White Horse Ordinary, finding the scene far livelier than he anticipated. Seated around the table was Parson Sampson, a jolly, handsome figure whose countenance seemed permanently illuminated by the fires of wit and claret. The chaplain regaled the gathering of country squires with a rapid-fire stream of *bons-mots* and scandalous gossip, eliciting roars of laughter from his audience. Though absent from London for several months, the parson possessed the freshest intelligence available to the local gentry: the latest intrigues at Kensington and Pall Mall, the behavior of Mr. Byng within the confines of prison, the shifting odds at Newmarket, and the current reigning toast of Covent Garden.

Parson Sampson's chronicles, however, were less concerned with historical accuracy than with moral vitality. To the rustic ears of his listeners, the precise identity of a lover mattered little; whether it was Lady Jane or Lady Mary harboring a secret flame, or whether the duel at Marybone Garden was fought by Harry Hilton or Tommy Ruffler, the essential truth remained the same—the stories were wicked, lively, and utterly captivating. The parson chatted and laughed with boundless energy, draining bottle after bottle, his spirited discourse keeping the company in a state of amused intoxication. In an era where the "Abbe Parson"—a clergyman unburdened by the strictures of modern piety, equally at home in the theater, the tavern, and the gaming table—was a fixture of high society, Sampson stood as a quintessential example. His voice boomed across the hunting-fields, and his songs rang clear through the halls of Covent Garden.

When Mr. Warrington finally entered, the atmosphere shifted. Whether driven by modesty or the effects of the vintage, the parson's rosy complexion deepened into a crimson flush. He leaned toward the squire in the drab coat seated nearby, whispering a Latin phrase, *"Maxima debetur,"* before rising abruptly. With a mixture of haste and stumbling, he rushed to greet the Virginian.

"My dear sir! My very dear sir!" Sampson cried, his voice thick with emotion. "My conqueror of spades, clubs, and hearts! How delightfully fresh and well you look, Sir Harry!"

Harry returned the greeting with genuine warmth, pleased to see the clergyman and quick to compliment his rosy cheeks. The squire in the drab coat, recognizing Harry, pulled up a chair beside him and urged the parson to resume his seat, promising to continue his tale of Lord Ogle and the grocer's wife. However, his sentence was cut short by a shout and an oath directed at the parson, who had inadvertently stepped on the gouty toe of a neighbor.

Apologizing profusely, Sampson turned back to Harry, bombarding him with news from Castlewood. My Lord Castlewood sent his affectionate greetings and had specifically commissioned Sampson to monitor the young gentleman's morals at Tunbridge Wells. Lady Viscountess and Lady Fanny had departed for Harrogate, Mr. Will had triumphed at Newmarket, and Molly, the housemaid, was weeping uncontrollably over Gumbo, Harry's faithful valet. Harry listened with a benevolent smile, for he was beloved throughout the region, and Sampson, with characteristic flair, wove the names of the highest nobility into his narrative of Harry's successes.

As the guests began to drift away one by one, Harry and the chaplain remained, the latter pouring toast after toast to the amusement of his host. By the end of the repast, Harry's nature had softened; the sharp edges of his youth melted into a warm, generous hospitality. Why should Mr. Sampson endure the expense of an inn when Harry's lodgings offered ample space? The chaplain's trunk was dispatched to the quarters, and Gumbo was summoned to ensure his comfort. Nothing satisfied Harry more than seeing the horses in the stable. Upon recognition, Sampson's mount—a steed Harry had once won from him—whinnied in joyful reunion, nuzzling his old master's coat. Harry, struck by the animal's loyalty, vowed by Jupiter to restore the horse to its rightful owner. Sampson, overcome with gratitude, seized Harry's hand to bless him, flung his arms around the horse's neck, and shed tears that mixed the richness of Bordeaux wine with pure emotion.

Arm in arm, the two friends made their way to Madame Bernstein's apartments, carrying with them the lingering scents of the stables. Madame Bernstein, an elderly woman of keen appetite for both conversation and entertainment, received the chaplain with open arms. She relished Sampson's broad jokes and rapid speech, much as she savored a newly invented dish. Her sole addiction, she confessed, was the game of cards, for unlike life, the cards did not cheat; a bad hand spoke the truth, and a strong suit of trumps was a form of divine flattery. Though she usually refused to play with anyone who lacked funds, the sight of Sampson's empty pockets prompted a momentary hesitation. Yet, it was evident that Harry's overflowing heart and wallet had already procured a small reserve of coin, enabling the chaplain to sit at the green baize table.

Harry had adapted to his new milieu with remarkable speed. His attire, though still black, was of the finest cut and quality, adorned with stars and ribbons. His stockings were drawn, his hair flowed over his shoulders, and he possessed the dashing air of a young Hamlet. Aunt Queensberry noted that he was likely the death of a dozen Ophelias, both here and in the colonies, yet she viewed his romantic pursuits with fond indulgence. His linens and lace were exquisite, and he indulged in shaving plates, magnificent brocade bedgowns, and the finer accoutrements of the fashionable world: French watches with painted backs, enamel snuff-boxes, and a retinue of grooms, jockeys, and tradesmen who waited in his anteroom.

Furthermore, Harry diligently cultivated the arts expected of a gentleman. Under the tutelage of a French fencing-master and a dancing-master, he acquired considerable skill and grace. Lady Maria, herself an elegant dancer, eventually conceded that no gentleman at court could execute the minuet with greater poise than Mr. Warrington. On horseback, too, he proved superior to the finest jockeys and nobles, declaring that none could ride better than his friend, Colonel George Washington of Mount Vernon.

The obsequious but affable Sampson found himself in better quarters than he had known in years. He became Harry's confidential leader, sharing the wisdom of the world and recounting tales, whether real or fancied, that delighted the younger Virginian. It was a classic dynamic of the age: the man of twenty looking up to the man of thirty, admiring the latter's seasoned jests, stale puns, and anecdotes polished by a century of dinner tables. To Harry, Sampson's college pleasantries and town stories were endlessly charming.

In the spirit of the times, there existed a symbiotic relationship between the experienced mentor and the aspiring youth. Just as the "toads" of the literary and social world had supposedly vanished in later eras, leaving behind an island free of parasites, so too did the reign of the Great Grandfather provide a sanctuary for figures like Sampson. These men, who accompanied lads to the gaming table and drank lemonade to replace Burgundy for "Master Hopeful," flourished in an environment where folly was celebrated and vice was merely a subject of mirth.

Thus, Harry's circle expanded. He had fencing and dancing masters, a tutor, a chaplain, and a secretary. This arrangement was detailed in a letter preserved in the Warrington correspondence, dated August 25th, 1756, from Mrs. Bligh's Lodgings at Tunbridge Wells to his mother, Mrs. Esmond Warrington, at Richmond, Virginia.

*Your honoured letter of 20 June, per Mr. Trail of Bristol, has been forwarded to me duly, and I have to thank your goodness and kindness for the good advice which you are pleased to give me, as also for the remembrances of dear home, which I shall love never the worse for having been to the home of our ancestors in England.*

*I writ you a letter by the last monthly packet, informing my honoured mother of the little accident I had on the road hither, and of the kind friends who I found and whom took me in. Since then I have been profiting of the fine weather and the good company here, and have made many friends among our nobility, whose acquaintance I am sure you will not be sorry that I should make. Among their lordships I may mention the famous Earl of Chesterfield, late Ambassador to Holland, and Viceroy of the Kingdom of Ireland; the Earl of March and Ruglen, who will be Duke of Queensberry at the death of his Grace; and her Grace the Duchess, a celebrated beauty of the Queen's time, when she remembers my grandpapa at Court. These and many more persons of the first fashion attend my aunt's assemblies, which are the most crowded at this crowded place.*

*Also on my way hither I stayed at Westerham, at the house of an officer, Lieut.-Gen. Wolfe, who served with my grandfather and General Webb in the famous wars of the Duke of Marlborough. Mr. Wolfe has a son, Lieut.-Col. James Wolfe, engaged to be married to a beautiful lady now

To the discerning eye, the truth concerning Harry Warrington and his entanglement with Cattarina was plain as day. Yet, within the gossipy halls of Tunbridge Wells, a different narrative held sway, particularly among the older matrons who viewed the Virginian through a lens of suspicion. Madame de Bernstein, a woman of considerable stature and ancient lineage, remained steadfast in her conviction that her nephew was not merely dissipated but morally corrupt. To her, an "Old Roman" at heart, the notion that Harry might emulate the vices of Rome was a comforting certainty; she eagerly embraced every scrap of scandal regarding his alleged Lovelace-like exploits. While the reality showed him offering tea and music to the company with genuine gallantry, treating ladies of substance with kindness, Madame Bernstein's vigilance had begun to slacken. The "silly love affair" commenced at Castlewood seemed to fade from her mind, replaced by a self-absorbed focus on her own rheumatic twinges and the management of her social calendar. Like many of her generation, she possessed the wisdom of years, yet those years had softened her once-fierce instincts. The dragon of her youth, capable of tearing out a thousand eyes, now trailed a languid tail, her scales grown soft and her claws reduced to a feeble pat. She could no longer turn swiftly, nor did her venom prove deadly; she was content to crawl home to her cave, admiring how time had tamed the once-devastating forces of female malice.

If the citizens of Oakhurst, despite their collective skepticism, could not resist the tide of rumors painting Harry as a rogue, surely Madame Bernstein, a woman who had witnessed more evil than the entire Oakhurst family combined, would be immune? Alas, the atmosphere of Tunbridge Wells was thick with malice. Every elderly lady in her circle believed every tale spun about Mr. Warrington's debauchery. When the little dancer returned to London, the gossip mills whispered that it was because heartless Harry had abandoned her for some other paramour—a name, or perhaps six, that the society eagerly traded like currency. A century prior, such gatherings at springs like Epsom or Bath were the epicenters of a freer, albeit often reckless, existence where ancestors of all stripes mingled, danced, and gambled. This bad reputation served Madame Bernstein well, assuaging her conscience regarding Harry's passion for Lady Maria. She felt so relieved by the rumors of his wild extravagances that when the chaplain arrived to escort Lady Maria home, the Baroness showed no desire to part. Instead, she kept her niece close, regaling her with tales of her cousin's sins, whispering platitudes about "boys being boys," and plotting to secure a suitable, fortune-rich wife for Harry. Poor Maria endured these pleasantries with quiet fortitude, a necessary shield against the old woman's meddling.

Centuries ago, a certain French Duke and Marquis traversed Europe and America, leaving behind a memoir that posterity wisely ignores. Having played the role of Don Juan across continents, he catalogued the beauties who fell to his charm. For the descendants of the fashionable circles Harry inhabited, it would be a delightful irony to discover their ancestors adorning the pages of such a candid chronicler. During his travels in North America, this nobleman, accustomed to the ease of European conquest, found the colonial life strangely resistant. Unlike the elegant sieges he laid in Europe, he secured not a single victory in Pennsylvania or New England. Travelers of the time often lamented the rudeness and barbarism of the Americans, yet the truth, observed by the narrator, was that American gentlemen conversed with modesty and the women of the colonies lived lives of purity. Harry Warrington, possessing a native colonial modesty, carried this trait to England. Though surrounded by the raucous talk of pleasure-seekers, he remained largely silent, refusing to indulge in double entendres or boast of any triumphs over the fair sex. He appeared shy and awkward whenever others recounted their own conquests.

Mr. Sampson, the chaplain, had noted this reserve early on at Castlewood, where Harry grew visibly uneasy as his cousin Will shared his choice stories. Lord Castlewood had sternly rebuked his brother, commanding him to keep his jokes for the usher's table and avoid offending their kinsman. Thus, the phrase "Reverentia pueris"—reverence for children—became a guiding principle for Sampson, ensuring he did not use his cynicism to wound an innocent youth. Touched by Harry's gift of a horse, the chaplain felt a genuine warmth toward the lad. "You see, sir," Sampson mused, acknowledging his worldly nature, "I must do as the rest of the world does. *Video meliora, deteriora sequor*—I see better things, but follow worse. I have a little sister at school, and while I strive to keep a decent tongue when speaking with her, I trust I may apply similar restraint to you."

Sampson spoke highly of Harry to the Baroness, who delighted in the praise. She adored the boy's good looks, his manly courage, his ready blushes, his bright eyes, and his deep, youthful voice. His blend of shrewdness and simplicity constantly amused her; she would have grown weary of a clever or witty suitor, but Harry's unique nature charmed her. "We must find a good wife for him," she declared to Sampson, already envisioning a match of suitable age and fortune. Sampson, too, harbored personal anxieties and had a candidate of his own in mind. He hoped that if his efforts succeeded in marrying Harry off, Lord Castlewood would reward him with a benefice or provision, as hinted at previously regarding Lady Maria.

Their bond deepened quickly. Sampson was a man of high spirits, good appetite, and unpretentious humor. He avoided sanctimonious hypocrisy, shielding Harry from shocking outbreaks of levity, introducing him only to the "minor mysteries" of the world. To Harry, he was simply a jolly companion, eager for a drink, a cockfight, a shooting match, or a gallop across the commons. Yet, beneath this boisterous exterior, Sampson maintained a decent conversation, striving to amuse rather than mislead his young patron. He was a man of immense animal spirits and natural wit, an experienced "toad-eater"—a term for a professional flatterer who climbed the ladder of success by ingesting the ambitions of the powerful. In Harry's time, a gentleman's toad-eater openly courted his patron, called him "Sir," ran errands, and stood until bidden to sit. Sampson wore this role with pride, referring to Harry as his young Virginian patron, acting as his aide-de-camp, giving orders to tradesmen, and managing his social obligations with the flair of an *ami du prince*.

Returning from the coffee-house, Sampson expressed his shock at Harry's reputation. "What a reputation, sir, they have made for you in this place!" he cried. "Monsieur de Richelieu was nothing to you!"

Harry, confused, asked what he meant by Richelieu, noting he had never served in Minorca. Sampson explained the local frenzy surrounding the Widow Patcham and Mrs. Hooper, both of whom were allegedly desperate for attention from Mr. Warrington. It was the talk of the whole resort.

Indignant, Harry paced the room. "Who says so?" he demanded. "I should like to meet the man who dares say so, and confound the villain!"

Sampson laughed nervously, advising caution. "I should not like to show him to you; it might be the worse for him."

"It's a shame," Harry continued, his face flushed with anger, "to speak with such levity about the character of ladies or gentlemen. Do you know, in our country, a fellow's ears would not be safe? Just before I left home, three brothers shot down a man for speaking ill of their sister."

"Serves the villain right!" Sampson agreed.

"They have started this calumny here already," Harry insisted, referring to the rumors about Cattarina. "And they said the very same thing about my Lord March. Isn't it shameful?"

"Indeed it is," Sampson replied gravely.

"I don't know what I should do if these stories reached my mother's ears. It would break her heart. Why, a mere week ago, a military friend, Mr. Wolfe, told me of the horrible lies circulating. Good heavens! What do they think a gentleman of my name and country can be? A seducer of women? They might as well accuse me of horse-stealing."

Sampson chuckled, referencing the legend of the Turkish Sultan who received bushels of ears as gifts. "If you collected all the ears of those who spread scandal against you, you would fill a basket!"

"And so I would," Harry vowed, his temper flaring. "Any fellow who speaks against a lady or a gentleman of honor shall pay the price."

"If you go down to the Well, you'll find a harvest of them," Sampson noted, describing the high tide of detraction. "But I shall be sorry for anyone you mark, for you're a man of your word."

Agreeing to proceed to dinner and then to Lady Trumpington's tea, the two gentlemen departed. That era saw wine-drinking as commonplace, and since General Braddock's army had returned from the colonies, Harry had developed a taste for filling bumpers and calling toasts, adhering to the point of honor among officers never

The secret whispered by Mr. Lambert into his wife's ear at the close of the previous chapter, and the subsequent joy it wrought throughout the Oakhurst household, is now revealed to us. With the hay harvest complete and the corn fields untouched, the farm horses were finally free from labor. "Why," asked Colonel Lambert, "should we not harness them to the coach, and pay a visit to Tunbridge Wells, taking friend Wolfe at Westerham along the way?"

Mamma, the children, and the honest Colonel alike embraced the proposal with delight. Instantly, the girls descended to gather their finest attire—calamancoes, paduasoes, falbalas, furbelows, capes, cardinals, sacks, negligées, solitaires, caps, ribbons, mantuas, clocked stockings, and high-heeled shoes, alongside countless other articles of toilette. Mamma's most prized robes, usually confined to the presses for rare, solemn occasions, were retrieved and retired immediately afterward to lavender and privacy. The brave Colonel donned his laced hat, waistcoat, and silver-hilted hanger; Charley, delighted, wore a *rasee* holiday suit formerly belonging to his father, which the Colonel had worn at his wedding—a garment Mrs. Lambert cut up with genuine sorrow. Even Ball and Dumpling, the family ponies, had their tails and manes tied with ribbons, while Chump, the ancient white cart-horse, was appointed as a unicorn leader to assist the carriage horses up the first five hilly miles from Oakhurst to Westerham.

The vehicle itself was an antique, rumored to have served in the procession that brought George I. from Greenwich to London upon his accession, having belonged to Mr. Lambert's father and been regarded by the family as one of the most splendid coaches in the three kingdoms. Brian, coachman and ploughman, occupied the box alongside Mr. Charley. The precious garments were secured in imperial bags atop the roof, the Colonel's pistols tucked into the carriage pockets, and a blunderbuss hung behind the box within easy reach of Brian, a veteran soldier. Fortunately, no highwayman molested the convoy, nor did any innkeeper levy contributions from the Colonel, whose slender purse and large family rendered him immune to depredators on the King's highway. A modest, reasonably priced lodging had been arranged for them by young Colonel Wolfe at his usual haunt, where he himself accompanied them on horseback.

These lodgings stood directly opposite Madame Bernstein's establishment. Upon reaching their quarters on a Saturday evening, the Oakhursts observed chair after chair discharging powdered beaux and patched beauties at the Baroness's door, where she was hosting one of her numerous card parties. Though the sun had not yet set—for their ancestors began their dissipation early and continued until late—the young country ladies and their mother watched the influx of guests through their window. Colonel Wolfe identified the arrivals for the girls, turning the viewing into a delightful spectacle comparable to attending the party itself. Hetty and Theo marveled at the scene, able not only to see the guests arrive but to peer through the Baroness's open windows and observe them inside.

Among the distinguished figures passing by was the Duchess of Queensberry. When Colonel Wolfe explained her identity, Martin Lambert promptly recited a score of lines from his favorite poet, Mat Prior: *"Kitty, beautiful and young."*

"Think that old lady was once like you, girls!" cried the Colonel.

"Like us, papa? Well, certainly we never set up for being beauties!" replied Miss Hetty, tossing up her little head.

"Yes, like you, you little baggage! Like you at this moment, wanting to go to that drum yonder—" quoting Prior again, *"Inflamed with rage at sad restraint / Which wise mamma ordained..."*

"We were never invited, papa," said the satirical Martin, "and I am sure if there is no beauty more worth seeing than that, the wit cannot be much worth the hearing."

"Oh, but he is a rare poet, Mat Prior!" continued the Colonel, urging caution. "Though, mind you, girls, you will skip over all the poems I have marked with a cross. A rare poet! To think you should see one of his heroines! 'Fondness prevailed, mamma gave way...' (she always will, Mrs. Lambert!)"

"I am sure it must have been very inflammable," said Mamma.

"So it was, my dear, twenty years ago, much more inflammable than it is now," remarked the Colonel.

"Nonsense, Mr. Lambert," answered Mamma.

Suddenly, Hetty ran forward, pointing to the little square and the covered gallery leading to Madame Bernstein's apartments, where a crowd of street urchins, idlers, and yokels watched the company.

"It's Harry Warrington!" exclaimed Theo, waving a handkerchief. Yet, the young Virginian did not see Miss Lambert. Walking arm-in-arm with a portly clergyman in a crisp, rustling silk gown, the two entered Madame de Bernstein's door.

"I heard him preach a most admirable sermon here last Sunday," said Mr. Wolfe, "a little theatrical, but most striking and eloquent."

"You seem to be here most Sundays, James," observed Mrs. Lambert.

"And Monday, and soon till Saturday," added the Colonel. "See, Harry has beautified himself already; his hair is in buckles, and I have no doubt he is going to the drum too."

"I had rather sit quietly generally of a Saturday evening," said the sober Mr. Wolfe, "at any rate, away from card-playing and scandal; but I own, dear Mrs. Lambert, I am under orders. Shall I go across and send Mr. Warrington to you?"

"No, let him have his sport. We shall see him tomorrow. He won't care to be disturbed among his fine folks by us country-people," said meek Mrs. Lambert.

"I am glad he is with a clergyman who preaches so well," said Theo softly, her eyes suggesting, *You see, good people, he is not so bad as you thought.* "The clergyman has a very kind, handsome face."

"Here comes a greater clergyman!" cried Mr. Wolfe. "It is my Lord of Salisbury, with his blue ribbon, and a chaplain behind him."

"And whom, mercy's name, have we here?" broke in Mrs. Lambert as a sedan-chair, covered in gilding and topped with no fewer than five earl's coronets, approached, borne by men in richly laced clothes and preceded by three footmen in splendid liveries. The Bishop, about to enter, stopped and ran back with respectful bows and curtsies to the sedan-chair, offering his hand to the lady who stepped forth.

"Who on earth is this?" asked Mrs. Lambert.

"Sprechen sie Deutsch? Ja, meinherr. Nichts verstand," quipped the waggish Colonel.

"Pooh, Martin."

"Well, if you can't understand High Dutch, my love, how can I help it? Your education was neglected at school. Can you understand heraldry? I know you can."

"I make," cried Charley, reciting the shield, "three merions on a field or, with an earl's coronet."

"A countess's coronet, my son. The Countess of Yarmouth, my son."

"And pray who is she?"

"It hath ever been the custom of our sovereigns to advance persons of distinction to honour," continued the Colonel gravely, "and this eminent lady hath been so promoted by our gracious monarch to the rank of Countess of this kingdom."

"But why, papa?" asked the daughters together.

"Never mind, girls!" said Mamma.

But the incorrigible Colonel pressed on. "Y, my children, is one of the last and most awkward letters of the whole alphabet. When I tell you stories, you are always saying 'Why.' Why should my Lord Bishop be cringing to that lady? Look at him rubbing his fat hands together, smiling into her face! It is not a handsome face any longer. It is all painted red and white like Scaramouch's in the pantomime. See, there comes another blue-ribbon, as I live. My Lord Bamborough. The descendant of the Hotspurs. The proudest man in England. He stops, he bows, he smiles; he is hat-in-hand, too. See, she taps him with her fan. Get away, you crowd of little blackguard boys, and don't tread on the robe of the lady whom the King delights to honour."

"But why does the King honour her?" the girls asked again.

"There goes that odious last letter but one! Did you ever hear of Her Grace the Duchess of Kendal? No. Of the Duchess of Portsmouth? Non plus. Of the Duchess of La Vallière? Of Fair Rosamond, then?"

"Hush, papa! There is no need to bring blushes on the cheeks of my dear ones, Martin Lambert!" said the mother, placing her finger to her husband's lips.

"'Tis not I; it is their Sacred Majesties who are the cause of the shame," cried the son of the old republican. "Think of the bishops of the Church and the proudest nobility of the world cringing and bowing before that painted High Dutch Jezebel. Oh, it's a shame! A shame!"

"Confusion!" burst out Colonel Wolfe, making a dash for his hat and rushing from the room. He had spotted the young lady he admired and her guardian

Colonel Lambert's first impulse, upon grasping the quiet truth revealed by his youngest daughter's turmoil, was anger directed at the young man who had stolen his child's heart. "A plague on all these rascals, whether English or Indian!" he bellowed to his wife. "I wish that fellow had broken his nose against every doorpost save ours."

"Perhaps it is Providence guiding us to cure him of being a scapegrace, my dear," Mrs. Lambert interjected gently, softening the storm. "That fall at our door holds something divine. You laughed at me, Martin, when I spoke of this before; yet if Heaven did not send this young gentleman to us, who did? And it may be for the blessing and happiness of us all that he arrived."

"It is hard, Molly!" groaned the Colonel. "We cherish and fondle them, rear them through sickness and health. We toil, we scheme, we hoard money in stockings and mend our own old coats. If they ache, we cannot sleep; if they desire a whim, we labor day and night to fulfill it. They are darling daddies and dearest papas. Yet on Tuesday morning I am king of my household, and on Tuesday evening, Prince Whippersnapper appears, and my reign ends. A whole lifetime is forgotten and forsaken for a pair of blue eyes, a pair of lean legs, and a head of golden hair."

"'Tis written that women leave all to follow their husbands," the matron said, laying her hand on her husband's arm. "I believe our courtship was not terribly long, Martin."

"'Tis human nature, and what can you expect?" sighed the Colonel. "And I believe I did my duty, though I admit I left my father for you."

"Excellent wench! Perdition catch my soul, but I do love thee, Molly!" the good Colonel declared. "But mark you, your father never loved me. And if I am ever to have sons-in-law..."

"Ever, indeed! Of course my girls must have husbands!" cried Mamma.

"Well, when they come, I shall hate them, madam, just as your father hated me—and quite rightly, for they take his treasure away."

"Do not be irreligious and unnatural, Martin Lambert! I say you are unnatural, sir!" the matron protested.

"Nay, my dear, I have an old tooth in my left jaw, here," he pointed out, tapping his chin. "'Tis natural for the tooth to loosen. But when the dentist pulls it, it is natural I should feel pain. Do you suppose, madam, I do not love Hetty better than any tooth in my head?" Though no woman resists the notion of her daughter finding a mate, however violently a father revolts against the intruder. Mothers and grandmothers are remarried anew in the unions of their young; their souls don the laces and muslins of forty years past, the postilion's white ribbons bloom once more, and they drive away in the post-chaise. What woman, regardless of age, has not stowed away bridal favors and raiment, wrapped in lavender, in the deepest corners of her heart?

"It will be a sorrowful thing, parting with her," Mrs. Lambert murmured, sighing.

"You have settled that point already, Molly," the Colonel laughed. "Should I not go out and order raisins and Corinth grapes for the wedding cake?"

"And then I shall have to leave the house in their care when I travel to her in Virginia. How many miles is it, Martin? Thousands, surely?"

"A hundred and seventy-three thousand, three hundred and ninety-one and three-quarters, my dear, by the nearest path," Lambert answered gravely. "By the other route, through Persia—"

"Oh, give me the path with the least sea, and your dreadful ships, which I cannot bear!" cried the Colonel's spouse. "I pray Rachel Esmond and I shall become better friends. She possessed a very high spirit when we were schoolgirls."

"Shall we not attend to the baby linen, Mrs. Martin Lambert?" her husband suggested, interjecting. Indeed, Mrs. Lambert likely thought there was no cause for wonderment, having noticed some lovely lace caps and bibs at Mrs. Bobbinit's shop that Sunday afternoon. When the discovery was made, and little Hetty lay upon her pillow with feverish cheeks, closed eyes, and a pitiful countenance, her mother regarded the child with perfect equanimity, seeming almost pleased by Hetty's distress.

The girl was not merely unhappy; she was enraged at herself for revealing her secret. Perhaps the knowledge had struck her suddenly, awakening her to her own state of mind. Now, the little maid chose to be as ashamed as if she had committed a crime and been caught. Indignant at her own weakness, she erupted into transports of self-loathing, vowing never to forgive herself for submitting to such humiliation. Thus, the young pard, wounded by the hunter's dart, chafes with rage in the forest, angry at the surprise of the steel in his side, snarling and biting at his sister-cubs and the leopardess, his spotted mother.

Little Hetty tore, gnawed, and growled so ferociously that I would not wish to be her fraternal cub, nor her spotted dam or sire. "What business has any young woman," she cried aloud, "to indulge in such nonsense? Mamma, I ought to be whipped and sent to bed. I know perfectly well that Mr. Warrington cares not a fig for me. I daresay he prefers French actresses and the commonest little milliner-girl in the toy-shop to me. And so he ought, and so they are superior to me. Why, what a fool I am to burst into tears like a ninny about nothing, simply because Mr. Wolfe said Harry plays cards on Sundays! I know he is not clever, like Papa. I believe he is stupid—I am certain he is stupid—but he is not so stupid as I am. Why, of course, I cannot marry him. How am I to cross to America and leave you and Theo? Of course, he likes somebody else, perhaps in America, or Tunbridge, or Jericho, or somewhere. He is a prince in his own country and cannot think of marrying a poor half-pay officer's daughter with twopenny to her fortune. Did you not tell me, when I was a baby, how I cried wanting the moon? I am a baby now, a most absurd, silly, little baby—do not talk to me, Mrs. Lambert, I am one. Only, there is this to say: he knows nothing of it, and I would rather cut out my tongue than tell him."

Dire were the threats Hetty leveled against Theo, should her sister betray her. As for the infant Charley, his mind wholly fixed upon cheese-cakes, he had neither remarked nor been moved by Miss Hester's agitation; and the parents, along with the kind sister, naturally promised to keep the little maid's secret.

"I begin to think it had been best for us to remain at home," sighed Mrs. Lambert to her husband.

"Nay, my dear," replied the other. "Human nature remains human nature. Surely Hetty's mother told me herself that she harbored the beginnings of a liking for a certain young curate before falling head-over-heels in love with a certain young officer of Kingsley's. As for me, my heart was wounded in a dozen places ere Miss Molly Benson took entire possession of it. Our sons and daughters must follow the paths of their parents, I suppose. Why, but yesterday, you scolded me for grumbling at Miss Het's precocious fancies. To do the child justice, she disguises her feelings entirely, and I defy Mr. Warrington to discern from her behavior how she is disposed toward him."

"A daughter of mine and yours, Martin," cried the mother with great dignity, "shall not fling herself at a gentleman's head!"

"Neither herself nor the teacup, my dear," answered the Colonel. "Little Miss Het treats Mr. Warrington like a vixen. He never visits us, yet she boxes his ears in one fashion or another. I protest she is barely civil to him; but, knowing what transpires within the young hypocrite's mind, I am not going to be angry at her rudeness."

"She needs no reason to be rude at all, Martin; and our girl is good enough for any gentleman in England or America. Why, if their ages suit, should they not marry after all, sir?"

"Why, if he desires her, should he not ask her, my dear? I am sorry we came. I intend to put the horses into the carriage and turn their heads homeward immediately."

"But mamma fondly said, 'Depend on it, my dear, that these matters are wisely ordained.' Depend upon it, Martin, it was not for nothing that Harry Warrington was brought to our gate in that manner; and that he and our children are thus brought together again. If that marriage has been decreed in Heaven, a marriage it will be."

"At what age, Molly, I wonder, do women begin and cease matchmaking? If our little chit falls in love and then falls out again, she will not be the first of her sex, Mrs. Lambert. I wish we were on our way home again, and, if I had my will, I would trot off this very night."

"He has promised to drink his tea here tonight. Would you

Our young Virginian, generous with wealth acquired with ease and cordial to all in his sphere, felt bound by the customs of fashion to entertain his country friends. He secured the Assembly Rooms for the occasion, inviting nearly every remaining guest from Bath Springs. Within, card tables occupied one chamber for those who could not forego the social gambles common to European society; another held a supper of champagne and bowls of negus; while the great hall was reserved for the dance, where Harry Warrington's guests enjoyed themselves in the homely fashion of our ancestors. Yet, I cannot imagine the amusement was particularly spirited. First, the minuets were called. Two or three couples performed them, including the high-ranking spinsters of the assembly. Among these, Lady Maria Esmond, an earl's daughter and the highest-ranked lady present save for the lame Lady Augusta Crutchley, danced the first minuet with Mr. Warrington, her cousin. He acquitted himself to the universal satisfaction of the room, moving with far greater elegance than Mr. Wolfe, who stood with Miss Lowther.

After concluding his dance with Lady Maria, Mr. Warrington requested the honor of walking the next minuet with Miss Theo. Accordingly, Miss Theo, blushing and radiant, performed her exercise to the great delight of her parents and the fury of Miss Humpleby, Sir John Humpleby's daughter of Liphook, who had expected, at the least, to follow Lady Maria. Following the minuets came the country dances. The music, performed by a harp, fiddle, and flageolet perched in a little balcony, played rather feeble and melancholy tunes throughout the evening. If one takes up an old book of music today and plays even a few of those airs, one wonders how people at any time could have found them otherwise than sorrowful. Indeed, there is scarce one air devoid of *amar aliquid*, a distinct tang of sadness. Perhaps it is because they are ancient and defunct, their plaintive echoes calling to us from the limbo of the past, consigned there for this century. Perhaps they were once gay when alive; but when our descendants now hear the works of certain modern masters, they will exclaim, "Bon Dieu, is this the music which amused our forefathers?"

Mr. Warrington had the distinction of a duchess's company at tea—the Duchess of Queensberry, the heroine of Colonel Lambert and Mr. Prior. Though the duchess carefully turned her back on a countess present, laughing loudly and pointing at the latter with her fan, almost the entire company pushed forward to bow, cringe, and smile before this countess, scarcely acknowledging her Grace of Queensberry, her jokes, or her airs. This countess was none other than the Countess of Yarmouth-Walmoden, the lady whom His Majesty George II, King of Great Britain, France, and Ireland, Defender of the Faith, delighted to honor. She had met Harry Warrington in the walks that morning and had been exceedingly gracious, telling him they would play a game of cards that night. Purblind old Colonel Blinkinsop, imagining the invitation was for him, had bowed profoundly. "Pooh! Pooh!" declared the Countess of England and Hanover. "I do not mean you. I mean the young Firshinian!" And thus everyone congratulated the youth on his good fortune. By nightfall, the world, presumably to demonstrate its loyalty, thronged around Lady Yarmouth. My Lord Bamborough was eager to invite her to quadrille; My Lady Blanche Pendragon, that model of virtue; Sir Lancelot Quintain, that pattern of knighthood; Mr. Dean of Ealing, that exemplary divine; and countless gentlemen, noblemen, generals, colonels, matrons, and spinsters of the highest rank watched for a smile from her or rushed to join her card table. Lady Maria waited upon her with meek respect, and Madame de Bernstein treated the Hanoverian lady with profound gravity and courtesy.

Harry's bow, though lowered only so far as hospitality required, provoked indignation in Miss Hester. She scarce spoke a word to her partner during their dance, and when he led her to the supper-room for refreshment, she remained little more communicative. To enter, they passed Madame Walmoden's card table, who good-naturedly called out to her host as he passed, asking if his "breddy liddle bardner" liked dancing.

"I thank your ladyship," said Miss Hester, tossing up her head, "I do not like dancing, and I do not like cards." Dropping a curtsey like a cheese, she strutted away from the Countess's table.

Mr. Warrington was deeply offended. Sarcasm from the young toward the old pained him; flippant behavior toward himself wounded him. Courteous in his simple way to all he met, he expected like politeness in return. Hetty knew perfectly well what offense she gave; she marked the displeasure reddening on her partner's honest face with a sidelong glance, yet wore her most ingenuous smile. As she approached the sideboard where refreshments lay, she artlessly asked: "What a horrid, vulgar old woman that is; don't you think so?"

"What woman?" asked the young man.

"That German woman—my Lady Yarmouth—to whom all the men are bowing and cringing."

"Her ladyship has been very kind to me," Harry said grimly. "Won't you have some of this custard?"

"And you have been bowing to her, too! You look as if your negus were not nice," Harmlessly continued Miss Hetty.

"It is not very good negus," said Harry, gulping.

"And the custard is bad too! I declare 'tis made with bad eggs!" cried Miss Lambert.

"I wish, Hester, that the entertainment and the company had been better to your liking," said poor Harry.

"'Tis very unfortunate; but I dare say you could not help it," cried the young woman, tossing her little curly head.

Mr. Warrington groaned in spirit, perhaps in body, clenching his fists and teeth. The little torturer artlessly continued, "You seem disturbed: shall we go to my mamma?"

"Yes, let us go to your mamma," cried Mr. Warrington, his eyes glazing, shouting at an unlucky waiter, "Curse you, why are you always standing in the way?"

"La! Is that the way you speak in Virginia?" asked Miss Pertness.

"We are rough there sometimes, madam, and can't help being disturbed," he said slowly, with a quiver running through his whole frame, looking down upon her with fire flashing in his eyes. Hetty saw nothing distinctly afterwards, nor did she speak again until she reached her mother. Never had she seen Harry look so handsome or so noble.

"You look pale, child!" cried her mother, anxious, like all *pavidae matres*.

"'Tis the cold—no, I mean the heat. Thank you, Mr. Warrington." She made him a faint curtsey, and Harry bowed a tremendous bow, walking elsewhere among his guests. He hardly knew what was happening at first, so angry was he.

He was aroused by another altercation, this time between his aunt and the Duchess of Queensberry. When the royal favorite passed the Duchess, Her Grace gave her Ladyship an awful stare out of eyes no longer bright as in the young days when they "set the world on fire"; she turned round with an affected laugh to her neighbor and shot at the jolly Hanoverian lady a ceaseless fire of giggles and sneers. The Countess pursued her game at cards, not knowing, or perhaps not choosing to know, how her enemy was gibing at her. A feud of many years' date existed between their Graces of Queensberry and the family on the throne.

"How you all bow down to the idol! Don't tell me! You are as bad as the rest, my good Madame Bernstein!" the Duchess exclaimed. "Ah, what a true Christian country this is! And how your dear first husband, the Bishop, would have liked to see such a sight!"

"Forgive me, if I fail quite to understand your Grace."

"We are both of us growing old, my good Bernstein, or, perhaps, we won't understand when we don't choose to understand. That is the way with us women, my good young Iroquois."

"Your Grace remarked that it was a Christian country," said Madame de Bernstein, "and I failed to perceive the point of the remark."

"Indeed, my good creature, there is very little point in it! I meant we were such good Christians because we were so forgiving. Don't you remember reading, when you were young, or your husband the Bishop reading when he was in the pulpit, how when a woman amongst the Jews was caught doing wrong, the Pharisees were for stoning her out of hand? Far from stoning such a woman now, look how fond we are of her! Any man in this room would go round it on his knees if yonder woman bade him. Yes, Madame Walmoden, you may look up from your cards with your great painted face and frown with your great painted eyebrows at me. You know I am talking about you; and I intend to go on talking about you, too. I say any man here would go round the room on his knees, if you bade him!"

"I think,

Colonel Lambert and his good wife had surely consulted together, resolving to remove their little daughter, Hetty, swiftly from the reach of this charming suitor. With complaints of the sort the poor maiden supposedly suffered, absence often acts as a cure for men; yet I hold that women are not so easily healed by the alibi of distance. Some will travel miles and live years apart, and the obstinate disease of affection remains, defying climate and space. One may whip, abuse, or insult them, and still these deluded creatures persist in their fidelity. Indeed, if I may speak after prolonged study, few methods secure the faithfulness of a partner better than judicious ill-treatment, a brisk dose of occasional violence as an alterative, and for daily diet, a cooling but constant neglect. Administer small quantities of love and kindness at sparing intervals; for taken too frequently, this medicine loses its effect. Those who are most indifferent to their husbands are often those cloyed by too much sugar and syrup. I have known a young woman, with every wish gratified, yawn in her adoring husband's face, preferring the conversation of a mere booby; whereas Chloe, whom Strephon flings his bootjack at in the morning and curses before the servants at dinner, creeps fondling to his knee at tea-time when he is rested, patting his head and playing his favorite tunes. When the butler or maid enters with the candles, she looks round proudly, saying, "Now, John, look how good my dearest Henry is!"

Make your game, gentlemen! There is the coaxing line when one is henpecked and the wife is bored out of existence; and there is the manly, selfish system where she answers to the whistle, knows her master, and frisks about him, licking the hand that is raised to do her good. What did the late lamented O'Connell say? "Hereditary bondsmen, know ye not, who would be free, themselves must strike the blow?" Of course you must, in domestic as well as political circles. Up with your cudgels, my enslaved boys!

These remarks will please women, for they have a taste for humor and understand irony. Young Grubstreet, who writes for penny papers, might say, "I told you so! He advocates the thrashing of women! He has no nobility of soul!" Nor do I, my eminent young friend, any more than you have ears. Ladies, I assure you I am only joking; I do not advocate thrashing your sex. Consider yours a hundred times more loving and faithful than ours.

But what use is it if Hetty's parents take her home, if she intends to be just as fond of Harry absent as present? Why not let her see him before Ball and Dobbin depart, and say, "Good-bye, Harry! I was wilful, but you were kind"? She would show no special emotion, too ashamed to betray her secret. Harry is too preoccupied to discover it. He does not know what grief lies behind Hetty's glances or hidden under her innocent smiles. He parts from her calmly, fancying she is happy to return to her music and flowers.

He did not ride partway homeward by his friend's carriage. Having other plans, he returned later to find the Lamberts gone from Tunbridge Wells. Their windows were open, the sign indicating the apartments were to let. A passing sorrow at the blank aspect of rooms once enlivened by such frank countenances may have crossed Harry's mind, but he dined at the White Horse, ate, and called fiercely for his bottle. Meanwhile, poor little Hester choked over her tea as the Lamberts arrived to sleep at Westerham. By morning, the young roses were wan, black circles around her eyes. It was the thunder and the heat; she could not sleep. Home they came. There was the gate where he had fallen, the bed he lay in, the chair where he sat—ages seemed to have passed! What a gulf between today and yesterday! Who was that little child calling chickens or watering roses yonder? Was she the same Hester Lambert? She seemed older than Theo now—who had always been so composed, so clever, so old for her age—but in a night or two, Hester had lived long, long years. Poppy and mandragora could never medicine them to the sweet sleep they tasted yesterday.

Maria Esmond watched the Lambert cavalcade drive away and felt a grim relief. When Harry entered his aunt's drawing-room, flushed with Barbeau's wine, she looked at him with hot eyes. He laughed, rattled in reply to his aunt asking which girl was his sweetheart, gaily declaring he loved them both like sisters, praising the Lamberts highly. "Why is Lambert not a general?" he asked. "His Royal Highness the Duke is very fond of him." Madame Bernstein, delighted with her boy's success, mimicked the Countess's German accent: "Elle ravvole de fous, cher bedid anche!" "You carry off the hearts of all the old women, doesn't he, Maria?" she sneered at her niece, who quivered under the stab.

"You were quite right not to perceive that she cheated at cards, and you play like a grand seigneur," continued Madame de Bernstein. "Did she cheat?" cried Harry, astonished. "I am sure, ma'am, I saw no unfair play." "No more did I, my dear, but I am sure she cheated. Bah! Every woman cheats, I and Maria included, when we can get a chance. But when you play with the Walmoden, you don't do wrong to lose in moderation. Cultivate her. She has taken a fancy to your beaux yeux. Why should your Excellency not be Governor of Virginia? Pay your respects to the Duke at Kensington. The Countess of Yarmouth will be your best friend."

"Why should you not introduce me, aunt?" asked Harry.

The old lady's rouged cheek grew redder. "I am not in favor at Kensington," she said. "All of us want to forget something or somebody. I dare say our ingenu here would like to wipe a sum or two off the slate. Wouldst thou not, Harry?"

Harry turned red, and so did Maria, and his aunt laughed a wicked laugh. What meant those guilty signals on their cheeks? What account was scored upon their memories? I fear Madame Bernstein was right; most folks have ugly reckonings on their consciences which they are glad to be quit of.

If Maria knew one cause of Harry's disquiet, the spinster would have been more unquiet. For some days, Harry had missed a pocket-book. He remembered having it when he drank claret at the White Horse and Gumbo carried him to bed. Seeking it in the morning, none of his servants had seen it. He inquired at the inn but found no trace. He dared not cry the book; he could only make cautious inquiries. If Lady Maria Esmond knew the book was lost, she would be in a terrible condition. The letters contained all sorts of disclosures: a hundred family secrets narrated by an artless correspondent, satire and abuse of persons they met, expostulations about his attentions to other ladies, scorn, scandal, jokes, protests of eternal fidelity—the usual farrago. Would she like those letters read by anyone else? Did she recollect what she said about the Miss Browns and the unfavorable opinion of Mrs. Thompson's character? Regarding Jones himself, whom she subsequently married after disputes broke off her engagement to Edward, would she like Mr. J. to see those remarks? She wouldn't. Be pleased to withdraw that imputation already cast in your mind upon Lady Maria Esmond. No doubt her letters were foolish, as most love-letters are, but it does not follow there was anything wrong. They are foolish when written by young folks, and how much more foolish when written by an old man to a young lass, or vice versa! No wonder Lady Maria should not like her letters read. The spelling mattered less than the writing itself. I for one am determined never to speak or write my mind out regarding anything or anybody. I intend to say of every woman that she is chaste and handsome; of every man that he is handsome, clever, and rich; of every book that it is delightful; of manners that they are gentlemanlike; of dinners luxurious; of conversation lively; of Xantippe that she has a sweet temper; of Jezebel that her color is natural; of Bluebeard that he was indulgent. What? A word against spotless Messalina? An unfavorable view of human nature? King Cheops not a perfect monarch? Oh, you railer at royalty! When this book concludes, I shall change the jaundiced livery, give them rose-colored coats with cherubs, and all characters shall be perfect angels.

Meanwhile, we are in a society of men and women from whose shoulders no wings have sprouted. There is Madame Bernstein, fallen asleep after dinner, eating and drinking too much. Mr. Harry Warrington has gone to play billiards with Count Caramboli; I suspect idleness is his failing. This is what Mr. Chaplain Sampson remarks to Lady Maria as they talk in a low tone, not to disturb Aunt Bernstein's doze.

"A gentleman of Mr. Warrington's means can afford to be idle," says Lady Maria. "Sure you love cards and billiards yourself, my good Mr. Sampson?"

"I don't say, madam, my practice is good, only my doctrine is sound," says the Chaplain with a sigh. "This young gentleman should have employment. He should appear at court,

Though the Baroness Bernstein had suffered the brunt of the battle described in the previous chapter, when she next encountered her niece, she displayed no trace of resentment or anger. "Of course, my Lady Maria," she said, "you cannot expect me, as Harry Warrington's close relative, to be delighted by the prospect of his marrying a woman who is as old as his mother and possesses no fortune whatsoever. Yet, if he chooses to commit such a folly, the matter is beyond my control. I suspect I would not have taken the offer of five thousand pounds seriously in the heat of our discussion. Was this arrangement already finalized at Castlewood? If I had known how far things had progressed, my dear, I might have spared some unnecessary opposition. Once a pitcher is broken, what railing can possibly mend it?"

"Madam!" interjected Maria sharply.

"Pardon me—I mean no disrespect to your ladyship's honor or character, which, I am sure, remain intact. Harry confirms it, and you confirm it—what further evidence could one desire?"

"You have discussed matters with Mr. Warrington, madam?"

"And he has acknowledged that he made you a pledge at Castlewood: you hold proof of it in his handwriting."

"Certainly I do, madam!" affirmed Lady Maria.

"Oh!" (the elder lady showed no flinch). "And I admit that, initially, I misinterpreted the tone of your letters to him. They implicated other members of the family..."

"Who have spoken most wickedly of me and labored to prejudice me in every possible way against my dear Mr. Warrington. Yes, madam, I admit I wrote against them to vindicate myself."

"But, naturally, you are pained to think that any wretch might seize possession of stories detrimental to our family and turn them into public scandal. Hence your recent unease."

"Precisely so," said Lady Maria. "From Mr. Warrington, I could conceal nothing henceforth, and I spoke freely to him. Yet, that is a vastly different matter from wishing the entire world to know the internal disputes of a noble household."

"Upon my word, Maria, I admire you, and I have done you an injustice. These—these twenty years, let us say."

"I am exceedingly glad, madam, that you conclude by granting me justice at all," replied the niece.

"When I saw you last night, opening the ball with my nephew, can you guess what occupied my thoughts, my dear?"

"I truly have no conception of what the Baroness de Bernstein thought of," said Lady Maria, with hauteur.

"I recalled that you had performed to that exact tune with the dancing-master at Kensington, my dear!"

"Madam, it was an infamous slander."

"Thereby the poor dancing-master received a beating for naught!"

"It is cruel and unkind, madam, to recall that slander—and I shall beg to decline residing any longer with anyone who repeats it," continued Maria, displaying considerable spirit.

"Do you wish to depart? I can imagine you will not find Tunbridge agreeable. It will be intensely hot for you if those letters are discovered."
