"There was not a single word against you in them, madam: regarding that, I can assure you."

"So Harry said, and did your ladyship justice. Well, my dear, we are weary of one another and shall be better apart for a while."

"That is precisely my own sentiment," said Lady Maria, bowing low.

"Mr. Sampson can escort you to Castlewood. You and your maid may take a post-chaise."

"We may take a post-chaise, and Mr. Sampson can escort me," echoed the younger lady. "You see, madam, I act like a dutiful niece."

"Do you know, my dear, I have a notion that Sampson has obtained the letters?" said the Baroness, frankly.

"I confess that such a notion has passed through my own mind."

"And you intend to depart in the carriage to coax the letters from him! Delilah! Well, they can serve no purpose for me, and I trust you may retrieve them. When shall you go? The sooner the better, you say? We are women of the world, Maria. We merely exchange epithets when in a passion. We do not require each other's company; and we part on good terms. Shall we proceed to my Lady Yarmouth's? 'Tis her evening. There is nothing like a change of scenery following one of those minor nervous attacks you have experienced, and cards dispel unpleasant thoughts better than any physician."

Lady Maria agreed to attend Lady Yarmouth's gathering and was dressed and ready first, awaiting her aunt in the drawing-room. As Madame Bernstein descended, she remarked that Maria's door remained ajar. "She holds the letters within her stays," the old lady surmised. The pair departed for their entertainment in separate carriages, exhibiting that charming cordiality and mutual respect which women can display both after and even during the bitterest quarrels.

That night, upon their return from the Countess's assembly, Mrs. Brett, Madame Bernstein's maid, presented herself to my Lady Maria's summons, which the lady had rung from her retiring chamber. Betty, Mrs. Brett, was ashamed to admit she was not in a fit condition to attend immediately. Betty had been reveling and merrymaking with Mr. Warrington's black gentleman, with my Lord Bamborough's valet, and several other ladies and gentlemen of that station; and the liquor—Mrs. Brett was shocked to acknowledge it—had proved too potent for Mrs. Betty. Should Mrs. Brett assist my lady in undressing? My lady stated she would dress without assistance and granted Mrs. Brett leave to withdraw. "She carries the letters in her stays," thought Madame Bernstein. They had bid each other an amicable goodnight on the stairs.

Mrs. Betty received a scolding the following morning when she came to wait upon her mistress from the closet adjoining Lady Maria's apartment, wherein Betty lay. She confessed, with contrition, her partiality for rum-punch, which Mr. Gumbo possessed the knack of brewing most delicately. She accepted her reproof with meekness and, having performed her usual duties regarding her lady's person, retired.

Now Betty was one of the Castlewood maids who had been so charmed by Gumbo, and she was a very handsome, blue-eyed lass upon whom Mr. Case, Madame Bernstein's confidential man, had also cast eyes of affection. Consequently, between Messrs. Gumbo and Case, there had arisen jealousies and even quarrels; which had caused Gumbo, who was of a peaceful disposition, to be somewhat shy of the Baroness's gentlemen, the chief of whom vowed they would break the bones or take the life of Gumbo if he persisted in his attentions to Mrs. Betty.

Yet on the night of the rum-punch, though Mr. Case found Gumbo and Mrs. Betty whispering in the doorway amidst the cool breeze, and Gumbo would have turned pale with fear had he been capable, no one could be more gracious than Mr. Case. It was he who proposed the bowl of punch, which was brewed and consumed in Mrs. Betty's room, concocted by Gumbo with exquisite skill. He complimented Gumbo on his music. Though usually a sober man, he insisted upon drinking more and more, until poor Mrs. Betty was reduced to the state that occasioned her ladyship's just censure.

As for Mr. Case himself, who lay outside the house, he was so ill with the punch that he kept his bed the entire following day and failed to muster the strength to appear and wait upon his ladies until supper-time; when his mistress, good-naturedly, rebuked him, remarking that it was not often he sinned in that manner.

"Why, Case, I could have sworn it was you I saw on horseback this morning galloping along the London road," said Mr. Warrington, who was supping with his relations.

"Me! law bless you, sir! I was in bed, and I thought my head would come off with the aching. I ate a morsel at six o'clock and drank a deal of small beer, and I am almost my own man again now. But that Gumbo, saving your honour's presence, I shall not taste a drop of his punch again." And the honest major-domo proceeded with his duties among the bottles and glasses.

As they sat after their meal, Madame Bernstein was sufficiently friendly. She prescribed strong fortifying drinks for Maria, against the recurrence of her fainting fits. The lady suffered such attacks not infrequently. She urged her to consult her London physician and to send up an account of her case via Harry. By Harry! asked the lady. Yes. Harry was departing for two days on an errand for his aunt to London. "I do not care to tell you, my dear, that it concerns business which will do him good. I wish Mr. Draper to insert him into my will, and as I am traveling upon a round of visits when you and I part, I think, for security, I shall request Mr. Warrington to carry my trinket-box in his post-chaise to London with him, for there have been robberies of late, and I have no fancy for being halted by highwaymen."

Maria looked blank at the notion of the young gentleman's departure, yet hoped she might secure his escort back to Castlewood, whither her elder brother had now returned. "Nay," said his aunt, "the lad has been tied to our

As Mr. Warrington broke his fast, the *London Advertiser* lay open beside his plate, bearing an announcement that called upon all lovers of manly British sport to witness a trial of skill between the renowned champions Sutton and Figg. With immediate resolve, Harry determined to attend these performances. He mounted the pair of horses he had bought the day prior and set off for the Wooden House in Marybone Fields. The youth knew the roads poorly; his carriage veered and tacked far more often than necessity demanded, sending him deep into the green lanes behind Mr. Whitfield's Round Tabernacle at Tottenham Road and through the grounds surrounding Middlesex Hospital. Eventually, however, he arrived at his destination to find a considerable assembly gathered to behold the valorous exploits of the two titans.

A motley crowd of London blackguards surrounded the doors of this temple of British valour, mingling with the horses and equipages of those persons of fashion who, like Mr. Warrington, came to patronize the spectacle. A variety of beggars and cripples hustled the young gentleman, whining for charity, while shoeblack boys scrambled over one another to polish his boots. Nosegay women and flying fruiterers pressed upon Mr. Gumbo, his faithful attendant, offering their wares. Piemen, padlocks, tramps, and strollers of every stripe hung upon the battle-ground like moths about a flame. A flag fluttered from the building, and on the stage before it, a manager, flanked by a drummer and horn-blower, repeatedly announced that the noble English sports were about to commence.

Mr. Warrington paid his admission and secured a seat in a gallery commanding a perfect view of the platform. Below, in the amphitheatre, sat Gumbo. When weariness overtook him, the faithful servant would issue forth into the outer world to drink a pot of beer or play a game of cards with his fellow lacqueys and the coachmen waiting outside. It was a scene where old society was encumbered by a prodigious quantity of servants. Gentlemen and ladies could scarce move without a vassal in attendance—sometimes two or three. Every theatre possessed a footman's gallery; an army of the liveried race crowded chapel-doors, swarmed anterooms, sprawled in halls and on landings. They guzzled, devoured, debauched, cheated, bullied visitors for tips—that noble, old race of footmen is well-nigh gone. Only a few thousand remain among us today. Grand, tall, beautiful, and melancholy, we still behold them on levee days, adorned with nosegays, buckles, plush, and powder. Yet the fatal decree has passed: Uncas with his tomahawk and eagle's plume, and Jeames with his cocked hat and long cane, are passing out of the world where they once walked in glory.

Before the principal combatants appeared, minor warriors and exercises were displayed. A boxing match took place, yet neither fighter proved particularly game, nor were they severely punished, affording Mr. Warrington and the spectators little pleasure. This was followed by cudgel-play, wherein the injuries inflicted were so trivial and unsatisfactory that the company began to hiss and grumble. "The masters! The masters!" shouted the people, demanding the arrival of the famous champions.

Soon, the intrepid Sutton strode up the steps to the stage, sword in hand. He saluted the company with his warlike weapon, making an especial bow to a private box occupied by a stout gentleman who seemed a person of consequence. Sutton was quickly followed by the famous Figg, to whom the stout gentleman waved a hand of approbation. Both men were shirtless, their heads shaven clean but marked by the cracks and scars of many former glorious battles. Upon each champion's burly sword-arm wore an "armiger," a ribbon denoting his color. Now the gladiators shook hands, and as a contemporary poet noted, "The word it was bilboe."

At the commencement of the combat, Great Figg dealt a blow so tremendous at his opponent that, had it struck the other's honest head, that comely noddle would have been shorn off as cleanly as a carving-knife slices a carrot. But Sutton interposed his own sword, catching the blade. The force of Figg's mighty delivery, however, was such that the weapon snapped in his hands, less constant than the heart of him who wielded it. Other swords were now delivered to the warriors. The first blood drawn spouted from the panting side of Figg amidst a yell of delight from Sutton's supporters. Yet the veteran appealed to his audience—and especially to the stout individual in the private gallery—to reveal that the wound was caused by the sword having broken during the previous encounter.

While this parley occurred, Mr. Warrington observed a gentleman in a riding-frock and plain scratch-wig entering the box of the stout personage. Harry recognized him with pleasure as his Tunbridge Wells friend, Lord March and Ruglen. Lord March, who was by no means prodigal of politeness, seemed to show singular deference to the stout gentleman. Harry remarked how his lordship received, with a profound bow, a handful of bank-bills drawn from a pocket-book and handed to him. While thus engaged, Lord March spotted the Virginian. Once his interview with the stout personage concluded, the nobleman crossed to Harry's gallery and warmly greeted his young friend.

They sat and beheld the combat waging with varying success, yet with immense skill and valour on both sides. After sufficient fighting with swords, the warriors fell to with the quarter-staff. The result of this long and delightful battle was that victory remained with the ancient champion, Figg.

During the heat of the contest, a thunderstorm broke over the building. Mr. Warrington gladly accepted a seat in Lord March's chariot, leaving his own phaeton to be driven home by his groom. Harry was in great delectation with the noble sight he had witnessed; he pronounced it something like true sport, and of the best he had seen since his arrival in England. As usual, associating any pleasure he enjoyed with a desire that his dear companion of boyhood should share the amusement, he began to sigh, "I wish..." and then stopped abruptly. "No, I don't," he said.

"What do you wish and what don't you wish?" asked Lord March.

"I was thinking, my lord, of my elder brother, and wished he had been with me," Harry replied. "We had promised to have our sport together at home, you see; and many's the time we talked of it. But he wouldn't have liked this rough sort of sport, and didn't care for fighting, though he was the bravest lad alive."

"Oh! he was the bravest lad alive, was he?" asked my lord, lolling on his cushion and eyeing his Virginian friend with some curiosity.

"You should have seen him in a quarrel with a very gallant officer, our friend—an absurd affair, but it was hard to keep George off him. I never saw a fellow so cool, nor more savage and determined, God help me. Ah! I wish, for the honour of the country, you know, that he could have come here instead of me, and shown you a real Virginian gentleman."

"Nay, sir, you'll do very well," said the amused nobleman. "What is this I hear of Lady Yarmouth taking you into favour?"

"I will do as well as another. I can ride, and, I think, I can shoot better than George; but then my brother had the head, sir, the head!" said Harry, tapping his own honest skull. "Why, I give you my word, my lord, that he had read almost every book that was ever written; could play both on the fiddle and harpsichord, could compose poetry and sermons most elegant. What can I do? I am only good to ride and play at cards, and drink Burgundy." And the penitent hung down his head. "But them I can do as well as most fellows, you see. In fact, my lord, I'll back myself," he resumed, to the other's great amusement.

Lord March relished the young man's naivete, as the jaded voluptuary still to the end always can relish the juicy, wholesome mutton-chop. "By Gad, Mr. Warrington," says he, "you ought to be taken to Exeter Change and put in a show."

"And for why?"

"A gentleman from Virginia who has lost his elder brother and absolutely regrets him. The breed isn't known in this country. Upon my honour and conscience, I believe that you would like to have him back again."

"Believe!" cries the Virginian, growing red in the face.

"That is, you believe you believe you would like him back again. But depend on it you wouldn't. 'Tis not in human nature, sir; not as I read it, at least. Here are some fine houses we are coming to. That at the corner is Sir Richard Littleton's; that great one was my Lord Bingley's. 'Tis a pity they do nothing better with this great empty space of Cavendish Square than fence it with these unsightly boards. By George! I don't know where the town's running. There's Montagu House made into a confounded Don Saltero's museum, with books and stuffed birds and rhinoceroses. They have actually run a cursed cut—the New Road they call it—at the back

A fortunate circumstance in my early youth acquainted me with a respectable gentleman, a native of a certain island, which some impartial critics of maritime jewels pronounce to be the first gem of the ocean. The tales this worthy gentleman imparted concerning his kinsfolk, who dwelt upon that "gem," were sufficient to curdle the blood of a young man like myself with horror at the sheer volume of wickedness existing in the world. Every conceivable crime—the Ten Commandments shattered into a universal pulp—stories of such roguery and knavery as no fiction writer could invent, along with murders and robberies rivaling those of Thurtell or Turpin, were accurately recalled and freely recounted by my informant regarding his nearest relations. It was a marvel how any of that brood survived outside the hulks. My brother Tim had brought his father's gray hairs to the grave with sorrow; my brother Mick had repeatedly robbed the parish church; and my sister Annamaroia had jilted a captain, eloped with an ensign, forged her grandmother's will, and stolen silverware for which the thief named Larry was eventually hanged. The lineage of Atreus paled in comparison to the race of the O'What-d'ye-call-'ems, from which my friend sprang, yet no earthly power would induce me to name the country of his birth.

What, then, was my naive astonishment to discover these murderers, rogues, parricides, and habitual forgers of bills of exchange writing to one another as "my dearest brother" and "my dearest sister"? For months at a time, they lived on terms of profound amiability. Tim, whose hands still reeked with the blood of his murdered parents, mixed a screeching tumbler for Maria. Mick, whose lips were blackened by the perjuries he had sworn in court regarding his grandmother's vanished testament or the slaughter of his poor brother Thady's helpless orphans, kissed his sister Julia's bonny cheek. They shared jolly nights, crying as they spoke of old times, the dear old Castle What-d'ye-call-'em where they were born, the quartered Eighty-first Regiment, the Major proposing for Cyaroline, and the tomb of their scent-ed mother (who had cheated them out of property). Heaven bless her soul! Their embraces at meeting and parting were so profuse, so touching, that the painful little stories faded from memory, and one forgot their previous assurances that, if told all, they could hang each other round.

Is there anything finer than forgiveness? Is there anything more rational than to call a man by every bad name under the sun, then apologize, regret hasty expressions, withdraw the decenser one flung at an enemy's head, and resume friendship as before? Some souls possess this admirable, angel-like gift. It was beautiful to witness our two ladies at Tunbridge Wells forgiving one another, smiling, joking, fondling almost in spite of the hard words of yesterday, forgetting bygones though they could not help remembering them perfectly. I wonder, can you and I do as much? Let us strive to acquire this pacable, Christian spirit. My belief is that you may learn to forgive bad language employed to you, provided you have a deal of practice and are accustomed to both hearing and using it. Embrace after a quarrel and mutual vituperation. Heaven bless us! Bad words are nothing when one is accustomed to them, and scarce need ruffle the temper on either side.

Thus the aunt and niece played cards amicably, toasted each other's health, divided wings of chicken, and pulled bones from the merry-thought pie, scratching their neighbors' eyes out in conversation but never each other's. We read how Peninsular warriors, when bugles sang truce, fraternized, exchanged tobacco pouches and wine, only to seize their firelocks and knock each other's heads off when the truce ended; likewise, our old soldiers, skilled in war yet enamored of quiet life, laid down their weapons for the nonce to hob-nob gaily. Of course, while drinking with Jack Frenchman, you keep your piece handy to blow his brains out should he make a hostile move; but meanwhile, *À votre santé*, mon camarade! Here's to you, Mounseur! Everything proceeds pleasantly. Regarding Aunt Bernstein's threatened gout? The twinges had subsided. Maria was so relieved! Yet a slight recurrence occurred last night. The Baroness was so sorry! Her niece must see the best doctor, take everything to fortify her, continue the steel even after leaving Tunbridge. How kind of Aunt Bernstein to offer bottled waters! Suppose Madame Bernstein whispers in confidence to her own woman, "Fainting fits! Pooh! Epilepsy! Inherited from that horrible scrofulous German mother!" What means have we of knowing the private conversations of old ladies and their attendants? Suppose Lady Maria orders Mrs. Betty, her ladyship's maid, to taste every glass of medicinal water, first declaring her aunt capable of poisoning her? Very likely such conversations occur. These are merely precautions—the firelocks our old soldiers carry at their sides, loaded and cocked, but currently lying quiet on the grass.

Possessing Harry's bond in her pocket, the veteran Maria chose not to press for immediate payment. She knew the world too well for that. Though bound to her, she granted him ample latitude and leave of absence on parole. It was not her aim to unnecessarily chafe and anger her young slave. She understood the disparity of ages and that Harry required pleasures and diversions. "Take your ease and amusement, cousin," said Lady Maria. "Frisk about, pretty little mousekin," purring grey Grimalkin in the corner, watching with green eyes. Concerning all that Harry was to see and do on his first visit to London, his female relatives naturally talked and joked. Both ladies knew perfectly what constituted a young gentleman's ordinary amusements in those days, speaking of them with the frankness characteristic of easy times.

Our wily Calypso consoled herself perfectly in the absence of her young wanderer, taking any diversion that presented itself. Mr. Jack Morris, the gentleman previously mentioned as enjoying the company of Lord March and Mr. Warrington, proved one such diversion. To live with titled personages was Jack Morris's delight; losing money at cards to an earl's daughter was almost a pleasure. Now, Lady Maria Esmond was an earl's daughter who was very glad to win money. Obtaining permission to escort Mr. Morris to the Countess of Yarmouth's assembly, she played cards with him—and everyone was pleased.

Thus the first eighty-four hours following Mr. Warrington's departure passed rather cheerily at Tunbridge Wells, until Friday arrived, bringing the sermon for which we saw Mr. Sampson preparing. The company at the Wells were eager to listen. Sampson possessed a reputation for being a most amusing and eloquent preacher; if there were no breakfast, conjurers, dancing bears, or concerts underway, the good folk of the Wells would endure a sermon. Knowing that Lady Yarmouth was coming, and aware of her immense power in granting livings and dispensing bishoprics—a fact confirmed by the Defender of the Faith's remarkable confidence in her ladyship's opinions—we may be sure Mr. Sampson prepared his very best discourse for her hearing. When the Great Man is at home at the Castle, strolling over to the little country church in the park, bringing the Duke, the Marquis, and cabinet ministers in tow, have you ever sat among the congregation to watch Mr. Trotter, the curate, and his sermon? He gazes anxiously at the Great Pew, falters over his text, thinking, "Ah! Perhaps his lordship may grant me a living!" Mrs. Trotter and the girls gaze anxiously at the Great Pew as well, watching the effects of papa's discourse—the well-known favorite—upon the assembled big-wigs. Papa's initial nervousness fades; his noble voice clears, warms to the subject, kindles. He produces his pocket-handkerchief. He approaches that exquisite passage which made them all weep at the parsonage. He has begun it! Then, what is that humming noise filling the edifice, causing hob-nailed Melibaeus to grin at smock-frocked Tityrus? It is the Right Honourable Lord Naseby snoring in the pew by the fire! And poor Trotter's visionary mitre vanishes with the music.

Sampson served as the domestic chaplain to Madame Bernstein's nephew. The two ladies of the Esmond family patronized the preacher. On the day of the sermon, the Baroness hosted a small breakfast in his honor. Sampson made his appearance, rosy and handsome, sporting a fresh-flowered wig and a smart, rustling new cassock purchased on credit from a church-admiring mercer at the Wells. Flanked by his patronesses' lacquemen, who trailed behind with great gilt prayer-books, Mr. Sampson marched from breakfast to church. Everyone remarked how well the Baroness Bernstein looked; she laughed, was particularly friendly with her niece, offering a bow and a stately smile to all as she moved on with her tortoiseshell cane. At the door, a dazzling conflux of rank and fashion gathered—all the fine company of the Wells trooping in, including Lady Yarmouth, conspicuous with vermilion cheeks and a robe of flame-colored taffeta. Shabby people were present as well, though these latter formed the vast majority. What an odd-looking pair, for instance, were those in ragged coats, one with carroty hair peeking beneath a scratch-wig, entering just as the organ ceased! Nay,

August 25th, 1756. The morning light filtered through the windows of Tunbridge Wells, illuminating a letter penned by Mr. Henry Esmond Warrington. Addressed to "My dear Lord March," it bore the satisfaction of a man who had finally secured his wager. "I have won all our three bets," he wrote, detailing his swift journey from Bromley to London in record time. He recounted driving his own horses, guided by a postilion, with Mrs. Betty safely tucked inside. Though two horsemen had shadowed them near Blackheath, their suspicious glances had driven them away, allowing Harry and his charges to arrive at the spa town just after eleven o'clock. With business concluded, Harry resolved to settle his gambling debts to Lord March, eager to offer his opponent a fresh challenge—and perhaps, a measure of revenge.

Back at the Bailiff's residence, the atmosphere had been heavy with dread until the witching hour. Lady Maria Esmond and the Reverend Sampson had played cards in the dim candlelight following a meager meal provided by the bailiff's wife. Then, the sharp *whirr* of approaching wheels shattered the silence. Before the carriage could even halt, the gates swung open. First emerged Mrs. Betty, her face aglow with a joy that bordered on hysteria, followed closely by a tall, dashing young gentleman. As the stranger stepped forward, the Reverend dropped his cards with a triumphant shout, while Lady Maria, drained of color, collapsed into his embrace. It was Harry Warrington, the rescuer. He had ridden through the night to liberate her from the clutches of tyranny and shame. Tears streamed down her cheeks as she buried her face in his shoulder, overwhelmed by the relief of being free. Leaning heavily on his arm, she stumbled home, safe at last, while Madame Bernstein, unaware of the drama unfolding in her house, slept undisturbed.

Inside the safety of the lodgings, Harry immediately issued orders. "You are both free to go home," he declared, his voice ringing with authority. To Betty, he bade her fetch his lady's hat and cloak; to Sampson, he suggested they share a pipe to soothe the jarring effects of the escape. The young clergyman, a man of profound and volatile emotions, was instantly undone. Seizing Harry's hand, he sobbed openly, praying fervently for divine blessing upon his generous patron. Harry felt a warm, swelling pride pulse through his veins. To aid the suffering, to transform sorrow into joy—it was a noble trade, and he wore his success like armor. As he stood beside his rescued princess, the world seemed to spin around him in a kaleidoscope of honor. Beautiful illusions danced before his eyes, crowning him as a conquering hero, a champion hailed by the streets. Yet, deep down, Harry knew the truth: his feat required little more than a kind hand and a silver tongue. He had merely disarmed the dragon guarding the gate and persuaded the tyrant to lay down his axe. Though his vanity swelled with the praise, his nature was gentle enough to accept it.

That evening, the scene shifted to the warmth of a tavern, where the atmosphere was thick with Burgundy and the intoxicating scent of camaraderie. Sampson remained under the spell of his liberation, his spirit soaring with gratitude. With every glass raised, his admiration for Harry climbed higher, painting the young man as the epitome of nobility. Harry, buoyed by his recent triumphs, embraced the flattery with a regal air. "The younger branch of our family," he proclaimed, pouring himself another glass, "has treated you poorly, Sampson—but rest assured, I shall stand by you!" He spoke with the grandeur of a man recounting the glories of his lineage, the House of Esmond. Sensing the chaplain's financial troubles regarding a sister at school, Harry produced a banknote, offering it freely. "Count on me whenever you need me," he urged, passing the money across the table.

Sampson, moved to tears by such unmerited kindness, protested his unworthiness, swearing oaths of devotion. "I do not deserve this!" he cried, his voice trembling. Harry waved off the sentiment with a dismissive laugh, noting the irony of the situation. "There was no money in that pocket-book I lost last week," he reminded him gently. "How do you know?" Sampson asked, his head bowing in shame. "Because I am a villain, sir," the chaplain confessed, revealing that he had discovered Harry's stolen letters and pocket-book the night of the incident at Barbeau's.

Harry's complexion flushed crimson as the truth laid bare. "Did you read the letters?" he demanded, rising abruptly. "They told me nothing I did not already know," Sampson admitted, his gaze fixed on the floor. "You have spies, sir—spies whom you are far too young and naive to detect." Harry's mind raced, connecting the dots about Lady Fanny, his cousin Will, and the crumbling fortunes of the older Esmond branch. "Do these rumors about Lady Maria hold truth?" he whispered, his voice tight with fear. "Oh, not for worlds!" Sampson hastened to assure him, though his tone betrayed a strange hesitation. "Though I admit, she seems older than you... and perhaps ill-suited, though she possesses every virtue."

"She is too old, I know she is," Harry insisted with feigned dignity, masking his turmoil. "But she has my word, and you saw how devoted she is." He pressed Sampson to retrieve the stolen papers, promising forgiveness. "Let me try to forgive myself!" Sampson wept, retreating to his chamber. Moments later, he returned, pale and shaken, holding out the empty pocket-book. "The letters are gone, sir," he confessed, describing how his trunk and belongings had been rifled while he was detained. "Someone else has taken them."

The room fell silent. Who could have entered? The landlady swore no one had approached, yet something vital had vanished. Was it Lady Maria, seeking evidence against the Baroness? Or was it the Baroness herself, the mastermind behind the chaos? The pieces fit together: the Baroness's secret journey to London, her knowledge of the local shoemaker where Sampson lodged, and the mysterious disappearance of the documents. Yet, the motive remained murky. Had the Baroness arrested Lady Maria to hide the theft? Or was this a trap laid by someone else entirely?

For the moment, however, Harry was too intoxicated by his victory to dwell on the shadows. Swollen with self-satisfaction over his winnings, his rescue mission, and the lavish supper sent by Monsieur Barbeau, he accepted Sampson's vows of loyalty with open arms. "I think, Sampson, you would," Harry mused, raising his glass. "My family—the House of Esmond—is accustomed to faithful friends, and we reward them well." They toasted the glory of the Esmond-Warrington line, and as the sun began to rise outside the shuttered window, casting a golden glow into the room, Harry drifted off to sleep, secure in his benevolence.

By noon, a servant arrived bearing a message from Madame de Bernstein. She wished to see her nephew, inviting him for a cup of chocolate. Harry complied, walking to the lodgings where he found the elderly Baroness waiting. Her demeanor had softened, replaced by a mixture of affection and maternal concern. She thanked him profusely for his generosity toward Lady Maria, recounting the terror of the seizure in church and the humiliation of losing three hundred guineas to Lady Yarmouth the previous night. "I could not stay in debt," she explained, explaining how she had sent her steward, Case, to London to raise funds. "Can you lend me some, child?" she pleaded.

Harry, blushing deeply, offered twenty-two pounds, lamenting his own extravagance in buying horses and clothes. But the Baroness refused, producing a note for fifty pounds instead. "Go and spend it, be merry!" she commanded, urging him to enjoy life. Her thoughts then turned sharply to his engagement. "You have engaged yourself to poor Maria, who is nearly twice your age?" she exclaimed, her eyes widening. "What will Mother Esmond say? She may live three centuries, and you will not have wherewithal to support yourselves!" She scoffed at the madness of their gaming habits, warning that Maria would eventually pawn Harry's coat just to keep playing. "It runs in our blood," she admitted, shaking her head. "I warned you! A lad of twenty promising eternal love on his knees, signing his heart's blood..."

"And who showed you my letter?" Harry interrupted, burning with shame. "An accident," the Baroness replied, explaining how Lady Maria, fainting from the stress of arrest, had dropped a sachet containing Harry's letter. "I opened it out of curiosity," she confessed, handing over the case. "And I burned it."

A sharp pang tore through Harry's heart. "Why didn't she destroy it?" he wondered silently. "I will give it back to Maria," he stammered, reaching for the locket. "No, my dear," the Baroness interjected, her voice firm. "I have burned the foolish letter. If you keep that promise, misery awaits you. She has entrapped you, Harry, and I have cut the strings. Go back! Betray me if you must, but do not follow her."

"I am not angry with you, Aunt—I wish I were," Harry said, his voice thick with emotion. "I shall not repeat what you told me." The old lady, seeing

Our friends at Tunbridge were growing wearied of the waters, their spirits restless with the desire to depart. Autumn was approaching, and Bath lay ahead as Madame de Bernstein's ultimate destination—a realm of greater cards, livelier company, and endless life. Before reaching that glittering capital, however, she intended to pay respects to her country acquaintances. Harry, with a grace that was distinctly lacking, had promised to escort Lady Maria and the chaplain to Castlewood.

Their carriage wheels rattled past Oakhurst village once more, gliding by the hospitable home where Harry had once been so warmly welcomed. Lady Maria, buoyed by a mischievous spirit, peppered the air with sharp remarks concerning the young ladies of Oakhurst, suggesting they were setting their caps at Harry, and noting her mother's obvious eagerness to secure him for one of them. Irritated, Mr. Warrington declared in a petulant voice that he would bypass his friends' doorstep, citing his ladyship's apparent disdain and abuse of them. That evening, at the inn where they halted a few miles further along the road, the atmosphere grew thick with ill humor.

At supper, Lady Maria's smiles failed to coax any warmth from Harry's countenance. Her calculated tears—so easily summoned—elicited no sympathy from Mr. Warrington. To her querulous complaints, he offered only a surly grunt. Eventually, the lady was compelled to retire to her chamber without securing even a brief tête-à-tête with her cousin; the obstinate chaplain, seemingly by some unseen decree, refused to leave the room. "Had Harry given Sampson orders to remain?" she wondered as she departed, drawing a deep sigh. With an expression of stubborn politeness, he bowed her to the door, consigning her to the care of the landlady and her maid.

Ten minutes after Lady Maria had vanished into her chamber, a horse galloped frantically from the inn-yard. An hour later, when Mrs. Betty returned to fetch her mistress's smelling-salts, she discovered Parson Sampson smoking a solitary pipe. Mr. Warrington was supposedly gone to bed—or perhaps taking a moonlit stroll—but how could anyone know where Mr. Harry truly was? When pressed by the maid, Sampson revealed the truth: Mr. Warrington was ready to depart the following morning and had taken his seat beside Lady Maria's carriage. Yet his brow remained black, shadowed by a dark spirit. He scarcely spoke to her throughout the journey. Within herself, Lady Maria whispered, "Great heavens! she must have told him that she stole it!"

In reality, as they ascended a steep hill roughly three miles from Oakhurst on the Westerham road, Lady Maria Esmond, leaning heavily on the arm of her beloved youth, had poured out the most sentimental vows, protests, and declarations of affection. She was deeply in love with him. As her affections deepened, his demeanor grew colder. Looking up into his face, she saw the sun illuminate features that, though fresh and well-preserved, betrayed the lines and wrinkles of two-score years. Poor Harry, feeling the weight of her arm pressing upon him, found it intolerable. The prospect of carrying such a burden for all eternity was a dreary thought, causing him to curse the moonlit walk, the stifling heat, and the hot wine that had prompted him to make a foolish pledge, binding him fatally.

Maria's praises and raptures annoyed Harry beyond measure. The poor girl spilled fragments of the few plays she knew that pertained to her situation, striving with every ounce of her power to charm her young companion. She called him, repeatedly, her champion, her Henrico, her preserver, vowing that her Molinda would remain faithful forever. She clung to him tightly. "Ah, child! Have I not thy precious image, thy precious hair, thy precious writing here?" she exclaimed, gazing into his face. "Shall it not accompany me to the grave? It would, sir, were I to meet with unkindness from my Henrico!" she sighed.

A strange tale unfolded before them. Madame Bernstein had given Harry the small silken case, burning the hair and note it contained; yet Maria still held the memory of it close to her heart. It was then, as Harry started suddenly and moved as if to drop her arm, that Lady Maria felt her first pang of remorse—not merely for telling a fib, but for being exposed in doing so, a far more compelling reason for repentance. *Heaven help us,* she thought, *if everyone were to perform penance for lying, would they ever emerge from sackcloth and ashes?*

Upon arriving at Castlewood, Harry's good humor had not improved. The Lord was absent, and the ladies were away. The sole family member present was Mr. Will, who had just returned from partridge shooting. His complexion turned ghastly upon seeing his cousin, who bestowed a sulky scowl of recognition upon him.

Yet, Will attempted to maintain a pleasant facade. They sat down to supper, their conversation initially civil if not particularly lively.
"Have you been to some races?" asked Mr. Warrington.
"To several," replied Will.
"Were you successful in your bets?"
"I hope so," said Mr. Warrington dryly. "Pretty well, then?"
"And you have brought back my horse sound?" asked Mr. Warrington.
"Your horse! What horse?" Will asked, bewildered.
"What horse? My horse!" Harry said curtly.
"Protest, I don't understand you," said Will.
"The brown horse for which I played you, and which I won of you the night before you rode away upon it," Mr. Warrington stated sternly. "You remember the horse, Mr. Esmond."
"Mr. Warrington, I perfectly recall playing you for a horse, which my servant handed over to you on the day of your departure."
"The chaplain was present at our play. Mr. Sampson, will you act as umpire between us?" Mr. Warrington requested gently.
"I am bound to decide that Mr. Warrington played for the brown horse," said Mr. Sampson.
"Well, he got the other one," said sulky Mr. Will, with a grimace.
"And sold it for thirty shillings!" Mr. Warrington remarked, maintaining his composure.
Will, ever waggish, chuckled. "Thirty shillings? And a devilish good price, too, for the broken-kneed old rip. Ha, ha!"
"Not a word more. 'Tis only a question about a bet, my dear Lady Maria. Shall I serve you some more chicken?"

Nothing could be more studiously courteous and gay than Mr. Warrington while the lady remained in the room. When she rose to leave, Harry followed her to the door, offering the most courtly bow of farewell. Standing before the closed door for a moment, he then commanded the servants to retire. Once the menials had departed, Mr. Warrington locked the heavy door, slipping the key safely into his pocket.

As the lock clicked, Mr. Will, who had been sitting over his punch and occasionally glancing askance at his cousin, asked, with one of the oaths common to his speech, what on earth Mr. Warrington meant by that maneuver.
"I guess there's going to be a quarrel," said Mr. Warrington, blandly, "and there is no use in having these fellows look on at rows between their betters."
"Who is going to quarrel here, I should like to know?" asked Will, looking very pale and clutching a knife.
"Mr. Sampson, you were present when I played Mr. Will fifty guineas against his brown horse?"
"Against his horse!" Will shouted.
"I am not such a fool as you take me for," said Mr. Warrington, "although I do come from Virginia!" He repeated his question. "Mr. Sampson, you were here when I played the Honourable William Esmond, Esquire, fifty guineas against his brown horse?"
"I must own it, sir," said the chaplain, casting a deprecatory look toward his lord's brother.
"I don't own no such a thing," said Mr. Will, forcing a laugh.
"No, sir: because it costs you no more pains to lie than to cheat," said Mr. Warrington, stepping toward his cousin. "Hands off, Mr. Chaplain, and see fair play! Because you are no better than a—"

Before he could finish, his cousin flung a wine bottle at Mr. Warrington's head. The Virginian bobbed just in time, sending the missile flying across the room where it shattered against the wainscot, destroying the portrait of an Esmond ancestor before splattering a pint of port wine over the chaplain's face and powdered wig. "Great heavens, gentlemen, I pray you to be quiet!" cried the parson, dripping with liquid gore.

However, gentlemen were not inclined to heed the Church's commands in moments of rage. Having failed to strike its mark, Mr. Esmond seized a large silver-handled knife and lunged at his cousin. But Harry caught up the attacker's right hand with his left, mimicking the boxers he had observed at Marybone, and delivered a rapid blow to Mr. Esmond's nose. This sent the younger man reeling backward against the oak panels, likely causing him to see a thousand visions. He dropped his knife as he retreated

The world, in the time of Harry Warrington, was far from idle in its debates regarding the literary supremacy of antiquity versus modernity. The learned, along with the general public, largely sided with the ancients, viewing them through a lens of timeless reverence. Yet, standing in the present moment, looking backward, one must admit that the "ancients" of yore are but the "moderns" of today. Just as we, living now, will eventually become the subjects of future judgment, so too did the scholars of the mid-eighteenth century regard their contemporaries with a mixture of awe and skepticism.

If one were to judge the intellectual prowess of the age, the argument would inevitably tilt toward the present. While the ancients possessed the formidable weight of Edward Gibbon, the universities of Cambridge and Glasgow produced professors of Greek who knew far more than the entire educational system of Europe—Athens included—had managed to cultivate centuries prior. Similarly, in the realm of science, humanity had barely progressed beyond the level of the "heathen" predecessors who had supposedly stunted the ancients' literary growth. But what of morality? Was the ethical climate of 1858 superior to that of a century past?

Consider the assembly of the House of Commons, as depicted by Mr. Disraeli. Had every member of Parliament a fixed price, as in the eras of Walpole or Newcastle? Or, perhaps more delicately, had almost every one of them sold themselves? As for the fairer sex, one need not label them a collective order of Vestals. However, the chronicles of a hundred years ago teemed with scandals so voluminous that one might be grateful not to inhabit such perilous times. On my conscience, I maintain that men and women of the current era are genuinely better. Not only are the "Susannas"—those paragons of virtue—more numerous, but the "Elders" are significantly less wicked.

One need only look to the literary landscape to understand this shift. Have you read *Clarissa*, *Tom Jones*, or *Roderick Random*? Contemplate the paintings of contemporary artists depicting the men and women of their day. Imagine describing the exploits of a figure like Mr. Lovelace, or Lady Bellaston, or that astonishing "Lady of Quality" who donated her memoirs to the author of *Peregrine Pickle*. How would the pure, outraged sensibilities of the nineteenth century react? They would blush, scream, flee the room, summon the young ladies, and issue a stern command to Mr. Mudie to cease sending such "odious" volumes.

"You are fifty-eight years old, madam," the narrator addresses his audience gently. "Perhaps you are too squeamish, crying out before injury occurs, when no offense was intended. It is possible the novelist's art suffers under the restraints imposed upon him, much like the honest statues of St. Peter's and the Vatican, ruined by the tin draperies in which pious nuns swaddle the fair limbs of marble. Yet, in your prudery lies a certain reason, as does the state censorship of the press. A page may contain matter dangerous to *bonos mores*; thus, out with the scissors, censor, and clip the prurient paragraph! We have no choice but to submit. Society, the despot, has issued his imperial decree. We may whisper that the statue looked better unveiled, that the moral tale would be stronger if complete. But away with him—a single word is forbidden!"

And yet, consider the pianofortes of the United States, clad in frilled muslin trousers. One depends upon it, that muslin covered not only the mahogany legs but also the notes within, muffling the music and halting the player. To what, then, does this prelude lead? It introduces us to Harry Warrington, Esquire, residing in Bond Street, London, and the life he, alongside many other young bucks of fashion, led during those times.

I confess I cannot escort my fair and young reader into these circles any more than Lady Squeams could guide her daughter to Cremorne Gardens on an ordinary evening. My dear Miss Diana (pshaw! I know you are eight-and-thirty, despite your shyness and desire to pretend you recently left school dinners behind), when your grandfather was a young man about town, a member of White's Club, dining at Pontac's off the feasts provided by Braund and Lebeck, and riding to Newmarket with March and Rockingham, he lived a life of which your noble aunt—the author of *Legends of the Squeams's; or, Fair Fruits of a Family Tree*—has given you the faintest notion.

This was before your grandmother adopted those serious views that defined her later residence at Bath, and before Colonel Tibbalt wed Miss Lye, the wealthy heiress of the soap-boiler. During those days, her ladyship's "wild oats" were sown. In youth, she was as giddy as the rest of the genteel world. At her Hill Street mansion, ten card tables stood ready on Wednesdays and Sundays, barring a brief interlude when Ranelagh opened on Sundays. Every night of her life she gambled for eight, nine, or ten hours. Everyone else in society did likewise. She lost, she won, she cheated, she pawned her jewels—who knows what else she was unwilling to pledge to fund her fury for play?

Recall that infamous after-supper duel at the Shakespeare's Head in Covent Garden, fought between your grandfather and Colonel Tibbalt. They drew swords and engaged solely in the presence of Sir John Screwby, who sat drunk beneath the table. Their combat was interrupted by Mr. John Fielding's people, and your grandfather was carried home to Hill Street wounded in a chair. Let me assure you, those gentlemen in powder and ruffles, who turned the toes of their buckled pumps with such delicate precision, were terrible fellows. Swords were drawn perpetually; bottle after bottle was drained; oaths roared unceasingly from conversations; tavern-drainers and watchmen were pinked and maimed; chairmen were belabored; citizens insulted by reeling pleasure-seekers.

Have you visited Cremorne with proper "vouchers"? Do you recall our great theaters thirty years ago? You were deemed too refined to attend a play. Yet, you have no conception of what the playhouses were, nor what the green boxes held, when Garrick and Mrs. Pritchard performed before them! For the sake of my children, I thank that worthy actor in his retirement who was the first to banish that shame from the stage. No, madam, you are mistaken. I do not boast of superior virtue. I do not claim you are naturally better than your ancestress in her wild, rouged, gambling, flaring, tearing days—not even than poor Polly Fogle, who is currently taken up for shoplifting and would have been hanged a hundred years ago. Only, I am heartily thankful that my temptations are fewer, having quite enough to contend with those of the present century.

Thus, if Harry Warrington rides down to Newmarket for the October meeting and loses or wins his fortune there; if he joins a party at the Shakespeare or Bedford Head; if he dines at White's Ordinary and sits down to macco and lansquenet afterward; if he boxes the watch and appears at the Roundhouse; if he briefly transforms into a wild, dissipated, harum-scarum young Harry Warrington—I, knowing the frailty of human nature, shall not be surprised. Nor, fully aware of my own shortcomings, do I intend to be savage toward my neighbor.

Mr. Sampson, however, was different. In his chapel in Long Acre, he whipped Vice tremendously; he gave Sin no quarter; he out-cursed Blasphemy with superior Anathemas; he knocked Drunkenness down and trampled on the prostrate brute wallowing in the gutter; he dragged out conjugal Infidelity and pounded her with endless stones of rhetoric—and yet, after service, he would come to dinner at the Star and Garter, make a bowl of punch for Harry and his friends at the Bedford Head, join a hand at whist at Mr. Warrington's lodgings or Lord March's, or wherever there was a supper and good company for him.

I often reflect, concerning Mr. Warrington's doings at this period of his arrival in London, that I may have taken my usual degrading and uncharitable view of him—for, you see, I have not uttered a single word of virtuous indignation against his conduct, and if it was not reprehensible, I have certainly judged him most cruelly. Oh, Truthful, Oh Beautiful, Oh Modesty, Oh Benevolence, Oh Pudor, Oh Mores, Oh Blushing Shame, Oh Namby Pamby—each with your respective capital letters to your honored names! Oh Niminy, Oh Piminy! How shall I dare to say that a young man ever was a young man?

No doubt, dear young lady, I am calumniating Mr. Warrington according to my heartless custom. Here is proof, in a letter from the Warrington collection, addressed by Harry to his mother. Within it, there is not a single word suggesting he leads a wild life. Such a letter from an only son to a fond and exemplary parent must be true:

"BOND STREET, LONDON, October 25, 1756.

"HONORED MADAM—I take up my pen to acknowledge your honored favor of July 10 per

Despite Harry Warrington's stubborn resolve to honor the dismal bargain his cousin had wrung from him, let no benevolent soul judge him harshly for lacking genuine pleasure in the arrangement. He would have gladly escaped it were it possible. Likely, the beating inflicted upon poor Will served this exact purpose. Harry probably reasoned thus: "A boxing match between us is bound to ignite a family quarrel. In that storm, Maria will undoubtedly side with her brother. I shall offer no retraction or apology. Then Will may challenge me to decide which of us is the stronger man. Amidst the feud, the agreement will surely dissolve, freeing me at last."

Thus, honest Harry laid his trap and lit the fuse. Yet, when the smoke cleared, no lasting damage emerged save a swollen nose and a bruised eye for William Esmond, who limped for a week. He issued no challenge to his cousin, Harry Warrington; consequently, neither slew the other. Will fell, rose again, and the score stood settled. One wonders how many sensible men would act similarly if they could settle their accounts privately, unseen by the gossips who record every tally.

Maria, however, did not align herself with her family. Upon hearing of the disturbance, she—and my lord—declared openly for her cousin. Lord Castlewood asserted that Will had struck the first blow, provoked by the chaplain's display. It was noted that Will was not uncommonly found quarreling while intoxicated. Mr. Warrington, conversely, displayed a becoming spirit in resenting the insult. It was for Will, not Harry, to beg forgiveness.

Harry refused any apology unless his horse were returned and his wager honored. Though the chronicler cannot unearth the final resolution of this betting dispute from the available papers, it is certain that shortly thereafter the cousins met in the homes of mutual acquaintances, avoiding further physical altercation.

Maria's elder brother had initially been content to see his sister, who had remained unmarried for years, wed any suitable suitor, particularly a gentleman from Virginia. In his eyes, she would simply retire to his wigwam in the forest, where her robe, stained by the brambles and mud of her journey through life, might be quietly disposed of. Naturally, Harry would outlive his elderly bride and console himself, or fail to, as he saw fit.

However, following an interview with Aunt Bernstein upon arriving in London, the lordship altered his stance. He went so far as to attempt dissuading Maria from the match, expressing pity for the young man condemned to a life of misery due to a foolish promise made at twenty-one.

"Mi-ser-y!" cried Maria, bewildered. "Pity?" My lord at Castlewood had deemed it no pity at all. Maria understood the meaning of pity. Her brother had confided in Aunt Bernstein, who had offered funds to break off the union. She grasped the implication behind my lord's words, yet Mr. Warrington was a man of honor, and she trusted him implicitly.

With this decision, my lord departed for White's or whatever haunt he favored. It is probable Maria guessed too accurately the nature of his conversation with Madame Bernstein.

"And so," he mused inwardly, "the end of my virtue is likely that the Mohock falls prey to others, rendering my sparing efforts futile. *Quem deus vult*—what was that schoolmaster's adage? If I do not have him, someone else certainly will. My brother has taken a slice; my dear sister intends to swallow him body and bone. Here I have been at home, respecting his youth and innocence, declining to play beyond the value of a sixpence, acting as his guardian and mentor. Why, I am but a fool fattening a goose for others to devour! Few good actions have I performed in this life, and here is this one benefiting whom?—others. Talk of remorse! By all the fires and furies, the remorse I feel is for things I haven't done and might have! Why did I spare Lucretia? She hated me ever after, and her husband met his predestined fate. Why have I let this lad go? Those of March and the rest, who do not want him, may pluck him! I hold a bad reputation; I am the wicked lord women warn their sons against! Pardi, I am not a penny worse, merely vastly unluckier than my neighbors, and it is only my cursed weakness that has been my greatest enemy!"

Manifestly, in recording a speech a gentleman only thought, the chronicler exaggerates for the patient reader, who holds no obligation to accept such a draft on credulity. Yet, have not Livy, Thucydides, and scores of historians fabricated speeches for their heroes which the latter never uttered? Knowing my Lord Castlewood's character so intimately, how may we not declare what passes within his mind and transcribe his thoughts? A whole pack of wolves hunts this lamb, destined to feast upon him soon, while one hungry old hunter stands by, denied a single cutlet? Who has not admired that noble speech of my Lord Clive, when reproached for his dealings with jaghires, lakhs, gold mohurs, diamonds, and pearls? "Upon my life," said the hero of Plassy, "when I reflect on my opportunities, I am surprised I took so little!"

To recount disagreeable tales of a gentleman until compelled is painful to a sensitive mind. Thus, though I knew from the first page of this history the sort of person my Lord Castlewood was and how he was esteemed, I have withheld much unpleasantness, permitting only the candid reader to perceive that he is a nobleman unlikely to earn our affection. It is true that my Lord March and other gentlemen with whom he associated would have considered betting with Mr. Warrington for his last shilling and keeping the winnings no more scrupulously than picking the bones of a chicken. They would exploit the game, their superior skill, or private knowledge of the horses; insofar, they followed the practices of all gentlemen. But when they played, they played fair; and when they lost, they paid.

Madame Bernstein was reluctant to reveal everything she knew concerning her nephew's family, moved by my lord's forbearance toward Harry upon his first arrival in Europe and pleased with his compliance with her wishes regarding the match. However, in the conversation she shared with her nephew, Lord Castlewood, speaking of Maria's designs on Harry, he voiced his mind with his customary cynicism. He declared himself a fool for sparing a lad whom no sparing could ultimately save from ruin, pointing out Harry's undeniable extravagances, his spendthrift associates, his nights at faro and hazard, and his rides to Newmarket. He asked why he alone should refrain from the young fellow?

In vain did Madame Bernstein plead that Harry was poor. "Bah!" exclaimed Castlewood. "He is heir to a principality which ought to have been his, ours, and might have restored our ruined family." Indeed, Madame Bernstein believed Mr. Warrington's Virginian property far exceeded reality. Were there not money-lenders in town who would advance sums on post-obits? Castlewood knew this to his cost; he had applied during his father's lifetime, and the cursed crew had devoured two-thirds of his miserable income. Speaking with desperate candor and ill humor, he alarmed Madame Bernstein, who resolved to caution him at the first opportunity.

That evening she began to pen a letter to Mr. Warrington. Yet, throughout her life, she had been slow with her pen and disliked its use. "I never knew any good come of writing more than *bonjour* or business," she often remarked. "What is the use of writing ill when so many clever people can do it well? And even then, it were best left alone." Consequently, she dispatched one of her men to Mr. Harry's lodgings, instructing him to visit her the following day for tea, when she intended to warn him.

But the next morning, illness confined her to chambers, preventing Mr. Harry from seeing her. She remained shut in for a couple of days. The following day, a great engagement arose, and the next, Mr. Harry embarked on some expedition of his own. In the whirlwind of London life, how often does a man see his neighbor, a brother his sister, or a schoolfellow his old friend? Many days passed before Mr. Warrington and his aunt finally had that confidential conversation she desired.

She began by mildly scolding him for his extravagance and madcap frolics (though, in truth, she was charmed by both). He replied that young men must be young men, and that it was in dutifully attending to his aunt that he had made the acquaintance with whom he currently resided. With some prelude, she then began to warn him regarding his cousin, Lord Castlewood. To this, he burst into a bitter laugh, stating that the good-natured world had already told him enough about Lord Castlewood. "To say of a man of his lordship's rank, or of any gentleman, 'Don't play with him,' is more than I like to do," continued the lady. "But..."

"Oh, you may say on, aunt!" said Harry, with something akin to an imprecation on his lips.

"And have you played with your cousin already?" asked the young man's worldly old monitress.

"And lost and won, madam!" answered Harry, gallantly. "It doesn't become me to say which. If we have a bout with a neighbor in Virginia, over a bottle, a pack of cards, or a quarrel, we don't go home and tell our mothers.

Young Harry Warrington resided in Bond Street, London, living upon the fat of the land and draining bumpers of the finest vintage. To the fashionable world, he was the epitome of the "Fortunate Youth"—young, wealthy, handsome, and blessed. Society, that fickle mistress, embraced him with open arms. Let us not, dear readers, cry too loudly against the selfishness of the world for favoring the young and beautiful while neglecting the aged and disfigured. If I possess the right to select my acquaintances—preferring the lively, well-dressed gentleman who amuses me at the club to the slouching, ill-washed misanthrope or the ceaselessly prating coxcomb—has not society itself a right to make similar choices? Harry was beloved because he was likable; rich, handsome, jovial, well-born, and brave. With boisterous drinkers, he shared songs and bottles; with sporting gentlemen, he loved every chase on foot or horseback. With ladies, he possessed a modest, blushing timidity that rendered him intriguing; and towards those of humbler station, he was magnificently liberal, ever eager to spare them annoyance. Our Virginian was indeed grand, high, and mighty. Yet, in those eras when rank still held sway, maintaining a distance from inferiors brought no unpopularity to a gentleman. Recall that in those days, the Secretary of State knelt before the King with his despatches, and the Under-Secretary dared not sit in his chief's presence. If I were a Secretary of State, I would find kneeling to present a dispatch a tedious ordeal; if an Under-Secretary, standing while the Right Honorable Benjamins and Edwards scrutinized papers would be unbearable. However, *modus in rebus* dictates certain lines must be drawn. I am merely half-pleased when Bob Bowstreet, whose connection to literature runs through Policemen X and Y, and Tom Garbage, an esteemed contributor to the *Kennel Miscellany*, propose fellowship as brothers-in-arms, slap me on the back, and address me as "old boy" or by my Christian name.

During the winter season of 1756–57, the town offered Mr. Warrington every conceivable pleasure, provided he asked. There were operas, though he derived but moderate delight from them amidst the prodigious satire assailing them as foolish, popish, unmanly, and meaningless. There were theaters hosting Mr. Garrick and Mrs. Pritchard at one house, and Mrs. Clive at another. Masquerades and ridottos frequented by the elite, along with private assemblies of lords and ladies beginning and ending with cards, were all within reach. However, Mr. Warrington preferred White's Club above all, finding the play there neither so high nor so fair as elsewhere.

One day, his kinsman, Lord Castlewood, escorted him to court, presenting Harry to His Majesty, who had recently arrived from Kensington. Yet, the gracious sovereign seemed disinclined toward Harry's introducer, perhaps harboring other reasons for his sulkiness. "Oh, heard of you from Lady Yarmouth," the King remarked, addressing Lord Castlewood in German, "the Earl of Castlewood shall tell him that he plays too much!" And with that, the Defender of the Faith turned his royal back.

Lord Castlewood recoiled, visibly frightened by this icy reception.
"What does he say?" asked Harry.
"His Majesty thinks they play too high at White's, and is displeased," the nobleman whispered.
"If he does not want us, we had better not return, that is all," said Harry simply. "I never, somehow, considered that German fellow a real King of England."
"Hush! For Heaven's sake, hold your confounded colonial tongue!" cried the lord, panic rising. "Do you not see the walls here have ears?"
"And what then?" asked Mr. Warrington, bewildered. "Why, look at the people! Hang me, if it is not quite a curiosity! They were all shaking hands with me, bowing, flattering me just now; and at present, they avoid me as if I were the plague!"

Then, a broad-faced, broad-shouldered gentleman in a scarlet-laced waistcoat and an old-fashioned wig stepped forward. "Shake hands, nephew," he declared. "I heard what you said. I have ears like the wall, look you. And, now, if other people show you the cold shoulder, I'll give you my hand;" and so saying, the gentleman extended a great brown hand, grasping Harry's firmly. "Something of my brother about your eyes and face. Though I suppose in your island you grow more wiry and thin. I am thine uncle, child. My name is Sir Miles Warrington. My lord knows me well enough."

"My lord looked very frightened and yellow," observed the narrative voice. "Yes, my dear Harry. This is your paternal uncle, Sir Miles Warrington."
"Might as well have come to see us in Norfolk, as dangle about playing the fool at Tunbridge Wells, Mr. Warrington, or Mr. Esmond—which do you call yourself?" the Baronet chided. "The old lady calls herself Madam Esmond, doesn't she?"
"My mother is not ashamed of her father's name, nor am I, uncle," said Mr. Harry, rather proudly.
"Well said, lad! Come home and eat a bit of mutton with Lady Warrington at three, in Hill Street—that is, if you can do without your White's kickshaws. You need not look frightened, my Lord Castlewood! I shall tell no tales out of school."
"I—I am sure Sir Miles Warrington will act as a gentleman!" stammered the lord, in much perturbation.
"Belike, he will," growled the Baronet, turning on his heel. "And thou wilt come, young man, at three; and mind, good roast mutton waits for nobody. Thou hast a great look of thy father. Lord bless us, how we used to beat each other! He was smaller than me, and in course younger; but many a time he had the best of it. Take it he was henpecked when he married, and Madam Esmond took the spirit out of him when she got him in her island. Virginia is an island. Ain't it an island?"
Harry laughed and said, "No!" And the jolly Baronet, departing, added, "Well, island or not, thou must come and tell all about it to my lady. She'll know whether 'tis an island or not."

"My dear Mr. Warrington," said the lord, with an appealing look, "I need not tell you that, in this great city, every man has enemies, and that there is a great, great deal of detraction and scandal. I never spoke to you about Sir Miles Warrington, precisely because I did know him, and because we have had differences together. Should he permit himself remarks to my disparagement, you will receive them *cum grano salis*, and remember that it is from an enemy they come." And the pair walked out of the King's apartments into Saint James's Street. Harry found the news of his cold reception at court had already preceded him to White's. The King had turned his back upon him; the King was jealous of Harry's favour with the favourite; Harry was *au mieux* with Lady Yarmouth. A score of gentlemen wished to compliment him upon his conquest. Before night, it was a settled matter that this was amongst the other victories of the Fortunate Youth.

Sir Miles related these matters to his wife and Harry when the young man appeared at the appointed hour at the Baronet's dinner-table, rallying him in his simple, rustic fashion. The lady, initially a grand and stately personage, admitted to Harry that the reputation the world had forged for him was so malign that, at first, she had given him but a frigid welcome. With the young ladies, Sir Miles's daughters, greetings consisted of "How d'ye do, cousin?" and "No, thank you, cousin," accompanied by numerous prim curtseys to the Virginian as they greeted and took leave of him. The little boy, the heir of the house, dined at table under the care of his governor; having his glass of port by Papa after dinner, he gave loose rein to his innocent tongue and asked many questions of his cousin. Finally, the innocent youth, gazing intently at Harry, asked, "Are you wicked, cousin Harry? You don't look very wicked!"
"My dear Master Miles!" the tutor protested, turning very red.
"But you know you said he was wicked!" cried the child.
"We are all miserable sinners, Miley," explained Papa. "Haven't you heard the clergyman say so every Sunday?"
"Yes, but not so very wicked as cousin Harry. Is it true that you gamble, cousin, and drink all night with wicked men, and frequent the company of wicked women? You know you said so, Mr. Walker—and Mamma said so, too, that Lady Yarmouth was a wicked woman."
"And you are a little pitcher," cried Papa. "And my wife, nephew Harry, is a staunch Jacobite—you won't like her the worse for that. Take Miles to his sisters, Mr. Walker, and Topsham shall give thee a ride in the park, child, on thy little horse." The idea of the little horse consoled Master Miles; for, when

Upon the rejection of his peace offerings, our warlike young American chief chose to be in great wrath—not merely against Colonel Lambert, but against the entire lineage of that gentleman. "He has humiliated me before the girls!" thought the young man, his brow furrowed beneath his wig. "He and Mr. Wolfe, who were forever preaching morality to me and affecting airs of superior protection, have again held me up to the family as a scapegrace and prodigal. They are so virtuous they will not even shake hands with me, forsooth; and when I seek to show them a little common gratitude, they fling my presents in my face!"

"Why, sir, the things must be worth a little fortune!" observed Parson Sampson, casting an eye of covetousness upon the two morocco boxes wherein rested Mr. Sparks's golden gewgaws on their white satin cushions.

"They cost some money, Sampson," replied the young man. "Not that I would grudge ten times the amount to people who have been kind to me."

"No, faith, sir, not if I know your honour!" interjected Sampson, seizing every opportunity to praise his young patron. "The repeater, they told me, was a great bargain, and worth a hundred pounds at Paris. Little Miss Hetty herself said she longed to have a repeating watch."

"Oh, what a love!" cried the chaplain, his eyes shining. "'Tis adorned with a little circle of pearls on the back, and a diamond knob for the handle! Why, 'twould win any woman's heart, Sir!"

"There passes an apple-woman with a basket. I have a mind to fling the thing out to her!" cried Mr. Warrington, fiercely.

When Harry ventured out upon business, which took him to the City and the Temple, his parasite did not follow him far into the Strand, turning away with a terror of Chancery Lane, its inhabitants, and precincts. Mr. Warrington proceeded to his broker, and they walked to the Bank, where they conducted some little business. After the signing of a trifling signature or two, Harry departed with a certain number of crisp bank-notes in his pocket. The broker escorted Mr. Warrington to one of the great dining-houses for which the City was famous; and afterwards showed him the Virginian Walk upon 'Change, through which Harry passed rather shamefacedly. *What would a certain lady in Virginia say,* he thought, *if she knew that I was carrying off in that bottomless gambler's pocket a great portion of my father's patrimony?* Those are all Virginia merchants, he reasoned, and they are all talking to one another about me, saying, "That is young Esmond, of Castlewood, on the Potomac, Madam Esmond's son; and he has been losing his money at play, and he has been selling out so much, and so much, and so much."

His spirits did not rise until he had passed under the Traitors' Heads of Temple Bar and was fairly out of the City. From the Strand, Mr. Harry walked home, looking in at St. James's Street by the way, but there was nobody there as yet, the company not coming to the Chocolate-House till a later hour. Arrived at home, Mr. Harry drew out his bundle of bank-notes, placed three of them into a sheet of paper which he sealed carefully, having previously inscribed within the sheet the words, "Much good may they do you. H. E. W." Directing this packet to the Reverend Mr. Sampson, he left it on the chimney-glass, instructing his servants to deliver it to that divine when he should come in.

Now his honour's phaeton was brought to the door, and he stepped in, intending to drive round the park; but the rain falling, or the east wind blowing, or some other cause arising, his honour turned his horses' heads down St. James's Street and was back at White's about three o'clock. Scarcely anybody had come in yet. It was the hour when folks were at dinner. However, there sat my cousin Castlewood, lounging over the Public Advertiser, having just come off from his duty at Court hard by. Lord Castlewood yawned over the newspaper. What shall they do? Shall they have a little piquet? Harry had no objection to a little piquet. "Just for an hour," said Lord Castlewood. "I dine at Arlington Street at four." "Just for an hour," said Mr. Warrington, and they called for cards. "Or shall we have 'em in upstairs?" said my lord. "Out of the noise?" "Certainly, out of the noise," said Harry.

At five o'clock, a half-dozen gentlemen had come in after their dinner, and were at cards, or coffee, or talk. The folks from the ordinary had not left the table yet. There the gentlemen of White's would often sit till past midnight. One toothpick pointed over the coffee-house blinds into the street. "Whose phaeton?" asked Toothpick 1 of Toothpick 2. "The Fortunate Youth's," said No. 2. "Not so fortunate the last three nights. Luck confoundedly against him. Lost, last night, thirteen hundred to the table. Has Mr. Warrington been here to-day, John?" "Mr. Warrington is in the house now, sir. In the little tea-room with Lord Castlewood since three o'clock. They are playing at piquet," said John. "What fun for Castlewood!" said No. 1, with a shrug. The second gentleman growled out an execration. "Curse the fellow!" he said. "He has no right to be in this club at all. He doesn't pay if he loses. Gentlemen ought not to play with him. Sir Miles Warrington told me at court the other day that Castlewood has owed him money on a bet these three years." "Castlewood," said No. 1, "doesn't lose if he plays alone. A large company flurries him, you see—that's why he doesn't come to the table." And the facetious gentleman grinned, showing all his teeth, polished perfectly clean. "Let's go up and stop 'em," growled No. 2. "Why?" asked the other. "Much better look out the window. A lamplighter is going up the ladder—a famous sport. Look at that old putt in the chair: did you ever see such an old quiz?" "Who is that just gone out of the house?" "As I live, it's Fortunatus! He seems to have forgotten that his phaeton has been here, waiting all the time. I bet you two to one he has been losing to Castlewood." "Jack, do you take me to be a fool?" asked the one gentleman of the other. "Pretty pair of horses the youth has got. How he is flogging 'em!" And they saw Mr. Warrington galloping up the street, scared coachmen and chairmen clearing before him; presently my Lord Castlewood was seen to enter a chair and go his way.

Harry drove up to his own door. It was but a few yards, and those poor horses had been beating the pavement all this while in the rain. Mr. Gumbo was engaged at the door in conversation with a country-looking lass, who tripped off with a curtsey. Mr. Gumbo was always engaged with some pretty maid or other. "Gumbo, has Mr. Sampson been here?" asked Gumbo's master from his driving-seat. "No, sar. Mr. Sampson have not been here!" answered Mr. Warrington's gentleman. Harry bade him go upstairs and bring down a letter addressed to Mr. Sampson. "Addressed to Mr. Sampson? Oh yes, sir," said Mr. Gumbo, who couldn't read. "A sealed letter, stupid! On the mantelpiece, in the glass!" said Harry; and Gumbo leisurely retired to fetch that document. As soon as Harry had it, he turned his horses' heads towards St. James's Street, and the two gentlemen, still yawning out of the window at White's, beheld the Fortunate Youth, in an instant, back again.

As they passed out of the little tea-room where he and Lord Castlewood had had their piquet together, Mr. Warrington had seen that several gentlemen had entered the play-room, and that there was a bank there. Some were already steadily at work, and had their gaming jackets on: they kept such coats at the club, which they put on when they had a mind to sit down to a regular night's play. Mr. Warrington went to the clerk's desk, paid his account of the previous night, and, sitting down at the table, called for fresh counters. This had been decidedly an unlucky week with the Fortunate Youth, and tonight was no more fortunate than previous nights. He called for more counters, and more presently. He was a little pale and silent, though very easy and polite when talked to. But he could not win. At last he got up. "Hang it! Stay and mend your luck!" said Lord March, who was sitting by his side with a heap of counters before him, green and white. "Take a hundred of mine, and go on!" "I have had enough for to-night, my lord," said Harry, and rising, he went away, ate a broiled bone in the coffee-room, and walked back to his lodgings

There is a certain class of human beings, often satirized in fiction, whose existence I confess I have rarely encountered in real life: the genuine religious hypocrite. These are individuals who preach incessantly without believing a single word of their own sermons—men who expound scripture, offer blessings, and deliver warnings with a solemnity that masks a complete lack of faith in either heaven or hell. Consider the pious crowds that flock to the churches on Sunday evenings: the maid-servants trailing ribbons, following the young apprentices; the schools of boys in their best coats; the neat maids and staid matrons marching with glistening prayer-books as the chapel bell rings. Are they truly devout? Likely not. The maid servant probably thinks of her sweetheart; the grocer calculates how to dispose of his sugar stock and whether the County Bank will accept his notes; the schoolboy memorizes Latin verses for the coming exercise; the young rake remembers the father's exposition at home but anticipates pie for supper; the clerk calling out the psalm has a troubled daughter and drones through his responses with a mind elsewhere; and the moment the parson hides his face on his cushion, he is likely fretting over a bill due on Monday. These people are not heavenly-minded. They remain firmly rooted in the world, treating religion merely as a convenient mental lockbox—a remedy to be dispensed when physical ailments arise. "My dear madam, you suffer from spasms? Try these drops!" "Have you been drinking too freely, sir? This pill will safeguard you against excess!" Who are more enthusiastic dispensers of spiritual and bodily physic than women? Indeed, a century past, every lady in the countryside maintained a still-room stocked with pills, powders, and potions for the entire village.

Lady Warrington assumed the role of physician to the consciences and digestions of her husband's tenants and household. She held the faith and health of the servants' hall in her keeping. Whether she possessed the divine wisdom to heal them effectively remains a matter for Heaven alone to judge; however, whether offering a pill or a doctrine, she administered them with equal conviction in her authority, and her disciples swallowed them obediently.
