She regarded herself as one of the most virtuous, self-denying, wise, and learned women in creation, and by constantly repeating this sentiment, she successfully persuaded many around her to adopt her views.

At Sir Miles's dinner, the sideboard displayed such an array of plate, and the livery-clad staff numbered so greatly, that it required a sharp mind to discern that the beer poured into the splendid tankards was of the poorest quality and that the grand silver dish held but a solitary joint of mutton. Yet, when Sir Miles toasted the King's health and smacked his jolly lips over his wine, he viewed the liquid as ambrosia. He turned to Harry Warrington and inquired if they drank such port in Virginia. Asserting that the wine Harry tasted in Norfolk was inferior, Sir Miles extolled the drink until Harry nearly believed it was exquisite, winking into his own glass as if trying to perceive the virtues his uncle so clearly admired in the ruby nectar.

Like many well-regulated families of this era, the Warringtons boasted two paragons among their grown daughters. One, the eldest, was the greatest beauty, recently engaged to Tom Claypool, a fact Lady Warrington shared with Harry in confidence. The second daughter, however, was the genius and angel of any young lady alive, according to her mother. Yet, despite her celestial reputation, she seemed determined to improve Harry's wits and morals. She sang to him at the harpsichord—a performance Harry found slightly out of tune for an angel—and offered endless advice, instruction, and conversation, perhaps with too much of the latter. Harry, preferring the company of a younger cousin who reminded him of Fanny Mountain at home, found himself somewhat weary of the constant tutelage. Nevertheless, after dinner, while Beauty departed on her own pursuits, the mother attended to her correspondence or the needs of the poor, and the father dozed in his armchair, the "Genius" remained to keep company with her young cousin.

A strange calm pervaded the house, a quiet sanctuary from the riot and dissipation Harry usually inhabited. The welcome he received was unqualified. Doors stood open to him at all hours. If Flora was absent, Dora was always ready to receive him. Within days of their acquaintance, Harry and his little cousin Miles had ridden together in the Park. Generous to every soul who crossed his path, Harry had even contemplated purchasing a superior horse for Miles, far better than the steed the boy currently rode, though circumstances later forced a sudden breakdown of Harry's own carriage and horses.

Although Sir Miles Warrington seemed to imagine Virginia to be an island, the ladies were far better grounded in geography and eagerly listened to Harry's accounts of his homeland. He, in turn, relished the opportunity to describe the vast extent of his estate, the rivers that bordered it, and its abundant produce. Drawing upon a childhood practice of surveying with a friend, Harry produced a map of his county, marking towns that were, in reality, little more than log-huts (though he wished, for the honor of his country, that they appeared more stately). There stood the Potomac and the James River; there were the wharves where his mother's ships and cargoes of tobacco bound for the sea. In truth, his estate was as large as an English county. He spoke of it without excessive boasting, yet the sight of the handsome young fellow, dressed in velvet and silver lace, drawing maps and pointing out hills and forests, conjured the image of a traveling prince describing realms to a royal mother. For fleeting moments, he almost fancied himself one. With miles of land where English gentlemen possessed mere acres, he captivated everyone. Not only did Dora listen intently, but the beautiful Flora also bowed her fair head in attention. Compared to young Tom Claypool, the brother baronet's son in Norfolk, with his massive boots, booming voice, and inheritance of merely five thousand acres, what chance did the "poor" heir have against this young American prince and charming stranger? So enchanted was Dora by Harry's sophistication that she began to view Flora as a mere coquette. Claypool, in his red waistcoat, sat silent before the splendid Harry in his ruffles and laces, listening as Harry discoursed on March, Chesterfield, Selwyn, and Bolingbroke—the entire galaxy of macaronis. Consequently, the mother grew to love Harry more and more, viewing him almost as a son. Her thoughts drifted anxiously to the spiritual welfare of the poor Indians and the enslaved in Virginia. What aid could she provide to dear Madam Esmond, a woman of precious virtue? She noted the serious demeanor of her butler and housekeeper, delighted as they were by the spiritual behavior and sweet musical gifts of Gumbo.

"Oh! Harry, Harry! You have been a sad wild boy!" the mother exclaimed. "Why did you not come to us sooner, sir, and avoid wasting your time among spendthrifts and the vain world? But 'tis not yet too late. We must reclaim thee, dear Harry! Mustn't we, Sir Miles? Mustn't we, Dora? Mustn't we, Flora?"

The three ladies gazed upward toward the ceiling. They resolved to reclaim the prodigal son. It became a contest of sorts: which sister would succeed most? Dora sat by, observing Flora. As for the mother, whenever the girls were away, she addressed Harry with increasing seriousness and tenderness. Determined to fill the void left by his absent parents, she vowed to be a mother to him. She presented him with a hymn-book, kissed his forehead, and acted from the purest love, tenderness, and religious regard toward her dear, wayward, wild, yet amiable nephew.

While these sentimentalities unfolded, Mr. Warrington kept his counsel regarding his disastrous affairs, which had reached a point of extreme ruin. After weeks favored by fortune, he had been abruptly abandoned by her. A few days sufficed to dismantle the heap of winnings he had amassed. Did Lord Castlewood, his own kinsman, deal unfairly with the young Virginian, robbing him through clandestine sessions? We need not insinuate disrespect for his lordship's character; nevertheless, Castlewood had won every shilling properly belonging to Harry and would have gambled for his reversions were it not for the young man's refusal to continue the battle once hopelessly defeated. Remembering a small reserve set aside for the poor clergyman, Sampson, Harry ventured it at the gaming table. That last resource sank with the rest of his possessions, and Fortune fled the storm, leaving the luckless adventurer almost naked on the shore.

A young man who is generous and hearty is seldom deeply afflicted by the loss of money. Harry would sell his horses and carriages, curtail his train, and live simply. If immediate funds were needed, surely his Aunt Bernstein would act as his banker, or his kinsman who had won so much from him, or his kind Uncle Warrington and Lady Warrington, who constantly preached virtue and benevolence, declaring their love for him as a son? He could call upon them at his leisure. Meanwhile, Sampson's tale of his landlord's distress moved the young gentleman, prompting him to raise a hasty sum for the clergyman by carrying all his trinkets to a pawnbroker's shop in St. Martin's Lane.

It so happened that this broker was a relative or partner of Mr. Sparks of Tavistock Street, from whom Harry had ostensibly purchased

A deep sense of remorse for his past follies mingled with a sharp wrath at the indignity of arrest as Harry found himself a prisoner in that dismal lock-up. Yet, beneath the present humiliation, he clung to the certainty that his confinement was merely momentary. In his mind, he counted twenty friends capable of securing his release: Mr. Draper, the obsequious businessman; his kind uncle, the Baronet, who had offered his home; Cousin Castlewood, his gaming companion; the noble patrons of the Chocolate House; and his good Aunt Bernstein. To which of these allies should he first appeal? The paramount concern, however, was discretion. Mr. Warrington, a man of prodigious self-importance, dreaded the vulgarity of a public arrest, fearing the scandal it would bring to his reputation. He reasoned, with a cynical detachment, that the world eventually learns everything it wishes to know, yet remains ultimately indifferent. "Psha!" he thought, dismissing the notion of disgrace. "Your shame is only vanity. Appear with a smiling countenance, and nobody cares." While Mr. Warrington chafed as if the entire globe were buzzing with his downfall, he resolved to handle the crisis quietly, avoiding the scrutiny of his relations or the nobles at White's. Instead, he dispatched a messenger at dawn to his friend, the Reverend Mr. Sampson, seeking the counsel and consolation of a man who understood his precarious position.

Unbeknownst to Harry, sending for Sampson was akin to inviting a lion into a den. The chaplain, still buoyed by recent favors from his patron, likely assumed Harry's troubles were trivial compared to his own. Upon delivering the message, Harry's spirits lifted slightly, and he requested breakfast. When the attendant inquired if he wished to dine in-house or join Mrs. Bailiff's table, Harry declined, insisting on departing before dinner time. The chamberlain, skeptical of the youth's resolve, closed the door, leaving Harry to his solitary meditations.

Before long, Sampson's messenger returned with a note stating the reverend would arrive shortly. However, the clock ticked past ten, eleven, and noon without his arrival. Just as despair threatened to overwhelm Harry, Gumbo—the faithful black servant—burst into the room, carrying a portmanteau and weeping bitterly. With vows of undying loyalty, Gumbo declared his readiness to sell himself into slavery again to save his beloved master. Harry, touched by the boy's devotion, rose and embraced him, ordering him strictly to remain silent about the arrest. Gumbo, however, was a creature of the slave trade, bound by a nature that thrived on subterfuge and gossip. Though he swore solemnly not to utter a word, his tongue betrayed him instantly. Having already spread the news of his master's captivity to the footmen's club, among the servants of Colonel Lambert and Lord Wrotham, and even to Sir Miles Warrington's household, Gumbo could not contain his joy at fulfilling his duty as a gossip. By the time he faced his master's stern gaze, promising silence, the rumor had already circulated widely among the gentry.

Hours dragged slowly, and Sampson failed to materialize. Finally, around midday, a letter arrived, sealed with a moist wafer and ink barely dry. It revealed that the chaplain, hastening to assist, had been seized by two villains holding a writ against him and imprisoned in a lock-up three doors away. Thus, both master and servant sat in chains, separated by a thin wall yet united in misery. The letter ended abruptly, refusing to detail the specific reasons for Harry's downfall, a mystery the reader would soon unravel.

While Sampson remained silent due to his own entanglements, Gumbo's loquacious nature ensured the news traveled swiftly. He had already boasted to the footmen's club and regaled the servants of the highest nobility with the tale of his master's arrest. Back at Long Acre, the divine learned of Harry's plight only after receiving the letter and the messenger's report. Eager to aid his patron, Sampson rushed to Lord Castlewood's residence, pleading passionately for assistance. He spoke of Mr. Warrington's generosity and the vast sums won from his cousin, hoping to stir the nobleman's conscience. Yet, Lord Castlewood cut him short with peevish curtitude. "Did I not tell you two days ago," the lord sneered, "that I was as poor as a beggar?" He claimed the winnings from Harry had been swallowed by other debts, and ironically, he had just written to Harry asking *him* for financial help—a letter which duly reached Harry's cell.

"My Lord!" Sampson cried, desperate. "I would pawn my cassock and bands for him!"
"Amen," retorted the lord, returning to his papers as Sampson departed in deepest despondency.

Meanwhile, Lady Maria Esmond, overhearing the chaplain's fruitless plea, intercepted him. She, too, had heard the dreadful news from the opera box. "Tell me, what is this has happened to Mr. Warrington?" she demanded, drawing Sampson into the dining room. When Sampson revealed Lord Castlewood's cruel refusal, despite having won hundreds from Harry, Lady Maria's composure shattered. She launched into a furious tirade against her own family, accusing them of a lack of honor, courage, and common feeling. She shrieked at her brother, accusing him of "pigeoning" Harry for his last shilling, and stormed toward the library, her voice ringing with righteous indignation. Lord Castlewood, initially surprised by her vehemence, quickly adopted a rational tone, arguing that Harry's money was rightfully spent on his debts and that the loss was a "bagatelle" compared to the principality Maria sought in Virginia. He dismissed her moral outrage as the natural folly of her sex, reducing her fiery defense to a gentle submission as he coaxed her down from her pedestal.

Back at Clarges Street, Madame de Bernstein, though usually unmoved by her nephew's escapades, was deeply shaken by Gumbo's report. Her sleepless night gave way to action. She summoned Mr. Draper, her man of business, to investigate Harry's debts. Draper discovered that Harry had been detained for various sums owed to a jeweller, a landlord, and others. Draper, acting with swift efficiency, arranged to settle these accounts, noting that a gentleman of Harry's rank required no further complications. However, the Baroness harbored a deeper motive. She recalled a minor circumstance from Tunbridge Wells involving a lawyer, a secret she entrusted to Draper, instructing him to ensure Harry emerged from prison "clear" of liabilities, specifically referencing a debt he ought to be released from.

Desperate and alone, Harry finally applied to Mr. Draper, writing a letter that explicitly forbade mentioning the contents to his aunt, Madame de Bernstein. He hoped to avoid the humiliation of petitioning her, preferring to seek aid from his male friends. First, he wrote to his cousin, the Earl of Castlewood, begging for a loan to cover his debts. The reply was cold and dismissive; Castlewood, claiming he was as destitute as Harry, mentioned that Harry's money had already satisfied his creditors. Next, Harry turned to his uncle, Sir Miles Warrington, composing a penitent letter exposing his sins as a prodigal son. The response, delivered by Gumbo, was equally disappointing. It contained a sermon by Mr. Whitfield and a letter from Lady Margaret, who, having opened her brother's mail in his absence, learned of Harry's extravagance. She offered heartfelt prayers and scriptural comfort but no money, gently critiquing his orthography.

Defeated, Harry sat staring into the candlelight, his face hidden in his hands. The bailiff's servant, seeing his distress, offered him beer and food, but Harry had no coin. Gumbo, however, produced a bundle of gold coins—five guineas each—from his own pockets, along with gold moidores and keepsakes from the girls. "Take everything," Gumbo sobbed, "I will sell myself tomorrow morning, but here is plenty for tonight." Overcome with emotion, Harry accepted the gift, breaking his fast with a relish that the jailors watched with approval. As Gumbo departed, shaking hands with the staff, the loyal bond between master and servant stood firm, a stark contrast to the treachery of the white world outside.

The gentle, generous nature of Mr. Gumbo gradually soothed the storm within his master's soul. By the time the sun rose on Harry Warrington's second night in the spunging-house, the raw edges of his fury had dulled, though the core remained iron-hard. While he accepted the kindness offered by Gumbo, Harry's spirit remained locked against the rest of the world. To him, the English were a breed of self-serving, unfeeling creatures. His pious Aunt Warrington, the haughty March, and the cynical Castlewood—all had failed him. He would not stoop to beg another favor from them, nor would he forgive their broken promises. There was no loyalty in this frozen, selfish nation. His resolve hardened: he would depart, trusting no Englishman, however high-born. Whether he marched with the King in Germany or returned to Virginia to live among the trees, hunt, manage his estate, and wed either Polly Broadbent or Fanny Mountain, he would choose any path over remaining amidst these "fine gentlemen." Thus, he rose with a mask of cheerfulness covering a vengeful heart.

Early in the morning, Gumbo appeared, having traveled from Bond Street. "I sought to bring more garments, sir," the faithful servant explained, "but the landlord, Mr. Ruff, forbade it."

Harry showed no interest in the clothing. Instead, he tore open a stack of envelopes, revealing a mountain of debts. The final letter, from the landlord, was a blunt ultimatum: no more of Mr. Warrington's effects would leave the house until the bill was settled, at which point the landlord would seize the goods and evict the black man. Harry hesitated to let Gumbo remove his own clothes and trunk. However, Gumbo revealed he had already secured shelter at Lord Wrotham's residence. "Colonel Lambert's household," Gumbo noted, glancing sharply at his master. "Indeed, when they learned you were taken, Miss Hetty swooned. Mr. Lambert, a kind soul, instructed me to tell you: if you need him, send word, and he shall come."

A flicker of gratitude touched Harry at the mention of Hetty's distress, though he dismissed Gumbo's account of her fainting as typical exaggeration. Yet, the mention of Colonel Lambert reignited the darkness in his eyes. *Send word to Lambert,* Harry ground out internally, *the man who spat in my face and rejected my gifts? Even if I starved, I would not accept a crust from him!*

Dressed and having eaten a scant breakfast, Harry dispatched Gumbo with a brief, peremptory note to Mr. Draper of the Temple, summoning the attorney immediately.

"The note read like a decree issued to a serf, not a summons to a freeborn English gentleman," Draper observed later, though he had always harbored a deep resentment for Harry's habitual condescension. "For a man in a debtor's prison to act so!" Draper muttered, shaking his head. "Though, hang him, I had a strong inclination to refuse the appointment." Despite his complaints, Draper obeyed. He found Mr. Warrington seated upon his bed, draped in a magnificent brocaded gown, his hair meticulously coiffed, treating his situation with the arrogance of a sovereign. He motioned for Gumbo to retrieve a chair for the lawyer.

"Madam, I confess, I am unaccustomed to such hauteur and airs!" the outraged attorney stammered, addressing the scene through the lens of the narrator's omniscience, who amusedly noted that the Baroness, observing the exchange, delighted in her nephew's insolence in adversity.

The strategy for this meeting had been plotted by the Baroness and Draper the previous day. Draper, usually a capable negotiator, faltered today, blinded by his own irritation and a fundamental inability to comprehend the young Virginian's psyche. As the narrator gently reminds the reader, those who lack gentility often wound others unintentionally, provoking scorn that they cannot decipher, leading to a cycle of rage and misunderstanding.

"Gumbo, bring a chair for Mr. Draper!" commanded Mr. Warrington, wrapping his gown around his legs as he sat upon the dingy mattress. "Sit, if you please, and let us discuss my affairs. I am grateful for your swift response. Have you heard of this calamity?"

Draper, aware of the rumors, replied gravely, "News travels swiftly, sir. I hastened to offer my humble services the moment you required them. Surely, your friends and family are distressed that a gentleman of your standing finds himself in such straits."

"I have been imprudent, Mr. Draper," Harry admitted, bowing slightly. "I lived beyond my means. I gambled with men vastly wealthier than myself, and a string of bad luck has drained my ready funds, leaving me liable for five hundred pounds."

"Five hundred pounds currently outstanding," Draper noted, pulling a long, grave face.

"Why, sir, it is a trifling sum!" Harry exclaimed, staring intently at the lawyer. "I believed that by appealing to one or two friends yesterday, I might settle the debt and return home. I was mistaken. Pray, assist me in raising the necessary funds forthwith."

"Hm!" Draper hummed, maintaining his somber expression. "Why, sir, it *can* be done!" Harry insisted.

In truth, Draper had previously proposed to the Baroness to discharge the debt instantly, securing Harry's release. She had declared her intention to make him her heir, and Draper, like everyone else, assumed Harry's Virginia inheritance was vast. Yet, despite holding the relevant notes and the Baroness's order, the lawyer stalled. "To access the property in England belonging to Mr. George Warrington, we must prove the succession and release the trustees," Draper explained, his tone heavy with bureaucratic obstruction. "Lady Esmond Warrington, your mother-in-law, will not permit her son to remain in prison, yet we must await correspondence from Virginia, a process taking six months. Furthermore, does your agent in Bristol possess authority to honor your drafts?"

"He is authorized only for two hundred pounds annually," Harry stated flatly. "I appear to have no recourse but to appeal to my aunt, Madame de Bernstein. She will stand as my security."

"My lady will do anything for you, sir," Draper assured him, recalling her frequent assurances. "If she gives the word, you may walk free from this place."

"Then proceed to her, Mr. Draper," Harry urged, his voice trembling slightly as he wiped his eyes. "Do not disguise the truth. Tell her where I am and what has happened. Inform her that I entrust myself to her affection and kindness to liberate me from this... this disgrace."

"Sir," Draper interjected, fixing his gaze on the youth, "I was with her ladyship yesterday when we discussed this entire, most unpleasant affair—I hesitate to use your term, 'disgrace.'"

"What do you mean, sir?" Harry asked, his cheeks flushing. "Does Madame de Bernstein know of my misfortune?"

"Every detail, sir," Draper confirmed. "The pawning of the watches, and all."

Harry turned a burning crimson. "It is an unfortunate business," the lawyer continued smoothly, "to pledge timepieces and other items for which you had never paid." Harry sprang from the bed, his eyes fierce enough to alarm the seasoned attorney.

"It may lead to litigation and unpleasant remarks in court, sir. Those barristers respect nothing, and when they secure a defendant in the box—"

"Good heavens, sir, do you truly suppose a gentleman of my rank cannot borrow a watch on credit without intending to defraud the tradesman?" Harry cried, agitated.

"Naturally, you meant every word honorably," Draper winked, masking his triumph. "However, the law may not share your sentiment. Your aunt describes this as the most imprudent action she has ever witnessed—to call it anything less would be unwise."

"Do you describe it otherwise yourself, Mr. Draper?" Harry asked, speaking slowly, striving to maintain composure.

Draper bristled. "Heaven forbid I speak thus between gentlemen—but as between a lawyer and his client, it is my duty to state plainly: 'Sir, you are in a very unpleasant scrape,' just as a physician must tell a patient, 'Sir, you are very ill.'"

"And you cannot assist me in paying this debt? You have come merely to accuse me of potential roguery?"

"Obtaining goods under false pretenses? Undoubtedly, yes. I cannot alter facts, sir. Do not look as though you intend to strike me. A young gentleman, receiving only two hundred pounds a year from his guardian, ordering diamonds and watches, and subsequently pledging them..." Draper paused, letting the weight of the accusation sink in. "What will the public think? I speak honestly. Do not be offended, Mr. Warrington."

"Proceed, sir!" Harry groaned.

Draper felt the momentum shift entirely to his advantage. "Yet, you asked if I could assist in settling this debt? And I say yes—and here lies the money in my pocket to effect payment immediately, if you consent—not my own funds, sir, but those of my honored client, your aunt, Lady Bernstein. She possesses the right to impose conditions, and I have brought them."

"State them, sir," Harry commanded.

"They are not harsh, designed solely for your benefit. If you agree, we

Driven from the room by the heat of Mr. Draper's morning reproof, Harry lingered near the threshold, his pride wounded and his spirit heavy. He had intended to call the lawyer back, to plead for a reprieve, but the shame of yielding to necessity—the crushing weight of his poverty—stuck in his throat. To humble himself before a man of law, to beg for delay, felt like a betrayal of every principle he held. "An hour hence will serve," he muttered, turning away with a sullen resignation. He collapsed onto the bed, burying his face in the pillows where bitter tears soon soaked the linen.

In the quiet torment of his isolation, a fresh anguish took root. He despised the marriage arrangement with Maria Esmond. It was not love, but a trap, a cunning maneuver executed by a sister who exploited his boyish innocence as easily as her brother had cheated him at cards. They were his kin, yet they had turned against him, sacrificing the rights of hospitality to feed their own greed. They had made a victim of the trusting youth who sought shelter within their walls. His heart ached with a sorrow so deep it brought him to his knees, his mind drifting to a fantasy of what might have been. *Had they crossed the Atlantic,* he thought, *to Virginia, I would have welcomed them as brothers.*

The somber silence was shattered by the creak of the door. Gumbo stood there, his grin wide and unfeeling, announcing the arrival of a lady. Behind the servant stepped the woman in the capuchin cloak—a vision of sorrow and desperation.

Harry sat upright, his countenance drained of color, his frame haggard. As Gumbo retreated, the lady rushed forward, her breath coming in ragged gasps. With a sob that tore at her throat, she threw her arms around his neck, clutching him with a maternal fervor that transcended mere duty. She buried her face in his shoulder, weeping openly, her lips pressing hot kisses against his pale cheek.

"Oh, my Harry!" she cried, her voice breaking. "Did I ever dream I should see thee here?"

Harry recoiled instinctively, startled by the sudden intimacy, but the woman sank to her knees beside the bed. Seizing his feverish hand, she pressed it to her lips, embracing his legs with a desperate tenderness. Her gaze held a profound, genuine compassion for his plight. The wretched state of his imprisonment, the hollow look in his eyes, filled her soul with a love and pity that stripped away any remaining reserve.

"I—I feared you would not send for me!" Harry whispered, a groan escaping him.

For a moment, she offered nothing but tears, more kisses, and the crushing weight of her embrace. Then, her voice softened. "Oh, my darling! My life! How can I bear to think of thee suffering?"

Though hardened by years of hardship, her heart was far from stone. Her tone surpassed even the devotion of a biological mother. "Some of these debts," she confessed, her voice trembling, "were born of my own folly. Thou hast spent thy gold upon trifles and gems to please me. Oh, how I curse those foolish gifts now! I had no notion I could ever bring myself to do this!"

From within her reticule, she produced a cascade of treasures: brooches, rings, a pocket-watch, and a handful of gold guineas, pouring them into Harry's lap. The sight of the wealth struck him with a jolt, stirring a tide of emotion that threatened to overwhelm him.

"Dearest, kindest cousin!" he sobbed, overwhelmed.

Words failed him, yet his gratitude and affection radiated from his silence. Suddenly, a spark of gaiety ignited within him. Smiling weakly, he tucked the trinkets away, vowing to gift them to Maria, and recounted the perilous bargain he had made to fund Maria's dowry, explaining how the lawyer had mocked him over it. He refused the twenty guineas, insisting he had enough for the present, yet he cherished the gesture as if it were worth a fortune. He promised never to forget her kindness; it had buoyed him when despair nearly crushed him. He vowed to inform his mother of her goodness.

As twilight deepened, the shadows lengthened, yet the gloom of the cell seemed lifted. The world was not devoid of warmth. Here was a creature who loved him truly. Even Castlewood, he reflected, was not entirely lost. Though hopelessly ruined, the Baron had expressed regret, promising to aid if he could. He would seek out Mr. Warrington, who was also detained, and together they would find a path forward. Until the night fully enveloped them, the cousins conversed with a renewed sense of affection.

Finally, Maria sighed, rose, and bid him farewell. As the door clicked shut, cutting off her soothing presence, it swung open again to admit Mr. Draper.

"The honor of your company, sir," the attorney said, his voice jarring against Harry's nerves.

"You waited long enough, sir," Harry replied curtly.

"A lawyer's schedule is rarely his own, sir," Draper retorted, adjusting his coat. "But rest assured, I am at your disposal. Have you packed your trunks? You remain in your dressing gown. Let me descend to arrange matters while you summon your attendant and freshen yourself. I have a carriage ready, and we shall dine with the old lady."

"Will you dine with the Baroness de Bernstein?" Harry asked.

"Not I. I have eaten. It is thou who art bound to dine with thy aunt, I presume?"

"Mr. Draper, you imagine far more than you know," Harry said, standing tall and folding his brocade robe around himself with a fierce dignity.

"What do you mean, sir?" Draper demanded, confused.

"I mean, sir, that I have given my word to a faithful and honorable lady. It becomes me not to retract it."

"For heaven's sake, sir!" Draper shrieked. "I tell you she has lost the bond! There is no obligation binding you! She is old enough to be—"

"That is enough, sir!" Harry stamped his foot, his anger flaring. "You seem to believe you are speaking to a common trickster. Take it as read, Mr. Draper, that you are not accustomed to dealing with men of honor."

"Pettifogger, indeed!" Draper roared, his face flushing with fury. "Men of honor! I assure you, I am as honorable a man as thou. I may not know so many gamblers and horse-jockeys, nor have I squandered my inheritance living like a lord on two hundred a year. I have not bought watches on credit and pawned them!" Springing toward the door, he lunged forward.

"That is the exit, sir," Harry declared, stepping into his path with fists clenched. "Thou cannot pass through the window, for it is barred."

"And my answer to my client is 'No,' then!" Draper screamed, his composure fracturing.

Harry advanced, his voice low and dangerous. "If thou utterest another word..."

The sentence died in his throat as Draper slammed the door and fled, leaving Harry alone in the stifling air. The lawyer had stormed out to Madame de Bernstein, delivering the most favorable account he could devise, yet emerging with no less fiery condemnation from the old woman than he had received from Harry himself.

"What? Shall I trust her, and yet abandon her?" Harry paced his chamber, the silk of his gown rustling loudly. "My dear, faithful, generous mistress! Should I rot in prison for a dozen years, I shall remain true to her."

With the stormy departure of his counsel, the desolate old woman was forced to sit down to the meal she had hoped to share with her nephew. The chair remained empty before her, the silver candlesticks gleaming, untouched. Dish after dish was placed before her by the silent major-domo, tasted, and rejected. Finally, the steward pressed his hand upon her arm. "It is past eight," he murmured. "Thou hast eaten naught today. It would do thee good to eat."

She could not swallow. She required her coffee. "Send Case," she commanded, her voice thin. "Fetch me my coffee."

The servants cleared the table, leaving her seated alone before the vacant chair. Moments later, the old servant returned, carrying no tray, his face etched with terror. "Mistress!" he gasped. "Mr. Warrington!"

The old woman let out a cry, rising from her chair only to sink back down, trembling violently. "So ye have come, sir? Art ye here?" Her voice shook, a fragile thread of sound. "Return the—" And then she screamed, "Good heavens, who is this?"

Her eyes widened, staring wildly, her painted face suddenly ghastly beneath the rouge. Clinging to the arms of her chair for support, she gazed upward as the figure approached.

A gentleman entered, his features and stature mirroring Harry Warrington almost uncannily. His voice, when it spoke, carried a haunting similarity to the prisoner's. He bowed deeply to the Baroness.

"Ye expected my brother, madam?" the stranger said softly. "I have but newly arrived in London. I sought his residence, where I encountered his servant at thy gate, bearing this letter for thee. Deeming it prudent, I resolved to deliver it unto thee before proceeding to him." He laid the letter gently upon the

Swift horses pulled the hackney-coach through the muddy chaos of Strand and Fleet Street, bearing young George Warrington toward the rescue of his imprisoned brother. To anyone familiar with Hogarth's depictions of eighteenth-century London, the scene would seem all too real: the weary pace of the vehicle, the poorly hung bodies, the treacherous pavements riddled with holes, and the vast, sucking quagmires of winter mud that threatened to swallow the wheels whole. For our young traveler, this chaotic drive from Piccadilly to Fleet Street stretched with a weariness equal to the journey from Marlborough to London he had undertaken that same morning.

Before arriving, George had penned urgent letters to Harry, first announcing his presence in Bristol, and later revealing the monumental news of his existence and safe return from captivity. Yet, a shadow of war loomed large; England and France were at odds, and French privateers relentlessly hunted British merchant vessels, often seizing them mere miles from shore. Tragically, the letter carrying the tidings of George's restoration likely fell into the hands of a French captain aboard an American ship captured near port. Consequently, the message informing Harry of his brother's arrival in England never reached his hands. It lay untouched in the apartments where Harry was held captive, delivered on the third morning following his imprisonment, only to remain sealed when the irascible Mr. Ruff, Harry's jailer, refused to surrender a single item of his ward's property.

Upon reaching London, George made straight for those apartments to locate his brother. The maid-servant who answered the knock recoiled in sheer terror at the striking resemblance between the visitor and her absent master, screaming and fleeing back to her mistress. The landlady, unwilling to admit the truth—that poor Harry was actually a prisoner held by her husband—devised a lie. She claimed Mr. Warrington had simply vacated his lodgings, offering no clue as to his whereabouts. George, however, knew better. Clarges Street, near Bond Street, was close to his mind. He recalled the maps he had studied and the stories of Aunt Bernstein's kindness recounted in Harry's letters to Virginia. Perhaps, he reasoned, Aunt Bernstein could guide him, or perhaps Harry was currently under her roof. He harbored a quiet hope that Madam Esmond had finally found cause to repent of her former harshness; indeed, it was time for a woman of her age to reflect, considering the sins of the past century were still fresh wounds to the virtuous. Rachel Esmond rarely spoke of her eldest daughter, nor did Madam Esmond mention her sister, yet the silence of female virtue on matters of sin remained inexorable, refusing to pardon crimes committed decades prior.

George's demeanor hardened as he presented himself to the old lady, influenced by the fearful tales his agent in Bristol had spun regarding his brother's current state. Gumbo, the servant, had barely recovered from the shock of seeing his "dead" master return when he managed to stutter out a few words concerning the young master's precarious situation. To George, it was a given that relations were duty-bound to rescue a brother in distress. *Oh, George,* the narrative whispered, *how little you truly understood the labyrinthine ways of London!* If a true philanthropist sought to rescue every poor soul he encountered, the combined wealth of all the American provinces would fail to suffice.

Yet, as George jolted through the darkening streets toward the house of his brother's captivity, the old lady's agitated expression struck a chord within him. *She feels for my poor Harry,* he thought, chastising his earlier judgment. *I have been too hasty.* Life, he realized, demanded constant self-rebuke for such precipitous judgments. Once the damage was done, however, no amount of regret could undo it. "Quick, coachman!" he urged, though the journey from Clarges Street to the Temple felt agonizingly slow. Gumbo, who knew the bailiff's house intimately, led the way. A series of doors opened and closed warily, guarded by a Jewish janitor who stared in awe at a second Mr. Warrington emerging from the shadows, lit by a flaring tallow torch. "Come to see that gentleman?" the janitor asked, pausing to identify the newcomer. "Yes, but wait. This is Mr. Warrington's brother from America. Gumbo must prepare his master first."

They were ushered into a small office, nominally belonging to Mr. Amos, where a stout gentleman in a cloak and laced hat sat by a guttering candle, conversing with the bailiff. A young porter preceded George, holding a candle that cast sufficient light over the cramped space.

"We are not angry any longer, Harry!" the stout gentleman exclaimed in a cheery tone, rising to extend a hand to the newcomer. "Thank God, my boy! Mr. Amos assures me there shall be no difficulty in securing bail for James and myself, and we shall settle your affairs by breakfast. Why, angels and ministers of grace! Who are you?" He stumbled back slightly as the stranger gripped his hand firmly.

The stranger, however, clung to the hand with renewed strength. "God bless you, sir!" he cried. "I know who you are. You must be Colonel Lambert, whose kindness to my poor Harry I have read of. And I am the brother you have heard of—the one left for dead at the Battle of Braddock, revived after eighteen months among the French. I live to thank God and thank you for your kindness to my Harry." His voice trembled with emotion.

"James! James! News!" shouted the stout gentleman to a figure in red robes who entered the room. "The dead have risen! Here is Harry Scapegrace's brother returned, and with his scalp intact!" George stood by the light, having removed his hat. "This is my brother-bail, Mr. Warrington!" the colonel announced. "And this is Lieutenant-Colonel James Wolfe at your service. There has been a slight misunderstanding between Harry and me, Mr. George, but he is now pacified, is he not, James?"

"He is full of gratitude," replied Mr. Wolfe, bowing respectfully to Mr. Warrington.

"Harry also wrote home of Mr. Wolfe, sir," George added earnestly. "I trust his friends will extend the same kindness to me."

"I wish he had no others but us," Mr. Lambert sighed. "His parents were too refined for him, ultimately leading to this plight."

"Nay, your honors, I have done my utmost to comfort the young gentleman," interjected Harry's landlord, recognizing the rank and integrity of the two officers. "Knowing your honor, and that your security is sound—if you desire, the young gentleman may depart tonight, and I shall rectify everything with the lawyer by morning."

"The debt amounts to five hundred pounds, I believe?" George queried. "With a hundred thanks to these gentlemen, I can pay the sum immediately into the officers' hands, providing the necessary acknowledgments and cautions. But I shall never forget, gentlemen, that you aided my brother in his hour of need. For this, I say thank you, and God bless you, in my mother's name and mine."

Meanwhile, Gumbo ascended the stairs to Harry's apartment. Though the young gentleman might have chided the negro for returning so late, Harry was deeply soothed by the conversation he had shared with the departing officer. Sitting over his pipe of Virginia tobacco, he sat in a somber mood; Maria's recent kindness and affection, though evident, had not entirely assuaged his troubles, leaving him with a lingering dread of the consequences to which that fidelity bound him. Suddenly, the homely features and eager, outstretched hand of Mr. Wolfe appeared to lift his spirits. Hearing that Mr. Lambert was below, the purpose of their visit became clear. Despite Harry's notorious ill-temper and suspicious nature, the gentleman was resolved to assist him, even defying Mr. Wolfe's counsel.

"For you were wrong, Mr. Warrington," the Colonel admitted frankly. "You would not have been corrected otherwise. You, a young man, resorted to hard words and unkind behavior toward your superior, one of the finest gentlemen walking God's earth. Observe his answer to your wayward temper. Will you bear with a friend who speaks plainly? Martin Lambert has acted as he always does—as the best Christian, the best friend, the most kind and generous of men. If you require further proof of his goodness, consider this: He has converted me, who, as I confess without shame, was angered by your treatment of him, and has absolutely brought me down here to stand as your bail. Let us both cry *Peccavimus*! Harry, shake hands with your friend! He awaits you in the room below. He would not come until he knew how you would receive him."

"I think he is a good man!" Harry groaned, his spirit breaking. "I was wild and angry when we last met, Colonel Wolfe. Nay, perhaps he was right in returning those trinkets; I was hurt by his action. Go down to him, will you be so kind, sir? Tell him I am sorry, ask his pardon, and..." He paused, rubbing his hand across his eyes, overcome by emotion. "God bless him for his generosity."

"Tell him all this yourself, Harry!" the Colonel urged, seizing the young fellow's hand. "No deputy could speak it half so well. Come with me now."

"You go first, and I will—I will follow, on my word I will. See! I am in my morning gown! I will but

When we were first introduced to Sir Miles Warrington within the opulent drawing-room of St. James's Palace, I must confess that, regarding his jovial, round visage, his ample waistcoat, and his hearty, rural demeanor, I anticipated finding an ideal companion. I expected to form an intimacy with the quintessential "Good Old Country Gentleman," a figure lauded by song and men alike, positioned nearest the gods in earthly felicity. To be such a gentleman implies possessing a vast, unencumbered estate with rents paid punctually by adoring farmers, save perhaps for one recalcitrant tenant whose stubbornness allows the gentleman to display his sublime charity. It involves hunting thrice weekly, loving sport above all, enjoying robust health and a magnificent appetite, seated in church amidst a chorus of village blessings as the parish's chief benefactor, consciously accepting one's role as a "miserable sinner" solely for form's sake to set an example for others. With a healthy constitution, wealth, respect from the vicar, reverence from tenants, and a family that laughs at stories of grouse shot in the gunroom, this perfect being—despite the label of "sin"—could scarcely be heartily dissatisfied with his spiritual state. Were one a Royal Highness entering church in perfect health, attended by a waiting clergyman, would one truly believe oneself a "miserable sinner"? Only when racked with gout, alone, fearing death, and denied claret by a physician would humility arise. But in sunshine and spirits, the word "miserable" is merely a formality; one cannot genuinely feel pity for oneself. Consider Colin Ploughman, suffering from ague, supporting seven children, owing two pounds annually for his cottage, and surviving on eight shillings a week. By comparison, the misery of a healthy, wealthy, jolly country gentleman is supportable; if he is a sinner, few dare accuse him.

Yet, such a man may grow selfish, content with himself, and unmatched in the surrounding miles. His admirable wife serves him and the parish; his children bow; the vicar reveres him; he commands the sessions; poachers tremble; hats fall before him at market. Around his great coach, laden with spotless daughters and a sublime lady, tradesmen cringe bareheaded, and farmwomen drop countless curtseys. From their cushions, the ladies gaze down benevolently, smiling on the poor, buying yards of ribbon with affability, condescending to purchase salts or flowers, and deigning to bargain for geese. Their procession mimics a royal progress, with a happy populace pressing around to bless them, tradesmen bowing, farmers' wives bobbing, and town boys cheering the red-faced coachman driving the fat bays, shouting, "Sir Miles for ever! Throw us a halfpenny, my lady!"

However, let us entertain a darker hypothesis: what if a market-woman hides her fat goose from Sir Miles's coach, terrified lest his lady insist on a bargain? What if no copper coins ever emerge from the royal coach window? What if Sir Miles regales his tenants with notoriously small beer and his poor with thin broth? Such behaviors, it seems, characterize many "fine old English gentlemen" and ladies. They patronize the poor without relieving them, shout "Amen!" as loudly as the clerk, perform all the forms of piety, discharge every etiquette of gentlemanhood, and treat virtue as a bargain purchase, convinced they honor it. When poor Harry sought aid from his relations, his aunt dispatched a tract and a blessing; his uncle, occupied elsewhere, could not answer. How much of this conduct permeates respectable life? One might imagine Lord and Lady Macbeth concocting a murder with some awkwardness, but consider Lord and Lady Skinflint consulting in bed about refusing their luckless nephew a helping hand, then descending to family prayers to discourse virtuously before their children and domestics, remaining together afterward, nose to nose. What thoughts occupy them? How can they maintain these virtuous airs? How dare they look each other in the eye?

Do they believe they have done wrong? Does Skinflint torture himself with remorse over the distress calling in vain and the hunger sent away empty? Not at all. He is indignant with the Prodigal for being a fool, not ashamed of himself for being a curmudgeon. What? A young man squanders opportunities? Spends a fortune on gamblers and spendthrifts? "Horrible, horrible!" he cries, urging caution by the unfortunate youth's behavior and asserting the consequences of extravagance. According to the great, established Church of the Pharisees, here lies an admirable opportunity for moral discourse and the assertion of virtue. "To think of his deceiving us so!" cries Lady Warrington. "Very sad, very sad, my dear!" responds Sir Miles, wagging his head. "To think of so much extravagance in one so young!" exclaims Lady Warrington, listing cards, bets, tavern feasts, carriages, riding horses, and the company of profligate youths and iniquitous persons. "Hush, my Lady Warrington!" cries her husband, glancing toward the spotless Dora and Flora, who hold down their blushing heads at the mention of the last "naughty persons." "No wonder my poor children hide their faces!" the mother continues. "My dears, I wish even the existence of such creatures could be kept from you!" "They can't go to an opera, or the park, without seeing 'em, to be sure," says Sir Miles. "To think we should have introduced such a young serpent into the bosom of our family! And have left him in the company of that guileless darling!" she points to Master Miles. "Who's a serpent, mamma?" inquires the youth. "First you said cousin Harry was bad: then he was good: now he is bad again. Which is he, Sir Miles?" "He has faults, like all of us, Miley, my dear. Your cousin has been wild, and you must take warning by him." "Was not my elder brother, who died—my naughty brother—not he wild too? He was not kind to me when I was quite a little boy. He never gave me money, nor toys, nor rode with me, nor..." Why do you cry, mamma? Sure I remember how Hugh and you were always fighting—" "Silence, sir!" cry out papa and the girls in a breath. "Don't you know you are never to mention that name?" "I know I love Harry, and I didn't love Hugh," says the sturdy little rebel. "And if cousin Harry is in prison, I'll give him my half-guinea that my godpapa gave me, and anything I have—yes, anything, except... except my little horse—and my silver waistcoat—and—and Snowball and Sweetlips at home—and—and, yes, my custard after dinner." This was in reply to a hint from sister Dora. "But I'd give him some of it," continues Miles, after a pause. "Shut thy mouth with it, child, and then go about thy business," says papa, amused. Sir Miles Warrington possessed a considerable fund of easy humour. "Who would have thought he should ever be so wild?" mamma goes on. "Nay. Youth is the season for wild oats, my dear." "That we should be so misled in him!" sigh the girls. "That he should kiss us both!" cries papa. "Sir Miles Warrington, I have no patience with that sort of vulgarity!" says the majestic matron. "Which of you was the favourite yesterday, girls?" continues the father. "Favourite, indeed! I told him over and over again of my engagement to dear Tom—I did, Dora—why do you sneer, if you please?" says the handsome sister. "Nay, to do her justice, so did Dora too," said papa. "Because Flora seemed to wish to forget her engagement with dear Tom sometimes," remarks the sister. "I never, never, never wished to break with Tom! It's wicked of you to say so, Dora! It is you who were for ever sneering at him: it is you who are always envious because I happen—at least, because gentlemen imagine that I am not ill-looking, and prefer me to some folks, in spite of all their learning and wit!" cries Flora, tossing her head over her shoulder and looking at the glass. "Why are you always looking there, sister?" says the artless Miles junior. "Sure, you must know your face well enough!" "Some people look at it just as often, child, who haven't near such good reason," says papa, gallantly. "If you mean me, Sir Miles, I thank you," cries Dora. "My face is as Heaven made it, and my father and mother gave it me. 'Tis not my fault if I resemble my papa's family. If my head is homely, at least I have got some brains in it. I envy Flora, indeed, because she has found favour in the sight of poor Tom Claypool! I should as soon be proud of captivating a ploughboy!" "Pray, miss, was your Mr. Harry, of Virginia, much wiser than Tom Claypool? You would have had him for the asking!" exclaims Flora. "And so would you, miss, and have dropped Tom Claypool into the sea!" cries Dora. "I wouldn't." "You would." "I wouldn't;" and *da capo* goes the conversation—the shuttlecock of wrath being briskly battled from one sister to another. "Oh, my children! Is

Across the threshold, if you care to follow, you shall find our young gentlemen at Lord Wrotham's estate—a residence graciously lent to the General and his household. There sits that familiar circle we met at Oakhurst and Tunbridge Wells. James Wolfe has pledged his attendance, yet currently attends to Miss Lowther with such devotion that a glimpse of her eye holds far more allure for him than the most exquisite dishes prepared by Lord Wrotham's chef or the promised desserts awaiting your arrival. You shall occupy the sixth seat; take Mr. Wolfe's place, for you may be certain he will not come. As for me, I shall stand near the sideboard, chronicling the conversation.

First, observe the radiant countenance of the ladies. Recall when Harry Warrington was seized by bailiffs; I intend to recount how Mrs. Lambert, upon hearing of her son's plight, flew to her husband. She implored him with desperate fervor: "Let Martin help!" She dismissed his recent rebelliousness, his anger over returned gifts, and their poverty, insisting that somehow Harry must be freed. "Did you not rescue Charles Watkins two years ago?" she asked, tears glistening. "He repaid every penny, and his wife prays for you. Has everything not prospered since? Could it not do the same for you? You are a Major-General, surely!" Her faith was absolute.

Martin Lambert, requiring little persuasion for kindness, yielded readily. Having resolved to seek James Wolfe and secure bail for Harry, he donned his hat, squeezed Theo's hand—though whether Theo divined his errand or if Mother Lambert had inadvertently revealed it remains unclear—and kissed little Hetty's flushed cheek before departing. Yet, the devoted mother followed him from the room.

Alone with him, the enthusiastic matron could no longer contain her emotions. She threw her arms around his neck, kissed him repeatedly, beseeching God's blessing, and sobbed openly upon his shoulder. This display of conjugal tenderness was interrupted by Old Mrs. Quiggett, the lord's housekeeper, who bustled in, presumably stunned by the scene.

"We've had a squabble and are making it up!" the gentleman declared, walking away. "Do not spread tales, Mrs. Quiggett!"

"Well, I never!" shrieked Mrs. Quiggett, a laugh like a venerable, hook-nosed cockatoo echoing through the halls as she shook her sides, causing keys and ancient ribs to jingle. "Well, I never!"

"Oh, Quiggett!" sobbed Mrs. Lambert. "What a man he is!"

"You've been quarrelling and reconciling? That is right."

"Quarrel with him? He tells the greatest stories! My General is an angel, Quiggett. I would worship him, fall at his boots, and kiss them! There has never been a man so good as my husband. What have I done to deserve such a soul? How dare I possess such a wonderful husband?"

"My dear," the old lady murmured, "there appears to be a pair of you. And what shall we have for supper?"

When Lambert returned late, announcing Harry's freedom and his brother's miraculous rescue, the household erupted in commotion. If Mrs. Lambert's General was an angel before, what transformation awaited him now? If she desired to embrace his boots in the morning, what further acts of humble devotion might she crave in the evening? Little Hetty nestled silently against her father, stealing a sip from his glass. Theo and his mother's faces shone with happiness, like twin moons. After supper, the four knelt at a signal, paying glad homage in joyful mirth, rejoicing with a purity akin to angels repenting sins. Then, a heavy knocking shattered the quiet. My lord was miles away in the country; it was past midnight. Mrs. Lambert, sensing the visitor, nodded.

"This is George," announced a young gentleman, ushering in another. "We visited Aunt Bernstein but could not sleep without thanking you, Aunt Lambert. You dear, dear, good—" Their words dissolved into silence. Aunt Lambert embraced Harry; Theo snatched up the pallid Hetty, hugging her back to life. George stood with his hat removed. Once Harry's reunion was complete, George approached Mrs. Lambert to kiss her hand, while the General brushed his lips gently across hers. All were bathed in a tender, happy glow—the warmth of forgiveness, the restoration of peace, the return of lost love.

"Came from Aunt Bernstein's," Harry explained, "seeing lights here. We couldn't sleep without bidding goodnight." "Indeed, a famous nightcap you've brought us, boys," the General remarked. "Shall you dine with us tomorrow?" They declined, needing to visit Madame Bernstein instead. And so, the following day arrived—the very day we now chronicle, hosting precisely this dinner.

In lieu of Lieutenant-Colonel James Wolfe, absent on private matters, my gracious reader has been invited to sit. Why sit? Not for empty fare, but to hear the statement of **Mr. George Esmond Warrington**. They sat not in the grand dining hall, but in a cozy study; the cloth was withdrawn, the King's health drunk, servants departed, and the guests settled into contemplative silence. With some hesitation and blushes, Mr. George began:

"At the table of our General, I recall the little Philadelphia agent, whose wit and shrewdness we noted at home, voicing objections to the campaign's conduct, which disaster later vindicated. 'Of course,' he argued, 'your Excellency's troops, once before Fort Duquesne, will never withstand such a general, army, and artillery. But do you calculate on reaching the place? Your march cuts through untrodden woods, over roads you must forge, stretching four miles. This slender line, threading the forest, invites endless attacks from unseen enemies practiced in ambuscades.' 'Psha, sir!' scoffed the General. 'The savages may frighten your raw American militia, but they will never impress His Majesty's regulars.' 'I heartily hope not, sir,' sighed Mr. Franklin, drawing a sigh from the Postmaster, who was naturally sneered at by the General's family as a pert civilian lacking comprehension.

"We despised the Indians on our side, and our commander mocked them. Our officers outraged the accompanying chiefs by mistreating their women; only seven or eight chiefs remained loyal. Had a couple of hundred faced us on that fatal ninth of July, the outcome would differ vastly. They would have repelled the French-Indian assault, preventing the ensuing panic. It is known now that the French nearly surrendered, never imagining a defense, and their own Indians protested the audacity of attacking such overwhelming force.

"I stood with the General during the initial firing. Aide-de-camps were sent forward one by one. Initially, the enemy's attack was met briskly, with cheers rising from our lines. Soon, however, our fire slackened as single shots rang from behind every tree and bush, felling man after man. We marched in orderly formation—skirmishers ahead, colors and two small guns in center, baggage trailing—with open ground for a mile or two, flanked by thick, tangled brushwoods. After brief frontal fire, the ambush opened from both sides of the surrounding woods. Men dropped rapidly, officers falling faster than the ranks. At first, cheering continued, guns opened on the wood, seemingly silencing the hidden foe. But the rifle fire persisted. Men halted, huddled together, ignoring orders to advance, firing wildly into the brushwood without effect. Those in front ran back, terrified and wounded, claiming five thousand Frenchmen and a legion of screaming Indian demons awaited them, scalping the fallen. We heard their cries as comrades fell. Nowhere could induce men to move forward. One aide after another was dispatched and never returned. Finally, it was my turn. Sent with a message to Captain Fraser of Halkett's, I was never to receive nor deliver it.

"Had not gone thirty yards when a rifle-ball struck my leg, sending me crashing to the ground. I recall a frenzied rush of Indians and Frenchmen, the former wailing their fiendish war-cries, the latter as fierce as their savage allies. I was horrified to see so few whitecoats remaining—perhaps a score, certainly fewer than fifty in that accursed action where two of Britain's bravest regiments were routed.

"One figure, half-Indian, half-French, wearing moccasins and a white uniform coat with a cockade, spotted me prostrate. Turning back, he raised his musket, clubbed over his head, intending to dash my brains out and strip me. Luckily, my little fusil, gifted by Harry before the campaign, lay within reach. Seizing it, I felled the Frenchman dead at six yards. Saved for the moment, I bled profusely and grew faint, swooning as I tried to reload; the weapon slipped from my lifeless hand.

"Scarcely conscious, yells and shots dim in my ears, I saw an Indian standing over the slain Frenchman, glancing toward me. He rifled the corpse, tearing open the coat, searching pockets, then scalped him. Clutching a bleeding knife in his mouth, he advanced. Through a film, like a dream, I saw him coming, powerless to resist. He knelt upon my chest, seizing my long hair with one bloody hand to lift my head. In doing so, he revealed a French officer advancing rapidly behind him.

"'Good God! It was young Florac, my seconds-in-the-duel at Quebec,' I cried. "'A moi,

For months, our existence at the fort was a weary rhythm of endurance. The Commandant and I shared a volatile bond, cycling through bitter quarrels and fragile reconciliations. Our evenings were filled with greasy card games, dismal duets featuring his asthmatic flute and my cracked guitar, and the harsh reality of our captives. Poor Fawn endured the blows and the vials of liquor dispensed by her master at whim, yet she remained a pillar of care, nursing her papoose, soothing her master's gout, and tending to prisoners shivering with ague. This stagnant life held steady until the early autumn of the preceding year, when a hunter arrived bearing news that electrified the tiny garrison.

General Montcalm had dispatched a significant reinforcement to secure French-held forts and expand their hold on enemy territory. Troops departing Quebec and Montreal traveled down the St. Lawrence and across the lakes in bateaux, laden with artillery and vast supplies. A commander of higher rank would soon replace Museau, posing a grave threat to my situation. He might exchange me, or worse, surrender me to the Indians in retaliation for the cruelties inflicted upon enemy officers. The men of the fort dreamed wildly of reinforcements, envisioning advances into Pennsylvania and New York, the seizure of Albany and Philadelphia, and the driving of the "Rosbifs" back to the sea. Yet, beneath this triumphant rhetoric, Museau found no genuine satisfaction in the prospect of a French conquest.

"Eh, Commandant!" I would say. "'Tis *fort bien*, but meanwhile, where is your farm in Normandy? Where is the pot of cider and the *trippes à la mode de Caen*?"

"Yes," he would reply, his voice dropping. "'Tis all well, my boy. But consider: when poor old Museau is replaced, where will you be? Few men possess the humanity I do. If a great garrison arrives, will my successors grant you the indulgences honest Museau provides? You will be penned like a pig awaiting slaughter. As surely as an officer falls into the hands of your frontier braves and suffers, so surely will you pay with your skin for his blood. They will give you to our red allies—the Brethren of La Biche yonder. Remember last year? What did they do to your countrymen captured at Braddock? Roasting was merely the least of their punishments, *ma foi*. Was it not, La Biche?"

Museau launched into a vivid, macabre catalog of tortures—eyes gouged, teeth and nails twisted, limbs severed. You, dear Miss Theo, would turn pale at the recollection. However, I spared you the full horror, for I promised mercy.

La Biche, however, remained unmoved by the tale of horrors. She had witnessed them herself. Born of the Senecas, whose villages lay near the Great Cataract between Ontario and Erie, her people swung between alliance with the English and hatred for them. In the endless cycles of tribal warfare, it was difficult to determine whether white or red skin proved the more savage.

"They may chop me into cutlets and broil me, Commandant," I said, meeting his gaze with feigned calm. "But again, you will never regain the farm in Normandy."

"Go fetch the whisky-bottle, La Biche," Museau commanded.

"And it is not too late, even now," I pressed. "I will reward the guide who returns me home. I promise, as a man of honor, ten thousand livres to whoever brings me a token—say, my watch and seal, inscribed with my grandfather's arms, hidden in a chest somewhere within this fort."

"*Ah, scélérat!* Howls the Commandant, with a hoarse laugh. 'Thou hast eyes, thou! All is fair prize in war.'"

"Think of a house in your village, a fine field with six cows, a orchard heavy with fruit."

"And Javotte at the door with her wheel, and a rascal of a child or two, cheeks as red as the apples! O my country! O my mother!" Museau whimpered. "Quick, La Biche, the whisky!"

That night, the Commandant sank into deep thought, as did La Biche, silent and melancholy. Sitting apart, nursing her child, I caught her eyes fixed upon me whenever I glanced her way. Her infant cried, and Museau, with his usual foulspeech, ordered it away to the lean-to where the unfortunate La Biche resided. Once she was gone, I spoke my mind freely, presenting reasons that his greed could not ignore.

"How do you know," he asked, "that this hunter will serve you?"

"That is my secret," I replied. "However, if you prefer, I may reveal it. When traders enter the settlements for barter, they often linger for days, seeking rest, drink, and camaraderie. Our new friend loved these things. He gambled with the men, traded furs for liquor, and delighted in the fort. I suspect he enjoys listening to my songs. For lack of better entertainment, I often sang and scraped my guitar, leading to many impromptu concerts, joined by the men in chorus or dance, until interrupted by the retreat drum."

Our guest, the hunter, attended one or two of these gatherings. Suspecting he understood English, I challenged the men: "Lads, I will sing you an English song." To the melody of "Over the hills and far away"—an air my grandfather once hummed in Marlborough's camp—I improvised doggerel verses: *"This long, long year, a prisoner drear; Ah, me! I'm tired of lingering here: I'll give a hundred guineas gay, To be over the hills and far away."*

"What is it?" asked the hunter. "I don't understand."

"'Tis a girl to her lover," I answered. Yet, I saw the twinkle in his eye; he understood.

The following day, when silence reigned, the trapper confirmed my suspicion. Passing me, he hummed the same tune, the burden of my verse, in perfect English.

"If you are ready," he said, "I am ready. I know your kin and the path to them. Speak to Fawn, and she will instruct you. What! You refuse to play?" He produced a deck of cards just as two soldiers approached. "*Milor est trop grand seigneur?* Bonjour, my lord!" He bowed mockingly, shrugged, and departed to seek other players.

I realized then that La Biche was to be my agent, and Museau's offer had been accepted. Fawn performed her role with faithfulness and dexterity. Having tended me through wounds and illness, assisted with my chamber tasks, and been permitted free movement to the river and fields, she was uniquely positioned to aid me. The trapper, having squandered most of his peltry earnings, gathered his flint, powder, and blankets, then departed. Three days later, Fawn signaled that the time for my trial of freedom had arrived.

Since my initial injury, I had been cared for by Florac in the officers' room. With the fort stripped of its senior officers save for an old lieutenant, my confinement varied: sometimes free, sometimes locked away on prisoners' rations, sometimes invited to share his mess by my tipsy jailer. This log house, identical to the others mounting four small-caliber guns (one guarding the bastion behind my cabin), overlooked the confluence of the Ohio and Monongahela rivers. Westward, past the guns, lay a small island where Duquesne stood, and beyond it, trees on the opposite shore.

"You see those trees?" La Biche asked me the day prior, speaking her broken French jargon. "He waits for you behind them."

During the day, the door to my quarters stood open, and La Biche moved freely. The eve before her return from the fields, carrying a pick and a basket of greens for soup, she sat on a bench outside. Leaning her pick against the doorframe, she watched me. Standing nearby, I was so stunned by the absurdity of the situation that I never considered using the tool she held. "Hide it," she whispered urgently. "You will need it soon." That afternoon, she pointed out the trees.

The next day, she appeared, feigning anger. "My lord! My lord! Why do you not attend the Commandant's dinner? He is ill! Entendez-vous?" Peering inside, she tossed a coil of rope at me.

"I am coming, La Biche," I said, hobbling after her on my crutch. As I entered the Commandant's quarters, she murmured, "*Pour ce soir*"—for tonight—and I knew the hour had struck.

Museau, oblivious to the scheme, grumbled about the cold soup and stared me down, talking of trifles while his servant watched. Later, smoking our pipes and playing piquet, with La Biche cowering in the corner, he noted my empty whisky bottle. "I must have another glass," he declared, rising to retrieve firewater from his locked inner room, deliberately keeping La Biche away from the temptation.

The moment his back was turned, La Biche rose. Before he returned, she knelt at my feet, kissing my hand, pressing it to her heart, and weeping openly on my knees. Confused by the depth of her silent devotion—a sentiment I had barely suspected—I failed to recover my composure when Museau returned. Though La Biche retreated to her corner, sh

George Warrington had recounted his tale to Madame de Bernstein the prior evening, though the performance was far less successful than it had been in Hill Street. The old lady, prone to nodding off whenever the narrative grew intense, would only rouse herself when he paused, declaring it "most interesting" and commanding him to proceed. Consequently, the young gentleman stammered, hesitated, flushed crimson, and spoke with a halting cadence that belied his confidence before his intimate circle. In contrast, the warm reception of Hetty's wondering gaze, Theo's sympathetic expression, and the gentle smiles of his parents—despite his mother's theatrical tendency to weep at the slightest provocation, a trait the General likened to a professional tragedienne—had served as sufficient encouragement for his modest eloquence. It was precisely to avoid such a cynical auditor that they had selected Lord Wrotham's residence as the venue for George's debut, hoping his words would land with favor rather than falling on weary ears.

"Oh, very good and most interesting, I am sure, my dear sir," Madame Bernstein cooed, raising three slender fingers encased in a lace mitten to mask a sudden, involuntary twitch of her lips. "And I daresay your mother was quite delighted to see you."

George gave a slight shrug and bowed low as his aunt lifted her keen, aged eyes to inspect him.

"She certainly was," the old lady continued, her tone dry and cutting, "delighted to see you, killed the fatted calf, and engaged in all such appropriate niceties. Though, nephew George, I confess I cannot understand why I call it a calf. You were never the prodigal son. I might as well address thee as 'poor Harry!' For you have undoubtedly wandered among swine. Indeed, evil companions have stripped both thy purse and thy coat clean."

"He returned to his family in England, madam," George interjected, his voice warming with defense, "and his friends were, of course, your ladyship's."

"Nay, he could not have sought counsel from worse advisors," the old lady retorted, tossing her head with dramatic flair. "I should have informed my sister sooner, had she deigned to write through him as she has done through you." Turning to her waiting-maid, she whispered excitedly, "By Jove! This young gentleman's mother is half-sorry that he has come back to life. I saw it in his face! Half sorry, and I am perfectly furious! Why did he not lie still beneath that tree? Why did that young Florac drag him to the fort? I knew those Floracs in the days of Monsieur le Régent. They were of the House of Ivry. No great house before Henri IV. His ancestor was the King's favorite. His ancestor—he! he!—his ancestress! Brett! Entendez-vous? Hand me my card-case. I detest the grand airs of this Monsieur George; yet, he bears a striking resemblance to his grandfather—the same look, and occasionally the same tones. Have you heard of Colonel Esmond when I was young? This boy has his eyes. I suppose I favored the Colonel because he loved me."

Engaged immediately to a card party—an amusement she never missed, weekday or Sabbath, so long as she possessed the strength to hold trumps or sit upright—the old lady soon dismissed her two nephews. To the elder, George, she offered a couple of fingers and a stiff, stately curtsy; to the younger, Harry, she extended two hands and bestowed a kindly pat upon his cheek.

"My poor child," she murmured, her voice softening, "now that thou art disinherited, thou shalt see how differently the world treats thee!" She pressed a small leather pocket-book into his hand. "There is only, in all London, a wicked, heartless old woman who will treat thee as before. Do not lose it tonight at Ranelagh. That suit of thine does not become thy brother half so well as it did upon thee! Present thy brother to everyone, and pace the room for at least two hours. Were I in thy shoes, I would retreat to the Chocolate-House and play as if nothing had occurred. While thou art there, perhaps thy brother may return to dine with me upon some chicken. My Lady Flint serves wretched suppers, and I wish to discuss thy mother's letter with him. Adieu, gentlemen!" With that, she departed toward her toilet, her chairmen and torchbearants already awaiting at the door.

The gentlemen proceeded to Ranelagh, where only a handful of Mr. Harry's acquaintances happened to be present. They strolled along the circular promenade, encountering Mr. Tom Claypool and several country friends, listened to the strains of music, and sipped tea within a private box. Harry assumed the role of host, introducing his brother to the various curiosities of the pleasure palace. Meanwhile, George proved far more agitated than his sibling had been on his initial visit. George possessed a profound love for music, having never before heard a full orchestra perform a composition by Mr. Handel with such satisfaction. A notable testament to Harry's newfound humility and fraternal regard was his ability to tolerate George's passion for the fiddle—a pursuit often deemed effeminate and unmanly in England, where patriotic pamphlets daily urged citizens to mock the frivolous accomplishments of foreign "Squallinis" and "Monsieurs." Yet, the spirit of the age seemed shifting; pride in ignorance was fading, and arrogance had vanished from polite society. Art had secured her letters of naturalization, residing here on terms nearly equal to the native born. Even if Mrs. Thrale chose to wed a music master today, her friends need not shudder at the union, nor withhold civility from Mr. Piozzi, provided she maintained a good fortune and a competent cook.

Following Ranelagh, and acting on Madame Bernstein's counsel, George returned to her ladyship's house, while Harry presented himself at the club, where gentlemen traditionally assembled for supper followed by gambling. Naturally, no one alluded to Mr. Warrington's brief, mysterious absence. His former landlord, Mr. Ruff, attended to him with the utmost gravity and politeness, as if no rift had ever existed between them. Mr. Warrington had ensured his trunks and belongings were removed from Bond Street in the morning, and he and his brother were now quartered in separate lodgings.

However, once supper concluded and the gentlemen prepared to ascend to the macco-table upstairs, Harry declared he would not wager further. "I have already burned my fingers," he remarked, "and can afford no further extravagance."

"Why, Mr. Morris," said a gentleman in a somewhat flippant tone, "you must have won more than you have lost, after all is said and done."

"And of course I know your business less well than you do, Mr. Morris," Harry replied sternly, remembering the other man's behavior upon learning of his arrest. "But I have another reason. A few months—or days, rather—ago, I was heir to a vast estate and could afford to lose a trifling sum. Now, thank God, I am heir to nothing." He glanced around, flushing deeply, at the cluster of gentlemen lounging near the tables or gathered by the fire.

"How do you mean, Mr. Warrington?" cried Lord March, eyes lighting up. "Hast thou lost Virginia, too? Who hath won it? I always fancied playing thee myself for that stake."

"And thereby produce an improved breed of slaves in the colony," suggested another voice.

"The rightful owner hath claimed it. Hast thou not heard of my twin elder brother?"

"Yes," came the response, "who perished in the affair of Braddock two years hence. Gracious goodness, my dear sir, I trust in Heaven he hath not returned to life!"

"He arrived in London two days past," Harry explained. "He was a prisoner in a French fort for eighteen months, escaping only recently. He left our house in Virginia shortly after his liberation."

"Haven't thou had time to order mourning, I suppose?" asked Mr. Selwyn, his nature good-humored and simple, though Harry scarcely grasped the jest until his brother elucidated it.

"Blasphemy, if I am not mistaken!" cried Lord March as they continued to discuss the matter after the young Virginian had taken his leave. "These savages practice the simple virtues of affection—they are barely civilized in America yet."

"They cherish their kin, and scalp their foes," chimed Mr. Walpole with a smirk. "It is not Christian, but natural. Wouldst thou care to attend a scalping match, George, and behold a fellow skinned alive?"

"A man's elder brother is his natural enemy," observed Mr. Selwyn calmly, arranging his money and counters before him.

"Torture is akin to broiled bones and pepper," Horry added. "Thou wouldst not relish simple hanging afterward, George!"

"I'm hanged if there's any man in England who would desire to see his elder brother alive," declared Lord March.

"No, nor his father either, my lord!" shouted Jack Morris.

"For the first time, I learn thou hast one, Jack. Give me counters for five hundred."

"I say, 'tis all mighty fine concerning dead brothers returning to life," Jack mused. "Who knows if this was not a scheme contrived between these two fellows? Behold a young fellow calling himself the Fortunate Youth, claiming to be a Virginian Prince and devil-knows-what, entering our society..."

A great laugh erupted at Jack's phrasing of "our

The Colonel Esmond, the maternal grandfather of our Virginians, had spent a significant portion of his long leisure in America composing memoirs of his early life. In these volumes, Madame de Bernstein—formerly Miss Beatrice Esmond before her spinsterhood—played a considerable role. George Warrington, having read his grandfather's manuscript countless times, knew his kinswoman intimately long before their physical meeting; he understood the spirit of the young, beautiful, and wilful lady from half a century prior, even as he now encountered her in the twilight of her days. One wonders whether it is sad or sweet for a woman whose bloom has faded to recall the vibrant era of her youth. When the heart grows withered, do the elderly truly love remembering how it once beat with warmth? When spirits grow languid, do we delight in thinking of former buoyancy?
