Thus do the buds of prime and the roses of beauty fall and wither, leaving bare branches to shiver in the winter chill.

"And that was a beauty once!" thought George Warrington as his aunt emerged from her evening rout, powdered and diamond-clad. "And that ruin was once a splendid palace. Crowds of lovers have sighed before those decrepit feet, and been bewitched by the brilliance of those eyes." His mind drifted to a fireworks display at Williamsburg on the King's birthday, where he later examined the skeletal remains of exploded Roman candles. To George, the dazzling career of Aunt Beatrice's youth mirrored the charred remnants of the fireworks. While honest Harry had witnessed those same spectacles, he lacked his brother's penchant for deep reflection; George, melancholy and introspective, drew profound lessons from the journals, whereas Harry simply enjoyed the pageantry.

After dinner, the Baroness tapped her cane toward her escritoire, instructing her attendant to retrieve a letter concealed beneath the inkstand. George recognized the superscription instantly—it was the correspondence he had carried from home.

"It appears by this letter," observed the old lady, fixing a scrutinizing gaze upon her nephew, "that since your return, differences have arisen between you and my sister."

"Indeed?" George replied smoothly, feigning ignorance. "I did not realize Madam Esmond had alluded to them."

The Baroness adjusted a large pair of spectacles over eyes that had once ignited passions, glanced over the document, and handed it to George. Reading aloud, he heard the words of his mother-in-law, dated Richmond, Virginia, December 26th, 1756:

*"Honoured Madam! And Sister!--I have received, and thankfully acknowledge, your ladyship's favour... My reply will be brought to you by my eldest son, Mr. Esmond Warrington, who returned to us so miraculously out of the Valley of the Shadow of Death..."*

The letter detailed Harry's miraculous rescue from captivity and his subsequent return to England, masquerading as the heir while George was presumed dead. The mother expressed profound grief at Harry appearing before the King and the family under false pretenses, yet noted that Harry's tender nature meant he would willingly relinquish his inheritance to his elder brother without a pang. However, the letter turned to financial matters, lamenting that an advantageous opportunity to invest Harry's funds in land and slaves in Virginia had been missed due to legal formalities and her elder son's scruples. Consequently, Harry remained dependent on his slender income, a situation exacerbated by his recent European travels and the necessity of maintaining appearances suitable for a grandson of the Marquis of Esmond.

"My mother intends to send additional funds," George read, noting a postscript regarding a proposed marriage to Lady Maria Esmond. *"Let my task now be to save for his less fortunate younger brother... The match is altogether unsuitable."*

"It took him out of prison yesterday, madam," George interrupted gently. "I think that was the best use to which we could put it."

"Nay, sir! I don't know any such thing!" cried the old lady, agitated. "Why not have kept it to buy a pair of colours for him, or to help towards another estate and some negroes? Besides, I had a fancy to pay that debt myself."

"I hope you will let his brother handle that," George countered diplomatically. "I ask leave to act as banker for this matter, considering I have borrowed so much from my mother, to be repaid to my dear Harry."

"Do you say so, sir?" The Baroness poured a glass of rich Burgundy, contrasting sharply with her grandfather's preferred claret. "Read on, and you will see your mother agrees. I drink to your health, nephew George!"

As George continued reading, the letter revealed further complications. The mother expressed concern over Harry's lack of humility during his captivity and hinted at a scandal involving an Indian woman named Silverheels. George recalled the painful memory vividly: the woman had traveled from the wilderness bearing a token from Museau, the French officer who had aided his escape, along with a dying request to send funds to Museau's relatives in Normandy. Upon returning home, the community had treated the poor woman with suspicion and scorn, turning her grief into ridicule. George had successfully petitioned the Governor for a pass to send her back to her people, but the incident had left a bitter stain on the family's reputation, a fact not yet reconciled with Madam Esmond.

Turning to the topic of Harry's marriage, the letter reiterated the mother's disapproval of Lady Maria. Despite Lady Maria's noble behavior during Harry's imprisonment—bringing jewels and gold to support him—the disparity in wealth and station rendered the union impossible. The mother, acknowledging her own limited resources compared to the vast estates of the Virginia planters, urged Harry to return to England to avoid further distress.

"As far as regards myself, madam," George said, folding the paper, "my mother's letter conveys no news to me. I always knew that Harry was the favorite son with Madam Esmond, as he deserves indeed to be. He possesses a hundred good qualities which I lack."

"Nay, that is not your fault," the Baroness teased, eyeing him slyly. "And, but that he is fair and you are brown, one might almost pass for the other."

George bowed, a faint blush coloring his pale cheek. "His disposition is bright, and mine is dark," he admitted. "Harry is cheerful, and I am otherwise. He knows how to make himself beloved by everyone, and it has been my lot to find but few friends."

"My sister and you have pretty little quarrels," the Baroness mused, recalling similar dynamics from her own youth. "There were such in our family in old days. If Madam Esmond takes after our mother—"

"My mother has always described hers as an angel upon earth," George interjected.

"Eh! That is a common character for people when they are dead!" the Baroness exclaimed dismissively. "Rachel Castlewood was an angel, if you like—at least your grandfather thought so. But let me tell you, sir, that angels are sometimes not very commodious to live with. They may be too good for us sinners, and the air down here doesn't agree with them. My poor mother was so perfect she could never forgive me for being otherwise. Ah, mon Dieu! How she oppressed me with those angelical airs!"

George lowered his eyes, reflecting on his own melancholy youth. He hesitated to reveal further family secrets to this cynical old worldling, who nonetheless seemed to understand him despite her reticence.

"I quite comprehend you, sir, though you hold your tongue," the Baroness continued. "A sermon in the morning, a sermon at night, and two or three on Sundays. That is what people call being good. Every pleasure cried 'fie' upon; all worldly people excommunicated; a ball an abomination of desolation; a play a forbidden pastime; and a game of cards perdition! What a life! Mon Dieu, what a life!"

"We played at cards every night, if we were so inclined," George smiled, defending his upbringing. "And my grandfather loved Shakespeare so much that my mother had not a word to say against her father's favorite author."

"I remember," the Baroness agreed. "He could recite whole pages by heart. Though, for my part, I like Mr. Congreve a great deal better. And then, there was that dreadful, dreary Milton, whom he and Mr. Addison pretended to admire!" she cried, tapping her fan.

"If your ladyship does not like Shakespeare, you will not quarrel with my mother for being indifferent to him, too," George said softly. "And indeed, I think—and I am sure—you do not do her justice. Wherever there are any poor, she relieves them; wherever there are any sick, she—"

"She doses them with her horrible purges and boluses!" the Baroness interrupted sharply. "Of course, just as my mother did!"

"She does her best to cure them! She acts for the best and performs her duty as far as she knows it."

"I don't blame you, sir, for doing yours and keeping your own counsel about Madam Esmond," the old lady conceded. "But at least, there is one point upon which we all three agree—that this absurd marriage must be prevented. Do you know how old the woman is? I can tell you, though she has torn the first leaf out of the family Bible at Castlewood."

"My mother has not forgotten her cousin's age, and is shocked at the disparity between her and my poor brother," George explained, blushing slightly. "Indeed, a city-bred lady of her time, accustomed to

The two young men, seated in the quiet intimacy of their reunion, had spent hours dissecting the landscapes of their separate homes. They traded the minutiae of their histories since their last parting—the condition of Harry's dogs, the growth of little Dempster, the health of old Nathan, and the blossoming beauty of Fanny. News flowed like water between them, carrying tidings of Savannah and Georgia, where Parson Broadbent's daughter was betrothed to Tom Barker. Harry confessed with a flush of shame how he had once been deeply infatuated with Miss Broadbent, how he squandered his pocket-money at cards and drowned his sorrows in strong waters alongside her father, solely to secure a pretext for her company. Yet, heaven forbid, he admitted, Madam Esmond would never have sanctioned such a union; her daughter was far beneath the station of the Colonel's wife.

Colonel George Washington's wife, however, was a different matter entirely—a woman of considerable fortune, good nature, and undeniable charm. George recounted the scene with humorous relish, describing the "ceremonial" of his mother's introduction to the young American lady. He painted the picture of his mother's terrifying politeness, a veneer of civility stretched thin over a chasm of disdain. "Never mind, George, my dear!" Mrs. Mountain had laughed, her matchmaking spirit undeterred by the failure of this particular alliance. "The Colonel has taken another wife, but I feel certain that at one time two young gentlemen I know of ran a very near chance of having a tall stepfather six feet two in his boots." Mrs. Mountain was, indeed, the architect of many rumors; it was she who had spread the tale of the "poor Indian woman," a slander Madam Esmond had greeted with icy scorn. When Parson Stack had delivered the gossip, the Colonel's wife had retorted that she would sooner suspect Mr. Esmond of stealing silverware or marrying a negro woman from the kitchen. Though she treated the unfortunate Biche with a chilling hauteur that drove the Indian girl to drown her disappointments in gin among the servants, Madam Esmond could not bring herself to condemn her openly, offering a perfunctory thanks for her attendance during her son's illness.

Yet, despite the scandal surrounding the mother's housekeeping and the father's debts, the core of the conversation turned inward, to the brothers themselves. George sighed, the weight of his exile evident in his tone. "The birthright is mine, Harry," he said, "but thou art the favourite, and God help me! I think my mother almost grudges it to me. Why should I have taken the pass, and preceded your worship into the world? Had you been the elder, you would have possessed the best cellar, ridden the finest nag, and been the most popular man in the country. Whereas I? I have not a word to say for myself, save to frighten folks with my glum visage." He lamented the inversion of natural order, cursing the fate that compelled him to correct what he perceived as a time "out of joint."

"Why, Georgy, you are talking verses, I protest you!" Harry exclaimed, laughing.

"I think, my dear, some one else talked those verses before me," George smiled, acknowledging the echo of poetry in his brother's jest.

"It's out of one of your books," Harry cried, his voice thick with emotion. "You know every book that ever was written, I do believe!" He recounted the thrill of seeing the two authors at Tunbridge, bowing low before them not out of admiration for their wit—which he modestly dismissed—but out of a deep, personal reverence for his brother's past words. "I say, dear, it's like a dream seeing you over again," Harry stammered, clutching at the memory of danger. "Think of that bloody Indian with his knife at your throat! I should like to give that Monsieur de Florac something for saving you... but I haven't got much now, only my little gold knee-buckles, and they ain't worth two guineas."

"You have got the half of what I have, child," George reassured him gently. "And we'll divide as soon as I have paid the Frenchman."

Upon hearing this, Harry burst forth into a torrent of blessings and oaths, swearing that there had never been such a brother in the world as George. For several days following his arrival, Harry's eyes seemed unable to leave his sibling's path. He would drop his knife or newspaper mid-meal to laugh aloud, and when walking the Mall or Hyde Park, he would gesture proudly to the crowds, declaring, "Look here, gentlemen! This is he. This is my brother, that was dead and is alive again! Can any man in Christendom produce such a brother as this?"

However, the warmth of reunion quickly cooled when the topic of Museau arose. Of course, Harry believed George must honor his promise to pay Museau's heirs the ransom sum. This issue had plagued George at home with terrible intensity. After Museau's death, Madam Esmond had argued with fierce rancour, insisting the bargain fell to the ground. To her, paying twelve thousand livres to a band of Norman peasants—whom Britain was at war with and who would likely funnel the money to the Pope—was nothing short of subscribing to wickedness. She viewed it as the wages of iniquity. While she allowed that George was of age and legally capable of squandering his patrimony, she could not be a party to such extravagance, especially given Harry's sudden deprivation of his inheritance. She urged him to give the money to his own blood, to Harry, rather than to a foreign peasantry. This dispute, raging between mother and son throughout George's final days in Virginia, remained unresolved. On the morning of his departure, Madam Esmond had visited his bedside after a sleepless night, asking if he still intended to fling away his father's property. He replied, grief-stricken and perplexed, that his word was passed and he must abide by his honour. Her answer was a prayer for Heaven to soften his proud heart, and the last image George retained of his mother was her pale, tearless face standing by his side before she slowly withdrew.

"Now," George resumed, shifting the mood back to self-reflection, "where didst thou learn the art of winning everybody to thy side, Harry? And how is it that you and all the world begin by being friends? Teach me a few lessons in popularity." He contrasted his own isolation with Harry's ubiquity. In Richmond, he noted, Mr. Esmond Warrington—the sole survivor of Braddock's Field—had initially been a favorite, receiving kindness from the townsfolk and a glowing sermon from the local parson. The neighbors flocked to visit the fugitive, and the Esmond carriage rolled out to exchange courtesies. But his mother, with her prodigious haughtiness, routed off the admirers and frightened them with her demeanor. Before the disaster at Fort Duquesne, George's popularity had already begun to wane. He was not jolly enough for the officers, nor did he care for their drinking bouts, dice-boxes, and swearing. To the ladies, he was too sarcastic; their tea and gossip stupefied him just as the men's bluster did. He felt profoundly lonely amidst the scandal and squabbles, often regretting his prison and longing for the silence of Duquesne.

"So the gentlemen determined I was supercilious and a dull companion," George mused, "and the ladies thought me cold and sarcastic—unable to tell if I was in earnest or not. Generally, they voted me a disagreeable fellow before my character was quite destroyed. And then came the appearance of the poor Biche! Oh, a nice character they made for me, my dear!" He cried out in transport of wrath. "A pretty life they led me after Museau's unlucky messenger appeared! The boys hooted the poor woman if she stepped into the street; the ladies dropped me half-curtsies and crossed to the other side. That precious clergyman went from tea-table to tea-table preaching on the horrors of seduction and the lax principles young men learned in popish countries. Then, the poor Fawn's appearance at home a few weeks after my return was declared a scheme between her and me. The best-informed agreed she had waited on the other side of the river until I signaled her to join me in Richmond. The officers bantered me at the coffee-house, cracking clumsy jokes about the woman I had 'selected.' Oh, the world is a nice charitable world!"

His anger boiled over as he recalled the news of Harry's troubles. "I was so enraged that I thought of going to Castlewood and living alone there—for our mother finds the place dull, and the greatest consolation in Mr. Stack's ministry—when the news arrived of your female perplexity. I think we were all glad that I should have a pretext for coming to Europe."

"I should like to see any of the infernal scoundrels who said a word against you," Harry roared, pacing the room with violent energy. "And break their rascally bones!"

"I had to do something like it for Bob Clubber," George noted calmly.

"What!" Harry cried, horrified. "That little sneaking, backbiting, toad-eating wretch, who hangs about my lord at Greenway Court and spunges on every gentleman in the country? If you whipped him, I hope you whipped him well, George?"

"We were bound over to keep the peace," George explained. "I offered to go into Maryland with him and settle our difference there

Lord Castlewood resided in Kensington Square, a residence spacious enough to shelter his entire noble household and conveniently situated for the Palace, given that His Majesty occasionally dwelt nearby. Though the square offered ample space for card-parties and evening gatherings, it lay a considerable distance from central London—a fact that troubled George Selwyn, who admitted to a distinct dread of traveling there alone at night. Whether the dangers lay with footpads concealing their faces beneath crepe or with ladies applying rouge at the quadrille table, the specific perils remained unknown, yet the sentiment of unease persisted.

About noon, the day following Harry's dramatic reappearance at White's, the virtuous kinfolk assembled for breakfast, a gathering that notably included Mr. Will, who was destined to commence his career as a servant later that afternoon. The ladies arrived first, settling into their cocoa, while Mr. Will joined them in his formal court attire. Finally, the Master of the House entered, appearing languid in a bedgown and nightcap, having yet to assume the necessary wig for the day. This state of undress, however, served as the vehicle for significant news—intelligence Will had procured the previous evening from the Star and Garter, corroborated by accounts heard at White's and witnessed at Ranelagh.

"Heard what? Seen what?" Lord Castlewood inquired, picking up his *Daily Advertiser*.

"Ask Maria!" Lady Fanny interjected, directing attention toward her elder sister. Lord Castlewood turned to Maria, whose countenance wore an expression of portentous sadness, rendering her as pale as a freshly laundered tablecloth.

"'Tis merely one of Will's usual elegant and polite inventions," Maria dismissed lightly.

"No!" Will swore vehemently, punctuating his denial with several oaths. "It was no fabrication of his. Tom Claypool of Norfolk saw both of them at Ranelagh, and Jack Morris emerged from White's having heard the tale directly from Harry Warrington's own lips. Curse him, I am relieved!" Will roared, slamming his hand against the table. "What say you to your Fortunate Youth, your Virginian protégé, turning out to be a mere second son?"

"The elder brother not deceased?" Lord Castlewood asked, bewildered.

"Not dead, so far as anyone knows," Will replied. "Never truly dead. In my estimation, it was likely a crossbreed of the two."

"Mr. Warrington is incapable of such duplicity!" Maria cried out.

"I never encouraged the fellow, I assure you," Lady Castlewood interposed gently. "Neither did Fanny. Not we, indeed!"

"Not we, indeed!" echoed Lady Fanny.

"The fellow is nothing more than a beggar, and I suspect he has yet to settle payment for the garments upon his back," Will continued, his tone shifting to satisfaction. "I am glad of it, for, hang him, I harbor a deep hatred for him!"

"You do not view him with favorable eyes; particularly since he blackened your reputation, Will!" Lord Castlewood grinned. "Thus, the poor fellow has found his brother, and consequently lost his estate!" As he spoke, he turned toward his sister Maria. Despite her outward display of profound woe, she must have inadvertently suggested something ludicrous to the humorist beside her. After gazing at her for a moment, Lord Castlewood burst into a shrill, uncontrolled laughter. This sudden outburst caused the poor lady's cheeks to flush, and soon her eyes overflowed with tears.

"It's a shame! It's a shame!" she sobbed, burying her face in a handkerchief.

Maria's stepmother and sister exchanged glances, their expressions grave. "We never quite comprehend your lordship's sense of humor," the stepmother remarked solemnly.

"I perceive no necessity for you to understand," Lord Castlewood replied coolly. "Maria, my dear, please excuse me if I have said—or done—anything to injure your sensibilities."

"Do anything!" Maria exclaimed, rising from the table and fixing a glare upon the entire assembly. "You plundered the poor lad while he prospered, and now you laugh at him in his ruin!"

"Excuse me, my dear sister, I was not laughing at him," Lord Castlewood said, his voice softening.

"Oh, never mind at what or whom else, my lord! You have stripped him of everything he possessed to lose. The whole world points the finger at you as the man who feasts upon his own flesh and blood. And now that you hold his inheritance, you make merry over his calamities!" With these words, she rustled from the room, casting defiant glares at every member of the party.

"Relate to us what has transpired, or what you have heard, Will, and my sister's grief shall not disturb us further." Will proceeded to recount the story of George Warrington's sudden apparition at length, doing so with immense exultation at Harry's defeat. Lord Castlewood expressed sorrow for Harry, acknowledging him as a good and brave youth whom he genuinely liked, despite the worldly nature of their relationship. While he had undoubtedly played Harry at cards and exploited market advantages, he reasoned that if others plucked the same peach, he might as well devour it himself. If this were the sole burden of his conscience, he need feel no great unrest.

"Where resides Mr. Warrington?" Lord Castlewood asked.

Will expressed his readiness to pronounce a state of reprobation if he knew the address.

"He shall be invited here and treated with every due respect," Lord Castlewood declared.

"Including piquet, I suppose!" Will growled.

"And will you take him to the stables to sell him one of your bargains of horseflesh, Will?" Lord Castlewood asked. "You would have won swiftly from Harry Warrington if you could, but you cheat so clumsily at games that you were struck with a cudgel. I desire, once more, that every attention be paid to our cousin Warrington."

"And that you are not to be disturbed when you sit down to play, of course, my lord!" cried Lady Castlewood.

"Madam, I desire fair play—for Mr. Warrington, for myself, and for every member of this amiable family," Lord Castlewood retorted fiercely.

"Heaven help the poor gentleman if your lordship intends kindness toward him," the stepmother observed with a curtsey. Had not a phaeton drawn up to the house at that precise moment, carrying the two young Virginians, this family dispute might easily have escalated further.

It was the carriage the prodigal son had purchased in the days of his prosperity. He still drove it; George sat beside him, with their negro servants trailing behind. Harry had meekly handed the whip and reins to his brother, offering to cede the entire property. "What business has a poor devil like me with horses and carriages, Georgy?" Harry had humbly asked. "Beyond the coat upon my back and the purse my aunt bestowed, I possess nothing in the world. You take the driving-seat, brother; it will ease my mind." George had laughed, claiming ignorance of the roads, while Harry asserted his knowledge, suggesting he would claim only half of the carriage, mirroring his division of George's wardrobe. "A bargain is a bargain," George had insisted. "If I share thy coats, thou must divide my breeches' pocket, Harry; that is but fair dealing!" Again and again, Harry had sworn that no brother existed on earth like his. How he rattled the horses over the road! How proud and pleased he was to drive such a companion! They arrived at Kensington in high spirits, and Gumbo's thunderous knock upon Lord Castlewood's door rivaled the finest footmen of St. James's.

Upon entry, only Lady Castlewood and her daughter, Lady Fanny, occupied the room. Will had no inclination to face Harry, Lord Castlewood remained undressed, and Maria had reasons for remaining absent until her eyes had dried. Yet, as the young gentlemen advanced, the scene resembled a carefully staged domestic comedy. Had they not interrupted a family row? Did they not arrive simpering and smiling, stepping over the delusive ashes of a still-burning domestic fire? Between the hall and the drawing-room, did not the family arrange itself in a tableau—the girl artlessly arranging flowers, another reclining over devotional works, the mother on the sofa with shopping lists concealed beneath cushions, and the honest husband extending a kindly hand? Oh, let us be thankful not only for faces but for masks; not only for honest welcomes but for hypocrisy, which hides unwelcome realities from us. For while I speak in this easy, chatty manner, what right have you, my good sir, to know what truly passes within my mind? Perhaps I am racked with gout, or burdened by college bills, or suffering from a grievous illness, or hosting a dreadful remnant of a party to which you were not invited. Yet I conceal my agony, wearing a merry smile, declaring, "Come to take pot-luck with us, Brown my boy!" I call this humbug beautiful self-denial, hypocrisy true virtue. If every man spoke his mind, what intolerable society would ours become!

As the young gentlemen were announced, Lady Castlewood advanced with perfect ease and good humor. "We have heard, Harry," she said, looking at him with special friendliness, "of this most extraordinary circumstance. My Lord Castlewood mentioned at breakfast that he should wait upon you today, Mr. Warrington, and, cousin Harry, we intend not to love you any the less because

Madame de Bernstein was scarcely less gratified than her nephews by the conclusion of Harry's final audience with Lady Maria. That evening, Lord George dispatched a billet detailing the proceedings, and shortly thereafter, his rarely-visited nephew, Lord Castlewood, arrived to pay his respects. With the business between Maria and Harry concluded, the necessity for reticence vanished; my Lord Castlewood was never one to hesitate when frankness served a purpose. "The game is played and over," he declared, shedding any remaining pretense. "Now that the moves, stratagems, and finesse are revealed, what need remains for hypocrisy?"

"She is my own sister," Lord Castlewood continued, his tone affectionate yet resigned. "She possesses few further opportunities to establish herself. While I may not have approved of the match in every respect, and I pity your ladyship's favorite Virginian youth, such a stroke of fortune could not simply be discarded. I was bound to support my flesh and blood."

"Your candour honors your lordship," Madame de Bernstein replied, "and your devotion to your sister is truly edifying!"

"Nay, we have lost the game, and I speak without bitterness," Lord Castlewood bowed smoothly. "It is not for you, who have triumphed, to harbor malice."

Madame de Bernstein protested that she had never been in better spirits. "Confess now, Eugene," she pressed, "that Maria's visit to Harry at the spunging-house—that touching surrender of all his gifts—was a stroke of your invention?"

"Pity for the young man, and a sense of what Maria owed to her friend, her affianced lover in misfortune, were surely sufficient motives for her actions," Lord Castlewood answered demurely.

"But 'twas you who advised her, my good nephew?"

With a shrug, Castlewood admitted he had counseled his sister to see Mr. Henry Warrington. "Yet, we might have won despite your ladyship," he mused, "had not the elder brother made his appearance. I have been attempting to console my poor Maria by reminding her that losing this wager was, after all, a piece of good fortune."

"Suppose she had married Harry, and then Cousin George had emerged?" the Baroness suggested.

"Effectivement!" cried Eugene, taking a pinch of snuff. "Let us be grateful to the grave for disgorging its dead in time! I must admit, Mr. George Warrington appears a man of sense, and not more selfish than other elder sons. My poor Molly fancied him a greenhorn, like his younger brother. She hoped he might share equally with Twin Junior; so infatuated was she, I believe she would have taken him. 'Harry Warrington with half a loaf might suffice,' I reasoned, 'but Harry Warrington with no bread, my dear!'"

"How no bread?" asked the Baroness.

"Well, no bread save at his brother's side-table," Eugene explained. "The elder brother said as much."

"What a hard-hearted wretch!" exclaimed Madame de Bernstein.

"Ah, bah! I play with you, aunt, *cartes sur table*!" Lord Castlewood laughed. "Mr. George merely did what anyone else would do; we have no right to be angry. Molly herself acknowledged as much after her initial grief subsided, and I brought her to listen to reason. The silly old creature! So wild about a young lad at her time of life!"

"'Twas a real passion, I almost believe," Madame de Bernstein observed.

"You should have heard her take leave of him. *C'était touchant*, I swear! Before George, I could not help myself. The young fellow, with muddy stockings and hair falling over his eyes, threw himself among us at dinner, making his offer to Molly in a frank, noble manner, and in good language. Her reply? *Begad*, it recalled Mrs. Woffington in the new Scottish play, *Douglas*. She clung to the lad, bidding him adieu in heartrending accents, then stepped out of the room in stately despair—no more chocolate, thank you. If she had committed a *mauvais pas*, no one could retreat with more dignity. 'Twas a masterly withdrawal after a defeat. We were starved out of position, but we retired with all the honours of war."

"Molly will not die of disappointment!" said the Baroness, sipping her tea.

"My lord snarled a grin, revealing yellow teeth. "He, he!" he chuckled. "She has suffered this malady severally before and recovered perfectly. It does not kill, as your ladyship knows, at Molly's age."

Indeed, how could her ladyship know? She did not marry Dr. Tusher until late in life, nor assume the title of Madame de Bernstein until later. As the poet Old Dido noted, ignorance of misfortune breeds lack of compassion for the wretched.

In the little world, people quarrel, abuse one another, and remain estranged for ages. But in the great world, differences are temporary. Parties separate, then reunite without inquiry, granting a stray prodigal or puppy an amnesty regardless of the ugly company kept during absence. For six months, the Castlewoods and Madame de Bernstein had battled for possession of poor Harry Warrington, leaving the two branches of the Esmond family apart. Now, with the matter settled, they were free to meet again as though no division had ever existed. Madame de Bernstein drove her great coach to Lady Castlewood's rout, and the Esmond ladies greeted her with smiles, loving each other as fervently as before.

"So, sir, I hear you acted like a hard-hearted monster regarding your poor brother Harry!" the Baroness exclaimed, delighted and brandishing her stick at George.

"I acted solely upon your ladyship's hint, wishing to discern whether his kin sought him for himself or his reputed wealth," George replied, flushing slightly.

"Nay, Maria could not marry a pauper utterly destitute, whose elder brother vowed to give him nothing!"

"I did it for the best, madam," George insisted, still blushing.

"And so thou didst, O thou hypocrite!" cried the old lady.

"Hypocrite, madam! And why?" asked Mr. Warrington, drawing himself up with considerable state.

"I know all, my infant!" the Baroness declared in French. "Thou art very like thy grandfather. Come, I embrace thee! Harry has told me everything, and thou hast divided thy little patrimony with him!"

"It was but natural, madam. We have shared common hearts and purses since birth. I merely feigned hard-heartedness to test those people yonder," George said, his eyes filling.

"And wilt thou divide Virginia with him too?" asked the Bernstein.

"I cannot say so. It would not be just," replied Mr. Warrington. "Land must pass to the eldest-born, and Harry would not have it otherwise. Perhaps I shall die, or my mother may outlive both of us. But half of what is mine belongs to him. Remember, he was extravagant only because he was mistaken regarding his position."

"But it is a knight of old, it is a Bayard, it is the grandfather come to life!" cried Madame de Bernstein to her attendant as she prepared to retire.

That evening, when the lads departed, it was to poor Harry she gave two fingers, and to George the rouged cheek—a mark of favor so profound it caused George to blush as deeply as the often-dyed rose beside it.

Though Harry Warrington was naturally devoid of envy and held his brother as his chief guide and superior, a profound sense of humiliation and disappointment oppressed the young man following his deposition from the status of Fortunate Youth and heir to boundless Virginian territories. Friends at Kensington promised and vowed to love him all the better after his fall; Harry bowed low and expressed gratitude, yet he could not help noticing that at the state entertainment hosted by Lord Castlewood, George was all-consuming to the cousins. All conversation, compliments, and attentions centered on George, while Harry received notice only from poor Maria. She followed him with wistful glances, her eyes conveying dismal reproaches, as if blaming him for accepting her generosity. *"Ah!"* her gaze seemed to whisper, *"It is mighty well of you, Harry, to accept the freedom I offered, but I never intended for you to be so pleased at being let off."* She had given him up, yet she did not fully forgive him for taking her at her word. She would not have him, and yet she would. Oh, my young friends, how delightful the beginning of a love affair, and how undignified, sometimes, is the end! What a romantic vista opens before young Damon and Phillis—or their middle-aged counterparts—when, after declaring their artless loves, they twine their arms around each other's waists, gazing upon the charming *pays du tendre* spread before them! Into that realm, so intimately linked, they wander until extreme old age. Rocks and roaring rivers may lie ahead, but surely Damon's strong love carries Sweetheart across them? Dragons and dangers may block the path, but shall not his courageous sword cut them down? Then, at eve, how they rest cuddled together like two babes in the woods, moss their couch, stars their canopy, arms their mutual pillows! This is the wise plan young folks adopt when embarking on the journey of love—and, O me!--they travel not a mile before encountering a great wall, forcing them

Our Virginians, possessing a wisdom far more common in youth than in age, had resolved to embrace strong Jacobite sentiments and to profess a profound, almost theatrical, affection for the exiled royal family. The Banished Prince, recognizing Madam Esmond's father as the legitimate Marquis of Esmond, found her forgiveness easy to grant; after all, the unfortunate Stuarts were so eager to acknowledge the superior merits of her lineage. Any lingering scandal regarding her sister, Madame de Bernstein, and the Old Chevalier, she dismissed with a wave of scornful elegance, declaring with perfect truth that the first two monarchs of the House of Hanover were scarcely less guilty of domestic impropriety than any Stuart. Yet, despite the moral equivalence, the *de facto* King remained the King. Having been solemnly crowned and anointed within the sacred walls of St. Paul's, and having utterly routed the *de jure* claimant in the field of battle, the reigning sovereign held undisputed authority. In Madam Esmond's clear estimation, it was both the sons' duty and the sovereign's pleasure for them to appear at court. If His Majesty were inclined to bestow a lucrative office or a ribbon of blue or red upon either lad, she entertained no surprise. She was convinced that the King knew the Virginian Esmonds as intimately as any member of the nobility. Consequently, the lads were strictly commanded to present themselves at court. Indeed, I suspect their mother would have been furious had she known that George had donned Harry's laced coat on the very morning he went to bow at Kensington.

A century prior, the King's drawing-room stood open to his nobility and gentry almost daily, allowing loyalty to indulge itself a dozen times a month in the august sight of the sovereign. Driven by a prudent avoidance of the enemy's fleets and a gracious sorrow for the potential loss of the royal person to French corsairs, the monarch had forsaken his beloved visits to Herrenhausen in Hanover. Compelled to reside, albeit unwillingly, amongst his loving subjects in Britain, he found solace in the company of a Hanoverian lady, the Countess of Walmoden, whose virtues had endeared her to the Prince. From her lips alone could the revered Defender of the Faith hear the accents of his native tongue.

It was Mr. Warrington who requested an assiduous courtier, his uncle, to present him to the King. For Mr. Lambert, also required to render thanks for his recent promotion, the journey to court lay inevitable. Thus, the two gentlemen departed together, hiring a hackney-coach, as Lord Wrotham's carriage was needed by its rightful owner, who had returned to town. At Kensington Palace Gate, the sentries recognized and saluted the good General, guiding the pair modestly on foot toward the sovereign's summer residence. Passing beneath the portico, they traversed a gallery leading to the great black marble staircase—richly decorated and painted by the renowned architect Mr. Kent—and moved through several sumptuously hung chambers, adorned with tapestries, portraits, and busts, until they reached the King's great drawing-room. Here hung the famous "Venus" by Titian and, among other masterpieces, the "St. Francis Adoring the Infant Saviour" by Sir Peter Paul Rubens. Within this grand hall, amidst the throng of courtiers, the gentlemen waited for His Majesty to issue from his private apartments, where he was currently conferring with certain personages the newspapers of the day termed "Majesty's Ministers."

George Warrington, having never before beheld a palace, had ample leisure to admire the splendor surrounding him. For the first time, he gazed upon fine paintings, delighting in the charming portrait of Sir Anthony Van Dyck depicting King Charles I, his Queen, and Family, as well as the magnificent "Esther Before Ahasuerus" by Tintoret, wherein all figures wore the resplendent Venetian habit. So entranced by these works was he that he barely registered the whispers of his friend, the General, who was murmuring into his young companion's nearly heedless ear the names of the distinguished persons arrayed around them.

"Yonder," intoned Mr. Lambert, "stand two of my Lords of the Admiralty, Mr. Gilbert Elliot and Admiral Boscawen—that same Boscawen whose fleet fired the first gun in your waters two years past. That stout gentleman, belted with gold, is Mr. Fox, who served as Minister and now accepts the role of Paymaster with a substantial salary."

"He carries the *auri fames* upon his person," observed George, noting the general's waistcoat. "Why, his vest is a perfect Potosí!"

*"Aliena appetens"*—"How goes the text?" continued General Lambert. "He loves to acquire money and squander it. Yonder stands my Lord Chief Justice Willes, conversing with my Lord of Salisbury and Doctor Headley. Should he serve his God as faithfully as he serves His King, he will surely be translated to some very high promotion in Heaven. He belongs to your grandfather's era and was cherished by Dick Steele yet despised by the Dean. Beside them sits my Lord of London, the learned Doctor Sherlock. The Lords of the Lawn Sleeves have lost half their honors now. I recall when I was a boy, still in my mother's hand, she compelled me to kneel before the Bishop of Rochester, who crossed the water to become Minister to a certain Perkin's Bishop. That handsome fair fellow is Admiral Smith; he presided over the court-martial of the poor Captain Byng and strove in vain to save him from penal punishment. They call him 'Tom of Ten Thousand' in the fleet. The French Ambassador had him broken when he was merely a lieutenant for making a French man-of-war lower its topsails to him, yet the King made him a captain the following day. That tall, haughty-looking gentleman is my Lord George Sackville. Now that I am a Major-General myself, he will treat me somewhat better than a footman. I wish my stout old Blakeney were here; he is the soldier's darling, as kind and brave as that poker-faced nobleman is brave—and I am your lordship's very humble servant."

"This is a young gentleman who has just arrived from America and was present at Braddock's tragic affair two years ago."

"Oh, indeed!" exclaimed the poker-faced nobleman. "I have the honor of speaking to Mr.—?"

"To Major-General Lambert, at your lordship's service, who served in His Majesty's army some time before you entered it. That, Mr. Warrington, is the first Commoner in England, Mr. Speaker Onslow. Where is your uncle? I shall be forced to present you personally to His Majesty if Sir Miles delays much longer." As he spoke, the worthy General directed his attention entirely to his young friend, paying scant regard to his colleague, who stalked away with a startled expression, seemingly amazed at the other's audacity. A hundred years ago, a nobleman was a nobleman and expected to be admired solely as such.

Presently, Sir Miles's red waistcoat appeared in sight, and many cordial greetings exchanged between him, his nephew, and General Lambert. For we have described how Sir Miles was the most affectionate of men. Had the General already left Lord Wrotham's house? It was time, as his lordship himself desired to occupy it. Very well; yet consider what a loss to the neighbors!

"We miss you, we positively miss you, my dear General," cried Sir Miles. "My daughters were in love with those lovely young ladies—I swear they were! My Lady Warrington and my girls debated endlessly over how to find an opportunity to acquaint ourselves with your charming family. We feel as if we were old friends already, indeed we do, General, if you will permit me the liberty of saying so; and we love you, if I may speak frankly, on account of your friendship and kindness to our dear nephews. Though we were a little jealous, I confess a trifle jealous of them, for they visited you so often. Often and often have I said to my Lady Warrington, 'My dear, why do we not make acquaintance with the General? Why do we not invite him and his ladies to dine with some other plain country gentlemen?' Carry my most sincere respects to Mrs. Lambert, I pray, sir, and thank her for her goodness to these young gentlemen. My own flesh and blood, sir; my dear, dear brother's boys!" He passed his hand across his manly eyes, choking almost with generous and affectionate emotion.

While they discoursed—George Warrington meanwhile restraining his laughter with admirable gravity—the doors of the King's apartments opened, and the pages entered, preceding His Majesty. He was followed by his burly son, His Royal Highness the Duke, a very corpulent Prince clad in a coat and face of blazing scarlet. Behind them trailed various gentlemen and officers of state, among whom George immediately recognized the famous Mr. Secretary Pitt, by his towering stature, eagle eye, hooked nose, and grave, majestic presence. As I behold that solemn figure passing, even a hundred years removed, I protest I feel a present awe and a desire to remove my hat. I am not frightened by George II, nor are my eyes dazzled by the portentous appearance of His Royal Highness the Duke of Culloden and Fontenoy; but the Great Commoner, the terrible Cornet of Horse! His figure seems to stride over our narrow island of a century past like a Colossus. I h

Life, I suspect, offers the novelist scant material save for the theater itself. When dealing with the martial arts, where men display valor or cowardice amidst danger and devotion, the writer may venture into reality. But for the merchant selling sugar and spice, the lawyer burying himself in ancient statutes, or the tailor measuring breeches, what narrative substance exists beyond the drudgery of their trade? To chronicle the mundane mechanics of a life spent in linen-drapery or apothecary business would yield a history as dry and unendurable as hobnails. Therefore, authors must depict men removed from their vocations—captured in their passions, loves, laughter, and hatreds—while allowing their professions to remain a silent, subaudient backdrop. Thus, we find ourselves wandering through the lobbies and pleasure-haunts of existence, while the serious world toils quietly in its chambers, unaware of our fleeting glimpses. Corydon carts litter and threshes barley alongside his courtships; Ancillula dresses nurseries and waits tables before snatching a brief moment through railings to kiss Boopis, the policeman; and all day long, boots beat the pavement before a stolen kiss can be secured. We only encounter these souls when they are disengaged from labor, and it is only then that we may unite them with the idle reader.

Poor Harry Warrington, however, found himself increasingly shunned by the "macaronis" and fine gentlemen of White's and Arthur's. The gaming tables and the Mall knew him no more. Madame de Bernstein, hoping to counter the world's indifference, urged her nephew to conquer it with courage, but Harry, too honest to mask his mortification or anger, could not parry slights with the artful flattery required by society.

"You pull a long face, Harry, and complain of the world's treatment of you," the old lady chided, her tone shifting from warmth to patronizing coldness. "Fiddlededee, sir! Everyone must endure impertinences. If you were beaten now, being poor and cast down, you would say nothing, bear it with a smile, and seek revenge ten years later. *Moi qui vous parle*—do I not have humble pies to eat? Now that you are no longer the Fortunate Youth, be the Clever Youth. Win back your place. Go to every rout and party, be civil to everyone, especially women. Show your spirit, which you possess in abundance. With economy and your brother's generosity, you can maintain a genteel figure. Your handsome countenance ensures you will attract a rich heiress. Seek out the merchants in the City; they won't know you're out of fashion at Court. When did you visit my Lady Yarmouth? She took a fancy to you!"

Yet, the old woman who once greeted him with kisses now received him with icy disdain. She became peevish, casting gibes at him before her guests, flushing his honest face with humiliation. Even her servants, formerly eager, ignored him. Lady Yarmouth grew indisposed when he called, and her staff showed no eagerness to offer him dinner, a stark contrast to his days as the Fortunate Youth. Devastated, Harry poured his sorrows to Mrs. Lambert. Sitting glumly in a window-seat while the ladies bustled about, he confessed his aunt's cruelty.

"I know you are the best people alive," he murmured, "and the kindest, and I must be the dullest company in the world... yes, that I am."

"Well, you are not very lively, Harry," observed Miss Hetty, beginning to command him, wondering if this was truly the hero she had taken him to be.

"If he is unhappy, why should he be lively?" Theo asked gently. "He has a good heart, and is pained by his friends' desertion. Surely there is no harm in that?"

"I would have too much spirit to show I was hurt," Hetty cried, clenching her fists. "And I would smile, though that horrible old painted woman boxed my ears. She is horrible, Mamma."

Theo turned red. "I mayn't like her, but there is no reason to call Harry's aunt names before his face."

"You provoking thing! You are always right!" Hetty exclaimed, angry at her consistency. "Indeed, Harry, it was wrong of me to make rude remarks about your relations."

"It doesn't matter about the others, Hetty," Harry said, rising with a flush of righteous anger. "But it seems hard that this one should turn upon me. I had grown so fond of her. It drives me mad when people I love turn away or act unkind."

"Suppose George were to do so?" Hetty suggested.

"You are very clever and lively, and you may suppose many things," Harry retorted, standing resolute, "but not that, Hetty. You don't know my brother as I do—you wouldn't take such liberty as to suppose my brother George capable of any unkind or unworthy act!"

Hetty turned pale, looking up at Harry before falling silent. Taking his leave, Harry apologized simply for any rough words, insisting he always defended George. Hetty offered no verbal reply, only a prim curtsey and a hand.

Later, making curl-paper confidences, Hetty admitted to Theo, "Oh! I thought it would be so happy to see him every day... and now I offend him. I can't help it. I know he isn't clever, Theo. But oh! Isn't he good, kind, and brave? Didn't he look handsome when he was angry?"

"Silly little thing," Theo replied, "you try to make him look handsome."

Among these youths, General Lambert applied the monosyllable "Bo" to his daughter-in-law's mother as a rebuke for her sentimental matchmaking propensities, dismissing the idea that the two Virginian lads would fall in love with the Lambert girls. "Little Het might have her fancy," the General noted, "but Harry evidently hadn't been smitten. Now, with an older brother over him, and unable to even claim his coat as his own, Master Harry wasn't a great catch."

"Oh yes: now he is poor, we will show him the door," Mrs. Lambert sighed.

"That is what I always do, isn't it, Molly? Turn my back on friends in distress?" the General asked.

"No, my dear! I am a goose, now, and that I own, Martin!" the wife conceded, producing a handkerchief. "Let the poor boy come to us. Ours is almost the only house in this selfish place where so much can be said for him. He is unhappy; to be with us puts him at ease!"

Accordingly, whenever crestfallen Hal needed a meal or an evening, Mr. Lambert's table opened a corner for him. George, too, was welcome. Though initially cooler and more mistrustful than his twin, George soon learned to love the Lamberts for their inherent goodness and their constant kindness to Harry. He saw the depth of Harry's misery and pitied his brother's depression. In his sarcastic vein, George would often tease himself for returning to life, joking that Florac had done better to leave his scalp as an ornament for an Indian's belt, lamenting that if he hadn't returned, Harry's favorite, Maria, would have married him anyway, thus avenging the gods upon Harry's prosperity.

"I never know whether you are laughing at me or yourself, George," Harry remarked.

"Precisely my own case, Harry, my dear!" George agreed. "But I know there has never been a better brother in the world, nor better people than the Lamberts."

"Never truer words spoken!" cried George, taking Harry's hand. "If I'm unhappy, it's not your fault—theirs, or mine. 'Tis fate, you see. 'Tis having nothing to do. I must work, and how, George? That is the question."

"We'll ask Mother," George said. "Until we hear from her..."

"Do you know," Harry whispered, alarm in his voice, "I don't think I'd like going back to Virginia?"

"In love with one of the lasses here?" George teased.

"Love 'em like sisters—with all my heart! But I've come out of that business, thanks to you. I don't want to go back. Hunting, duck-shooting, tobacco-planting, whist-playing, sermons over and over again—that's all there is at home. For a poor fellow like me, what chance is there? Until I do something, George, I shall be miserable!"

"Have I not always said so? Are you not coming round to my opinion?"

"What opinion, George? You know pretty much whatever you think, I think, George!"

"That Florac had best have left the Indian to take my scalp, my dear!"

At this, Harry burst out with an angry exclamation, and they continued to puff their pipes in friendly union.

Though they lived together, little interchange occurred beyond affection. Harry, never venturing near George's books, sat dumb as a mouse while his brother studied. Soon, they moved from the Court end of town, Madame de Bernstein pishing and pshaing at their departure. George, however, developed a fondness for Sir Hans Sloane's new reading room and museum in Montagu House, taking cheerful lodgings in Southampton Row, Bloomsbury, overlooking Hampstead. Meanwhile, Lord Wrotham's family arrived in Mayfair, and Mr. Lambert, detained by business, shifted to furnished apartments in Soho

Once the theatrical performances drew to a close, our friends departed for Mr. Warrington's lodging, where the Virginians had arranged a sumptuous supper. Mr. Warrington, eager to honor them in the finest style, extended an invitation that the General and his wife gladly accepted, delighted at the prospect of making their young friends happy. The gathering comprised the General and Mrs. Lambert, their collegiate son, their two radiant daughters, and Mr. Spencer of the Temple—a newfound acquaintance George had met at the coffee-house—and all shared the landlady's fare with high spirits. While the precise arrangement of seats remains somewhat elusive, it is evident that Miss Theo sat beside the chickens, while Mr. George Warrington found himself opposite Miss Hetty and a substantial ham, both of whom vied for Mr. Harry's attention. Mrs. Lambert likely occupied George's right hand, leaving the central trio of the General, his son, and the Templar to fill the remaining spaces.

Mr. Spencer, having attended the rival theatre previously, had once introduced George to the actors' greenroom. Conversation naturally drifted back to the play, with several members of the party adamant in their delight. "As for what our gentlemen say, sir," Mrs. Lambert interjected to Mr. Spencer, "you must not credit a word of it. 'Tis a masterpiece, yet my husband and Mr. George conducted themselves abysmally." "We laughed at the wrong moments and wept when silence was required," the General admitted candidly. "You caused the spectators in the boxes to shout 'Hush!' and the pit to demand 'Silence!' I have never witnessed such behavior, and I blush for you, Mr. Lambert!" "Mother considered it a tragedy, whereas we viewed it merely as amusement," the General explained. "George and I acted perfectly, did we not, Theo?" "Not when I was looking your way, Papa!" Theo replied. The General retorted, "Was there ever such a saucy creature?" To which Theo demurred, "You know, sir, I spoke only when prompted. I was deeply moved by the play, particularly by Mrs. Woffington's beauty and acting. I felt sorrow for the mother losing her child, and I am sorry, Papa, if I erred in feeling so!" "Women lack the cunning of men, you know, Theo," Hetty chimed in from across the table, glancing slyly at Harry. "Next time we attend a play, Brother Jack, please pinch us when we ought to weep or nudge us when laughter is due." "I wish we had seen the duel," the General mused. "The clash between little Norval and the towering Norwegian would have been exquisite sport. You should write to Jack and propose it to Mr. Rich, the manager." "I have not seen that scene," Harry said solemnly, wondering if his comment carried wit amidst the general mirth. "However, I saw Slack and Broughton at Marybone Gardens!" he added. "It requires no giant," Harry continued, "to topple that diminutive figure in red boots. For my part, I could hoist him over my shoulder." "Mr. Garrick is small, yet he possesses a presence that commands awe," Mr. Spencer noted. "Recall his portrayal of Macbeth! That dagger scene was terrifying! You should have observed our host, ladies! I introduced Mr. Warrington to Mr. Garrick and Mrs. Pritchard in the greenroom, and Lady Macbeth graciously accepted a pinch from his snuffbox." "Did the Wife of the Thane of Cawdor sneeze?" the General inquired with dramatic gravity. "She thanked Mr. Warrington in a voice so hollow and tragic that he recoiled, spilling his rappee, for Macbeth sneezed three times." "Macbeth! Macbeth! Macbeth!" cried the General. "And the philosopher standing near Mr. Johnson declared, 'Mind, Davy, lest thy sneeze awaken Duncan!' who, coincidentally, was conversing with the three witches seated against the wall." "Have you been behind the scenes?" Theo exclaimed, eyes wide with longing. "To see the ropes, smell the tallow, gaze upon the pasteboard gold and tinsel jewels, and behold the painted matrons?" "Do not look too closely," warned the skeptical host, sipping his hock. "You were angry with your father and me." "Nay, George!" the girl protested. "Nay? I say yes! You angered us because we laughed when you sought to weep. Speaking for myself and others," George bowed to the General, "we refrained from tears not out of unfeeling hearts, but because we stood behind the author's curtain. Up close, we saw through the young hero's rant and tinsel; the pale mother's pallor was mere chalk, her grief nothing more than a pocket-handkerchief. Admit it, Theo, you deemed me unfeeling?" "If you deduce it without confession, what purpose serves my admission?" Theo countered. "Suppose I were to perish," George ventured, "and saw Harry grieve. Then you would witness genuine affliction, a true tragedy, and mourn accordingly. Yet you would remain unmoved if you saw the undertaker in his weeds and black cloak!" "Indeed, but I should," Mrs. Lambert assured, "as would any daughter of mine." "Perhaps we might find weepers of our own, Mr. Warrington," Theo suggested, "in such a dire event." "Would you?" George cried, his cheeks flushing alongside Theo's, both aware of Hetty's watchful gaze. "The older writers grasped little of the pathos," Mr. Spencer, the wit of the Temple, observed. "Consider Sophocles and Antigone," John Lambert called out. "Faith, our wits ignore him save when an Oxford scholar reminds us!" Spencer dismissed the ancients, preferring the moderns. "Shakespeare, however, fails to capture the elegance and pathos of contemporary dramatists. Has he ever matched Belvidera, Monimia, or Jane Shore? Can his comic females rival Congreve?" As he distributed snuff, someone queried if Spencer himself had penned a play. "Many idle gentlemen have tried," Spencer conceded. "Mr. Garrick returned a piece of mine." "I confess I possess four acts of a play in one of my boxes," George revealed. "I wager it rivals the best," Harry whispered to his neighbor. "Is it a tragedy or comedy?" Mrs. Lambert asked. "A tragedy, rife with two or three grisly murders!" George replied. "Let us stage it and have the audience watch their steps! Though my favorite genre remains tyranny," the General declared. "The tragedy! Fetch it instantly, Gumbo!" Mrs. Lambert commanded the footman. Gumbo bowed, confirming the request. "In the large cowhide trunk," George instructed gravely. "Yes, sir," Gumbo responded with elevated formality. "But my tragedy lies buried beneath layers of linen, packages, books, and boots, Hetty." "Fret not; retrieve it and cast the linens from the window!" Hetty cried. "The trunk resides with our agent in Bristol," George announced. "Gumbo must secure post-horses, and we shall await its return until the morrow thereafter." The ladies emitted a comical "Oh!" while the General, perhaps more seriously, proposed gratitude for their escape. "Let us consider returning home. Our young hosts have treated us nobly. We shall drink a final toast to Madam Esmond Warrington of Castlewood, Virginia. Imagine, boys, finding a tall, handsome stepfather upon your return! Ladies of her age have long since remarried." "To Madam Esmond Warrington, my old schoolmate!" Mrs. Lambert toasted. "I shall pen a letter detailing this splendid supper her sons bestowed upon us, and, Mr. George, I shall omit mention of your playhouse misbehavior!" With this, the company dispersed; the General's coach, accompanied by a servant holding a flambeau, awaited his family.

Following such an illustrious entertainment, it seemed natural for Mr. Warrington to visit his guests, ensuring their safe arrival and rest. Their coach could easily have traversed the countryside behind Montague House toward Oxford Road, potentially falling prey to highwaymen, or the ladies might have succumbed to chills or restless sleep following the emotional exertion. Thus, no excuse existed for his inquiry, nor for delaying his departure from the Sloane Museum. Perhaps, as he strolled away, his thoughts wandered, remembering little of what he had read.

"What signifies this eagerness, this hesitation, this shilly-shallying?" the narrator pondered, observing Warrington's trembling hand as he knocked at Mr. Lambert's lodgings in Dean Street. "Does any young man read? Does any aged soul recall the vigor of youth? Time has passed, yet memory endures. Like Shakespeare's Harry promising survival to his soldiers at Agincourt, we may, even now, gallop briefly in the paddock, kicking up our old heels before being halted. I care less for clowns and Pantaloons now, nor for the fairy whose verses I find tedious; yet I delight in witnessing children at a pantomime. I do not dance or dine, but I love to watch Eugenio and Flirtilla twirl, or Lucinda and Ardentio crack a cracker. Burn your little fingers, children! Let kind flames blaze from your eyes, then draw close and share the sweetmeat, crunching it between

To the loyal hearts of Britain, the account of Mr. George Warrington's conduct toward a gallant and gracious Prince—the beloved son of the greatest monarch and Captain-General of the Army—must inevitably stir a spirit of respectful indignation. Consider the inestimable favor slighted; consider the chance of promotion cast aside like a discarded glove. It seemed to Will Esmond, whose tongue was always heavy with blasphemy, that his cousin's behavior demanded the strongest curses in the book. He voiced his delight that the "confounded young Mohock" was effectively cutting his own throat, wishing nothing more than to see the younger brother descend to hell, followed closely by the elder. Upon first meeting him, Will declared he would ensure George understood precisely what he thought of him.

"If you intend to insult George, at least take care that his brother Harry is out of hearing!" cried Lady Maria, her voice sharp enough to summon further imprecations from Mr. Will regarding his twin kin.

"My lord!" said Will. "No more squabbling! We cannot have warriors galore in the family."

"I never heard your lordship claimed as one!" Maria retorted.

"Never, my dear; quite the contrary! Will is our champion, and one is quite sufficient for the house. So I dare say, with the two Mohocks present; George is the student, and Harry is the fighting man. When you intended to quarrel, Will, what a pity it was you had not George, instead of the other, to your hand!"

"Your lordship's hand is famous—at piquet," observed Will's mother.

"It is a pretty one," said my lord, examining his fingers with a simpering air. "My Lord Hervey's glove and mine were of a size. Yes, my hand, as you say, is more fitted for cards than for war. Yours, my Lady Castlewood, is pretty dexterous, too. How I bless the day when you bestowed it on my lamented father!" In this playful exchange, as in other games of skill, his lordship found satisfaction, possessing a cool head capable of beating his entire family around.

When Madame de Bernstein learned of Mr. Warrington's blunder, she was exceedingly angry, storming and scolding her household. She would have scolded George herself, but age had eroded the courage of her youth, and she harbored a lingering fear of her nephew, treating him with undue respect. "You will never make your fortune at court, nephew!" she groaned when he visited her soon after his discomfiture.

"It was never my wish, madam," said Mr. George, in a stately manner.

"Your wish was to help Harry? You might have been of service to your brother had you accepted the Duke's offer. Princes do not love to have their favors refused, and I do not wonder that his Royal Highness was offended."

"General Lambert said the same thing," George confessed, turning slightly red. "And I see now that I was wrong. But you must remember I had never seen a court before, and I suppose I am scarce likely to shine in one."

"I think possibly not, my good nephew," said the aunt, taking a pinch of snuff. "And what then?" asked George. "I never had ambition for that kind of glory, and can make myself quite easy without it. When his Royal Highness spoke to me—most kindly, as I own—my thought was, 'I shall make a very bad soldier, and my brother would be a very good one.' He possesses a hundred qualities for the profession in which I am deficient; he would serve a Commanding Officer far better than I ever could. Say the Duke is in battle, and his horse is shot, as my poor chief's was at home; would he not be better borne by a beast with courage and strength to carry him anywhere, than by one that could not support his weight?"

"Au fait. His Royal Highness's charger must be a strong one, my dear!" remarked the old lady.

"Expende Hannibalem," muttered George with a shrug. "Our Hannibal weighs no trifle."

"I do not quite follow you, sir, and your Hannibal," the Baroness observed.

"When Mr. Wolfe and Mr. Lambert remonstrated with me as you have done, madam," George replied with a laugh, "I made this same defense. I told them I offered the Prince the best soldier in the family, and the gentlemen allowed that my blunder at least had some excuse. Who knows but they may set me right with his Royal Highness? The taste I have had of battles has shown me how little my genius inclines that way. We saw *The Scottish Chiefs* last night, and when the hero, young Norval, spoke of longing to follow some warlike lord to the field, I thought to myself, 'How like my Harry is to him, save that he does not brag!' Harry is currently pining for a red coat, and if we do not mind, will take the shilling. He keeps the map of Germany perpetually under his eyes and follows the King of Prussia wherever he goes. He fears neither men nor gods. As for me, I love my books and quiet above all else, preferring to read about battles in Homer or Lucan."

"Then what made a soldier of you at all, my dear? And why did you not send Harry with Mr. Braddock, instead of going yourself?" Madame de Bernstein pressed.

"My mother loved her younger son the best," George answered darkly. "Besides, with the enemy invading our country, it was my duty, as the head of our family, to go on the campaign. Had I been a Scotchman twelve years ago, I should have been..."

"Hush, sir! Or I shall be more angry than ever!" the old lady exclaimed, though with a perfectly pleased expression.

George's explanation succeeded in appeasing Madame de Bernstein, whose principles were, we fear, somewhat loose. However, to the loyal hearts of Sir Miles Warrington and his lady, the young man's conduct delivered a severe blow. "I should have thought," her ladyship said, "from my sister Esmond Warrington's letter, that my brother's widow was a woman of good sense and judgment, and that she had educated her sons in a becoming manner. But what, Sir Miles, what, my dear Thomas Claypool, can we think of an education which has resulted so lamentably for both these young men?"

"The elder seems to know a power of Latin, though, and speaks French and German too," noted Mr. Claypool. "I heard him with the Hanover Envoy at the Baroness's rout. The French he jabbered easily; and when he was at a loss for High Dutch, he and the Envoy began in Latin, talking away until all the room stared."

"It is not language, but principles, Thomas Claypool!" the virtuous matron cried. "What must Mr. Warrington's principles be when he could reject an offer made by his Prince? Can he speak High Dutch? So much the more ought he to have accepted his Royal Highness's condescension and made himself useful in the campaign! Look at our son, look at Miles!"

"Hold up thy head, Miley, my boy!" urged Papa.

"I trust, Sir Miles, that as a member of the House of Commons and an English gentleman, you will attend his Royal Highness's levee tomorrow and say, if such an offer had been made to us for that child, we would have taken it, though our boy is but ten years of age."

"Faith, Miley, thou wouldst make a good little drummer or fifer!" Papa chuckled. "Shouldst like to be a little soldier, Miley?"

"Anything, sir, anything! A Warrington ought to be ready at any moment to have himself cut in pieces for his sovereign!" cried the matron, pointing to the boy. The child, however, as soon as he comprehended his mother's proposal, protested with a loud roar, amidst which he was spirited away by Screwby. In obedience to these conjugal orders, Sir Miles attended the levee the next day and made a protest of his love and duty, which the Prince graciously accepted, remarking:

"Nobody ever supposed that Sir Miles Warrington would ever refuse any place offered to him."

A compliment indeed, repeated everywhere by Lady Warrington, demonstrating how implicitly the august royal family relied on the loyalty of the Warringtons.

Consequently, when this worthy couple saw George, they received him with a ghastly commiseration—a look akin to that extended by dear relatives when we have done something fatal or clumsy, entered a room just as the company has been abusing us, or seen our names in the commercial columns of the London Gazette. Who does not know that face of pity? Whose dear relations have not so deplored him, not dead, but living? If you have never been in scrapes, never sown a handful of wild oats, always remained fortunate, good, and careful, if butter has never melted in your mouth and an imprudent word escaped your lips, if you have never sinned and repented, then, sir, you are a wiseacre who will not waste time over an idle novel, and it is not *de te* that the fable is narrated.

It was hardly fair for Sir Miles to turn upon George, angry with his nephew for refusing the promotion offered by his Royal Highness, for Sir Miles himself had agreed with George's view

If Harry Warrington harboured a passion for martial pursuits, the atmosphere of Europe in 1757 was thick enough with the smoke of battle to fan it into a blaze. Though the Hanoverian cause had stumbled, the King of Prussia stood triumphant, his victories echoing through every salon and society where Harry moved. Yet, fortune seemed determined to spare him the horrors of the battlefield. Had Harry ridden to Rossbach on that infamous "Gunpowder Fête" or to the slaughter at Leuthen, the pages of history would have been filled with accounts written by another pen. It was a mercy, I say, that Harry did not follow the Borussian King. Imagine, if you will, the clash of my jaunty yellow phaeton against the grim chariot of thundering war; could my meek little Pegasus withstand the shock of a foaming, flame-breathed steed? I am content, dear reader, to let the great battles wait for later volumes, lest I be forced to compete with masters who paint the grim realities of conflict far more vividly than I could.

Yet, Harry did not merely avoid war; he pined for it. His existence devolved into a sulky, useless languor. He haunted military coffee-houses, his sole reading material limited to newspapers, dismissing literature and novels alike as foolish trifles. He possessed a hearty laugh, but it often came a beat too late, long after the joke had evaporated. Was it necessary for every gentleman to be a scholar? Why should one friend be deemed inferior for never having penned a couplet? Ruined, idle, and dependent on his brother's charity, Harry slept over books he could not comprehend, his great, strong hands finding no work to grip. Consider Achilles, exiled to the court of King Peleus to escape harm, yet idled away among women, eventually fathering Pyrrhus. A mettlesome youth like Harry, trailing behind distaffs or serving coffee, risked falling into mischief far worse than any battlefield.

In the circle of the Lambert family, the reaction to Harry's state varied sharply. The two elder sisters, particularly Theo, offered an untiring, maternal pity, the sort reserved for a few kind souls alone. They sought to cheer his grief, soothe his wrath, and mend the wounds inflicted by Fortune. But Hetty, the youngest, possessed a heart that seemed hardened and savage toward him. While the world was in arms, she chafed that Harry carried no musket. To her, it was harvest time; surely he could wield a sickle and reap a sheaf of glory instead of loafing around.

"Papa," Mrs. Lambert observed after a scene where Miss Hetty had been firing little verbal shots at Harry's quivering self-esteem, "how savage the little thing is with him!"
"Mamma," Theo interjected, "her conduct is abominable. She deserves to be whipped. Perhaps it is because she likes him better than any of us?"
Mrs. Lambert smiled gently. "Your father is so superior to the world, he has spoiled us."
"And so brave?" Mrs. Lambert pressed.
"I think Mr. Wolfe, Harry, or George are very brave," Theo admitted. "As for learning, Mr. George is certainly the most learned and witty. He chooses the finest things in books, loving them despite his satire. He treats the children to apples and gives coins to beggars with a charm I have rarely seen. Even regarding *Tartuffe*, he admits it is clever, though a mere villain is too mean a subject for a tragedy's lead."
"But you know the verses by heart?" Mrs. Lambert asked.
"Oh yes, Mamma! ... Nonsense!" Theo replied, blushing like a rose and fleeing the room. Her cheeks burned not merely from modesty, but from a fluttering heart, perhaps culling fresh flowers in honor of George's arrival. Where George came, Theo descended; where Harry knocked, she remained upstairs, sending a brother or sister to greet him. Theo, with her keen eyes, noted instantly the distinction between Harry's golden hair and George's dark locks, ensuring she never mistook them, whereas Hetty showed no excitement whatsoever when the black-haired gentleman entered.

One afternoon, Harry sat on a couch, draped in despondency, when Miss Hetty descended. After the usual pleasantries, she inquired, "Pray, sir, what makes your honour look so glum?"
"Ah, Hetty!" Harry sighed. "I have nothing else to do but to look glum. Remember when we were boys? I was always asking for holidays to swing on gates or make ducks and drakes. Those do-nothing days were the most melancholy. What have I to do now from morn till night?"
"Breakfast, walk—dinner, walk—tea, supper, and a pipe of your Virginia," Hetty tossed back.
Harry confessed that returning to the Chartreux, he wished to plead with the master to teach him Latin and Greek again, to repair his childhood idleness. "I might as well go to my books," he murmured. "Why do you laugh?"
"I laugh to fancy you at the head of a class!" Hetty cried.
"I shouldn't be," Harry humbled himself. "George might lead any class, but I am no bookman. I neglected myself. If our tutors had caned us, it might have done me good."
"What does Tom say about the caning?" Hetty asked, tapping her foot.
"It makes me see that I was idle when I should have worked," Harry replied simply. "I have no genius for books. Am I good for anything? Only to spend my patrimony, lounge at coffee-houses, or gallop behind hounds."
"Such a great, brave, strong fellow good for nothing?" Hetty exclaimed. "I would not confess as much to any woman, if I were twice as useless!"
"What am I to do?" Harry asked, his voice dropping. "I seek leave to join the army, but Madam Esmond ignores me. I have spent all I have and borrowed heavily from my brother. I cannot ask for more. If Mother would send me, I would jump to go."
"Eh!" Hetty scoffed. "A gentleman of spirit does not want a woman to buckle his sword on! What of Sir John Armytage? Engaged to a famous beauty, Miss Howe, yet he volunteered immediately when the King asked."
"He used to frequent White's," Harry noted.
"So?" Hetty marched about the room, adopting a courageous military stance. "An obstacle to glory? I think no woman of spirit would say 'Stay!' when her country says 'Go!' My father longs to serve, and both my brothers are destined for the Church. As for me, I know I should have made a famous little soldier!" Striding boldly, she mimicked a warrior.
Harry watched her with tender admiration. "I hardly think I could see a musket on those shoulders, Hetty, nor a wound on that pretty face."
"Wounds! Who fears wounds?" Hetty cried. "Muskets? Men think us good only for puddings. Look, George read Tasso to us yesterday. Hear these verses: 'Alas! why did not Heaven these members frail / With lively force and vigour strengthen, so / That I this silken gown... Might for a breastplate and a helm forgo; / Then should not heat, nor cold, nor rain, nor hail, / Nor storms withhold me; but I would, both day and night, / In pitched field or private combat, fight.'"
"Silken gown?" Harry inquired, puzzled.
"Well, sir, it's Calimanco," Hetty corrected. "'Then should not heat... Withhold me; but I would, both day and night, / In pitched field or private combat, fight.'"
"Why are both my brothers to be parsons?" Hetty demanded. "One of Papa's children ought to be a soldier!"
Harry laughed, a gentle, kind sound. He felt no desire to strike such a tender little warrior. "I think," he said, extending a finger, "here is a digit nearly as large as your arm. How would you stand before a great, strong man?"
"Do you suppose any scoundrel would dare injure you?" Harry paced the floor, agitated by the thought of a French rogue offending Miss Hester Lambert. This silent courage subjugated Hetty; she believed in his strength, causing her specially to admire him. Yet, Miss Hetty was no virago. Had she truly been bold, she might have preferred a soft, literary suitor, but nature drew the burly hero to the frail, tender woman. If Harry fell in love with her, the end would likely be a union of opposites.
