For a while, Harry barely felt the sting of Hetty's sarcasm, letting her shoot her arrows without parrying. Did she intend to shame him by suggesting a weak woman would don armor? The simple fellow either melted at the prospect of her danger or laughed at the absurdity.
"What is the use of a strong hand if it only holds silk for my mother?" Hetty cried. "James Wolfe seems weakly and ill, coughing like a ghost-seer."
"I never understood why a man should fear a ghost," Harry said.
"Haven't you seen one?"
"No. Nathan in his night-shirt at home. Our nurses lie."
"George was frightened."
"Yes, but he is different from me."
"George says you would have made a better soldier."
"If I had been allowed to try," Harry

George Warrington allowed his legal studies no hindrance to his comforts or his constitution. Madam Esmond, in her letters, had taught him that though he sat amidst nameless students in hall-commons, he bore a name of ancient dignity and could stand in rank with the foremost of his country. She urged him to study as a gentleman, not as a mere professional drudge. To this injunction, the young man complied. Thus, he was not of the rank-and-file of the law, but a volunteer in her service, akin to those young gentlemen of whom we have spoken. While the law a century prior was still a jealous mistress demanding exclusive attention—far less rigorous than she is nowadays, who scarcely grants her votaries time for amusement, recreation, sleep, or dinner—it allowed its disciples considerable leisure. Murray, it is said, might have been an Ovid but preferred the ermine of the Lord Chief Justice. Perhaps Mr. Warrington might have risen to a peerage and the woolsack had he studied longer, been a dexterous courtier, and a favorite of attorneys. As it was, he behaved to Themis with decent respect, yet he loved letters far more than law. The black-letter of Chaucer was infinitely more agreeable to him than the Gothic pages of Hale and Coke.

In those quaint times, letters were truly loved, and authors held sway as authorities. Gentlemen appealed to Virgil or Lucan in Courts and Parliament. "What said Statius, Juvenal?" they asked, "let alone Tully or Tacitus!" on such points. Their reign is now ended; the good old Heathens are no more in fashion than the worship of Jupiter and Juno. The age of economists and calculators has superseded theirs; Tooke's *Pantheon* is deserted and ridiculed. Now and then, perhaps, a Stanley kills a kid, a Gladstone bangs up a wreath, a Lytton burns incense, in honor of the Olympians. Yet, who cares at Lambeth, Birmingham, or the Tower Hamlets for ancient rites? Who the plague are the Muses, and what is the use of all that Greek and Latin rubbish? What is Elicon, and who cares? Who was Thalia, pray, and what is the length of her *i*? Is Melpomene's name in three or four syllables? And do you know from whose design I stole that figure of Tragedy which adorns the initial *G* of this chapter?

It has been noted how Mr. George, in his youth and during the long leisure he enjoyed at home and later in his imprisonment within the French fort on the banks of the Monongahela, whiled away his idleness by paying court to Melpomene. The result of their union was a tragedy, omitted from Bell's Theatre, though I dare say it is no worse than some pieces printed there. Most young men pay respects to the Tragic Muse first, falling in love with women far older than themselves. Let the candid reader own, if he ever had a literary turn, that his ambition was of the highest; however, in riper age, he might come down in pretensions, thinking translating an ode of Horace or turning a song of Waller or Prior into decent alcaics was about the utmost of his capability. Tragedy and epic engaged his green, unknowing youth, and no prize but the highest seemed fit for him.

Thus, upon coming to London, George attended theatrical performances at both houses, frequented the theatrical coffee-houses, and heard the opinions of critics. He might be seen at the Bedford between plays or supping at the Cecil alongside wits and actors. Here he gradually acquainted himself with the players and those writers and poets known to the public. The tough old Macklin, the frolicsome Foote, the vivacious Hippisley, and the sprightly Mr. Garrick himself occasionally graced these houses of entertainment. Our gentleman, by his wit, modesty, and the high character for wealth he possessed, became very much liked in the coffee-house circles. Actors drank bowls of punch with him; critics supped at his expense with great affability. To be intimate with an author or actor had been the delight of many a young man; actually to hob-nob with Bobadil or Henry the Fifth, to accept a pinch from Aristarchus's own box, or to hand Monimia to her chair, were privileges delighting to any of a poetic turn. No wonder George Warrington loved the theatre. Moreover, he took satisfaction in thinking his mother only half-approved of plays, thereby feasting on fruit forbidden at home. He gave several elegant entertainments to the players, and it was even said that one or two distinguished geniuses had condescended to borrow money of him.

As he polished and added new beauties to his masterpiece, he sought the counsel of friends. Mr. Spencer, his new acquaintance of the Temple, gave a breakfast at his chambers in Fig Tree Court, where Mr. Warrington read part of his play. The gentlemen present pronounced it to have uncommon merit. Even the learned Mr. Johnson, invited, remarked that the piece showed talent. It warred against the unities, to be sure, but these had been violated by others, and Mr. Warrington might sacrifice them as well. There was a quality in Mr. W.'s tragedy reminding Mr. Johnson both of *Coriolanus* and *Othello*. "And two very good things too, sir!" the author pleaded. "Well, well, there was no doubt on that point; and 'tis certain your catastrophe is terrible, just, and being in part true, is not the less awful," remarked Mr. Spencer.

The plot of Mr. Warrington's tragedy was indeed full of battle and murder. A favorite book of his grandfather had been the life of Old George Frundsberg of Mindelheim, a colonel of foot-folk in the Imperial service at the Battle of Pavia and during the wars of the Constable Bourbon. One of Frundsberg's military companions was a certain Carpzow, or Carpezan, whom our friend selected as his tragedy hero. The first act, as it stood in Sir George Warrington's manuscript, was set before a convent on the Rhine, besieged by Lutherans under Carpezan. A godless gang, they had pulled the beards of Roman friars and torn the veils of hundreds of religious women. A score trembled within the walls, promising surrender if succours did not arrive before midday. Meanwhile, there was an armistice, and sentries looked on with hungry eyes as soldiers gambled on the grass before the gate.

Twelve o'clock! *Ding, ding, dong!* sounded the convent bell. No succours had arrived. "Open gates, warder! Admit the famous Protestant hero, the terror of Turks on the Danube and Papists in the Lombard plains—Colonel Carpezan!" See, he came, clad in complete steel, his hammer of battle over his shoulder, having battered so many infidel sconces, flags displayed, trumpets blowing. "No rudeness, my men," said Carpezan. "The wine is yours, and the convent larder and cellar are good: the church plate shall be melted. Any of the garrison who choose to take service with Gaspar Carpezan are welcome, and shall have good pay. No insult to the religious ladies! I have promised them a safe-conduct, and he who lays a finger on them hangs! Mind that Provost Marshal!" The Provost Marshal, a huge fellow in a red doublet, nodded.

"We shall see more of that Provost Marshal," Mr. Spencer explained to his guests. "A very agreeable acquaintance, I am sure," said Mr. Johnson, wagging his head over his tea. "This scene of mercenaries and camp-followers is novel and stirring, Mr. Warrington."

The Abbess and her oldest ladies appeared before the conqueror. Conqueror as he was, they heard him in their sacred halls. They knew of his violent behavior in conventual establishments; that hammer of his had smashed many sacred images, and pounds of convent plate melted. No wonder the Abbess-Princess of St. Mary's, a lady of violent prejudices and noble birth, disliked the lowborn heretic lordling her convent. She told Carpezan a bit of her mind. In this scene, where the lady gained somewhat the upper hand, the audience at the Temple was pleased. Terrible as he might be in war, Carpezan was shaken at first by the Abbess's brisk charge of words; conqueror as he was, he seemed at first conquered by his actual prisoner. But such an old soldier was not to be beaten ultimately by any woman. "Pray, madam," said he, "how many ladies are there in your convent for whom my people shall provide conveyance?" The Abbess, with a look of trouble and anger, said that besides herself, the noble sisters of Saint Mary's House were twenty-three. She was going to say twenty-four, then corrected herself. "Ha! why this hesitation?" asked Captain Ulric, one of Carpezan's gayest officers.

The dark chief pulled a letter from his pocket. "I require from you, madam," he said sternly, "the body of the noble lady Sybilla of Hoya. Her brother was my favourite captain, slain by my side in the Milanese. By his death, she becomes heiress of his lands. 'Tis said a greedy uncle brought her hither and fast immured the lady against her will. The damsel shall

Even the faithful Gumbo could not find solace for the loss of his beloved master. To judge by his tears and wailing upon hearing the news of Mr. Harry's enlistment, one might have believed the negro's heart shattered by the separation. Indeed, he sought sympathy wherever he turned, drifting to the maid-servants at Mr. Lambert's lodgings. There, amidst the dusky youth, he found comfort in the society of women. Their fair and tender bosoms possessed a genuine capacity for pity toward the poor African, rendering the darkness of Gumbo's complexion no barrier to affection, any more than Othello's blackness repelled Desdemona. I venture to say that Europe has never been so prudish regarding Africa as in that certain respectable quarter. Nay, some Africans—take the Chevalier de St. Georges, for instance—have become notorious favorites of the fair sex.

Thus moved Mr. Gumbo in his humbler walks. The Lambert servants wept freely in his company, and the maids kindly regarded him not merely as Mr. Harry's man, but as their brother. Hetty could not suppress a laugh when she discovered Gumbo roaring because his master had gone off as a volunteer—and notably, had not taken him along. "He would save Master Harry's life any day," Gumbo declared, "and would rather be cut into twenty thousand pieces than let harm come to him!" Yet, nature required support, and he condescended to fortify his weary frame with generous supplies of beer and cold meat from the kitchen. It was true that he was greedy, idle, and prone to lying, yet Hetty bestowed upon him half a crown and treated him with exceptional kindness. Her tongue, usually so pert and quick-witted, fell strangely silent now, as if it had never known a jest. She moved through the house with a humility and meekness that seemed transformative. She was submissive to her mother, grateful to John and Betty when serving dinner, patient with Polly when the latter pulled her hair during combing, and enduring toward Charley from school when he trampled on her toes or disrupted her workbox. Silent in Papa's presence, she was utterly transmogrified. If Papa had commanded her to roast the leg of mutton or walk to church arm-in-arm with Gumbo, she would have curtsied gently and whispered, "Yes, if you please, dear Papa!" But what sort of meal was some poor volunteer consuming, with cannonballs raining around their heads? And what of church? When the prayers of war-time were intoned, how her knees struck together as she knelt, hiding her face in the pew! She lowered her gaze when the parson read, "Thou shalt do no murder," from the communion rail, fearing his eyes were fixed upon her. Her mind wandered constantly to travelers by land and sea, sickenng as she scanned newspapers for news of the Expedition. She watched Papa closely upon his return from the Ordnance Office, searching his countenance for signs of fortune or misfortune. Was he safe? Had he been promoted to General? Was he wounded and imprisoned? Ah, me! Or worse, had both his legs been severed by a single shot, like that pensioner they had seen in Chelsea Gardens the day before? She would have walked upon wooden legs all her life if his sacrifice brought him home safely; indeed, she felt she ought never rise from her knees until he was returned. "Haven't you heard of people, Theo?" she asked, "whose hair turns gray in a single night? I should not wonder if mine did—I wouldn't wonder in the least." Peering into the glass, she scrutinized her reflection for evidence of this phenomenon.

"Hetty dear, you used not to be so nervous when Papa was away in Minorca," remarked Theo.

"Ah, Theo! One can plainly see that George is not with the army, but safe at home," rejoined Hetty. Upon this, the elder sister blushed deeply, looking profoundly pensive. *Au fait*, if Mr. George had actually been in the army, that would have constituted another pair of boots. Meanwhile, we shall spare anyone further harrowing of their kind feelings and simply state that Harry, for the present, was as secure as any officer of the Life Guards stationed at Regent's Park Barracks.

The first expedition in which our gallant volunteer was engaged may be termed successful, though certainly not glorious. The British Lion, or any other lion, cannot always encounter a worthy adversary to combat, nor a battle-royal to deliver. Suppose he ventures forth seeking a tiger that refuses to emerge, and instead lays his paws upon a goose, gobbling it up? Lions, we know, must subsist like any other animal. But suppose, advancing into the forest in search of that aforementioned tiger and bellowing his challenge of war, he spies not one, but six tigers rushing toward him? This manifestly is not his game. He tucks his tail between his royal legs and retreats into his own snug den as swiftly as possible. Were he to attempt fighting six tigers, you might fairly write that Lion down an Ass.

Now, Harry Warrington's first feat of war unfolded thus. He and approximately 13,000 other fighting men embarked in various ships and transports on the 1st of June from the Isle of Wight. At daybreak on the 5th, the fleet steamed into the Bay of Cancale in Brittany. For a while, Harry and the gentlemen-volunteers enjoyed the pleasure of surveying the French coast from their vessels, while the Commander-in-Chief and the Commodore reconnoitered the bay in a cutter. Cattle were spotted, and some dragoons trotted off into the distance; moreover, a small fort armed with a couple of guns displayed sufficient audacity to fire upon His Grace the Duke of Marlborough and the Commodore within the cutter. By two o'clock, the entire British fleet was anchored, and signals were given for all the grenadier companies of eleven regiments to board flat-bottomed boats and assemble around the Commodore's ship, the *Essex*. Simultaneously, Mr. Howe, hoisting his broad pennant aboard the *Success* frigate, drew near the shore as close as possible, followed by other frigates, to protect the landing of the troops. Now, under the command of Lord George Sackville and General Dury, the gentlemen-volunteers, the grenadier companies, and three battalions of guards pulled ashore.

On this occasion, the gentlemen-volunteers could perform no heroic deeds, for the French, who ought to have remained to fight, fled. Having silenced the fire of the small fort that had disturbed the Commander-in-Chief's reconnaissance, the frigates enabled the army to assault it immediately, capturing the entire garrison prisoner and shooting the commander in the leg. Indeed, he was but an elderly gentleman who had gallantly fired his two guns, telling his conquerors, "If every Frenchman had acted like me, you would not have landed at Cancale at all."

The advanced detachment of invaders took possession of the village of Cancale, where they lay upon their arms throughout the night. Our volunteer was jested at by his comrades regarding his eagerness to tread the war-path and bring in two or three scalps of Frenchmen. None such, however, fell beneath his tomahawk; the only person slain on the whole day was a French gentleman riding with his servant, who was surprised by Volunteer Lord Downe, marching forward with a company of Kingsley's. My Lord Downe offered the gentleman quarter, which he foolishly refused. Whereupon the gentleman, his servant, and the two horses were straightway shot.

The following day, the whole force was landed and advanced from Cancale to St. Malo. All villages through which the troops passed were emptied, and the roads were so narrow in many places that the men were forced to march single file, vulnerable to being shot down from behind the tall, leafy hedges had there been any enemy to disturb them.

At nightfall, the army arrived before St. Malo and were greeted by a discharge of artillery from the town, which caused little damage in the darkness. Taking cover under this barrage, the British set fire to ships, wooden buildings, pitch and tar magazines within the harbor, creating a prodigious conflagration that raged all night.

This feat was accomplished without any attempt by the French to molest the British force. However, as it was confidently asserted that a considerable French force remained within the town of St. Malo, despite their refusal to engage, His Grace the Duke of Marlborough and Lord George Sackville resolved not to disturb the garrison. Consequently, they marched back to Cancale again—and thus, eventually, boarded their ships.

If this were not a veracious history, would it not have been easier to send our Virginian on a more glorious campaign? Exactly four weeks after his departure from England, Mr. Warrington found himself once again at Portsmouth. He addressed a letter to his brother George, which the latter hurried to Dean Street to receive as soon as it arrived.

"Glorious news, ladies!" cried George, finding the Lambert family gathered at breakfast. "Our champion has returned. He has undergone all sorts of dangers, but has survived them all. He has seen dragons—upon my word, he says so."

"Dragons! What do you mean, Mr. Warrington?"

"But not killed any—he says so, as you shall hear. He writes:

"'DEAREST BROTHER—I think you will be glad to hear that I am returned, without any commission as yet; without any wounds or glory; but

How relentless shall the vanity of mankind prove itself, nay, how particularly so among the French? Can it truly be that following the skirmish at Saint-Cast—a mere affair of severing a rear guard, as you know—they proved so unfeeling as to waste countless pounds of powder upon the Invalides in Paris, boasting and blustering over our misfortune? Does any true magnanimity exist in cheering and shouting because five or six hundred brave souls were taken by ten thousand on a shore, and met that fate which awaits the last of the line? I once fancied designing a faithful portrait of the rejoicings in London upon our "glorious" success at Saint-Malo. I pictured the polished cannons drawn in procession by our gallant tars; the sturdy horse-grenadiers prancing past; the rabble waving hats, screaming cheers, pickpocketing, while our friends watched from a balcony in Fleet Street, blessing this scene of British triumph. But now that the French Invalides have been so vulgar as to mimic the Tower, erecting their Saint-Cast against our Saint-Malo, I scorn to touch upon this stale subject. I declare *nolo*, not *malo*: content, for my part, if Harry returns from one expedition and another with his skin intact. Have I ever said he was bruised? Have I not, for fear of distressing my fair young reader, declared he was as well as he had ever been in his life? The sea air had bronzed his cheeks, and the ball whistling past his side-curl had missed its mark. The ocean had soaked his gaiters and garments, yet it had not swallowed his body. True, he had shown the lapels of his coat to the enemy; but for as brief a moment as possible, withdrawing from their gaze with all speed. And what, pray, are lapels but reverses? Coats possess them, as do men; and our duty is to wear them with courage and good humor.

"I can tell you," said Harry, "we were all forced to run for it; and when our line broke, it was whoever reached the boats first who was most fortunate. The French horse and foot pursued us down to the sea, intermingled among us, hacking down our men and bayoneting them where they lay. Poor Armytage was shot ahead of me and fell; I took him up and staggered through the surf to a boat. It was fortunate that the sailors in our vessel were not terrified, for the shots were whistling around their ears, shattering the blades of their oars, and riddling their flag with bullets; yet the officer in command remained as cool as if he were drinking a bowl of punch at Portsmouth, which we shared upon landing, I can assure you. Poor Sir John was less fortunate than I. He never lived to reach the ship, and the service has lost a fine soldier, while Miss Howe has lost a true gentleman to her husband. Such casualties, you see, must happen; and thus his brother secures the promotion—the baronetcy."

"It is of the poor lady I am thinking," said Miss Hetty, to whom our volunteer was recounting his tale. "And the King. Why did the King encourage Sir John Armytage to go? A gentleman could not refuse a command from such a quarter. And now the poor gentleman is dead! Oh, what a state his Majesty must be in!"

"I have no doubt his Majesty will be in a profound state of grief," said Papa, shaking his head.

"Now you are laughing! Do you mean, sir, that when a gentleman dies in his service, almost at his feet, the King of England feels nothing?" Hetty asked sharply. "If I thought that, I vow I would support the Pretender!"

"The sauce-box would make a charming little headpiece for Temple Bar," remarked the General, seeing clearly the mixture of remorse, consternation, and gratitude swirling within Miss Hetty's little heart now that the danger had passed. "No, my dear. Were kings to weep for every soldier, what a burden you would place upon them! I hold a higher regard for his Majesty than to suppose him so weak; and if Miss Hester Lambert obtained her Pretender, I doubt whether she would find any greater happiness. That family was never renowned for excessive sentiment."

"But if the King sent Harry—I mean Sir John Armytage—directly into the war wherein he lost his life, ought not his Majesty repent deeply?" the young lady pressed.

"If Harry had fallen, no doubt the court would have gone into mourning: as it stands, gentlemen and ladies wore colored clothes yesterday," observed the General.

"Why should we not light bonfires for a defeat, and don sackcloth and ashes after a victory?" asked George. "I protest I do not wish to thank Heaven for assisting us to burn the ships at Cherbourg."

"Yes, you do, George! Though I have no right to speak, and you are not even half as clever. Yet when your country wins, you are glad—I know I am. When I flee before the French, I am ashamed; I cannot help it, though I did it," Harry admitted. "It does not seem right to me, somehow, that Englishmen should have to do this," he added gravely. George smiled but chose not to ask his brother what, on the other hand, was the Frenchman's opinion.

"'Tis a bad business," continued Harry, solemnly; "but 'tis lucky 'twas no worse. The story regarding the French is that their Governor, the Duke of Aiguillon, was something akin to what you call a dampened chicken. Our entire retreat might have been cut off, only, to be sure, we ourselves were in a great haste to move. The French local militia behaved famously, I am happy to report; and there were numerous gentleman volunteers with them, who displayed themselves in the front exactly as they ought to. They say the Chevalier of Tour d'Auvergne engaged despite the Duke of Aiguillon's orders. Officers brought us a list of our prisoners and wounded to General Bligh and Lord Howe. He is a lord now, since news of his brother's death reached home, George. He is a brave fellow, whether lord or commoner."

"And his sister, who was to have married poor Sir John Armytage—think what her state must be!" sighed Miss Hetty, who had lately become quite sentimental.

"And his mother!" cried Mrs. Lambert. "Have you seen her ladyship's address in the papers to the electors of Nottingham? 'Lord Howe being now absent upon public service, and Lieutenant-Colonel Howe with his regiment at Louisbourg, it rests upon me to beg the favor of your votes and interests that Lieutenant-Colonel Howe may supply the place of his late brother as your representative in Parliament.' Isn't this a gallant woman?"

"A laconic woman," said George.

"How can sons fail to be brave who have been nurtured by a mother like that?" asked the General.

Our two young men exchanged glances.

"If one of us falls defending his country, we have a mother in Sparta who would think and write thus," said George.

"If Sparta lies anywhere near Virginia, I reckon we have it here," remarked Mr. Harry. "And to think that we should both have met the enemy, and both have been beaten by him, brother!" he added pensively.

Hetty looked at him, recalling him only as he had been the other day: tottering through the water toward the boats, his comrade bleeding on his shoulder, the enemy in hot pursuit, bullets flying everywhere. It was she who had driven him into that peril. Her words had stung him. Now that he had returned, he never rebuked her. Unless directly questioned, he spoke scarcely of his adventures at all. He was exceedingly grave and courteous with Hetty; with the rest of the family, especially frank and tender. Yet those taunts of hers had wounded him. "Little hand!" his looks and demeanor seemed to say, "Thou shouldst not have been lifted against me! It is ill to scorn anyone, much more one who has been so devoted to thee and all thine. I may not be swift of wit, but insofar as the heart goes, I am the equal of the best, and the best of my heart thy family has known."

Harry's grievance, coupled with his magnanimous endurance of it, served to restore him in Miss Hetty's esteem to the place he had lost during the preceding months of inglorious idleness. The reverence the fair pays to the brave she granted him. She was no longer pert in her answers, nor sarcastic in her observations regarding his conduct. In a word, she was a humbled, altered, and improved Miss Hetty.

And all the world seemed to turn toward Harry, as he turned toward the world. He was no longer sulky or indolent; he no longer despaired of himself or defied his neighbors. The colonel of his regiment reported his behavior as exemplary and recommended him for one of the commissions vacated by the casualties during the expedition. Although its conclusion was unlucky, it was, for him, fortuitous. His brother-volunteers, upon returning to St. James's Street, spoke highly of his conduct. These volunteers and their actions became the theme of universal praise. Had he been a general commanding, slain in the moment of victory, Sir John Armytage could scarcely have garnered more sympathy than the nation showed him. The papers swarmed with letters concerning him, and men of wit and sensibility vied with one another in composing epitaphs in his honor. The fate of his affianced bride was bewailed

To those of my gentle readers who habitually stroll through the celebrated gardens of Regent's Park, where nature's creatures—fish, birds, beasts, and beasts of burden alike—are housed in exchange for a glimpse of their antics, I confess a peculiar distraction. While gazing into the glass tanks, observing the swimming patterns of various fins, I found my thoughts drifting inevitably back to the Virginians.

Among the aquatic beauties, there was one particular flounder, a creature of remarkable grace, gliding through his emerald bath with a fluidity that belied his humble reputation. To the uninitiated eye, who had devoured thousands of such specimens in Greenwich waters, this specimen appeared merely ordinary. Yet, as with mankind, so it is with flatfish; viewed in their natural element, they reveal an agility and health that transforms them into objects of genuine beauty. Remove them from the water, however, and watch how quickly that splendor vanishes, replaced by clumsy flapping and a desperate gasping for air. But if taken gently and returned to the Thames, they revive.

Such, I observed, was the case with Harry Warrington. For a long time, he had been "floundering" in a sea of difficulties, far removed from his proper element. Yet, once he found his footing, a transformation occurred. With the crisp tap of an epaulette on his shoulder, he rose from the mud of his former life into a soldier of striking vigor. He embraced his new profession with a zest that was infectious, mastering the intricacies of camp life with eager speed. Though I lack the literary flair of my friend Lorrequer to paint the full canvas of a military campaign—the mess nights, the parades, the camaraderie under canvas—I trust the reader can, with a bit of imagination, visualize Harry Warrington in his scarlet coat and buff facings, radiant with the joy of bearing the King's colors and eager to master every duty of his trade.

It was a mutual admiration society between the brothers. Each delighted in the virtues of the other, and George, seeing Harry's ascent, felt a surge of pride that bordered on delirium. In a heartfelt letter to his mother in Virginia, George recounted Harry's triumphs, carefully omitting any boastfulness on Harry's part, knowing his brother's modesty would never permit it. He detailed how Harry had secured his commission and then ventured further, offering to subsidize the cost of buying a higher rank. To George, helping his brother was not merely charity; it was a restitution. Harry, believing himself entitled to the entire fortune as an elder brother, had squandered resources he did not truly possess. Now, freed from this heavy misapprehension, George felt it was his solemn duty to restore what Harry had lost, viewing his own financial contribution as a fitting thank-offering for his brother's renewed prosperity.

Having settled matters regarding Harry, George turned his pen to his own affairs, addressing his mother on a subject of profound personal import. He spoke of the Lamerts, those true friends who, unlike his own blood relatives, had shown him nothing but kindness. Their benevolence was matched by the General's high standing in the army, and by Mrs. Lambert's tender, almost maternal, affection for the Esmond children. It was within this circle that George had found a love so deep it anchored his future hopes. He had formed an attachment for the elder Miss Lambert, a bond he believed essential to his happiness. Though he had not yet made formal proposals, he was candid with his mother: his feelings were plain, and the young lady and her parents appeared favorably disposed. Describing Miss Lambert as a model of perfection, George declared his conviction that his life's happiness hung in the balance, dependent entirely on securing her hand.

*Why do you not produce this letter?* one might ask, given the Editor's possession of the entire Warrington correspondence. *Why not?* Because it is cruel to expose the raw, incoherent vows of a young man's heart. To listen to the wild raptures of passion, to record the secrets whispered in twilight embrasures, or to bottle the hot tears of devotion, is a violation of the sacred. Love, wherever it blooms—in the glance between lovers, in the doting look of a mother, in the joy of a father watching his daughter whirl around the room with her captain—is holy. Let us not play the role of eavesdroppers, dissecting the throbs of delicate hearts or printing private confessions. Down with those who vulgarize the mysteries of love! To betray a lover's trust is to invite disaster; beware the man who prints your secrets, for he will surely cast you adrift before long.

While Harry sought glory in the smoke of battle, rushing toward reluctant dragons and dragging wounded comrades from the火线, George pursued a quieter, sweeter path. He penned sonnets to his mistress's brow, paced the darkness beneath her window watching her lamp, and crafted endless pretexts to send notes that required no answers yet somehow compelled them. He plucked verses from his favorite poets and flowers from Covent Garden for her adornment. He walked to St. James's Church, his mind elsewhere, singing along with the prayers he barely heard. He showered affection upon every relation of Miss Theo—from her siblings at school and college to Miss Hetty, his sparring partner in wit, and finally, to Mamma, who, according to Martin Lambert, was already half in love with him. Fathers may grow sulky at the prospect of a son-in-law, but mothers often become sentimental, falling in love with their own loves anew.

Meanwhile, Gumbo and Sady were perpetually on the move between Southampton Row and Dean Street, orchestrating summer festivities and proposing outings to Ranelagh, Hampstead, Vauxhall, and Marylebone Gardens. George desired the famous tragedy copied out for the stage, and who better to execute such beautiful Italian handwriting than Miss Theo? As the sheets passed back and forth, they were accompanied by a constant stream of notes expressing gratitude, inquiry, and admiration. *See,* marked a packet in Warrington's neat hand, *"T's letters, 1758–9."* Would we dare open them? No. Those virgin words were whispered for ears alone. Years later, the husband might read them with sweet pangs of memory, but to display them publicly would be sacrilege. Kind readers need only accept our assurance: the young lady's letters were modest and pure, the gentleman's respectful and tender. In truth, we have said little, yet in these few months, Mr. George Warrington had decided he had found the woman of women. She may not be the fairest, nor the wittiest—why, there were cousins like Flora, Coelia, and Ardelia, far more handsome—but her sweet face pleased him above all others. Her voice was the dearest to his ear, and in her company, he felt his intellect sharpen, his thoughts become eloquent. She possessed a generosity, nobility, and wit that made him delight in her presence. And regarding the young lady, may Heaven grant that we do not judge women too harshly: she endowed her young hero with every ounce of wit, wisdom, beauty, valour, and virtue imaginable.

When George's letter arrived home, it stirred no small excitement around Madam Esmond's fireside. He was in love and wished to marry! It was natural, and a prudent step to keep him out of harm's way. If he united himself with a well-bred Christian young woman, Madam saw no objection.

"I knew they would be setting their caps at him," remarked Mountain. "They fancy his wealth equals his estate. He hasn't mentioned if the young lady has money. I fear otherwise."

"People would set their caps at him here, I dare say," replied Madam Esmond, casting a grim look at her dependent. "And try to catch Mr. Esmond Warrington for their own daughters, who are no richer than Miss Lambert may be."

"I suppose your ladyship means me!" said Mountain. "My Fanny is poor, as you say; and 'tis kind of you to remind me of her poverty!"

"I said people would set their caps at him. If the cap fits you, *tant pis!* as my papa used to say."

"You think, madam, I am scheming to keep George for my daughter?" cried Mrs. Mountain. "I thank you, on my word! A good opinion you seem to have of us after the years we have lived together!"

"My dear Mountain, I know you much better than to suppose you could ever fancy your daughter would be a suitable match for a gentleman of Mr. Esmond's rank and station," said Madam, with much dignity.

"Fanny Parker was as good as Molly Benson at school, and Mr. Mountain's daughter is as good as Mr. Lambert's!" shrieked Mrs. Mountain.

"Then you did think of marrying her to my son!" exclaimed the mistress of Castlewood. "I shall write to Mr. Esmond Warrington and say how sorry I am that you should be disappointed!" And so it came to pass that Mrs. Mountain was indeed disappointed, harboring some ambitious views for her daughter; otherwise, why should she have been so agitated at the notion of Mr. Warrington's marriage?

In response to her son, Madam Esmond wrote that she was deeply gratified by the fraternal love George exhibited. It was right, she argued, to compensate Harry, whose false expectations had led him to adopt a costly lifestyle he could not sustain as a younger son. Regarding the purchase of his promotion, she offered to halve the expense with Harry's

Colonel James Wolfe, newly arrived from America following Harry's enlistment, stood at the center of a whirlwind of expectation. Having risen from Lieutenant-Colonel of Kingsley's to command a fresh battalion awarded for his gallantry at Cape Breton, Wolfe was the darling of the public imagination. The nation, weary of failures like Marlborough's lineage and Sackville's conduct at Minden, hung upon his lips, hoping he was the general who would restore military glory. While Mr. Lambert's duties kept him anchored in London, the bond between the older officer and his younger friend, now a Colonel, flourished with renewed vigor. Lambert delighted in his friend's ascent, and the two shared long, open evenings where Wolfe, possessing a heroic simplicity reminiscent of Nelson, spilled his heart without reserve. To the cynical circles of St. James's, he might seem naive, yet within their intimate circle, he was a beacon of frankness.

There was a boyish romance to Wolfe's spirit; he cherished music and poetry above the carnage of battle, confessing on his final day that he would trade a victory for Gray's *Elegy*. This duality drew him inevitably to George Warrington. Both men, swept by passion and eager for happiness, found solace in sentiment. As Warrington later recalled to Harry, "I admired Mr. Wolfe's simplicity... He was much in love, and he wanted heaps and heaps of laurels to take to his mistress." Wolfe, convinced that honor was a sin worth committing, hungered for glory with the fervor of a knight seeking dragons. Standing in contrast to Wolfe's chivalrous ardour was the Virginian's own steady, almost Roman constancy—a tension that fascinated observers.

While Lambert's family settled comfortably in Southampton Row, Warrington's worldly pursuits drew him deeper into London's theatrical heart. Though he visited Castlewood briefly, finding solace in the memories of his grandfather and Colonel Esmond, his true sanctuary lay in the vibrant social life of the capital. It was here, amidst the company of the elegant and the literati, that his tragedy, *Carpezan*, found its path to the stage. Through the influence of the enthusiastic Chaplain Sampson rather than Mr. Johnson, the play secured acceptance at Covent Garden under Mr. Rich's management, bypassing Garrick who had just engaged the author of *Douglas*.

The night of the premiere was a triumph of camaraderie and art. Following an elegant supper at the Bedford Head, the group adjourned to the theatre. The cast was formidable: Barry as the terrifying Carpezan, Shuter as the old nobleman, Reddish as Ulric, and the young Dublin actor Hagan, playing the King of Bohemia with such grace and elegance that he won universal acclaim. Mrs. Woffington, though appearing aged in the first act, delivered a chilling murder scene that terrified and delighted the audience, while Miss Wayn's singing added a haunting beauty to the climax. Yet, the play's heartbeat was the execution of Sybilla. When her head fell, the tension broke, and the house erupted. Friends rushed to the coffee-house to confirm the total success. Barry announced the repetition immediately, declaring it the debut work of a young gentleman from Virginia.

In the boxes, the atmosphere was electric with emotion. Theo Lambert fluttered with anxiety as the verdict hung in the balance, her eyes sparkling with relief when the victory was sealed. Harry, however, bore the weight of the performance physically; his pallor, remarked upon by Mrs. Lambert, exceeded even Barry's chalk-like whiteness. Yet, when the curtain fell, Harry roared with the enthusiasm of a man who had seen his creation breathe life. Wolfe and General Lambert cheered wildly, while Mrs. Lambert wept, a display Hetty dismissed with sharp wit, though deep down she shared the joy, alongside the Chartreux's little Charley and Lucy from boarding school. Even Lord Castlewood and Lady Maria were present, the latter enchanted by the tragedy and the Chaplain's fervor.

A riotous supper followed, surpassing even Garrick's recent failure with *Agis*. Toasts flowed freely, and the spirits soared. Lady Maria raised "The King of Hungary"—played by Mr. Hagan—who, drunk on wine and role-playing, vowed eternal servitude to the Countess despite having suffered a "death" on stage hours prior. His excessive gallantry, attributed partly to the punch, unsettled the sober-minded Esmonds and Lords, yet the enchantment of the evening held sway. Later, as coaches departed, the King of Hungary retreated with a throbbing headache, while the poet Warrington, astounded by the sheer volume of his own speech-making, found himself besieged by guests the next morning who had been invited to dine with him again.

The aftermath of the play shifted focus to domestic matters. As Mrs. Lambert prepared her daughters for bed, the air crackled with excitement. George Warrington, radiant with news, revealed his secret weapon against the temptations of the greenroom: the eyes of Miss Theo. He declared himself free from fear, for in her gaze lay an amulet against danger. The mother, amused yet stern, chided Hetty for her blunt comparison of the actress's age to George's mother, reminding her of the Fifth Commandment and the propriety of their youth. Yet, beneath the surface, a strange infatuation simmered. Mrs. Lambert doted on Theo, dressing her elaborately and constantly consulting her about George's appearance and health, creating a dynamic where George often stood at a distance, save for one brief, natural moment when he stooped to pick up her dropped scissors, causing a flush that neither parent seemed to notice.

Then came the letter from Virginia, penned in Madam Esmond's neat hand. George trembled as he broke the seal. His mother approved of his attachment to Miss Lambert and his intentions regarding Harry, offering a warm welcome to the young couple. She spoke of her lack of ready cash but promised abundance for future generations, expressing a maternal desire to see her grandchildren near her in her twilight years. Though the letter lacked a formal financial settlement, it conveyed a profound, if somewhat vague, consent. George, ecstatic, fled to Soho to share the tidings. The Lambert household erupted in celebration; General Lambert embraced his son-in-law, calling him a treasure, while Mrs. Lambert wept copiously. Hetty, overhearing the scene, feigned indifference with a biting laugh, only to embrace her sister passionately in private, vowing to protect Theo's heart forever.

However, the reality of George's situation soon surfaced. Upon reading Madam Esmond's letter in full, General Lambert's countenance darkened. There was no dowry, no substantial provision—only a promise of hospitality. The truth of George's depleted patrimony, squandered on bills, commissions, and his own ransom, hit hard. He confessed his inability to support a family, having exhausted his inheritance. The prospect of returning to Virginia to be dependent on his mother repelled him; he envisioned a life of "slavery" and sought to remain in England, perhaps studying law or writing more tragedies. A new plan formed instantly: another tragedy. He began drafting works immediately, showing them to Theo, who found them superior to *Carpezan*, while Hetty yawned over the ink-stained pages.

The news of the engagement rippled through the families, reaching Sir Miles Warrington via a ceremonious letter. Initially, Sir Miles assumed his nephew was financially secure, perhaps even wealthy. But when the truth emerged—that George was penniless, dependent on his mother, and marrying a girl of similar means—the old man's indignation knew no bounds. He viewed the union as a scandal, a product of "respectable" people inveigling a prodigal into ruin. He wrote to his sister in Virginia, condemning the Lamberths' judgment and George's behavior, treating his nephew with icy disdain, denying him invitations to family gatherings, and serving him nothing but small beer. To Harry, however, Sir Miles showed a modicum of leniency, acknowledging his service at Braddock, though he still regarded him as a boy who needed to amend his ways.

Despite the storm brewing with the Warrington relatives, George found solace in the company of the Esmond cousins. Lord Castlewood, a man of wit and questionable morals, maintained a friendship with the Virginians, though his advice was often tinged with cynicism. Lady Maria, though her romance with Harry had ended, retained a warm affection for the brothers, delighting in their stories of valor and theatrical success. One afternoon in Kensington Gardens, George and Theo encountered Lady Maria accompanied by a gentleman in a smart suit and handsome lace hat. It was the King of Hungary, played by Mr. Hagan, who claimed he frequented the gardens to study his parts. They strolled together to Lord Castlewood's door, exchanging a thousand compliments on Theo's beauty and virtue, a moment of lightness that masked the underlying tensions of their lives.

That evening, Harry returned to London and found George at breakfast. A letter from Lady Maria lay on the table, addressed to "Common Garden." Harry teased George about potential love letters, but George, with a peculiar expression, denied any romance, dismissing it as merely a discussion about the play. Later, at dinner, Mrs. Lambert pressed about George's meeting with the actor portraying the King in Kensington Gardens. George deflected, describing it as a casual encounter where the actor studied his lines and the actress crossed paths to visit a colleague. The matter was laid to rest, at least for the moment.

Yet, shadows lengthened over the household. One evening after Christmas,

Nature shudders at the thought of separation, and I shall therefore offer no commentary on the private turmoil within Harry Warrington's heart as he prepares to bid farewell to his kin. Let us not forget the thousands of others standing in identical desolation. Consider Mr. Wolfe, whose mother waits with lips ready to press his cheek, while his father lies cold in death—a victim of the glorious campaign at Louisbourg. Consider his beloved, awaiting a final embrace. Look further to the sea: stout Admiral Holmes, commanding *The Somerset*, *The Terrible*, *The Northumberland*, *The Royal William*, *The Trident*, *The Diana*, and *The Seahorse*—his flag flying proudly above *The Dublin*—stands on the dock to tear himself away from Lady Holmes and his daughters. And what of Admiral Saunders, who sails the following day? Is he immune to the human spirit?

Away drifts William and his crew of boisterous mariners, cutting through tumbling swells, while poor Black-eyed Susan remains on the shore, watching the vessel shrink until it vanishes into the gloom of the setting sun. It fades into the West. Night descends swiftly, a heavy curtain over the ocean. They are gone, yet their spirits linger at home for a fleeting moment. In a silence heavy with a tenderness that words cannot capture, every man turns inward, thinking of those left behind. What a vast, sorrowful hymn of prayer ascends to the Father—from the wife kneeling in tears beside a vacant bedside, from the mother and children gathered round the fire, pouring out their pleas, to the sailor on deck gazing upward at the stars as his ship carves a path through the midnight roar. Tomorrow, the sun will rise, and we shall resume our mundane tasks of labor and duty.

George accompanies his brother, remaining at Portsmouth while they wait for the winds to fill the sails. He shakes Mr. Wolfe's hand, gazes one last time at his brother's pale countenance, and watches the ships depart amidst the clamor of ringing bells and the thunder of cannon fired from the coast. The next day finds George back home, returning to that occupation which, for nearly every man past thirty, proves the most self-absorbing and consuming of worldly pursuits. A pang of deep sadness strikes him as he glances into the lodgings where Harry once slept, seeing his half-burned papers lingering in the grate. Soon, however, he is off to Dean Street again, whispering affectionately to his devoted sweetheart by the flickering light of a dying fire. She is ecstatic at his return—so incredibly happy! yet she feels a profound shame regarding this joy. "Is it not cruel to be so happy?" she asks, considering the melancholy state of poor Hetty. "Poor little Hetty!" George reflects, understanding the sting of her grief. "Indeed, it is selfish to rejoice when she is so sad. It makes one quite wretched to see her." "Do not, sir!" Theo exclaims, her voice trembling with guilt. "Well, I ought to be wretched, and it is terribly wicked of me if I am not." One understands her soft-hearted repentance perfectly. Perhaps her "Do not" was spoken in one of those moments when the darkness seemed to swallow the room whole. Servants arrive with supper and candles; the family gathers, and conversation flows freely. The destination of the fleet is now common knowledge throughout the realm. The force aboard is deemed sufficient to crush the French in Canada, and under an officer of Wolfe's caliber, to correct the blunders and disasters of previous campaigns. He appeared dreadingly ill, indeed, but he possesses a great soul housed within a frail body. The ministers and the nation hold their breath, hoping for everything from him. After supper, as per custom, Mr. Lambert convenes his modest household—including George Warrington, who has become something of a fixture—and as he offers prayers for travelers by land and sea, Theo and her sister kneel side by side. Thus, as the ship races farther and farther into the West, these fond thoughts chase it across the waves; the night slips away, and the sun rises anew.

A day or two more, and everyone returns to their books or customary labors. As for George Warrington, that celebrated dramatist is already engrossed in another composition. When the tragedy of *Carpezan* had played for some thirty or forty nights, other geniuses inevitably took possession of the theater. There may have been souls who marveled at how quickly a town could grow weary of such a masterpiece, unable to bear seeing actors in different costumes reciting other men's verses. Yet George, of a skeptical temperament, accepted the fate of his tragedy with philosophical equanimity, pocketing the proceeds with quiet satisfaction. From Mr. Dodsley, the bookseller, he received the standard hundred pounds; from the theater manager, two hundred or more. With such praise from critics and friends, he set to work preparing a new piece, confident it would surpass his first performance in success.

Over these studies, and the other charming enterprises that engage him, months slip by. This is happy business! The happiest season of youth and life, when love is first spoken and returned; when the dearest eyes shine with welcome daily, and the fondest lips never tire of whispering their sweet secrets; when the parting glance accompanying "Good night!" serves as a delightful promise of tomorrow; when the heart overflows with love and happiness, leaving so little to spare for the world; when days close with glad prayers and open with joyful hopes; when doubt seems like cowardice, misfortune impossible, and poverty merely a sweet trial of constancy! Theo's elders, gratefully recalling their own primes, sit softly by to witness this pretty comedy enacted by their young people. And in one of his later letters, dutifully penned to his wife during a temporary absence, George Warrington records how he had gazed up at the windows of the dear old house in Dean Street, wondering who sat in the chamber where he and Theo had shared such bliss.

Meanwhile, we may discern how time passes and how our friends are occupied through extracts from George's letters to his brother.

*Why have you returned to rugged rocks, bleak shores, burning summers, and nipping winters, when you might have been harvesting laurels aplenty in Germany? Kingsley's gardens are returning covered with them, as Jack-a-Green on May-day. Our six regiments did wonders; and our cavalry would have done likewise if my Lord George Sackville had allowed them. But when Prince Ferdinand cried 'Charge!' his lordship either could not hear the order or could not translate the German word for 'Forward'; and so we only beat the French, failing utterly to annihilate them as we might have, had Lord Granby or Mr. Warrington commanded. My lord has returned to town and is shouting for a Court-Martial. He held his head high enough in prosperity; in misfortune, he displays such a constancy of arrogance that one almost admires him. He looks as if he envied poor Mr. Byng, and the fact that he was not shot was a mere lack of proper regard.*

*The Duke has received notice to prepare for departure from this world of grandeur, victory, downfall, and disappointment. An attack of palsy has visited His Royal Highness; and pallida mors has just peeped in at his door, saying, 'I will call again.' Tyrant as he was, this prince has been noble in disgrace; and no king has ever possessed a truer servant than ours has found in his son. Why do I favor the losing side, and am I inclined to revolt against the winners? Your famous Mr. P----, your chief's patron and discoverer, I have heard speak in the House of Commons twice or thrice. I revolt against his magniloquence. I wish some little David would topple over that swelling giant. His thoughts and his language are perpetually posturing. I prefer Barry's manner, though the other is the more awe-inspiring actor.*

*Pocahontas progresses rapidly. Barry enjoys his part as Captain Smith; and although he insists on wearing a red coat with blue facings and an epaulet, I have a fancy to dress him exactly like one of the portraits of Queen Elizabeth's gentlemen at Hampton Court—with a ruff, a square beard, and square shoes. 'And Pocahontas—would you like her to be tattooed?' asks Uncle Lambert. Hagan's role as the warrior who loves her, and who, seeing her partiality for the captain, nobly rescues him from death, I trust will prove a hit. A strange fish is this Hagan: his mouth full of stage plays and rant, yet good, honest, and brave, if I do not err. He is angry at having been cast recently for Sir O'Brallaghan in Mr. Macklin's new farce, *Love à la mode*. He claims he does not care to disgorge his tongue with imitations of that rascal's brogue. As if there were any need for imitation, when he possesses such an admirable twang of his own!*

*Shall I tell you? Shall I hide the circumstance? Shall I hurt your feelings? Shall I set you in a rage of jealousy, causing you to ask for leave to return to Europe? Know, then, that though Carpezan is long since dead, Cousin Maria is forever making her appearance at the playhouse. Tom Spencer has spied her out night after night in the gallery, and she attends specifically on the nights when Hagan

Though Lord Castlewood had previously maneuvered with Harry regarding business matters, a strange and enduring friendship persisted between the Earl and his cousin, George Warrington. They met with the warmth of close kinship, bound by a mutual, albeit self-serving, affection. If George sought favors—whether financial grants, introductions to the Court, or seats in the House of Lords—he found his cousin ever ready to oblige. Lord Castlewood, a man of wit and charm, was a delightful companion who seemed willing to sacrifice nothing short of his own immediate interests for the sake of his relative.

Indeed, the Earl went so far as to pledge his influence to secure a position for Mr. George Warrington, a boon eagerly grasped by the Virginian. George, who showed no inclination to return to America, clung to this promise with fervor. He despised the notion of submitting once more to his mother's "servitude" and the stifling atmosphere of his plantation. While a life of idle leisure in Virginia might have suited him had he possessed true independence, reality offered him only the status of a pampered, yet undervalued, favorite among his mother's many subjects. He dreaded the prospect of returning with his young bride to a home filled with wearisome routines, tedious subordination, petty squabbles, and the inevitable jealousies that threatened to unravel their union. In contrast, London promised freedom, congenial society, and a glimmer of future distinction, however bleak his circumstances might appear.

Consequently, Lord Castlewood's assurance was accepted with alacrity. Although the Earl's own position, despite his title, was far from enviable, and his past conduct was notorious, George and his host were blinded by the desire to believe the best. When the Earl revealed his engagement to Miss Lambert, he did so with a grace that softened the blow to her parents. He visited them with prodigious cheerfulness, praising George to the lambeths and the young lady to George, until these charitable souls forgot his infamous reputation, convinced that the world must be wicked indeed to malign a man of his apparent refinement. With the Lamberts, Lord Castlewood appeared transformed; he was witty, lively, and seemingly devoid of his former vices. He claimed to be improved by their society, leaving his "wickedness and worldliness" behind when he entered their drawing-room, only to reclaim them the moment he stepped back into his carriage. Whether he was genuinely better or merely playing a role, the Lambert elders, seeing his open admiration for Miss Theo, felt no cause for suspicion.

"It don't affect my sleep, and I am not going to break my heart because Miss Lambert prefers somebody else," the Earl remarked lightly. "Only I wish, when I was a young man, madam, I had had the good fortune to meet with somebody so innocent and good as your daughter. I might have been kept out of a deal of harm's way... innocent and good young women did not fall into mine, or they would have made me better than I am."

Mrs. Lambert, moved by his words, whispered softly, "Sure, my lord, it is not too late!"

Startled, the Earl inquired, "Not too late, madam?"

Blushing, the matron replied, "It is too late to court my dear daughter, my lord, but not too late to repent. We read, 'tis never too late to do that."

With a plaintive tone, the Earl shook his head. "Perhaps I know my own heart better than you. A man who learns languages late can never get the practice of them on his tongue. And so 'tis the case with goodness; I can't learn it at my age. I can only see others practise it, and admire them." He paused, his expression melancholy. "When I am on—the side opposite to Lazarus, will Miss Theo give me a drop of water? Don't frown! I know I shall be there, Mrs. Lambert. Some folks are doomed so; and I think some of our family are amongst these. Some people are vacillating, whereas some are predestined angels, and fly Heavenwards naturally."

"Oh, my lord, and why should you not be of the predestined? Whilst there is a day left..." urged the hopeful lady.

"But how can they avail?" he asked sadly, reading the prayer in her eyes. "You don't know what a life ours is of the world... It is Fate holds the reins of the chariot." Yet, as he took her hand and bowed with a tragic grace, every word rang with a hollow truth. Weak and bad men were not strangers to good feelings; they simply lacked the endurance to sustain them. The tears of the libertine added zest to his pleasures, a debauch of sentiment. Nevertheless, Mrs. Lambert, knowing little of the depths of his soul, continued to pray for him with the persistent kindness of a woman. To her, he was a fascinating figure, a reprobate with potential for redemption, and she could not bear to lose sight of him.

As the illness of the soul was treated, an intimacy blossomed between the Earl and the Lamberts. The Ladies of Castlewood, maintaining impeccable decorum, frequented Mrs. Lambert's drawing-room, extending invitations to her card assemblies. Play, the universal vice, flowed freely between the courts and cities, abetted by bishops and peers alike. Mrs. Lambert, grateful for the welcome and delighted by the attention paid to her daughters, became a regular fixture at these gatherings.

Meanwhile, the machinations of Madam Esmond, the formidable matchmaker, faced a barrier. An American family of Dutch extraction, strongly recommended by Madam Esmond, arrived in England. Their views were clear enough for the astute Mrs. Lambert to discern, yet the plans were stymied. George was already betrothed to Miss Theo, and Harry, the object of Hetty's affections, had departed for military service in Canada—a fact for which Hetty's mother was surprisingly relieved. Laughingly, Mrs. Lambert advised George to sever his ties with Theo and pursue Miss Lydia, describing her as ten times richer and handsomer than her rival.

"I say she is handsomer, and she is richer, and perhaps even cleverer," George admitted, though with evident reluctance. "But say she is all these? So is Mr. Johnson much cleverer than I am... so is Mr. Hagan the actor much taller and handsomer: so is Sir James Lowther much richer: yet pray, ma'am, do you suppose I am going to be jealous of any one of these three?"

"No, George," said Theo, with an honest gaze that silenced any lingering jealousy. "If, after this pair of speeches, mamma takes occasion to leave the room for a minute to fetch her scissors, or her thimble, or a bootjack and slippers, or the cross and ball on the top of St. Paul's, or her pocket-handkerchief which she has forgotten in the parlour—if, I say, Mrs. Lambert quits the room on any errand or pretext, natural or preposterous, I shall not be in the least surprised, if, at her return in a couple of minutes, she finds George in near proximity to Theo, who has a heightened colour, and whose hand George is just dropping—I shall not have the least idea of what they have been doing."

With Harry away and the engagement solid, Mrs. Lambert turned her目光 to her own schemes, envisioning a wealthy, handsome wife for her son Jack. However, the ambitions of the American family, the Van den Boschs, were unfolding elsewhere. When Mr. Van den Bosch first arrived, he associated only with the City merchant class, taking his daughter Lydia to Islington, Highgate, and the Artillery Ground. He attended Baptist meetings and, secretly, the theaters, treating a Lord Mayor's feast as the height of civilization.

When George first waited upon his mother's friends, he encountered Mr. Draper of the Temple, who was attentive to the young lady, noting her substantial dowry. Mr. Drabshaw, a young Quaker gentleman and nephew of Madam Esmond's Bristol agent, was equally vigilant, viewing George with alarm. Desiring to honor his mother's neighbors, George invited them to an entertainment at his apartments, where he inevitably met his friends from Soho. Forced to acknowledge Miss Lydia's beauty, the group marveled at her features—the foot of a fairy, the arms and neck of a brown huntress. She carried a plaintive accent from home, a sound which, though mocked by the Cockneys, was undeniably charming when uttered by such a countenance.

Six months after her schooling at the Kensington Academy, Miss Lydia returned to her grandfather's side, determined to enlarge her sphere. The obedient grandfather, thwarted in his youth and fearful of repeating his mistakes, relented. Having realized the severity of his own discipline had produced an unhappy son, he vowed to ensure Lydia's happiness. He relocated from Monument Yard to an elegant house in Bloomsbury, abandoning his circle of tradesmen for the company of the great. Soon, the junketings ceased, replaced by Sunday services at Queen Square Church—a shocking departure from his former Baptist allegiance—and social calls at Kensington.

At this juncture, rumors swirled regarding young Mr. Lutestring and the Quaker Mr. Drabshaw, who had allegedly fought a boxing match over the young lady. Mr. Draper, formerly a staunch admirer, now spoke ill of the old gentleman's lineage, revealing that Van den Bosch was a speculator from Albany, whose son had married a servant, fallen into disgrace, and died. Confronted with these tales, the placid old gentleman laughed them off

Before we turn our attention further to the adventures of Miss Lydia, it becomes necessary to unravel the cryptic remark in Mr. Warrington's correspondence regarding Lady Maria Esmond—a sentence that may still puzzle the unsophisticated reader. How, indeed, could a person of steady morals foresee such a destiny? How could virtuous youth imagine that a lady of noble lineage, ancient pedigree, and mature judgment—who had been passionately devoted to her cousin merely a dozen months prior—could so entirely lose her senses as to fall in love with a man of humble origin, many years her junior, and wed him in defiance of the world? Or rather, not openly against the world, but secretly behind its back, for it was Parson Sampson who privately bound them in matrimony within the confines of his Mayfair chapel.

Pause, however, before you render your final condemnation. Did Lady Maria possess a fleeting affection for her youthful cousin, thereby forfeiting the right to love anyone else? Must men enjoy the exclusive liberty of changing hearts, while women are censured for seizing occasional solace? There exists nothing more crude and unphilosophical than Hamlet's scathing reproach to his mother concerning her second union. The reality, likely, is that this tender, clinging creature sought something to grasp onto; with her father removed from the scene, she coiled herself around Claudius. Indeed, we have witnessed females so determined in attachment that they entwine themselves around two gentlemen simultaneously. Why, pray, must there not exist wedding tables following funeral feasts? If you offered grace for yesterday's banquet, does that preclude hunger today? Your innate, refined appetite for tonight's repast proves that at eight o'clock tomorrow evening, you will undoubtedly crave your supper. As for me, when Flirtilla or Jiltissa once favored me—with minds conjuring images of breathtaking beauty and exalted rank—I invariably recalled that a season would arrive when their affections would shift elsewhere. Life serves us *à la russe*, dishes devoured one by one, much like the inhabitants of Polyphemus's cavern. *Hodie mihi, cras tibi*: there are anthropophagites who consume dozens of us—the aged, the young, the soft, the sturdy, the plump, the lean, the plain, the fair—and there is no escape; eventually, one by one, according to fate, we vanish down their insatiable throats. Recall Lady Ogresham! All remember last year how she seized poor Tom Kydd, consumed him, gnawed his bones, and cast them aside. Now, Ned Suckling lies beneath her gaze, trembling and entranced. Behold the poor, timid creature, panting and helpless under those great eyes! She drags herself nearer, step by step; he retreats, inch by inch. Soon, a few faint squeals of pity will rise, followed by—"hobblegobble"—and he will disappear! Alas, it is pity as well. I knew, long before Harry Warrington claimed it, that Maria Esmond had lost her heart countless times; yet I liked to dream that he would retain it, and that, lamenting misfortune and broken order, she would preserve her love in decorous singleness. If, in some fit of senile madness, I fell in love tomorrow, I would still strive to believe I held the freehold of my beloved's heart—not merely a tenancy on a brief lease of a dilapidated apartment, where dusty wineglasses bore the stains of countless lips and tattered sofas smelled of the previous occupant's boots. Dear, dear nymph! To be cherished and beautiful! Should I harbor a passing infatuation for Glycera (whose complexion truly rivaled splendid Parian marble), and you a fancy for Telephus, with his low collars and ridiculous neck—those follies are surely ended, are they not? We love each other forever now, do we not? Yes, eternally; and Glycera may depart for Bath, while Telephus takes his pink throat to Jack Ketch, *n'est-ce pas*?

No. We never conceive of alteration, my dear. Whichever way the winds blow, or time flies, or spoons stir, our potage, now boiling hot, shall never grow cold. Passing whims we may have indulged in days past; truly, your obsession with Telephus (do not frown so, my darling creature, nor deepen the wrinkles on your brow)—indeed, it was the town's gossip—and regarding Glycera, she behaved dreadfully ill to me. Well, well, now that we understand one another, our hearts are united forever, and we can regard Sir Cresswell Cresswell and wave his wig away. Yet this Maria of the last century possessed an unruly mind. You, my love, who know the world, understand that considerable matters must have transpired in her career that neither endure the light nor edify the telling. You know (not, my dear, that I imply you possess worldly wisdom, but you have heard others say—you have heard your mother declare)—that an elderly flirt, having played the fool with one passion, will inevitably play it with another; that flirting resembles drinking, and when the brandy is exhausted, Glycera—who has adopted the habit—will turn to gin. Thus, if Maria Esmond has found a successor to Harry Warrington and installed a new Sultan in the precious empire of her heart, what, after all, could you reasonably expect? That realm was akin to the Low Countries, accustomed to conquest and perpetually open to invasion.

And Maria's current master was none other than Mr. Geoghegan or Hagan, the young actor who had starred in George's tragedy. His voice was so moving, his eyes so radiant, his bearing so noble, appearing so handsome in his gilded leather armor and voluminous buckled periwig as he recited the poet's fiery verses, that the lady yielded her heart to him, just as Ariadne surrendered hers to Bacchus when her tryst with Theseus concluded. The young Irishman was deeply touched and elated by the high-born damsel's favor. Though he might have preferred a Lady Maria Hagan younger in years, it would have been difficult to find one more tender in disposition. She clung to him tightly. Indeed, when it became necessary to reveal their marriage and her furious relatives disowned her, she retreated to his modest lodging in Westminster with him.

General Lambert brought the news home from his office in Whitehall one day and merrily shared it with his household. In those simpler times, a joke was often improved by being slightly broad; a fine lady might laugh at a jovial page of Fielding and weep over a letter of Clarissa, causing your present ladyship's eyes to pop out in horror. The merry General uttered every manner of wag upon this marriage, speculating on George's role in facilitating it, mocking Barry's jealousy upon hearing the news. He vowed it was cruel that Cousin Hagan had not selected George as his groomsman, suggesting the first child be named Carpezan or Sybilla after the tragedy, and so forth. They would scarcely afford a carriage, but they might acquire a chariot adorned with pasteboard dragons from Mr. Rich's theater. The babe might be baptized in Macbeth's cauldron; Harry and Harlequin ought certainly to serve as godfathers.

"Why shouldn't she marry him if she loves him?" asked little Hetty. "Why shouldn't he love her because she is somewhat older? Mamma is a little old, and you love her none the worse. When you married my mamma, sir, I heard you say you were very poor; yet you were very happy, and no one laughed at you!" Thus did this impudent little soul speak, guided by her tender age and unaware of Lady Maria Esmond's former follies.

"So her family has abandoned her?" George described their wrath, noting how Lady Castlewood had entered mourning, how Mr. Will swore to have the rascal's ears cut off, and how furious Madame de Bernstein was, the angriest of all. "It is an affront to the family," declared haughty little Miss Hett, "and I can easily imagine how ladies of such standing must be indignant at their relative's union with someone of Mr. Hagan's station; but to desert her is a far graver matter."

"Indeed, my dear child," cried Mamma, "you speak of things you do not comprehend. After Lady Maria's conduct, no respectable person can visit her."

"What conduct, Mamma?"

"Never mind," cried Mamma. "Little girls cannot be expected to know, nor ought they to be too curious to inquire, what Lady Maria's conduct has been! Suffice it, miss, that I am shocked her ladyship should ever have been here; and I repeat, no honest person should associate with her!"

"Then, Aunt Lambert, I must be whipped and sent to bed," said George, with mock solemnity. "I own to you (though I did not confess sooner, seeing the affair was not mine) that I visited my cousin the player and his ladyship, his wife. I found them in very dismal lodgings in Westminster, where the wretch has the shame to keep not only his wife but his old mother and a little brother, whom he sends to school. I found Mr. Hagan and departed with a liking, nay, almost a respect for him, though I admit he has made a very imprudent marriage. But how imprudent some folks are about marriage, are they not, Theo?"

"Impudent, if they marry spendthrifts like you," replied the General. "Master George found his relations, and I'll be bound to say he left his

The petty skirmish between George and his cousin had effectively severed George's frequent visits to Bloomsbury. Now, Mr. Will was even more assiduous in his attentions, and with both parties legally bound to keep the peace, George found it increasingly difficult to restrain his hands from striking his cousin. Yet, amidst this tension, the naive Lydia possessed a peculiar, shifting manner of receiving her friends. Six weeks prior, she had been madly jealous of George's preference for another; now, she seized every opportunity to compliment Theo in conversation. "Miss Theo is such a quiet, gentle creature," Lyddy insisted, convinced that George was simply the ideal husband for her. "How fortunate that horrid quarrel was prevented!" she mused, recalling how the constables had arrived just in time. To her ears, the image of Mr. Esmond cursing and swearing, raging at his thwarted duel, was nothing short of ridiculous. "But the arrival of the constables saved your valuable life, dear Mr. George," she said with a soft smile. "And I am sure Miss Theo ought to bless them forever." She pressed further, asking if he wouldn't stop to dine with Mr. Esmond today. "You don't like being in his company?" she ventured. "He can't do you any harm; and I am sure you will do him none." Such kind speeches, delivered by a small girl to a gentleman with such strange inadvertence in the presence of other men and ladies, were unlikely to make Mr. Warrington eager for the society of the young American lady.

For several days, George's meeting with Mr. Will remained unknown in Dean Street, for he wished to spare those kind folks the disturbance of his quarrel. However, once the ladies became aware of the incident, a great flurry ensued. "You were actually going to take a fellow-creature's life, and you came to see us, and said not a word!" Theo exclaimed, horrified. "Oh, George, it was shocking!"

"My dear, he had insulted me and my brother," George pleaded. "Could I let him call us both cowards and sit by saying, 'Thank you'?"

The General sat by, looking very grave. "You know you think, papa, it is a wicked and un-Christian practice," Theo continued, "and have often said you wished gentlemen would have the courage to refuse!"

"To refuse?" said Mr. Lambert, still glum.

"It must require a prodigious strength of mind to refuse," Jack Lambert observed, looking as gloomy as his father. "And I think if any man were to call me a coward, I should be apt to forget my orders."

"You see brother Jack is with me!" cried George.

"I must not be against you, Mr. Warrington," Jack declared.

"Mr. Warrington!" cried George, flushing red.

"Would you, a clergyman, have George break the Commandments and commit murder, John?" Theo asked, aghast.

"I am a soldier's son, sister," the young divine replied dryly. "Besides, Mr. Warrington has committed no murder at all. We must soon be hearing from Canada, father. The great question of the supremacy of the two races must be tried there ere long!" As he spoke, he turned his back on George, who eyed him with wonder.

Hetty, looking pale at Jack's original remark, was led out of the room by some artful pretext from her sister. George rose and followed the retreating girls to the door. Returning moments later, he gasped, "Great powers, gentlemen! I believe, on my honour, you are giving me the credit of shirking this affair with Mr. Esmond!" The clergyman and his father exchanged looks.

"A man's nearest and dearest are always the first to insult him," George flashed out.

"You mean to say, 'Not guilty'? God bless thee, my boy!" cried the General. "I told thee so, Jack." He rubbed his hand across his eyes, blushed, and wrung George's hand with all his might.

"Not guilty of what, in heaven's name?" asked Mr. Warrington.

"Nay," said the General. "Mr. Jack, here, brought the story. Let him tell it. I believe 'tis a ---- lie, with all my heart." Uttering this wicked expression, the General walked out of the room.

The Rev. J. Lambert looked uncommonly foolish. "And what is this--this d----d lie, sir, that somebody has been telling of me?" George asked, grinning at the young clergyman.

"To question the courage of any man is always an offence to him," said Mr. Lambert. "And I rejoice that yours has been belied."

"Who told the falsehood, sir, which you repeated?" bawled out Mr. Warrington. "I insist on the man's name!"

"You forget you are bound over to keep the peace," Jack reminded him.

"Curse the peace, sir! We can go and fight in Holland. Tell me the man's name, I say!"

"Fair and softly, Mr. Warrington!" cried the young parson. "My hearing is perfectly good. It was not a man who told me the story which, I confess, I imparted to my father."

"What?" George asked, the truth suddenly occurring. "Was it that artful, wicked little vixen in Bloomsbury Square?"

"Vixen is not the word to apply to any young lady, George Warrington!" Lambert exclaimed. "Much less to the charming Miss Lydia. She is artful—the most innocent of Heaven's creatures! She is wicked—that angel!"
