With unfeigned delight that the quarrel should be over, and devout gratitude that blood consanguineous should not be shed, she had spoken in terms of the highest praise for George's decision to decline the quarrel, expressing deep sympathy for his painful but necessary method of averting it.

"What method?" demanded George, stamping his foot.

"Why, of laying an information, to be sure!" said Mr. Jack. On this, George burst forth into language much too violent for repetition, highly uncomplimentary to Miss Lydia.

"Don't utter such words, sir!" cried the parson, now taking his turn to be angry. "Do not insult, in my hearing, the most charming, the most innocent of her sex! If she has been mistaken in her information regarding you, and doubted your willingness to commit what, after all, is a crime—for a crime homicide is, and of the most awful description—you, sir, have no right to blacken that angel's character with foul words: and, innocent yourself, should respect the most innocent as she is the most lovely of women! Oh, George, are you to be my brother?"

"I hope to have that honour," answered George, smiling. He began to perceive the other's drift.

"What, then, what—though 'tis too much bliss to be hoped for by sinful man—what, if she should one day be your sister? Who could see her charms without being subjugated by them? I own that I am a slave. I own that those Latin Sapphics in the September number of the *Gentleman's Magazine*, beginning *Lydicae quondam cecinit venustae* (with an English version by my friend Hickson of Corpus), were mine. I have told my mother what hath passed between us, and Mrs. Lambert also thinks that the most lovely of her sex has deigned to look favourably on me. I have composed a letter—she another. She proposes to wait on Miss Lydia's grandpapa this very day, and to bring me the answer, which shall make me the happiest or the most wretched of men! It was in the unrestrained intercourse of family conversation that I chanced to impart to my father the sentiments which my dear girl had uttered. Perhaps I spoke slightingly of your courage, which I don't doubt—by Heaven, I don't doubt: it may be, she has erred, too, regarding you. It may be that the fiend jealousy has been gnawing at my bosom, and—horrible suspicion!--that I thought my sister's lover found too much favour with her; I would have all my own. Ah, dear George, who knows his faults? I am as one distracted with passion. Confound it, sir! What right have you to laugh at me? I would have you to know that *risu inepto*"

"What, have you two boys made it up?" cried the General, entering at this moment, amidst a roar of laughter from George.

"I was giving my opinion to Mr. Warrington upon laughter, and upon his laughter in particular," said Jack Lambert, in a fume.

"George is bound over to keep the peace, Jack! Thou canst not fight him for two years; and between now and then, let us trust you will have made up your quarrel. Here is dinner, boys! We will drink absent friends, and an end to the war, and no fighting out of the profession!"

George pleaded an engagement as a reason for running away early from dinner. Jack must have speedily followed him, for when the former, after transacting some brief business at his own lodgings, came to Mr. Van den Bosch's door in Bloomsbury Square, he found the young parson already in parley with a servant there. "His master and mistress had left town yesterday," the servant said.

"Poor Jack! And you had the decisive letter in your pocket

Gazing across the fire toward the armchair where my beloved companion has sat for the latter half of my existence, I frequently find myself asking—middle-aged gentlemen being permitted to recycle their jests, inquiries, and tales—whether any pair of youths ever displayed such folly and imprudence as we did upon our union, back in the year the old King passed? My son, who has leaped prodigiously far in the fervor of fox-hunting, remarks that he views the gaps and rivers he once crossed with terrifying hindsight, filled with astonishment at his own temerity in taking such desperate chances; yet, there remains no more ardent sportsman in either county than Miles. He loves his diversion so wholly that he cares for nothing else. He has fractured his collarbone and suffered a hundred tumbles (to his mother's horror), just as I, thinking perhaps of a poem or my address at Quarter Sessions, was cast over my old mare's head, who slipped on a stone as we dreamed along a park lane at four miles per hour. Miles's reckless play has been the joy of his life, just as my marriage has been the blessing of mine; and I never reflect upon it without giving thanks to Heaven. Mind, I do not set up my devotion as an exemplar. I tell no young folk, "Go forth and wed upon twopence a year," lest the vestry-men turn pale; yet my wife is notorious as a desperate matchmaker. When Hodge and Susan enter my justice-room discussing allowances, we urge them to spend their half-crown weekly at home, add a modest contribution of our own, and summon the vicar.

Now, when I pose a question to my dear oracle, I know precisely what the answer shall be; and thus, perhaps, the reason why I so constantly consult her. I need only wear a specific expression, and my Diana draws her reflection from it. If I say, "My dear, do you not think the moon was fashioned of cream cheese tonight?" she replies, "Well, Papa, it did look remarkably like cream cheese, indeed—you are unmatched in your droll similes." Or, if I remark, "My love, Mr. Pitt's speech was exquisite, though I fear he falls short of what I recall of his father," she answers, "No one equaled my Lord Chatham." Then one of the girls cries out, "But I've often heard our Papa say Lord Chatham was a charlatan!" Whereupon Mamma declares, "How like she is to her Aunt Hetty!"

As for Miles, *Tros Tyriusve* holds no sway over him. He attends solely to the sporting notices in the Norwich paper. Provided there is good scent, he cares not for the state of the realm. I believe the rascal has never read my poems, let alone my tragedies (for I mentioned *Pocahontas* to him recently, and the dunce mistook her for a river in Virginia). Regarding my Latin verses, how can he comprehend them when I know he cannot construe Corderius? Why, this notebook lies openly on the little table at my corner of the fireside, and anyone may read it who takes the trouble to lift my spectacles from their case; yet Miles never has. I insert caricatures of Miles therein, jokes directed at him; but he never learns nor heeds them. Only once, in lieu of a neat drawing of mine, executed in China ink, depicting Miles asleep post-prandium—a piece my friend Bunbury would not disavow—I discovered a crude sketch of myself vaulting over my mare Sultana's head, titled "The Squire on Horseback, or Fish Out of Water." The fellow roared with laughter, and all the girls tittered when I stumbled upon the page! My wife confessed she had never been so terrified as when I returned to my book; yet I can endure a joke against myself, having heard many, though (strange to relate, for one who dwelt among the chief wits of the age) I never heard a truly good one in my lifetime. Pray do not mind, Miles, though thou art no wit, I love thee none the less (there never was any love lost between two wits in a family); though thou lackst great beauty, thy mother deems thee as handsome as Apollo, or His Royal Highness the Prince of Wales, who was born in the very same year as thee. Indeed, she always finds Coates's portrait of the Prince strikingly similar to her eldest boy, keeping the print in her dressing-room to this very day.

*[Note, in a female hand: "My son is not a spendthrift, nor a breaker of women's hearts, as some gentlemen are; but that he was exceedingly like H.R.H. when they were both babes, is most certain, the Duchess of Anester having herself remarked him in St. James's Park, where Gumbo and my poor Molly used often to take him for an airing. Th. W."]*

In that same year, with what divergent prospects! did my Lord Esmond, son of Lord Castlewood, likewise appear to adorn the world. At that juncture, my Lord Castlewood and his humble servant had already arrived at a coolness, and heaven knows! my honest Miles's godmother brought no gold pap-boats to his christening! Matters have mended since, *laus Deo*—*laus Deo*, indeed! for I suspect neither Miles nor his father would ever have accomplished much for themselves, nor by their own wits.

Castlewood House bears quite a different visage now from that venerable countenance it wore in the days of my youth, when it was etched with the wrinkles of time, the scars of ancient wars, and the cracks and blemishes that years had marked upon its hoary features. I love best to remember it in its old shape, as I saw it when young Mr. George Warrington descended at the owner's invitation to attend his Lordship's marriage with Miss Lydia Van den Bosch—"an American lady of noble family of Holland," as the county paper announced her Ladyship to be. Then the towers stood as Colonel Warrington, the Marquis (as Madam Esmond would wish to term her father), had seen them. The woods (thinned not a little, it is true) stood, yea, some of the selfsame rooks may have cawed overhead which the Colonel had witnessed sixty years prior. His portrait hung in the hall which might have been his, had he not preferred love and gratitude to wealth and worldly honor; and Mr. George Esmond Warrington—that is, *Ipse Ego*, who writes this page down—as he traversed the old seat, pacing the long corridors, the smooth dew-spangled terraces, and the cool, shadowed avenues, felt momentarily as if he were one of Mr. Walpole's cavaliers, complete with ruff, rapier, buff-coat, and gorget, and as if an Old Pretender, or a Jesuit emissary in disguise, might emerge from behind any tall tree-trunk surrounding the mansion, or from within any antique carved cupboard inside it. I harbored the strangest, saddest, most pleasant, old-world fancies as I walked the grounds; I imagined tragedies, intrigues, serenades, escalades, Oliver Cromwell's Roundheads battering the towers, or bluff Hal's Beefeaters pricking over the plain before the castle. I was then courting a certain young lady (Madam, your Ladyship's eyes required no spectacles then, and above the brow thereof there was never a wrinkle or a silver hair), and I recall writing a ream of romantic description, under my Lord Castlewood's franks, to the lady who never tired of reading my letters then. She says I send her but three lines now, when I am away in London or elsewhere. 'Tis that I may not fatigue your old eyes, my dear!

Mr. Warrington deemed himself authorized to commission a genteel new suit of clothes for my Lord's marriage, and accompanied by Monsieur Gumbo, made his appearance at Castlewood a few days prior to the ceremony. I may mention that it had become expedient to dispatch my faithful Sady home aboard a Virginia vessel. A severe inflammation, attacking the throat and lungs, and proving fatal in countless instances in that year of Wolfe's expedition, had seized and nearly slain my poor lad, for whom his native air was pronounced the best remedy. We parted with an abundance of tears, and Gumbo shed as many when his master departed for Quebec: yet he possessed attractions in this country and none for the military life, so he remained attached to my service. We found Castlewood House brimming with friends, relations, and visitors. Lady Fanny was there under compulsion, a sulky bridesmaid. Some of the neighborhood virgins also attended the young Countess. A bishop's widow herself, the Baroness Beatrix, brought a holy brother-in-law of the bench from London to bind the holy knot of matrimony between Eugene, Earl of Castlewood, and Lydia Van den Bosch, spinster; and for some time before and after the nuptials, the old house in Hampshire wore an aspect of gaiety to which it had long been unaccustomed. The country families came gladly to pay their compliments to the newly wedded pair. The lady's wealth became the subject of everyone's talk, and no doubt did not diminish in the telling. Those naughty stories which were rife in town, and spread by her disappointed suitors there, took some little time to travel into Hampshire; and when they arrived, they found the region disposed to treat Lord Castlewood

"You know, my dear ones, from those tales of magic and fairies we so love, that at every wedding there is usually one soul left to curse the stars for missing out, swearing vengeance with a fiery heart. Thus, it needs no wonder that Cousin Will should roar like a trapped beast at the news of his brother's betrothal to the colonial heiress. First, he bolted from the house, his oath ringing in the air that he would never return. Yet, the world—and even the swearer himself—knew that Will's promises were often just hot wind. After a day or two, the scent of the marriage feast dragged him back. He could not bear to resign his knife and fork at the Castlewood table. So he returned, eating and drinking there in token of revenge, raising a bumper to toast the young bride while muttering prayers for her ruin beneath his breath. When her father gave her away in the chapel, Will offered responses choked with smothered curses, and I, your uncle, vowed to love, honor, and cherish her, though I felt the weight of a thousand grudges.

Many grumbled at this union, and Mr. Warrington heard no end of abuse directed at my lady and her grandfather. Old friends from the City, his legal counsel, the Dissenting clergyman who welcomed them to England, and poor Jack Lambert, the orthodox young divine whose eloquence Will had hoped to wield in private, all bore bitter fruit from this treachery. Each had a story of being enslaved, encouraged, and jilted by the young American. The lawyer, who held such an accurate ledger of her properties, estates, money, slaves, ships, and expectations, was ready to vow that the whole account was a lie—that New York and Virginia were myths, that Mr. Van den Bosch possessed no lands, and that the Guinea trade and the negroes themselves were nothing but black falsehoods invented by a wily old planter. The Dissenting pastor moaned over his lost lamb, questioning if such a small, wily monster could truly be called a lamb. Poor Jack Lambert sadly acknowledged to his mother the possession of a lock of black hair, which he bedewed with tears and apostrophized in language unfit for a cleric. As for Mr. William Esmond, he, with the shrieks and curses he freely indulged, even under his sister-in-law's own pretty little nose, called upon Acheron to witness that no such artful young devil existed as Miss Lydia. He swore she was an infernal female Cerberus and called down all the wrath of heaven upon his swindling rascal of a brother, who had cajoled him with fair words and filched his prize.

"Why," said Mr. Warrington, when Will expatiated on these matters, "if the girl is such a she-devil as you describe, you are all the better for losing her. If she intends to deceive her husband and give him poison, as you say, how lucky for you that you are not the man! You ought to thank the gods, Will, instead of cursing them, for robbing you of such a fury, and cannot be better revenged on Castlewood than by allowing him her sole possession."

"All this was very well," Will Esmond said; but, not unjustly, perhaps, remarked that his brother was not the less a scoundrel for cheating him out of the fortune he expected, a sum he had risked his life to win.

George Warrington was at a loss to know how his cousin had been made to risk his precious existence—for which, perhaps, a rope's end might have been a fitting termination—upon which Will Esmond, with candour, told his kinsman how the little Cerbera had actually caused the meeting between them, interrupted by Sir John Fielding's men. He recounted how Lydia was always saying that George was a coward for sneering at Mr. Will, and the latter doubly a poltroon for ignoring his kinsman's taunts; how George had run away and nearly died of fright in Braddock's expedition. "Deuce take me!" said Will. "I never was more surprised, cousin, than when you stood to your ground so coolly in Tottenham Court Fields yonder, for me and my second offered to wager that you would never come!"

Mr. Warrington laughed, thanking Mr. Will for this opinion of him. "Though," he said, "'twas lucky for me the constables came up, or you would have whipped your sword through my body in another minute. Didn't you see how clumsy I was as I stood before you? And you actually turned white and shook with anger!"

"Yes, curse me!" said Mr. Will, turning very red. "That's my way of showing my rage; and I was confoundedly angry with you, cousin! But now 'tis my brother I hate, and that little devil of a Countess—a countess! a pretty countess, indeed!" With another rumbling cannonade of oaths, Will saluted the reigning member of his family.

"Well, cousin," said George, looking him queerly in the face, "you let me off easily, and I dare say I owe my life to you, or at any rate a whole waistcoat, and I admire your forbearance and spirit. What a pity that a courage like yours should be wasted as a mere court usher! You are a loss to His Majesty's army. You positively are!"

"I never know whether you are joking or serious, Mr. Warrington," growled Will.

"I should think very few gentlemen would dare to joke with you, cousin, if they had a regard for their own lives or ears!" cried Mr. Warrington, who loved this grave way of dealing with his noble kinsman, watching with droll interest the other choking his curses, grinding his teeth because afraid to bite, and smothering his cowardly anger.

"And you should moderate your expressions, cousin, regarding the dear Countess and my lord your brother," Mr. Warrington resumed. "Of you they always speak most tenderly. Her ladyship has told me everything."

"What everything?" cried Will, aghast.

"As much as women ever do tell, cousin. She owned that she thought you had been a little *épris* with her. What woman can help liking a man who has admired her?"

"Why, she hates you, and says you were wild about her, Mr. Warrington!" said Mr. Esmond.

"Spretæ injuria formæ, cousin!"

"For me—what's for me?" asked the other.

"I never did care for her, and hence, perhaps, she does not love me. Don't you remember that case of the wife of the Captain of the Guard?"

"Which Guard?" asked Will.

"My Lord Potiphar," said Mr. Warrington.

"Lord Who? My Lord Falmouth is Captain of the Yeomen of the Guard, and my Lord Berkeley of the Pensioners. My Lord Hobart had 'em before. Suppose you haven't been long enough in England to know who's who, cousin!" remarked Mr. William.

But Mr. Warrington explained that he was speaking of a Captain of the Guard of the King of Egypt, whose wife had persecuted one Joseph for not returning her affection. On which Will said that, as for Egypt, he believed it was a confounded long way off; and that if Lord What-d'ye-call's wife told lies about him, it was like her sex, who he supposed were the same everywhere.

Now the truth is, that when he paid his marriage-visit to Castlewood, Mr. Warrington had heard from the little Countess her version of the story of differences between Will Esmond and herself. And this tale differed, in some respects, though he was far from saying it was more authentic than the ingenuous narrative of Mr. Will. The lady was grieved to think how she had been deceived in her brother-in-law. She feared that his life about the court and town had injured those high principles which all the Esmonds are known to be born with; that Mr. Will's words were not altogether to be trusted; that a loose life and pecuniary difficulties had made him mercenary, blunted his honour, perhaps even impaired the high chivalrous courage "which we Esmonds, cousin," the little lady said, tossing her head, "which we Esmonds must always possess—leastways, you and me, and my lord, and my cousin Harry have it, I know!" says the Countess. "Oh, cousin George! and must I confess that I was led to doubt of yours, without which a man of ancient and noble family like ours isn't worthy to be called a man! I shall try, George, as a Christian lady, and the head of one of the first families in this kingdom and the whole world, to forgive my brother William for having spoken ill of a member of our family, though a younger branch and by the female side, and made me for a moment doubt of you. He did so. Perhaps he told me ever so many bad things you had said of me."

"I, my dear lady!" cried Mr. Warrington.

"Which he said you said of me, cousin, and I hope you didn't, and heartily pray you didn't; and I can afford to despise 'em. And he paid me his court, that's a fact; and so have others, and that I'm used to; and he might have prospered better than he did perhaps (for I did not know my dear lord, nor

Our relations at Castlewood detained us until the turn of the year, and though a fortnight to the unreasonably devoted youth appeared an eternity, he eventually returned to the side of his beloved. Madame de Bernstein was relieved to depart her father's household, and as we distanced ourselves from the estate, her conversation grew bolder. Of the "battle royal" that transpired between her and her niece, she remained silent; however, the old lady ceased speaking of raising *cette petite*. Indeed, when she mentioned the young Countess, she did so with a nervous laugh, devoid of genuine malice. Regarding her nephew, Eugene, she declared he was destined to be henpecked for the remainder of his days, asserting that mere domestic order would suffice. She praised the "little old vulgar American gentleman"—Mr. Van den Bosch—as shrewd, proposing he serve as a steward. She casually noted that the Countess's mother was a convict transported from England, likely hardened by the labor of the rope-walk, but dismissed this as gossip unworthy of the town crier. To her, birth meant little compared to virtue; the young woman would prove honest for her own sake, master English quickly, and her illustrious title would grant her entry into any circle. Whether her father were a grocer, a smuggler, or a dealer in human souls, the Countess of Castlewood could afford to be anyone's daughter. Once presented, it was her aunt's duty to stand by her.

Mr. Warrington, hoping the bonds tying Madame de Bernstein to her nephew would extend to her niece, spoke of his visit to Mr. Hagan and his wife, seeking his aunt's favor. Yet the old lady proved obdurate concerning Lady Maria, begging that her name never be uttered, and spent the ensuing two hours recounting a string of anecdotes regarding her niece. Though I, who write this book open to the eyes of the innocent (*virginibus puerisque*), decline to record these tales, I must affirm that if the Countess sinned, she was not alone in doing so, and if she repented, others in the family would do well to emulate her. As for Mr. Hagan, it is known that after leaving the stage, he lived an exemplary life, renowned for the elegance and eloquence of his sermons. His lady adopted extreme religious views and commanded deep respect within her sect; when I last encountered her, she spoke of a peculiar spiritual illumination, which I privately suspected stemmed from the liberal consumption of spirits. Nevertheless, I recall a time when both she and her husband were kind to me and mine, offering sympathy when it was needed, proving that even the most zealous can possess a heart capable of compassion.

I have often recounted how easily fortunes shifted in my fickle aunt's regard, with each of us brothers alternately embraced and cast aside. My moment of glory followed the success of my play. Introduced to the town wits, I held my place among them with reasonable ease, earning praise for my breeding from the macaronis and the fashionable set. Had my purse been deeper, I might have pursued a career among them, had I not fallen in love with a pair of kind eyes superior to the brightest orbs of the Gunnings or Chudleighs, or the painted beauties of the Ranelagh ring. Because I was fond of your mother, children, it was rumored that my tastes were low, a sentiment lamented by my genteel family. Thus, my godly Lady Warrington and my worldly Madame Bernstein laid their aged heads together in dismay. "With his name, he might marry anybody," whispered Meek Religion, keeping one eye on Heaven and the other on profit. "I meddle with no man's affairs, and I admire genius," my uncle countered, "yet it is a pity you consort with those poets and authors, and that sort of people, when you might have secured a lovely creature with a hundred thousand pounds."

"But if I had promised her, uncle?" I asked.

"Promise, promise!" he retorted, turning on his heel. "These matters are questions of arrangement and prudence requiring a careful look-out. When you first committed yourself to little Miss Lambert, you had not yet seen the lovely American lady whom your mother, a good mother naturally, wished you to marry. Your duty to your mother—your duty to the Fifth Commandment—warranted breaking with Miss L. and fulfilling your excellent mother's intentions regarding Miss [whose Dutch name I forget]. A name is nothing, but a plum, Master George, is something to behold! Consider my dear little Miley, placed at a dancing school with Miss Barwell, Nabob Barwell's daughter. I do not disguise my wish that these children contract an attachment enduring through their lives! I tell the Nabob so. One day, emerging from the House of Commons, we witnessed them walking a minuet. It was beautiful, bringing tears to my eyes, for I have a feeling heart, George, and I love my boy!"

"But if I prefer Miss Lambert, uncle, with twopence to her fortune, to the Countess with her hundred thousand pounds?"

"Why then, sir, you have a singular taste, that's all," said the old gentleman, leaving me. I could perfectly understand his vexation at my inability to view the world through his lens. Nor did Aunt Bernstein relish the engagement I had made, nor the family with whom I spent so much time. Their simplicity wore on the old woman of the world, who disliked their company as much as a certain individual dislikes holy water. Chafing at my constant dallying at my sweetheart's lap, I began to fall from her favor, rising again only to descend once more. Harry became the family hero.

He wrote brief letters from the seat of war where he was engaged. Madame Bernstein cared little initially for the content, finding them simple and lacking in interest. However, we in London had already learned of the action on the glorious First of August at Minden, where Wolfe's old regiment stood among the six British regiments that achieved victory. At that same hour, the young General lay in bed, visible from Quebec, stricken by fever, and perhaps by rage and disappointment at the setback his troops had just suffered.

Having arrived in the St. Lawrence in June, the fleet carrying Wolfe and his army disembarked on the last day of the month onto the Island of Orleans, opposite the great cliff of Quebec. Following the great action wherein his General fell, the dear brother who accompanied the chief wrote home a simple letter detailing his modest share in that glorious day. He added nothing to the myriad accounts already written of the battle on the thirteenth of September, save for a testimony from a brother aide-de-camp standing by his side: that the General never spoke after receiving his death wound, rendering the famous phrase attributed to the dying hero no more authentic than an oration by Livy or Thucydides.

From his vantage point on the island, lying in the great northern channel of the river, the General eagerly scanned the horizon for a chance to engage his enemy. He landed above the city and below it, striking wherever an opening presented itself. It was surely a prodigious error on the part of the Marquis of Montcalm to accept battle on equal terms, for the British General possessed no artillery. When we executed our famous *scalade* of the heights and stood upon the Plains of Abraham, we were indeed closer to the city, yet as far removed from its walls as ever.

The game played between the brave chiefs of those two gallant little armies, lasting from July until Mr. Wolfe won the crowning hazard in September, must have been as engaging as any match ever contested by eager players. On the very first night after landing, as my brother narrated, the sport began. At midnight, the French sent a squadron of fireships down upon the British vessels discharging stores at Orleans. Our seamen regarded it as fine sport to tow the fireships clear of the fleet and ground them ashore, where they burned out.

Upon hearing that our ships had entered the river, the French commander marched to Beauport, ahead of the city, establishing a strong position. Once our stores and hospitals were secured, our General crossed from his island to the left shore, drawing nearer to his foe. Behind him lay the ships in the river, but before him stretched a countryside bristling with arms. The Indians in the forest seized our advance parties, murdering them with horrible tortures, while the French, allied with their savage friends, displayed equal ferocity. The Montmorenci River roared between Wolfe and the enemy, blocking direct assault. Bent on discovering another point of vulnerability, the General circled the town of Quebec, skirting the left shore beyond. Everywhere was guarded, including his immediate front, and after running the gauntlet of batteries up and down the river, he returned to his post at Montmorenci.

On the right flank of the French position, across the fordable Montmorenci at low tide, sat an enemy redoubt. Wolfe desired it. Perhaps defending it would force the French chief from his lines, precipitating a battle. Determined to gamble these odds, Wolfe planned to ferry the bulk of his army from the Island of Orleans and attack from the St. Lawrence. Timing his assault for shallow waters, he ensured his lieutenants, Murray and Townsend, could cross the Montmorenci, launching the desperate game on the last day of July.

First, and next General Monckton, his second in command, departed from Point Lévi, crossing the St. Lawrence under a storm of shot and artillery. No sooner were the troops landed than they rushed at the French redoubt in disorder, falling in great numbers before it, and

If your father, young gentlemen, had possessed the most ordinary share of prudence, neither this chapter of his history would exist, nor would you yourselves have been born to plague him in a hundred ways. Instead of shouting and laughing in the passages when he seeks quiet for his books, you would not wake him from his afternoon doze—a habit becoming a healthy country gentleman—nor mislay his spectacles nor steal his newspaper. You would not ruin him with tailors', mantua-makers', and tutors' bills, nor break his nights with feigned illnesses when your mother refuses silence for half an hour. And when Joan cannot sleep, what utility remains in Darby donning his nightcap? Every trivial ailment you have suffered has terrified her so profoundly that I protest I have known no tranquility. Were I not the most long-suffering creature in existence, I would gladly be rid of the entire pack.

Now, having outgrown childhood with its petticoats, chicken-pox, smallpox, whooping-cough, and scarlet fever, what proposal must this unconscionable woman make but to convert the south rooms into a nursery for potential grandchildren and arrange for the Captain to wed early simply because we did? She claims he is too fond of Brookes's and Goosetree's when in London. She possesses the perversity to suggest that while an entrée to Carlton House may be pleasurable, it is perilous for a young gentleman, urging Miles to flee temptation and sow his wild oats before marrying, just as we did. "Marry!" I exclaim to my dear creature, "we had no business to marry at all!" By the laws of common prudence and duty, I should have withdrawn from my slight engagement with Miss Theo—who would have married someone else anyway—and secured a wealthy spouse. Your Uncle John was a parson incapable of fighting; poor Charley was merely a schoolboy; and your grandfather was too elderly to summon me to account with sword or pistol. I maintain there was never a more foolish match in the world than ours, and our relatives were entirely justified in their anger. What purpose serve relations, indeed, but to quarrel and find fault? When Hester marries, do you mind, Master George, to dispute with her if she chooses a husband not of your selection? When George secures his living, having emerged senior wrangler and fellow of his college, Miss Hester, do you not toss your little nose at the young lady he fancies? As for you, my little Theo, I cannot part with you. You must not abandon your old father, for he enjoys having you play Haydn for him and peel his walnuts after dinner.

They both married, as I observe by the note in the family Bible: Miss Theodosia Warrington to Joseph Clinton, son of the Reverend Joseph Blake, who later became Master of Rodwell Regis Grammar School; and Miss Hester Mary, in 1804, to Captain F. Handyman, R.N.—ED.

While they enjoyed the blessing (forsooth!) of meeting daily, billing and cooing, the two young people, your parents, existed in a fool's paradise, little heeding the world around them or its tattling meddlers. Rinaldo was as brave a warrior as ever slew a Turk, yet you know he loved dangling in Armida's garden. Pray, my Lady Armida, what did you mean by casting your spells over me in youth, ensuring that glory, fashion, gaming tables, nor the company of witty men could keep me from your apron strings or out of reach of your simple prattle? Pray, my dear, what did we say to one another during those endless hours? I never slept after dinner then. Which of us was wittier? Was it I or you? How did our conversations become so delightful? I recall that year I cared not to attend the trial and hanging of my Lord Ferrers, while the entire world flocked to see him. The King of Prussia's capital had fallen; if the Austrians and Russians had encamped round the Tower, there could have been less stir in London: yet Miss Theo and her young gentleman felt no inordinate emotion of pity or indignation. To us, the fates of Leipzig or Berlin meant nothing. The truth is, that dear old house in Dean Street was an enchanted garden of delights. I have been idle since, but never as happy. Shall we order the postchaise, my dear, leaving the children to manage the household, and drive up to London to see if the old lodgings are still let? You shall sit at your old window seat and wave a little handkerchief as I ascend the street. Say what we did was imprudent. Would we not do it over again? My good folks, if Venus had walked into the room and challenged the golden apple, I was so infatuated I would have given it to your mother. And had she possessed the choice, she would have preferred her humble servant in a threadbare coat to my Lord Clive with all his diamonds.

Once, to be sure, and briefly in that year, I entertained notions of fleeing to the highway to be caught and hanged like my Lord Ferrers, or joining the King of Prussia to request that some of his enemies strike me down, or enlisting in the India service to perform a desperate exploit ending in my destruction. Ah me! That was indeed a dreadful time! Your mother scarce dares speak of it now, save in a whisper of terror, or think of it—it caused such cruel pain. She remained unhappy for years following the anniversary of that day, until one of you was born upon it. Suppose we had been parted: what would have become of us? What would my lot have been without her? As I contemplate that possibility, the whole world becomes a blank. I do not say we were parted now. It has pleased God to grant us thirty years of union. We have reached the autumn of our lives; our successors are appointed and ready; and whichever of us is first called away knows the survivor will follow ere long. Yet we were actually parted in our youth, and I tremble to think what might have been had not a dearest friend brought us together.

Unknown to myself, and likely intending only my advantage, my relatives in England chose to write to Madam Esmond in Virginia, representing what they deemed the folly of the engagement I had contracted. Every one of them sang the same song. I saw these letters and burned the whole cursed pack years afterward when my mother showed them to me at home in Virginia. Aunt Bernstein offered forward advice. A young person of no wonderful beauty, of no family, with no money—could there ever be such an imprudent connection, and ought it not, for dear George's sake, to be broken off? She cited several eligible matches for me. With my name and prospects, 'twas a shame I should waste myself on this young lady; her sister ought to interpose—and so forth.

My Lady Warrington must also write, and in her peculiar manner. Her ladyship's letter was adorned with scripture texts. She dressed her worldliness in phylacteries, pointing out how I lived in an unworthy society of players and the like, who she could not claim were absolutely without religion (Heaven forbid!), but who were deplorably worldly. She would not accuse an artful woman of inveigling me for her daughter, having vainly tried to captivate my younger brother. She was far from maligning the young woman I had selected; but it was certain, she argued, that Miss L. possessed no fortune or expectations, and her parents might naturally wish to compromise me. She had taken counsel, etcetera. She had sought guidance where it lay, etcetera. Feeling her duty, she had resolved to speak. Sir Miles, a man of excellent judgment in worldly affairs (though seeking something better), fully agreed with her opinion and desired her to write, entreating her sister to interfere so the ill-advised match should not proceed.

And who else must put a little finger into the pie but the new Countess of Castlewood? She penned a majestic letter to Madam Esmond, stating that having been placed by Providence at the head of the Esmond family, it was her duty to communicate with her kinswoman and warn her to break off this marriage. I believe the three women conferred privately beforehand; and packet after packet, they dispatched their warnings to the Virginian lady.

One raw April morning, as Corydon went to pay his usual duty to Phillis, he found not his charmer with her dear smile, ready to welcome him, but Mrs. Lambert, with very red eyes, and the General as pale as death. "Read this, George Warrington!" he said, as his wife's head dropped between her hands; and he placed a letter before me, the handwriting of which I recognized. I could hear now the sobs of the good Aunt Lambert, and to this day, the noise of fire-irons stirring a fire in a room overhead gives me a tremor. I heard such a noise that day in the girls' room where the sisters sat together. Poor, gentle child! Poor Theo!

"What can I do after this, George, my poor boy?" asked the General, pacing the room with desperation etched upon his face.

I did not read the entirety of Madam Esmond's letter, for a sickness and faintness seized me; but I fear I could recite some parts by heart now. Its style was

Once the initial shock of our reunion subsided, the little maid, Hetty, launched immediately into a torrent of accusation.

"So, you have finally arrived to inquire after Theo, and you feel contrite that your neglect brought her to such illness?" she demanded, her voice trembling with a mixture of fury and despair. "For six weeks she has languished, and you have uttered not a single word of concern! How very kind of you, Mr. George, I am sure!"

"Kind?" Mr. Warrington gasped, the word escaping him like a broken breath.

"I suppose you label kindness the act of visiting her every day for a year, only to vanish without a trace?" Hetty retorted, her shoulders rising in a sharp shrug. "A magnificent promise, indeed, to render my darling ill and then abruptly declare, 'Farewell, Theo,' and depart forever. Gentlemen make such vows, I assume, because they intend to honor them. I would not toy with a poor child's heart and abandon her if I were a man. What crime has she committed against you, but to be foolish and far too affectionate? Pray, sir, by what divine right do you whisk her away from us, only to desert her because an elderly American lady disapproves? She was content among us prior to your arrival. She adored her sister—there had never been a sibling so devoted—until she encountered you. And now, merely because your mother believes a younger gentleman might secure a better fortune, you must abandon her, simply because!"

"Great powers, child!" I cried, feeling the heat rise in my cheeks at her stubborn logic. "Did I draw back? Am I not the one forbidden entry into your household? Did your father not demand, upon my honor, that I cease all attempts to see her?"

"Honor!" she exclaimed, her voice cracking. "And you, who pretend to be our superiors, expect us to venerate you! I declare, George Warrington, you ought to retreat to your schoolroom in Virginia, have your black nurse tuck you into bed, and petition your mother for permission to step outside. Oh, George! I never imagined my sister was surrendering her heart to a man lacking the spine to stand by her; instead, he fled at the first obstacle! When Dr. Heberden mentioned attending to you, I resolved to visit, and you truly look ill, which brings me no comfort. I presume it is fear of your mother that plagues you. Yet, I shall not inform Theo of your sickness. She cannot abandon her solemn engagements, break free from the room, and stride into the world the next day as if nothing had transpired! That is reserved for men, our masters in courage and wisdom—to desert an angel, yes, an angel ten thousand times superior to you; an angel who once loved me until she met you, and who was the blessing of our lives—all of us—is what you term honor? Do not tell me, sir! I despise you all! Are we mere servants to your wit, your tragedies, and your verses? I consider them often tedious. I shall not spend my nights copying your manuscripts, nor waste hours gazing from a window, neglecting everyone to spy upon your stately walk with your hat cocked! If you are departing—and God bless you—give me back my sister! Return to me my darling of the olden days, who cherished every soul around her until she saw you. And you abandon her solely because your mother seeks a richer bride for you! Oh, valiant gentleman! Marry the woman your mother selects, and let my dear perish here, forsaken!"

"Heavens, Hetty!" I cried, stunned by the ferocity of her reasoning. "Am I the one wishing to abandon your sister? Did I not offer to uphold my promise? Was it not your father who denied me access and compelled me to swear never again to seek her sight? All I possess is my word and my honor!"

"Honor, indeed!" she shrieked, her composure crumbling. "You keep your pledge to him, yet shatter it for her! A wretched sort of honor! If I were a man, I would soon reveal exactly what I thought of your honor! Only I forgot—you are bound to maintain peace and must not... Oh, George, George! Do you not perceive the agony tearing me apart? I am distraught, scarcely knowing what I utter. You must not leave my darling. They are unaware at home. They do not comprehend as I do; I know her best, and she will succumb to death if you depart. Speak that you will stay! Have compassion on me, Mr. Warrington, and restore me to my dearest!"

Thus, the warm-hearted, frantic creature swayed from indignation to supplication, from scorn to tears. Was my little physician correct in describing her plea as such? Was there no alternative remedy to what Hetty cried out for? Had not others endured the same cruel severance, suffered the same exhaustion and fever, lain hopeless of survival, and yet, miraculously, recovered to limp through life? Perhaps, save for the fact that love is inherently selfish, disregarding the grief and passions of others, or that our bond was so intense and unique that we believed no other lovers could suffer as we did—in this passionate young advocate for her sister—we might have witnessed proof that a loving heart could be struck by the disease of love and endure it silently, eventually healing. What had occurred in Hetty's own case? Our sister and I, basking in our easy triumph and intimate confidences, had frequently discussed this matter, egotistical creatures that we were, perhaps deriving a secret zest and sense of security from her lesser attachment. It was akin to sitting by a fireplace while the winter howled without, or walking beside the great river and watching ships toss at sea. We clung to one another more tightly, and wrapped in our own felicity, regarded others' misfortunes with complacent pity. Let the truth be as it may. Granted that we might have been separated, and eventually survived the rift—much my skeptical old self admits. Yet, at that time, I was eager to share Hetty's terrors and apprehensions, willingly choosing to believe that the life dearest to me in the world would be sacrificed if severed from mine. Was I mistaken? I would not claim so now. I may harbor doubts regarding myself (or perhaps not, I know), but concerning her, never; and Hetty found in me a willing partner in her alarms and fears. I considered imparting some of these feelings to our doctor; but the good gentleman silenced me. "Hush," he said, with a comical expression of terror. "I must hear none of this. If two individuals who happen to know one another chanced to meet and converse in my patients' chamber, I could not help myself; but regarding matchmaking and courtship, I am your humble servant! What will the General do when he returns to town? He will have me stationed behind Montagu House as surely as I am a living doctor, and alive I wish to remain, my good sir!" With that, he skipped into his carriage, leaving me there to meditate. "And you and Miss Hetty must have no further meetings here, mind you," he had previously warned.

"Oh, no! Of course, we would have none!" I assured myself. "We are gentlemen of honor, and so forth, and our word is our bond." Furthermore, to have seen Hetty was not that an inestimable boon, and would we not be eternally grateful? I felt so refreshed by that drop of water I had consumed that I believed I could endure for an indefinite period. I walked away with Hetty to Soho, never once contemplating arranging a new rendezvous. But the little emissary was more perceptive, asking if I intended to visit the Museum to read. "Oh, yes, sometimes, my dear," I replied, "but I am too wretched for reading now; I cannot discern what lies upon the paper. I do not care for my books. Even *Pocahontas* grows wearisome to me. I..." I might have continued indefinitely, when, "Nonsense!" she declared, stamping her little foot. "By Jove, I declare, George, you are more stupid than Harry!"

"How do you mean, my dear child?" I asked.

"When do you depart?" she pressed. "You leave at three o'clock. You cross over to Tottenham Court Road. You traverse the village and return via Green Lane, leading back toward the new hospital. You know you do! If you stroll there for a week, it will do you no harm. Good morning, sir! Please, do not follow me any farther." And she dropped a curtsey, veiling her face, and departed.

That Green Lane, lying north of the new hospital, is now entirely built over with residences. In my time, when the virtuous King George II still reigned, it was a shabby rural thoroughfare of London, so perilous that city dwellers venturing to their villas and country houses at Hampstead and the outlying villages would return in groups during the night, accompanied by waiters carrying lanterns to protect them from highwaymen prowling the town's outskirts. The churches of Hampstead and Highgate, each crowning its hill, formed the backdrop of the vista one beheld turning away from London. For one, two, or three days, Mr. George Warrington enjoyed viewing this landscape and walking back in the direction of the new hospital.

Along the lane stood several small establishments of

Captain Miles Warrington, you possess a rare distinction: the favor of the Duchess of Devonshire, the attentions of Mrs. Crew, the gaze of the Queen of Prussia, and the admiration of countless others. Yet, if you secure a lady's heart, do you invariably march into the mess to recount your triumphs?"

"Not a fool, Squire!" retorted the Captain, scrutinizing his sideburns in the mirror.

"Have you, Miss Theo, recounted every syllable spoken to Mr. Joe Blake, Jr., in the shrubbery this morn?"

"Joe Blake, indeed!" cried Theo Junior.

"And you, Mademoiselle? Did you reveal the contents of that scented billet, franked by Sir Thomas? Observe her blush! It rivals the crimson curtain, I swear! Nay, Mademoiselle, we all harbor secrets," the Squire declared, executing a bow of his finest French persuasion. "No, Theo, there was naught in the shrubbery save nuts, my child! Nor do we divulge everything, not even to the most benevolent of fathers. And were I to recount my journey in a landau along Hampstead Road on the twenty-fifth of May, 1760, may Chevalier Ruspini pluck every tooth from my skull!"

"Do tell, Papa!" urged Mama. "Or, since Jobson drives us, perchance you will summon him from the stables! I demand the tale!"

"What is this mystery?" asked Mademoiselle, her charming French accent softening the query, directed at my wife.

"Eh, ma fille!" whispered the lady. "Should I answer what I said? I said 'Yes!'—see, that is all I said." Thus, it was my wife who had betrayed us, not I. Our exchange, as the carriage raced toward Hampstead and thundered back, amounted to this: Theo had not sworn to defy her honored parents. We, however, resolved to wed no other soul—not if we lived to Methuselah's age, nor would she accept the Prince of Wales himself. She had surrendered her heart with her father's consent, nay, his command; it was not hers to reclaim. Yet, a father of such kindness must eventually relent. If George kept his promise—whether today, in twenty years, or in another world—she knew she would never break hers.

Hetty's face lit with pure delight when I concluded my brief audience, seeing Theo's countenance wear a tranquil sweetness. "All the physician's medicine has done her less good," the devoted sister murmured. After their act of defiance, the girls returned home. I relinquished my post beside my cherished invalid. Hetty bounded back to her seat, and Charley scrambled onto his box. He later confessed that the sermon he had heard was dreadfully dull and obtuse; the little fellow was too orthodox to appreciate dissenting preachers.

Hetty was not the solitary observer of her sister's transformed visage and elevated spirits. Upon the girls' return, their mother embraced both, particularly the invalid, with an ardour surpassing the norm. "Nothing compares to a country ride," Aunt Lambert observed. "It has done her dear Theo wonders. She traversed the road to Hampstead, did she not? She requires another ride tomorrow. Blessed be heaven, Lord Wrotham's horses stand at our disposal thrice weekly, and the sweet child shall enjoy their advantage!" As for the notion that Mr. Warrington might have encountered the children during their drive, Aunt Lambert never entertained it, or at least, never voiced it. I leave it to those intrigued by the matter to conjecture whether Mrs. Lambert could possibly have imagined her daughter and her sweetheart reuniting. Do women aid one another in matters of love? Do they scheme, intrigue, weave small lies, fashion romantic opportunities, dangle the rope-ladder, coax and wheedle guardians, and divert attention while Strephon and Chloe whisper in the twilight or flee to Gretna Green? My dear young ones, some folk are thus constituted; and some kind souls, having loved tenderly and truly in their own time, remain kindly and tenderly disposed toward their young successors as they commence their own playful games.

Miss Prim differs. If she hears of two youths attached, she snaps at them as fools or imagines every conceivable malice. Possessed of a humpback, she bites at everyone else's. I believe if she spotted a pair of turtles cooing in a wood, she would cast down her gaze or hurl a stone to startle them. Yet, I speak of your grandmother, Aunt Lambert, who was a veritable syllabub of human kindness. Furthermore, concerning the affair currently under discussion, how am I to discern whether she knew anything of it or not?

Thus, all she imparted to Theo upon her return was, "My child, the country air has wrought miracles for you, and I hope you shall venture out again tomorrow, and the day after, and so on."

"Pray, do you not deem the ride has effected a wondrous transformation in the child, and ought she not be compelled to breathe the fresh air?" Aunt Lambert asked the General as he arrived for supper.

"Yes, assuredly! If a coach-and-six benefits little Theo, she shall have it," Lambert proclaimed. "Or he shall drag the landau up Hampstead Hill himself if horses are lacking!" And so, the generous man would have squandered his guineas, his breath, or his blood to grant his child pleasure. He was enchanted by his girl's altered countenance; she devoured a morsel of chicken with appetite; she sipped a negus he prepared, which seemed superior to the kind doctor's best remedies, which, God witting, had yielded little benefit. Mama was gracious and joyful. Hetty shone with radiance. It resembled an evening at home at Oakhurst. Not for months past, not since that fatal, cruel day unspoken of, had they shared such a delightful night.

Yet, if the other women sought to coax and cajole the simple father, Theo herself was too honest to sustain that sweet, fond delusion. When, for the third or fourth time, he returned to the delightful topic of his daughter's restored health, asking, "What hath wrought this? Is it the country air? Is it the Jesuit's bark? Is it the new medicine?"

"Cannot you divine, dear, what it is?" she said, placing a hand upon her father's with a tremble in her voice, though her eyes remained open and bright.

"And what is it, my child?" asked the General.

"It is because I have seen him again, Papa!" she declared.

The other two women turned pale, and Theo's heart began to pound, her cheeks flushing white as she continued to gaze into her father's terrified face.

"It was not wrong to behold him," she pressed, more urgently; "it would have been wrong not to inform you."

"Great God!" groaned the father, recoiling his hand, his countenance etched with dreadful grief, until Hetty ran to her nearly swooning sister, clutching her to her bosom and crying out, "Theo knew nothing of it, sir! It was my doing—it was all my doing!"

Theo lay upon her sister's neck, kissing it twenty, fifty times.

"Women, women! Are ye playing with my honor?" cried the father, bursting forth with a fierce exclamation.

"Aunt Lambert sobbed wildly, 'Martin! Martin! Speak not a word to her!'" Hetty shrieked again, collapsing backward, staggering toward the wall, for Theo had fainted upon her shoulder.

I took my breakfast the following morn, with whatever appetite I possessed, when my door opened and my faithful black announced, "General Lambert." Instantly, by the General's visage, I perceived that the prior day's transaction was known to him. "Your accomplices did not confess," the General stated as soon as my servant departed, "but sided with you against their father—a testament to how desirable clandestine meetings are. It was from Theo herself I learned she had beheld you."

"Accomplices, sir!" I replied (perhaps not unwilling to deflect the conversation from the crux of the issue). "You know how fondly and dutifully your young ones regard their father. If they oppose you herein, it must be because justice stands against you. A man like you shall not erect *sic volo, sic jubeo* as the sole law in his household!"

"Pshaw, George!" cried the General. "Though we are parted, God forbid I should wish our love to cease. I had promised you you would not seek to see her."

"Nor did I go to her, sir," I said, turning a deep shade of red, for though this was truth, I owned it was untrue.

"You mean she was brought to you?" asked Theo's father, in great agitation. "Shall you shelter yourself behind Hester's petticoat? What a fine defense for a gentleman!"

"Well, I will not screen myself behind the poor child," I rejoined. "To speak as I did was an attempt at evasion, and I am ill-accustomed to dissimulation. I did not infringe the letter of my agreement, but I acted against its spirit. From this moment I annul it altogether."

"You break your word given to me!" cried Mr. Lambert.

"I recall a hasty promise made suddenly at a moment of extreme excitement and perturbation. No man can forever be bound by words uttered at such a time; and

Years later, sorting through the dusty archives of my home, I stumbled upon a packet labeled with Mother's precise, familiar script: "From London, April, 1760. My son's dreadful letter." When it finally became mine, I burned it without hesitation. I could not allow a chronicle of such domestic ruin and estrangement to linger in our family history, perhaps to serve as a grim warning to future generations of wayward sons. With equal resolve, I have since consigned to the flames the reply she sent me during this season of tyranny, rebellion, and irritation.

Blinded by the agony of separation from my mistress, and rightly identifying Mrs. Esmond as the architect of the deepest misery I had ever known, I penned a letter home to Virginia. Though I could have written with greater moderation, I strove to maintain an outward facade of extreme respect and silence. I spoke of not understanding her motives, yet I laid the burden of my future wretchedness squarely at her feet, accusing her of deliberately ruining my life. I detailed how she had severed me from a virtuous, innocent youth, casting down her hopes, health, and joy forever. I feigned ignorance of her actions, claiming they were matters for God alone to judge, yet I could not hide the truth: the wound she had inflicted was mortal. I told her that the bond of allegiance I owed her was irrevocably broken; I could never again be the dutiful, respectful son I had been.

Madam Esmond's reply arrived in a vessel of profound dignity. Her penmanship remained as elegant as ever, yet her tone was chilling. She offered no reproaches, no harsh words. Instead, she calmly referred the case between us to that awful Tribunal of God, asking for counsel. She stated humbly that regarding her conduct as a mother, she stood ready to face judgment. Then she turned the question back upon me: Could I, as a son, answer for myself? Could I demonstrate, when the Great Judge demanded it, that I had fulfilled my duties and honored my parents?

*O popoi!* My grandfather, in his memoirs, often cited a line of Homer illustrating how, in trouble and grief, men invariably summon the gods. When pride, avarice, ambition, and the lust to dominate stir within us, do we not endlessly beset Heaven to rule in our favor? Consider our great American quarrel: did not both sides appeal to the skies regarding the justice of their causes, sing *Te Deum* for victory, and confidently declare that righteousness would prevail? Was America right merely because she triumphed? If so, surely Poland was wrong because she fell?—Yet, how am I drifting into this tangent about Poland and America, while my thoughts return to a little woman, now gone, who appealed to Heaven and confronted it with a thousand scriptures, simply because her son desired a marriage contrary to her wishes?

We plead, we curse, we kneel, we beg for blessings, we scream for legal sentences; meanwhile, the vast current of the world rolls on. We pant, strive, struggle; we hate, rage, weep passionate tears; we reconcile, race, and win—or lose. We pass away, and other little strugglers rise in our wake. Our days are spent, our nights arrive, and another morning breaks, shining on us no more.

I recall sending a duplicate of my letter to Madam Esmond, announcing my revolt and disobedience (perhaps I was secretly proud of the composition). I showed it to Mr. Lambert, wishing him to understand the state of my relations with my mother and my determination to view my separation from Theo as nothing short of a forced exile. I promised her, *in secula seculorum*, that whenever I could see her again, I would. I implied that Theo was bound to me just as firmly. Her poor father understood this truth. He might have separated us, yes—he might even have administered poison, and the gentle, obedient creature would have swallowed it and died—but the guilt would have been his alone. Let him answer for that. Yet, he was tender-hearted, weak in resolve, and unable to subject either of his children, especially this one, to torture. We had tried to part; we could not. He sought to divide us, but it was beyond his power. The barriers were raised, yet the maid within and the knight without continued to love each other. The wall stood firm, but Pyramus and Thisbe whispered across it. Amidst his grief and confusion, Uncle Lambert possessed a keen sense of humor, recognizing the sorry nature of his role. Light began to pierce the rough lime-plaster of the wall; the lovers were working their hands through, then their heads—it was high time for the wall to retreat.

I cannot recall the stages of this transition day by day, nor does it greatly concern the instruction of future ages. When my descendants encounter their own love-scrapes, they will find their own remedies. I believe I ceased visiting Dean Street, as the open-air exercise was deemed most salutary for Miss Lambert. I acquired a fine horse and rode beside her carriage. The elderly woman at Tottenham Court soon recognized us both, nodding and winking with friendly familiarity as we passed. I suspect the old goody was not unaccustomed to lending the hospitality of her roadside cottage to young couples.

The physician and the country air wrought a prodigious cure upon Miss Lambert. Hetty invariably served as duenna, listening with exemplary patience to the endless amatory conversations that occurred, sometimes even during holidays, when Master Charley rode my horse once I had entered the carriage. Oh, the quantity of love-making Hetty heard! When we traveled in a closed carriage, she would kindly glance out the window. How many letters were exchanged! How much running to and fro! Gumbo's bandy legs were perpetually on the trot between my quarters and Dean Street, while Mrs. Molly, the girl's maid, constantly returned answers to Bloomsbury. By the time autumn leaves turned pale, Miss Theo's roses were in full bloom again, and Doctor Heberden pronounced his cure complete.

During this blessed interval, Mr. Warrington completed his grand tragedy, *Pocahontas*. This time, it was not only accepted by Mr. Garrick, following favorable remarks from his friend Dr. Johnson, but my friend and cousin, Hagan, was engaged by the manager to play the hero, Captain Smith. Hagan's engagement was secured only when needed. I had assisted him and his family with means disproportionate to my resources, particularly given my feud with Madam Esmond. Her reply to my angry missive of April arrived towards autumn, sent from Virginia with war for war, countermand for countermand. However, these menaces frightened me little; my poor mother's thunder could not reach me. My conscience, or perhaps my casuistry, supplied alternative interpretations for her biblical texts, rendering her oracles weightless in scaring me from my purpose. I told no one of my new love, nor any member of my maternal or paternal families, who had bitterly opposed the union. Why wrangle with them? It was better to seize the day (*carpere diem*) with its sweet loves and pleasures, leaving the railers to grumble and the elders to advise at their leisure.

Besides Madam Esmond, I must admit, in the frantic rage of my temporary separation, I sent notes of wondrous sarcasm to Uncle Warrington, Aunt Madame de Bernstein, and Lord or Lady of Castlewood (I forget which individual specifically). I thanked them for the trouble they had taken in preventing the dearest happiness of my life, promising a corresponding gratitude from my obliged relative. Business brought the jovial Baronet and his family to London somewhat earlier than usual, and Madame de Bernstein was never sorry to return to Clarges Street and her cards. I saw them. They found me perfectly well. Concluding the match was broken off, I chose not to undeceive them. The Baroness took heart at my cheerfulness, making many sly jokes about my philosophy and prudent behavior as a man of the world. As ever, she was bent upon securing a rich match for me, and I fear I paid many compliments at her house to a wealthy young soap-boiler's daughter from Mile End, whom the worthy Baroness wished to place in my arms.

"You court her with infinite wit and *esprit*, my dear," my pleased kinswoman observed, "but she does not understand half you say, and the other half, I think, frightens her. This *ton de persiflage* is very well in our society, but you must be sparing of it, my dear nephew, amongst these roturiers."

Miss Badge married a young gentleman of royal dignity, though his fortunes were shattered, from a neighboring island; and I trust Mrs. Mackshane has ere this pardoned my levity. There was another person at my aunt's house who did not understand my irony much better than Miss Badge herself; she was a lady who had seen the reign of James the Second, and who lived and breathed worldly wisdom in King George's time. I loved to be with her, though, lest my little readers access this volume, I shall omit a hundred stories of the great old folks she had known—the great old days of George the First and his ladies, of St. John and Marlborough, of the reigning Majesty and the late Prince of Wales, and the causes of their quarrel—but my modest muse pipes for boys and virgins. So, my dear wife, you need not shake your powdered locks at me! Papa is not going to scandalize his nursery with old-world gossip, nor bring a blush over our chaste bread-and-butter.

However, I

Those of us who had played a part in the guilt of that morning felt a treble burden of shame when we beheld the effect our conduct had wrought upon him, the man whom above all others we loved and revered. The shock to the good man was strange, yet pitiful to behold, especially for those who had delivered the blow. His cherished child had deceived and defied him—I confess to you, my dear, that in the light of eternity, we would never, could never, repeat such a deed; indeed, the entire family seemed to have conspired in a league against his peace. Oh, dear, kind friend and father! We know thou hast pardoned our trespass—within the Heaven wherein thou dwellest among purified spirits who learned on earth the art of loving and forgiving! With such a man, love and forgiveness were natural duties; beneficence flowed from him as easily as breath, accompanied by a sweet, smiling humility. To wound either virtue was to become savage and brutal, akin to torturing a child or striking a nursing mother. Once the deed was done, all we guilty ones groveled in the dust before the injured man. I shall pass over the scenes of forgiveness, reconciliation, and our shared worship, followed by the final separation when the good man departed for his government and the ship sailed away before us, leaving me and Theo stranded on the shore. We stood there, hand in hand, horribly abashed, silent, and consumed by guilt. My wife did not seek me until her father had gone; in the interval between the ceremony of our marriage and his departure, she had remained at home, occupying her old place by her father's side and beside her sister. He was as kind as ever, yet the women were almost speechless amongst themselves; Aunt Lambert, for once, unkind and fretful in her temper; and little Hetty, feverish and strange, muttering, "I wish we were gone. I wish we were gone." Though admitted to the house and forgiven, I slunk away during those last days, seeing my wife for only a minute or two in the street or within the confines of her family. She was not truly mine until they were gone. We proceeded to Winchester and Hampton for what may be termed our wedding. It was, alas, a dismal business. For a while, we felt utterly lonely, grieving for our dear father as though we had buried him, or driven him to the grave by our undutifulness.

I instructed Sampson to announce our marriage in the newspapers—a task for which my wife would later hang her head in sorrow before the poor fellow. I took Mrs. Warrington back to my old lodgings in Bloomsbury, where there was ample room for us, and our modest married life commenced. I wrote home a letter to my mother in Virginia, omitting particulars but stating that since Mr. Lambert was about to depart for his government, I felt honor-bound to fulfill my promise to his dearest daughter; furthermore, I declared my intention to complete my studies for the Bar and qualify for employment at home or in any colony. My good Mrs. Mountain answered this letter, acting on the desire of Madam Esmond, who deemed it best for the sake of peace that my communications be conducted thus. I found my relatives in a fury that was perfectly amusing to witness. The butler's face, as he said, "Not at home," at my uncle's house in Hill Street, was a blank tragedy worthy of study by Garrick himself. My poor little wife rested on my arm, and we tripped away, laughing at the fellow's *accueil*, only to encounter my lady in a street stoppage in her chair. I took off my hat and made her the lowest possible bow. I affectionately asked after my dear cousins. "I—I wonder you dare look me in the face!" gasped Lady Warrington. "Nay, don't deprive me of that precious privilege!" said I. "Move on, Peter," she screamed to her chairman. "Your ladyship would not impale your own husband's flesh and blood!" I retorted. She rattled up the glass of her chair in a fury. I kissed my hand, removed my hat, and performed another of my very finest bows.

Shortly afterwards, walking in Hyde Park with my dearest companion, I met my little cousin exercising on horseback with a groom behind him. As soon as he saw us, he galloped up, the groom powdering afterwards and bawling out, "Stop, Master Miles, stop!"

"I am not to speak to my cousin," said Miles, "but telling you to send my love to Harry is not speaking to you, is it? Is that my new cousin? I'm not told not to speak to her. I'm Miles, cousin, Sir Miles Warrington Baronet's son, and you are very pretty!" "Now, duee now, Master Miles," said the groom, touching his hat to us, and the boy trotted away laughing and looking at us over his shoulder. "You see how my relations have determined to treat me," I said to my partner. "As if I married you for your relations!" said Theo, her eyes beaming with joy and love into mine. Ah, how happy we were! How brisk and pleasant the winter! How snug the kettle by the fire (where the abashed Sampson sometimes came and made the punch); how delightful the night at the theatre, for which our friends brought us tickets of admission, and where we daily expected our new play of *Pocahontas* would rival the successes of all former tragedies.

The fickle old aunt of Clarges Street, who had received me on my first coming to London with my wife with a burst of scorn, presently mollified. As soon as she came to know Theo—who she had previously pronounced to be an insignificant little country-faced chit—she fell utterly in love with her, inviting her to tea and supper every day whenever there was no other company. "As for company, my dears," she would say, "I don't ask you. You are no longer *du monde*. Your marriage has put that entirely out of the question." Thus she sought to have us come to amuse her, entering and exiting by the back stairs. My wife was a fine lady enough to feel only amused at this reception; and I must do justice to the Baroness's domestics, who treated us with a respect we could not have received if we had been duke and duchess. Madame de Bernstein was greatly tickled and amused by my story of Lady Warrington and the chair. I acted it for her, recounting anecdotes of the pious Baronet's lady and her daughters, which pleased the mischievous, lively old woman.

The Dowager Countess of Castlewood, now established in her house at Kensington, gave us that sort of welcome which genteel ladies extend to their poorer relatives. We visited her ladyship's drawing-rooms at Kensington once or twice; but, losing more money at cards and spending more on coach-hire than I cared to afford, we speedily gave up those entertainments. I dare say we were no more missed or regretted than other people in the fashionable world who are carried by death, debt, or other accident out of the polite sphere. My Theo did not in the least regret this exclusion. She had made her appearance at one of these gatherings, attired in some little ornaments left by her mother, by which the good lady set some store; but I thought her own white neck far prettier than these poor twinkling stones. There were dowagers whose wrinkled old bones blazed with rubies and diamonds, which, I am sure, they would gladly have exchanged for her modest parure of beauty and freshness. Not a soul spoke to her—except, to be sure, Beau Lothair, a friend of Mr. Will's, who prowled about Bloomsbury afterwards and even sent my wife a billet. I met him in Covent Garden shortly after and promised to break his ugly face if ever I saw it in the neighborhood of my lodgings, and Madam Theo was molested no further.

The only relative who came to see us (Madame de Bernstein never came, sending her coach or making inquiries via her woman or major-domo) was our poor Maria, who, with her husband, Mr. Hagan, often took a share of our homely dinners. Then we had friend Spencer from the Temple, who admired our Arcadian felicity and gently asked for our sympathy regarding his less fortunate loves; and twice or thrice the famous Doctor Johnson came in for a dish of Theo's tea. A dish? A pailful! "And a pail the best thing to feed him, sar!" said Mr. Gumbo, indignantly; for the Doctor's appearance was not pleasant, nor his linen particularly white. He snorted, grew red, and sputtered while feeding; he flung his meat about and bawled out contradictions, annoying my Theo, whom he professed to admire greatly, by declaring every time he saw her, "Madam, you do not love me; I see by your manner you do not love me; though I admire you, and come here for your sake. Here is my friend Mr. Reynolds that shall paint you: he has no ceruse in his paint-box that is as brilliant as your complexion." Indeed, Mr. Reynolds, a most perfect and agreeable gentleman, would have painted my wife, but I knew what his price was and did not choose to incur that expense. I wish I had now, for the sake of the children, that they might see what yonder face was like some fifty years ago. To me, madam, 'tis the same now as ever; and

The English public, unfamiliar with the history of Pocahontas as we Virginians are, who still cherish the memory of that gentle soul, heard Mr. Warrington compose a brief ballad on the subject. At his friends' urging, he printed it in the magazines shortly before the tragedy's premiere. Sampson and I deemed this move a stroke of cunning genius. "It is like ground-bait, sir," declared the zealous clergyman, "and you shall see the fish rise in multitudes on the great day!" He and Spencer insisted they had heard the poem discussed and lauded in various coffee-houses, with credit mistakenly assigned to Mr. Mason, Mr. Cowper, and even the renowned Mr. Gray. I suspect poor Sam spread these rumors himself; had Shakespeare been rumored the author, he would have hailed *Pocahontas* as the poet's finest achievement.

My connection with Captain Smith began in childhood, within the hallowed library of my grandfather. I recall sitting at his knees, the favorite volume resting on my lap, decoding the exploits of our Virginian hero. I devoured tales of his wanderings, sufferings, and escapes, both across the Atlantic and within Europe. Reading the book now, in England, transported me back to those early days, flooding my mind with vivid memories. My grandfather would paint pictures for me of Smith battling Turks on the Danube or being dragged forth by savages to execution. I marveled at the terrifying duel between Smith and Bonny Molgro, the third and most fearsome champion. With his prodigious turban, scimitar, and whiskers, Bonny Molgro seemed a figure of legend. After dispatching his first two foes, Smith faced Molgro. According to my cherished tome, they clashed with battle-axes whose sharp edges caused both rider and steed to reel in confusion, the Christian losing his weapon. Yet, through the agility of his horse and his own judgment, Smith drew his falchion, piercing the Turk beneath the armor, through back and body. Though Molgro dismounted, he fell swiftly, meeting his end as the others had. For this feat, Duke Segismundus bestowed upon him a shield bearing three Turkish heads and an annual pension of three hundred ducats.

In my tragedy, disregarding time and place—a liberty poets enjoy—Smith performed similar feats on the banks of the Potomac and the James. Our "ground-bait" verses ran thus:

>
> Wearied arm and broken sword
> Wage in vain the desperate fight;
> Round him press the countless horde,
> He is but a single knight.
> Hark! a cry of triumph shrill
> Through the wilderness resounds,
> As, with twenty bleeding wounds,
> Sinks the warrior, fighting still.
>
> Now they heap the fatal pyre,
> And the torch of death they light;
> Ah! 'tis hard to die of fire!
> Who will shield the captive knight?
> Round the stake with fiendish cry
> Wheel and dance the savage crowd,
> Cold the victim's mien and proud,
> And his breast is bared to die.
>
> Who will shield the fearless heart?
> Who avert the murderous blade?
> From the throng, with sudden start,
> See, there springs an Indian maid.
> Quick she stands before the knight,
> Loose the chain, unbind the ring,
> I am daughter of the king,
> And I claim the Indian right!
>
> Dauntlessly aside she flings
> Lifted axe and thirsty knife;
> Fondly to his heart she clings,
> And her bosom guards his life!
> In the woods of Powhattan,
> Still 'tis told, by Indian fires,
> How a daughter of their sires
> Saved the captive Englishman.

Needless to say, the plot required no lengthy description; my children could consult the shelves at any hour. Nor was I eager to recount it again to them, for Captain Miles and the parson had fallen asleep during our previous reading. However, anyone versed in drama or fiction could easily construct a narrative from this sketch. An Indian king, a loving princess, her attendant enamored of the British captain's servant, a traitor within the fort, a valiant Indian warrior pining for Pocahontas, a medicine-man and priest capable of treachery and torture—all these elements, combined with wilderness accidents, war dances, cries mimicked by Gumbo, the arrival of the English fleet, and references to recent Canadian victories, promised success.
