Yet, ominous signs preceded the opening night. Difficulties arose from a peevish, jealous, and timid management, alongside violent prejudices held by certain influential circles. Was it surprising, then, that *Pocahontas* failed? I laughed at the malignity of critics who faulted the performance. How ironic, they praised *Carpezan* as a masterpiece while sneering at a far superior and more intricate work. I maintained that Hagan's portrayal was so admirable that a certain actor-manager ought to feel jealousy, and without external interference, the piece would have triumphed. Orders had been issued to ensure failure, according to Sampson. "The house swarmed with Macs, by George," the honest fellow swore, "and they should have the galleries washed with brimstone." He vowed that even Mr. Garrick himself would not have allowed the play to succeed, growing particularly enraged during the grand scene in the second act when Smith (played by poor Hagan) was bound to the stake and saved by Pocahontas, eliciting thunderous applause and sympathy from the entire house.

Those curious enough may refer to the published tragedy, available in octavo or the subsequent quarto edition of my Collected Works, to judge the merit of the scene, the elegance of the verses, and the richness of the language. One cause of failure was my strict adherence to historical accuracy. I had copied a figure of Sir Walter Raleigh from the Museum, complete with frills and beard, and, thanks to Theo's contribution of her mother's finest lace, dressed Hagan precisely after this drawing. No one could appear more authentic. Similarly, I dressed Miss Pritchard as Pocahontas, informed by my own observations of indigenous attire at home. Would it be believed that the audience tittered when she first appeared? Eventually, they grew accustomed, but just as she rushed into the prisoner's embrace, causing many to tearfully sympathize, a fellow in the pit shouted, "Bedad! Here's the Belle Savage kissing the Saracen's Head!" An impudent roar of laughter erupted, continuing intermittently throughout the performance. Like the wag in Mr. Sheridan's *The Critic*, who demanded multiple morning guns, the playwrights were not satisfied with one jab; they fired two or three. This wretched tavern joke—the ignorant failing to recognize that Pocahontas herself was the Belle Sauvage from whom the tavern derived its name—was repeated ad nauseam. As each new character entered, the friend in the pit greeted them with tavern names: the English governor became "The Goat and Boots," his lieutenant Barker, with his broad visage, "The Bull and Mouth," and so on. The curtain fell amidst a shrill storm of whistles and hisses, particularly targeting poor Hagan whenever he spoke. Sampson spotted Master Will in the green boxes with some of his acquaintances, convinced the treacherous scoundrel was among the conspirators. "I would have flung him over into the pit," the faithful Sampson declared, executing his threat, though he had to retreat due to two of Mr. Nadab's followers prowling the lobby. Thus, our hoped-for profits were shattered, our dreams broken!

Before the curtain rose, I glanced into the house from the stage, noting it was reasonably full, especially Mr. Johnson in the front box, accompanied by Mr. Reynolds, unable to hear or see clearly, forming a duo to critique my tragic endeavor. I also spotted Lady Maria in the lower gallery, where she once again sat to watch her beloved actor portray a leading role. As for Theo, she frankly admitted she preferred not attending unless I commanded otherwise, and I, foreseeing the pain of witnessing her suffering if things went awry, acquiesced to her wish to stay away.

Of a relatively even temperament, confident in bearing either good or bad fortune without disturbance, I dined lightly at the Bedford and arrived at the theater moments before the play commenced, intending to remain until victory or defeat was decided. Indeed, I could foresee the likely outcome. Everything around exuded a gloom and disaster. Miss Pritchard suffered from a headache; the barber who powdered Hagan's wig acted like a wretch, ruining the appearance; among the gentlemen and ladies in the green room, only doubtful faces prevailed; and the manager—a flippant, if not impertinent gentleman, who himself looked as dismal as a mute at a funeral—had the audacity to suggest, "For Heaven's sake, Mr. Warrington, go get a glass of punch at the Bedford, and don't frighten us all here by your dismal countenance!"

"Sir," I replied, "I have a right, for five shillings, to comment upon your face, but I never granted you authority to remark upon mine." "Sir," he retorted petulantly, "I most heartily wish I had never seen your face at all!" "Yours, sir!" I countered, "has often amused me greatly; and when painted for Abel Drugger, it is

Having already laid bare my present sentiments as an aged gentleman concerning that precipitous leap into matrimony, which I urged upon my beloved partner when neither of us had fully emerged from adolescence, I pause to reflect. As a man and a father—with a proper reverence for the necessity of sustenance, the imperative of settling accounts with the baker, and a motley crew of spirited children who might abscond to Scotland tomorrow, citing parental precedent for their audacity—I recognize the gravity of recounting the early days of Sir George Warrington and Theodosia’s union. Prudence dictates a display of *mea culpa*, a demure posture, and a humble admission of repentance from my armchair, offering myself as a cautionary tale to the rash and impulsive among the youth.

Yet, truth be told, the matrimonial existence foreseen with such grim prophecy by my dear relations has defied their somber expectations. It has known trials, certainly, but I recall them without bitterness; it has harbored passions that brought grief, yet time, by divine ordinance, has acted as a benign healer. There were days of scarcity, borne with a fortitude that left our sympathetic kin marveling, and precious rewards so profound I scarcely dare whisper them to paper, speaking of them only with reverential awe to the ears of Him to whom all prayers ascend. To marry without financial security is undeniably hazardous, a breach of social code, perhaps even a crime; yet, do not thousands of souls annually commit this "crime" trusting solely in Heaven, health, and labor? Does not every young couple entering the state of matrimony ignore the necessity of a fully furnished cottage, a stocked cellar, a cupboards brimming with plate, and a strongbox overflowing with coin? Were the laws governing the genteel classes universalized, the growth of the world would surely stall. The aristocracy trembles at the edge of the abyss in silk stockings and waiting for a golden bridge, while the poor fear not to wet their bare feet in the stream, planting them firmly in the current and trusting in fate and strength to carry them across. Who would wish to consign a daughter to want? Who would advise a son to risk the countless perils of impoverished marriage—to strip a beloved girl of comfort and competence, subjecting her to debt, misery, isolation, sickness, and the myriad shadows of *res angusta domi*? I gaze upon my wife and beg pardon for imposing a burden so steeped in pain and peril upon one of such gentleness. Reflecting on her endurance, I am filled with gratitude for the trials and for that unyielding love and constancy with which God blessed and fortified her. On the matter of marriage, I am far from impartial: my own union was so imprudent, yet so blissful, that I cannot offer counsel. I have weathered poverty and found it, strangely, tolerable. Had I not traversed that path, I could never have known the sweetness of friendship, the joy of gratitude, or the astonishing consolations that sometimes attend a scanty meal and a narrow fire, cheering the weary soul at the close of a long laborsome day. At least, it is certain that regarding the decent poor, a vast amount of superfluous pity is wasted. Well-meaning individuals, stepping briefly from the sunshine of wealth into narrow obscurity, became blinded, stumbling over obstacles invisible to us, surprised at our cheerful resignation as we drank small beer and gave thanks over cold mutton.

My father-in-law, the esteemed General, wed his Molly when he was merely a subaltern in a foot regiment, possessing a purse no fuller than my own. Their fortunes rose and fell. I suspect—though my wife would never confess—that they married without seeking parental permission, a liberty possible in their youth. Yet, so gratified were they with their marital luck that they grudged none of their children's hardships, nor were they daunted by the prospect of the difficulties we inevitably faced. When I presented my financial statements to Mr. Lambert, I likely sought to mislead both parties. Convinced I commanded a fortune of two thousand pounds, the worthy gentleman departed for Jamaica with ease of mind regarding his darling daughter's future, at least for some years. After covering family expenses, he left scarcely richer than his son-in-law. His wife's contribution to Theo consisted of trifles, some lace from Aunt Lambert, and twenty new guineas in a purse crafted by her mother and sisters. However, in assessing my stock, I counted as a good debt a sum my honored mother never acknowledged until her final hour. She argued that funds I had disbursed were investments in the estate destined for me upon her death. Any surplus she possessed was reserved for my poor brother, who, lacking resources, would never have spent his own means had he not believed himself the sole heir of the Virginian property—a point the good lady emphasized repeatedly in her correspondence—but for a half-hour accident of birth. Now, he distinguishes himself in the service of King and Country. Purchasing his promotion, she deemed it her duty, as would he had circumstances allowed. Upon completing my bar studies and theatrical diversions, Madam Esmond declared me welcome to return home and assume the colonial position to which my birth entitled me. She conveyed this message to me more than once through Mountain, prior to news of my marriage reaching her.

Need I recall her expressions of maternal indignation when informed of my step? Upon the pacification of Canada, my dear Harry requested leave and dutifully visited Virginia. He wrote of his reception and the splendid entertainments my mother hosted in honor of her son. Castlewood, uninhabited since our departure for Europe, was reopened to colonial friends. The friend of Wolfe and the soldier of Quebec was received with becoming honors. Indeed, dismal quarrels ensued, for Harry persisted in his friendship with Colonel Washington of Mount Vernon, whose praises Harry never tire of singing. I allow the gentleman every virtue; in the struggles that terminated so fatally for England, I can admire as well as his warmest supporters General Washington's glorious constancy and success.

If these skirmishes between Harry and our mother were frequent, as his letters described, I wondered why he remained at home. A natural suggestion occurred to me, which I hesitated to share with Mrs. Warrington; for we had discussed Hetty's romantic attachment to my brother, wondering why he had never discerned it. I need not say that my gentleman had found a young lady at home more to his taste than our dear Hester, thus accounting for his prolonged stay.

Soon, however, a letter arrived containing not a full confession but an admission of this intriguing fact. A person was described, unnamed—a Being of beauty and perfection, like other young ladies under similar circumstances. My wife requested to see the letter. Reluctantly, I showed it, handing it to her with a grave countenance. To my astonishment, she read it without exhibiting corresponding sorrow.

"I have thought of this before, my love," I said. "I feel with you for your disappointment regarding poor Hetty."

"AH! poor Hetty!" sighed Theo, gazing down at the carpet.

"It would never have done," I remarked.

"No—they would not have been happy," Theo murmured.

"How strange he never should have found out her secret!" I continued.

She looked me full in the face with an odd expression. "Pray, what does that look mean?" I asked.

"Nothing, my dear—nothing! Only I am not surprised!" said Theo, blushing.

"What!" I exclaimed, with a strange commotion within me. "Do you mean to tell me, Theo, that you ever cared for anyone but me?"

"Oh, George," she whimpered, "when I was at school, there was—one of the boys of Doctor Backhouse's school, who sat in the loft next to us; and I thought he had lovely eyes, and I was so shocked when I recognized him behind the counter at Mr. Grigg's the mercer's, when I went to buy a cloak for baby, and I wanted to tell you, my dear, and I didn't know how!"

I ventured to see this creature of lovely eyes, having had my wife describe the fellow's attire. I observed a little bandy-legged wretch in a blue camlet coat, with his red hair tied by a dirty ribbon, about whom I forbore even to reproach my wife; nor will she ever know that I looked upon him, until she reads this confession. If our wives saw us as we truly are, I thought, would they love us as they do? Are we as much mistaken in them as they in us? I look into one candid face at least, and think it has never deceived me.

Lest I encourage my young people to emulate my own imprudence, I shall not reveal with how small a capital Mrs. Theo and I commenced life. The unfortunate tragedy brought us naught; though reviewers, in recent times, have spoken somewhat favorably of its merits, and Mr. Kemble himself has done me the honor of commendation. Our kind friend Lord Wrotham intended to publish the piece by subscription and sent me a banknote, requesting a hundred copies for his friends. Yet, I was always averse to such methods of levying money, and, preferring my poverty *sine dote*, I locked up my manuscript, with my poor girl's verses inserted at the first page. I know not why the piece should have offended at court, save for the fact that an actor who had eloped with an Earl's daughter performed a principal part. Sentiments I had placed in the mouths of certain Indian characters—who declaimed against ambition and the British desire for rule—were pronounced dangerous and unconstitutional; so that the little hope

Our little Prince arrived in this world scarcely days after the gracious monarch who commanded the regiment. Great illuminations and thundering cannons saluted the birth of the Royal George, allowing multitudes to gaze upon him as he lay behind gilded railings at the Palace, tended by noble nurses. Yet, few guardians watched over the cradle of our own small heir. No courtiers, no faithful retainers offered their salutes, save for our trusty Gumbo and kind Molly, who, it must be said, loved and adored the little descendant of my poverty with a loyalty that warmed our hearts.

Why did we not name our boy George, like the paragon above and like his father? I chose instead the name of a minor scamp within my own lineage—a title many generations of the Warringtons had worn. But the love and kindness displayed by this tiny stranger moved me deeply at a time when affection from my own blood felt scarce. Thus, Theo and I resolved that our child should bear the name of this single, bright friend of my paternal race.

We penned letters to inform our royal parents of this auspicious event, and boldly announced his birth in the *Daily Advertiser*, specifying our humble abode at Church Street, Lambeth. "My dear," wrote Aunt Bernstein in reply to my proclamation, "how could you point out to the entire world that you dwell in such a thoroughfare as that wherein you have hidden yourself? I kiss the little mamma, and send a token for the child." This token proved to be a magnificent silk coverlet, edged with lace fit for a prince. It held little practical value—the cost of the lace alone would have served us far better—but Theo and Molly were utterly delighted. Consequently, my eldest son's cradle was draped in a covering finer than any nobleman's.

Dr. Heberden visited my wife repeatedly, ensuring everything proceeded smoothly. He had recommended a local surgeon who attended to her needs; fortunately, my dear patient required little more than the attentive care afforded by our landlady and her loyal attendant. Soon, our modest precinct was once again graced by the gilded wheels of Lady Castlewood's chariot. She delivered a pot of jelly, which she believed Theo might relish, undoubtedly having been served at one of her ladyship's lavish banquets on a prior day. She regaled us with tales of the court ceremonies and the splendor surrounding the newborn heir to the crown.

Just then, our good Mr. Johnson paid us a call on a day when the Countess's carriage blazed with magnificence near our gate. He was somewhat taken aback by her grandeur and offered her bows—more respectful than graceful. She addressed me as "Cousin" with affable warmth and assisted in transferring her gift of jelly from a silver dish to our crockery pan with immense benevolence. The Doctor sampled the sweetmeat and pronounced it excellent. "The great, sir," he remarked, "are fortunate in every respect. They can employ the most skilled artisans of culinary art and gather the most amiable wits around their tables. If, as you suggest, sir—and your judgment based on the dish seems plausible—this confection has already graced His Lordship's table, it has done so in worthy company. It has trembled beneath the gaze of celebrated beauties, been tasted by ruby lips, and shared the attention of a distinguished assembly amidst fruits, tarts, and creams, which I am certain were equally delicious." With these words, the kindly Doctor consumed a significant portion of Lady Castlewood's bounty. However, regarding the epithet "delicious," I am compelled to note that my poor wife, after sampling the jelly, set it aside as unpalatable, and Molly, throwing up her head, declared it to be moldy.

Our boy, however, enjoyed the distinct privilege of having an Earl's daughter for his godmother. This honor was bestowed by his cousin, our late Lady Maria, whose kindness and devotion to both mother and infant exceeded all praise. Having lost her own solitary chance at maternal happiness, she doted on our child in a manner profoundly touching to witness. Captain Miles was indeed a handsome gentleman, his uniforms of the Prince's Hussars as splendid as any that ever adorned a soldier of fashion. Yet, possessing a heart too generous and being a true gentleman, surely he would remain grateful when he recalled that his own infant limbs had been clad in some of the garments prepared for the pauper's child. Sampson christened him in the very chapel at Southwark where our wedding rites had been performed. Never had the words of the Prayer-Book been read with greater beauty and impressiveness than by the celebrant of that service; save at its conclusion, when his voice faltered, leaving him and the small congregation wiping away tears. "Mr. Garrick himself, sir," observed Hagan, "could not have read those words so nobly. I am certain no innocent soul ever entered this world accompanied by wishes and blessings more tender and sincere."

Now, I must recount how the Captain acquired his name of Miles. A couple of days prior to the christening, when it was still intended that our firstborn should bear his father's name, a rhythmic patter of horsehooves echoed up to our gate. Who should pull the bell but young Miles, our cousin? I suspect he had disobeyed his parents on this undutiful journey.

"You know," he said, "Cousin Harry gave me my little horse; and I cannot help liking you, because you are so like Harry, and because they always speak of you at home, and it is a shame! And I have brought my whistle and coral beads, given to me by my godmamma Lady Suckling, for your little boy. And if you are so poor, Cousin George, here is my gold moidore. It is worth ever so much, and it is no use to me, because I may not spend it, you know."

We lifted the boy up to Theo in her chamber (he climbed the stairs proudly in his little tramping boots). Theo kissed him and expressed her thanks, and since that day, his moidore has rested safely in her purse.

Later, my mother, writing through her usual ambassador, informed me of her royal surprise and displeasure upon learning that my son had been christened Miles—a name unknown, at least within the Esmond family. I hesitated to reveal the reason at the time. However, when I eventually told Madam Esmond how my boy came by his name, I saw a tear roll down her wrinkled cheek. I learned subsequently that she had questioned Gumbo extensively about the boy who gave his name to our Miles—the Miles Gloriosus of Pall Mall, Valenciennes, and Almack's.

In the beginning, when Harry and I were mere unruly children who scorned our tutor, who would have guessed that Mr. Esmond Warrington of Virginia would one day rise to command a nation? Yet, here I stand, the "Bearleader" of a continent. My mother, when we reunited, could never speak plainly of those wild, terrible years; instead, she whispered of "that dreadful time, my love," and "those years when difference tore us apart." She referred to my former pupil, the worthy man who shipped me barrels of the spirit that built his fortune, not as a friend, but as "your wealthy Lambeth acquaintance," refusing ever to utter his name or touch a drop of his beer. Even at Warrington Manor, where we brewed our own ales, the good Mr. Foker faithfully sent a couple of butts to Ipswich annually. His son, a dashing youth wedded to an earl's daughter, and his father, a kindly soul, were the architects of the most welcome guineas I ever held.

But it was not merely the gold that sustained me; it was the office of Governor, a burden I embraced with trembling hope. Mr. Foker's brewery stood near Pedlar's Acre in Lambeth, close to the sanctuary where a Bavarian surgeon, originally named Voelker, attended my wife during her confinement. This gentle physician, having met me through the Lancashire surgeon who cared for the brewer's kin, developed a deep attachment to my family. He smoked his pipes in my garden, regaled us with songs of "Prinz Eugen," and introduced me to his patron. When Mr. Foker discovered I could sing the German ballads and understand his tongue, a profound friendship blossomed. The brewer's wife placed her carriage and chair at Mrs. Warrington's disposal; his daughter adored our infant—the Captain, now a disfigured man, was once a beauty—and his son, Master Foker, a victim of bullying at Westminster due to his father's trade, was entrusted to my care.

Mr. Foker was a shrewd businessman, yet his heart was warm. I laid bare my finances to him, and his respect for me grew. He laughed at the paltry aid offered by my noble relations—a moldy jelly from Lady Castlewood, a contemptuous glance—but his eyes filled with tears hearing of little Miles's moidore. "I vow," he declared, "they shall drink as much beer as they can!" He treated Lady Maria with obsequious deference, housing her when Hagan traveled to Dublin and funding his studies. When he learned the extent of my destitution, he viewed my refusal to drain my brother's funds for Harry's promotion not as prudence, but as "exalted virtue." Privately, he lauded me and my wife to the stars, and even Madame de Bernstein, whom I deemed selfish, began to treat us with a gentleness that startled me. Later, I learned that our distant relation, the Baron, knew Foker from his apprenticeship in Munich, a connection that likely smoothed the Baroness's path to favor.

Thus, a life that ought to have been bleak was softened by kindness and fortune. My "bear," the colony I led, was a docile cub, dancing to my piping. Better to guide him than to wait in the antechambers of bookshops for the whims of managers. Yet, the shadow of Lady Castlewood loomed large. Her invitations to tea and supper were rare, for the household was ruled by the iron fist of her father, Old Van den Bosch. The Countess confessed to my wife that her husband, Lord Castlewood, was a slave to her father, who guarded the keys to the strong-box. "Law!" she cried, "if he had access to the money, he would melt the plate! We keep him respectable, or he would vanish into debt." It was a gilded cage, and the victim, my Lord, fled to clubs whenever his father's grip slackened. Would I have traded my poverty for such ignominy? No. I earned my wages, however meager, and I remained, in their eyes, intolerably sulky.

Into this world stepped a figure who seemed to tame Fortune itself: my wife. Her poverty was worn with a smile so sweet, a grace so easy, that the niggard goddess melted before her. Tradesmen bowed to her as if she were a duchess; the landladies adored her. She moved through the chaos of life with the serene purity of the lady in *Comus*. We borrowed the Fokers' carriage for drives, once stopping at the Protestant Hero for a syllabub where the hostess insisted on addressing my wife as "Ladyship." We visited Mr. Johnson, drank tea with the ingenious Mr. Goldsmith, and sought the refuge of Aunt Bernstein.

My aunt, the Dowager Countess, was a complex figure. To the world, she was a tyrant, a hoarder of resources who mocked the young Countess's attempts to emulate her. "Scraping and bowing!" she would scoff. "She ain't no more fine than any other lady!" Yet, behind closed doors, she was lonely. She spoke of her fading beauty with a tragic candor, lamenting how quickly time consumed youth. "Why have we such a short time?" she asked, comparing her brief span to Adam's millennia. "It were better not to live at all, than to live without love." And yet, beneath the veneer of arrogance lay a terror of solitude. When the Countess visited, the Baroness trembled, fearing the scrutiny of the young generation. She would bawl at the Countess, accusing her of seeking her money, only to retreat when the door closed, leaving the young lady to depart with a confused sense of being both reviled and needed.

As autumn deepened, the shadows lengthened. The Baroness, frail and paralyzed, sank deeper into a fever dream. The chaplain, Sampson, tried to comfort her with sermons, but she recoiled, demanding only her cards. "I love cards, and play, and court," she whispered, her mind drifting to a bygone era. Suddenly, she saw me not as Henry Esmond, but as the Prince Henry of old France, the lover she had betrayed. "Il a juré de m'épouser," she shrieked, "O yes! They keep their promises! Si non, tu le tueras, cousin!" The hysterical laughter, the frantic delusions, shattered the fragile peace of the room.

Her end came swiftly. A priest, a Catholic stranger, entered her chamber, and the light faded from her eyes. When the will was read, it revealed a modest fortune—pensions dead, a few hundred pounds, and jewels. Harry inherited the bulk, a sum of four thousand pounds that transformed his fate, allowing him to buy an estate and secure a future. I, too, touched a portion, a hundred pounds that buoyed me when my stock was low. But the greatest gift was not money; it was the news from Jamaica. My brother-in-law, the General, and my sister Hetty wrote of their joy, of the children's love, and of the endless stream of half-crowns that flowed from their pockets to George. They begged me to join them, promising a life of ease.

Yet, tragedy struck before the journey could be taken. Young Miles, the little friend who had once dragged his gun through a hedge, was killed by a snapped trigger. The boy returned home to die in agony, leaving a vault beneath the yew trees where I hoped one day to lie. My children would spell the epitaph, a monument to a heartbroken father's love. As we ate the turkey sent by the surviving boy, choked by Lady Warrington's stern letter condemning his disobedience, I realized the circle was closing. The troubles and consolations of life swirled around us, a chaotic dance of love, loss, and the relentless march of time.

The storm of hardship had finally broken, and I stood on shore, though battered, no longer drowning. The death of my little kinsman, Miles, altered the landscape of my fortune entirely. With the Warrington line seemingly barren, I became the next heir to a substantial estate. "The woman is capable of committing any crime to disappoint you," Sampson had vowed with grim certainty regarding my aunt and uncle; yet, in truth, Lady Warrington possessed no such treacherous heart. Instead, she was cruelly struck by the blow, and her religious counselors guided her to view the tragedy as a divine chastisement. They told her, "Whilst your son lived, your heart was turned away from the better world," urging her to submit to the Heavenly Will, dismissing her former desires for rank and title as vanity compared to the heavenly crown awaiting her.

The accident sent shockwaves through the chapels of her fervent sect, where sermons were preached endlessly on the providential nature of the event. Yet, I refrained from judging the bereaved mother's path; grief and disappointment drive souls toward whatever refuge offers solace. True to her nature, however, she attempted reconciliation. A year after the loss, while visiting London, she summoned me. There, in her customary didactic manner, she delivered a homily on my station and hers. She marveled at Heaven's decree, noting how dreadfully her poor child's disobedience—gone out hunting without permission—had been punished, thereby allowing me to profit. She expressed a fervent hope that if I ever inherited the family honors, I would alter my ways: sever ties with my unworthy associates, abandon the theater and its licentious crowd, and return to peace and sacred duty. Her speech was filled with Scriptural language, which I shall not attempt to mimic. Before I departed, she handed me a packet of sermons for Mrs. Warrington and a small book of hymns by Miss Dora, a future figure of eminence in that society who later married the celebrated preacher, Mr. Juffles.

Though the poor lady forgave me, she could never bear the sight of our boy again. Following the loss of our second child, my aunt and her daughter flocked to the grieving mother, even inviting us to join them. But my uncle, now almost daily a guest in our household, became a distraction. He sat for hours gazing at Miles, showering him with toys and sweets, begging the child to call him "Godpapa." When the sting of our own grief struck me anew—even twenty-five years later—I understood his sorrow. But my wife, who had not yet known the agony of losing her own child, could not truly pity him. It was only when I held my little baby's coffin in my hands that I was fully reconciled to my uncle.

The widower sent his coach to follow our humble funeral procession. Afterwards, he took little Miles out, where the boy, clad in his new black clothes, prattled incessantly, forgetting his grief in the delight of fresh air. How the innocent chatter of the child stabbed the mother's heart! Would we ever wish for that wound to heal? I know her face so well today that I can tell when, sometimes, she thinks of the loss. It is not a grief for a parting so long ago; it is a communion with a soul we love in Heaven.

Soon, we returned to our bright lodgings in Bloomsbury. My young bear, whom I could no longer lead due to growing responsibilities, transferred to the Chartreux School. Thanks to Charley's influence and an excellent private tutor, the boy flourished, keeping a good standing and even declaring that if he ever had a son, he would send him there too.

With business to attend to, I could no longer guide my pet. Having been fully reconciled to the family, particularly for Miles's sake, my uncle—who was such an obsequious supporter of Government that the Minister surely relied on his vote—used his influence in my favor. I was honored to be gazetted as one of His Majesty's Commissioners for licensing hackney-coaches, a post I filled with credit until a quarrel with the Minister eventually deprived me of it. I also took my degree at the Temple and appeared in Westminster Hall in gown and wig. Later, accompanying my good friend Mr. Foker to Paris, I was received *bras ouverts* by my dear American preserver, Monsieur de Florac. He introduced me to his noble family and the polite circles of the capital, though I spent little time there, preferring to remain close to Foker, whose acquaintances lay chiefly among the bourgeoisie, including Monsieur Santerre, a great brewer who would later distinguish himself in blood, not merely beer. Mr. Foker required my services as an interpreter, and I was eager to repay the kindness he had shown me. Meanwhile, the ladies resided at Mr. Foker's villa in Wimbledon, amused by the "Parisian letters" I sent to them via my friend Mr. Hume, which were later published in a neat volume.

While I quietly discharged these minor official duties in London, the troubles brewing in America began to gather force, destined to culminate in the Great Separation. When Mr. Grenville proposed his stamp duties, I told my wife the bill would certainly create discontent at home, as we were ever anxious to extract wealth from England while paying back as little as possible. However, I never anticipated the prodigious anger the scheme unleashed. It was with us as with families or individuals: a pretext is given for a quarrel, but the real cause lies in long-brewing bickerings and prior animosities. Many foolish exactions, petty tyrannies, and the habitual insolence of Englishmen toward foreigners and colonists—all folk who dared to think their rivers as good as the Abana and Pharpar—set Britain and her colonies to quarrel. The astonishing blunders of the English system brought this quarrel to an issue. Whether I, for one, was wrong to deplore it remains unclear. Had I been in Virginia instead of London, 'tis very possible I should have taken the provincial side, perhaps driven into revolt by that resolute mistress of Castlewood, just as England drove the colonies. Was the Stamp Act the true cause of the revolution? It was a tax no greater than that cheerfully paid in England. Ten years earlier, when the French threatened our territory and we implored succor from home, would the colonies have rebelled over this tax? Do not most people consider the tax-gatherer the natural enemy? Against the British in America were arrayed thousands of high-spirited and brave souls, alongside countless others who found profit or private reasons to engage in the strife. I protest I do not know now whether my motives were selfish or patriotic, which side was right, or if both were not. I am sure we in England had nothing to do but fight the battle out; and having lost the game, I do vow and believe that, after the first natural soreness, the loser felt no rancour.

What made brother Hal write home from Virginia, which he seemed exceedingly loth to quit, such flaming patriotic letters? My kind, best brother was always led by somebody; by me when we were together (he had such an idea of my wit and wisdom that if I said the day was fine, he would ponder it as a saying of the Seven Sages), and by some other wiseacre when I was away. Who inspired these fiery epistles, this boisterous patriotism? "He is rebelling against Madam Esmond," I surmised. "He is led by some colonial person—by that lady, perhaps," hinted my wife. Hal never told us who "that lady" was; indeed, he besought me never to allude to the delicate subject in my letters to him, writing, "for Madam wishes to see 'em all, and I wish to say nothing about you know what until the proper moment." No affection could be greater than that which his letters showed. When he heard (from the informant I have mentioned) that in the midst of my own extreme straits I had retained no more than a hundred pounds out of his aunt's legacy, he was for mortgaging the estate he had just bought. He had more than one quarrel with his mother in my behalf and spoke his mind with a frankness I would never have ventured. Until her angry recriminations—charging him with ingratitude after she had toiled and saved so much—he did not know that our mother had cut off my supplies to advance his interests. By the time this news reached him, his bargains were made, and I was fortunately quite out of want.

Every scrap of paper we ever wrote was taped and docketed by our thrifty parent at Castlewood. We boys were more careless about our correspondence, I especially so, perhaps choosing to look down upon my younger brother's literary performances. But my wife is not so supercilious; she has kept no small number of Harry's letters, as well as those of the angelic being whom we were presently to call sister.

"To think whom he has chosen, and whom he might have had! Oh, 'tis cruel!" cried my wife when we received that notable letter in which Harry first made us acquainted with the name of his charmer.

"She was a very pretty little maid when I left home; she may be a perfect beauty now," I remarked, reading over the longest letter Harry ever wrote on private affairs.

"But is she to compare to my Hetty?" asked Mrs. Warrington.

"We agreed that Hetty and Harry were not to be happy together, my love," I said.

Theo kissed her husband

When Mother heard I had accepted a home at Warrington, I suspect she was scarcely delighted. Perhaps she withdrew her financial support deliberately, aiming to starve me into a surrender and force a return to Virginia, back under her thumb. Yet, until her final breath, we never resolved the pecuniary dispute between us. She severed my allowances; I said not a word, managing instead to subsist without her aid. I never heard that she repented of her stinginess, nor did she acknowledge it publicly, save perhaps through Harry's private communications to me later. In subsequent meetings, marked by a sudden gentleness in her demeanor and an unusual reverence for my wife, Madam Esmond may have intended for me to infer a tacit admission of her error. Still, she offered no apology, and I asked for none. With Harry provided for—a fact I could not begrudge—all of Mother's savings and frugal schemes ultimately flowed to my advantage, for I was her heir. Once, a few guineas would have been more vital to me than hundreds arriving when I needed them less; but when we finally reconciled, the era of necessity had long passed. I possessed ample means, rendering noble schemes unnecessary, and I held no ill will toward the doctor for his fees. Mother likely suffered inwardly, guilt-ridden over the hunger my children had endured and the relief strangers had provided for needs she, in her pride, had refused to address. Was she prouder than I? A single word of explanation between us might have healed the rift years prior, but neither spoke. When I recognize a wrong within myself, I crave to seek pardon, yet this act serves my own pride, allowing me to apologize to the self I perceive as lacking. Thus, I rarely debase myself to perform a meanness. Consider the man whose entire existence is a tapestry of lies, schemes, and subterfuge. What fellowship do they keep when alone? Daily, I observe men whose smiles are artifice and winks hypocrisy. Do they wear masks in solitude, before their own consciences? If I overlook an injury, it is not born of Christian forgiveness, but because I can afford to remit the debt and disdain settling accounts. I know a few sweet souls—perhaps I cultivated them—who offer forgiveness as naturally as a plant springs from the earth. I know how to remit, I say, not how to forgive. I wonder if proud men take pride in their pride.

Consequently, I displayed no sign of submission toward my parent in Virginia, and we remained estranged for years, exchanging only occasional brief tidbits—the birth of a child, or similar matters—through my wife and hers. Following our initial tribulations in America regarding the Stamp Act, further troubles befell me in London. Though I stood with the Tories in our colonial quarrel, convinced the Imperial Government rightfully levied taxes, a letter I published to a Member of the House of Burgesses proved disastrous. In it, I freely addressed the habitual insolence of the mother country and voiced sentiments so offensive to those in power that I was stripped of my position as hackney-coach licenser. To my Uncle's terror and horror, I had fallen into disgrace. He, who could never love a disgraced man, had grown to cherish my wife and our little boy dearly, yet viewed me with a pitying contempt that endlessly amused me. Nature endowed him with a scorn for poverty and a corresponding love for success and fortune. Any deviation from the regular path shocked him; truth-telling turned him pale. Yet, despite the dreadful shocks I gave him, he continued to see Theo, the child, and me. He bestowed upon me a mournful recognition when we met, viewing me as a melancholy, lost creature beyond worldly help. Never mind. I must devise a new scheme for life, and at this juncture, the repayment of Harry's debt to me enabled me to survive, if not thrive, for months or years to come.

Strange fatuity of youth! How dare we be so poor, so little cast down?

Then, His Majesty's royal uncle, the Duke of Cumberland, fell down and perished in a fit. Strangely, his death wrought a remarkable change in my fortunes. My poor Sir Miles Warrington missed no court ceremony to which he could introduce himself. Drawing-rooms, christenings, balls, and funerals were his domain. If a prince or princess ailed, his coach waited at their door. Leicester Fields, Carlton House, Gunnersbury—they meant nothing to him now but attending the stout Duke's funeral. Standing bareheaded in the rain at this interment, he contracted a severe cold and throat inflammation. Before almost anyone had heard of his illness, a lawyer waited upon me at my Bloomsbury lodgings, saluting me as "Sir George Warrington."

Party strife and fears of the future vanished. We laid the poor gentleman beside his little son in the family churchyard, where so many of his race repose. Little Miles and I were the chief mourners. An obsequious tenantry bowed and curtsied before us, striving to conciliate my honor. The Dowager and her daughter withdrew to Bath promptly, leaving me and my family to take possession of the house, of which I have been master for thirty years. Be not too eager, O my son! Have patience, and I too shall sleep beneath those yew trees, while the people toss their caps for Sir Miles.

The records of a prosperous country life are easily and briefly told. The steward's books reveal what rents were paid and forgiven, what crops raised, and in what rotation. Visitors, their durations of stay, pensioners cared for by my wife, their treatments, deaths, the year I served as sheriff, and the frequency of hounds meeting near us are all narrated in our house journals, available to any heir willing to read them. We could not afford the magnificent Hill Street mansion occupied by my predecessor, so we took a smaller dwelling, wherein we nonetheless spent more lavishly. We made but half the ostentatious display with liveries, equipages, and plate for which my uncle was famed; yet our beer was stronger, and my wife's charities perhaps more costly than those of the Dowager Lady Warrington. Doubtless, she believed there was no harm in spoiling the Philistines, for she sold us her remaining country-house goods at unconscionable prices, and I submitted to most of her extortions with unutterable good humor. What value she imagined for potted plants in her greenhouses! What price she set on that horrid old spinet in her drawing-room! And the framed pieces of worsted work, executed by the accomplished Dora and the lovely Flora—if they had been masterpieces of Titian or Van Dyck, surely my Lady Dowager could not have valued them higher. Though we paid so generously, and were, I may say without boast, far kinder to our poor than she had ever been, for a while we bore the very worst reputation in the county, where tales of my disrepute circulated freely. I hoped perhaps to succeed to my uncle's seat in Parliament as well as his lands, but I discovered, somehow, that I was voted a person of dangerously radical opinions. I would not bribe; I would not coerce my tenants to vote for me in the 1668 election. A gentleman descended from Whitehall with a pocket-book full of bank-notes, and I found I had no chance against my competitor.

Bon Dieu! Now that we were at ease regarding worldly means—now that obedient tenants bowed and curtsied as we proceeded to church, driving to visit friends or neighboring towns in the great family coach drawn by four fat horses—did we not often regret poverty, and that dear little cottage at Lambeth where Want ever prowled at the door? Did I not long to be bear-leading again, vowing that translating for booksellers was not such hard drudgery? Upon visiting London, we made sentimental pilgrimages to all our old haunts. I am certain my wife embraced every landlady. You may be sure we invited all the friends of those old times to share the comforts of our new home. The Reverend Mr. Hagan and his lady visited us more than once. His appearance in the pulpit at B------ (where he preached finely, as we thought) caused an awful scandal there. Sampson came too, another unlucky Levite, and was welcome as long as he stayed among us. Mr. Johnson talked of coming but put us off once or twice. I suppose our house was dull. I know I myself would remain silent for days, fearing that my moodiness must often try the sweetest-tempered woman in the world who lived with me. I cared little for field sports. Killing one partridge seemed so like killing another that I marveled how men could spend days pursuing such slaughter. Their fox-hunting stories commenced at four o'clock, when the tablecloth was removed, and lasted until supper-time. I sat silent, listening; day after day I fell asleep. No wonder I was not popular with my company.

What confession is this I am making? Here was the storm over, the rocks avoided, the ship in port, and the sailor not over contented? Was Susan, whom I had sighed for during the voyage, not the beauty I expected to find? First, Susan and the entire family can consult William's logbook, so, madam, I shall not put my secrets down there. No, Susan, I never withheld secrets from thee. I never cared for another woman. I have seen greater beauties, but none suited me as well as your ladyship. I have met Mrs. Carter, Miss Mulso, Mrs. Thrale, Madame Kaufmann, the angelical Gunn

A mother's plea, gentle and deep, might have melted a heart far harder than ours, yet we lingered. We spoke of a swift journey to Virginia, of securing the cabin aboard *Young Rachel*, but destiny held us back twice. First, our youngest child fell ill, rendering the ocean voyage perilous for her fragile frame; naturally, Mother could not leave her side, so *Young Rachel* sailed alone that year. The following season brought another obstacle: my beloved worship's first assault of gout, a malady that consumed me with pain yet, paradoxically, sharpened my wit and buoyed my spirit afterward. Finally, a sorrow far deeper intervened—the grim tidings from Jamaica.

Two years after settling at the Manor, our dear General returned from his governorship. He had amassed greater worldly wealth than when he departed, yet he bore a loss that riches could not heal, a grief so profound he saw no future remaining in the West Indies. His wife—my Theo's mother, the tenderest and kindest friend I have ever known save one—had succumbed to fever abroad. In her final moments, her deepest regret was that she would not live to witness our children and ourselves thriving in prosperity.

"She sees us, though we do not see her," my husband whispered, "and she thanks you, George, for having been good to her children."

Indeed, we feared he would not long join her. His entire existence had revolved around her love and his service to her; to exist apart from her felt like non-existence. Watching him sit with us, broken by this calamity, was heartbreaking. My wife, through her demeanor and countless gentle gestures, constantly summoned the memory of his lost beloved to soothe the widower's soul. We offered whatever cheer we could muster, yet little Hetty became, beneath heaven's eye, his primary anchor. She had declined numerous advantageous matches proposed within the island, and upon returning to England, Lord Wrotham's heir bowed low before her. Yet Hetty insisted she loved staying with her father best; as long as he needed her, she desired no husband.

"Nay," we urged when the final proposal for her hand was made, "let the General remain six months with us at the Manor, and you may have him at Oakhurst for the other six."

But Hetty declared her father could never endure Oakhurst again now that her mother was gone. She would wed no man for a coronet or gold—not she! The General, grave and thoughtful, noted that her lack of desire for marriage stemmed, perhaps, from an unspoken disappointment in her youth, a wound she never revealed. Respecting her silence, we remained mute. Meanwhile, my brother Lambert had secured a college living near Winchester and a wife to adorn his parsonage. While we professed a moderate affection for this lady, welcoming her warmly, her perspective on Hetty's determined celibacy differed sharply from mine. Mrs. Jack was a chatty woman, prone to speaking her mind with excessive liberty and taking pride in her ability to inflict pain upon her friends.

"My dear Sir George," she declared with a bright smile, "I have often told our dear Theo that I would not have a pretty sister in my house to make tea for Jack while I am upstairs, nor to hover in the kitchen or nursery, nor to appear neatly dressed whenever I am baking pies or attending to the children. I trust Jack implicitly, of course, but I should like to see him look at another woman! And so I do with Jemima, though they do not convene in my house when I am upstairs—I promise you! Thus, I warned my sister Warrington."

"Am I to understand," the General interjected, "that you have done my Lady Warrington the favor of warning her against her sister, my daughter Miss Hester?"

"Yes, pa, of course!" cried Mrs. Jack, flushed and voluble. "A duty is a duty, and a woman is a woman, and a man is a man, as I know very well. Do not tell me! He is a man. Every man is a man, with all his sanctified airs!"

"You yourself have a married sister, with whom you were staying when my son Jack first had the honor of making your acquaintance?" remarked the General.

"Yes, of course! Everyone knows that, and I have been as good as a mother to her children!"

"And shall I gather from your conversation that your charms proved a powerful temptation for your sister's husband?"

"Law, General! I know not how you can suggest I ever said such a thing!" Mrs. Jack cried, turning red.

"Do you not perceive, my dear madam, that it is you who have insinuated as much, not only regarding yourself, but concerning my own two daughters?"

"Never, never, never, as I am a Christian woman!" she shrieked, indignant. "It is most cruel of you to say so, sir. And I truly say a sister is best out of the house, that I do! And as Theo's time draws near, I warn her, that is all."

"Have you discovered, my good madam, whether my poor Hetty has stolen any of the spoons? When I came to breakfast this morning, my daughter was alone, and there must have been a dozen pieces of silver on the table."

"Law, sir! Who ever spoke of spoons? Did I ever accuse the poor dear? If I did, may I drop down dead upon this hearth-rug at this very instant!" She paused, offended. "And I am not accustomed to being spoken to thus. Both Jack and I have observed it, and I have fulfilled my duty!" With that, Mrs. Jack flounced from the room in tears.

"And has the woman the impudence to tell you this, my child?" the General asked when Theo, somewhat delicate, approached the tea-table.

"She has told me every day since her arrival," Theo replied softly. "She enters my dressing-room to remind me, then retreats to my nursery, saying, 'Ah, I would not have a sister prowling about my nursery, that I would not.' Ah, how pleasant it is to have amiable and well-bred relatives," she sighed sarcastically.

"Thy poor mother has been spared this woman," the General groaned.

"Our mother would have mended her ways, papa," Theo said, kissing him.

"Yes, dear." And I observed that both were at their prayers.

Yet, let it be admitted, loving one's relatives is not always easy, and living among neighbors can prove less amusing than anticipated. From Jack Lambert's sullen demeanor the following day, it was clear his wife had imparted her version of the exchange. Jack was sulky, though not dignified; his anger did not diminish his appetite. He delivered a sermon to us that was utterly dull. Little Miles, clad once more in sable, sat beside his grandfather, his small hand clasped in the kind old man's.

Should he stay to manage the house during our Virginian excursion? The housekeeper must submit to Hetty's full dominion, and the butler's keys passed to him. For Gumbo, I did not expect with too much grace, would accompany us to Virginia; having united himself with Mrs. Molly in matrimony, he had populated a cottage in my park with several tawny Gumbos. Under the guardianship of our General and his daughter, we left our home. Traveling to London, then to Bristol, our agent—who greeted us with obsequious prayers for our prosperity and vows that children as beautiful as ours (we now had an infant to accompany Miles) were unseen on any ship before—accompanied us. The voyage passed without accident. How strange the sensation of landing at Richmond! A coach and a host of negroes awaited to receive us; nearby, a gentleman on horseback, attended by men in our livery, dismounted and rushed to embrace us. Neither of us was little charmed to behold our dearest Hal. He rode with us to our mother's door. There she stood on the steps to welcome us, and Theo knelt to ask her blessing.

Harry rode in the coach as far as Mother's house but refused to enter further, claiming it would ruin the sport. "She sees me," he admitted, "and we are fairly good friends; but Fanny and she fare best apart. There is no love lost between them, I can promise you. Come to me at the Tavern when thou art free. Tomorrow, I shall have the honor of presenting her sister to Theo." Having sent a negro boy home to fetch his wife to pay respects to Lady Warrington, Harry leapt from the carriage, leaving us to meet our mother alone.

Having spent considerable time with fine company and paid our respects to the King and Queen at St. James's, I had encountered no stater than the man who welcomed us. Raising my wife from her knee, he embraced her and led her into the house. It was a plain, wood-framed dwelling with a gallery, typical of Virginian homes; yet, if it had been a palace housing a little empress, our reception could not have been more courteous. Old Nathan, still the major-domo, presided over a score of kind black faces, grinning with welcome. Names I remembered from childhood were forgotten by the buxom lads and lasses who had grown, while some I had left with dark complexions now sported snowy locks, and others born since my departure peered from doorways with wide eyes and bare feet. "I'm little Sip, Master George!" "I'm Dinah, Sir George!" "I'm Master Miles's boy!"

"What twisted law of destiny conspires to cast me perpetually into the minority?" I mused, looking out over the chaotic scene unfolding around me. "Were a bill introduced to surrender this realm to the Pretender of Rome or the Grand Turk, and to bow the neck of the kingdom to a foreign creed, it would surely pass—with me, naturally, casting the solitary vote against it. Yet, here in Virginia, I find myself a pariah twice over: to the Patriots, I am a disloyal Tory; to the Tories, a treacherous republican. The times are utterly unhinged. Oh, cursed fate! Within a year of landing in Virginia, I longed for the tranquil banks of the Waveney. But circumstances rendered flight impossible. To abandon my mother, a solitary woman, to face the looming specter of civil war would have been unthinkable, and she, steadfast and proud, refused to flee. Nor could a man of spirit desert his post. Thus, amidst the clamor, a consensus formed among the well-disposed at His Excellency's table, fueled by generous claret and overflowing tankards. We swore an oath, a *statue signum*, a rallying cry: *Signifer!* All Cavaliers would unite behind it. And there, over wine, His Excellency spoke with the fervor of the bravest soul alive.

Of all my acquaintances in Virginia, none stood more consistently firm than Madam Esmond. Following the influx of gentry to Williamsburg, many proposed a grand ball to honor Her Excellency, the Governor's lady. However, word of the Boston Port Bill swept through, prompting the House of Burgesses to adopt a furious protest, decreeing a solemn day of fasting and humiliation to pray against the calamity of civil strife. Yet, the invitations for the ball had already been sent and accepted. The gentlemen resolved to proceed with the festivities on the appointed eve, intending to don sackcloth and ashes only in the aftermath.

"A ball!" Madam Esmond exclaimed, her voice sharp with disbelief. "I am to attend a gathering hosted by rebels who plan to insult His Majesty openly within a week! I would rather die!" She promptly dispatched a letter to the stewards, stating that, given the perilous state of the nation, she could not in conscience attend.

Her surprise, however, proved immediate and profound. The following time she ventured abroad in her carriage, she was greeted by a hundred voices—white and black alike—shouting "Huzzah, Madam!" and "Heaven bless your ladyship!" They clearly interpreted her refusal as an act of supreme patriotism. Determined to dispel any misunderstanding, Madam leaned from her chair and cried out, "God save the King" as loudly as her lungs would permit. The crowd echoed her cry. Everyone, it seemed, shouted "God save the King" those days.

That same night, my poor Harry, serving as a Burgess and a host of the feast, donned his scarlet coat of Wolfe's Regiment—a garment he would soon be forced to discard—and departed with Madam Fanny to the ball. My Lady Warrington and her humble servant, being strangers and essentially English in spirit, were granted permission to attend the assembly from which Madam Esmond had wisely abstained. I, too, held the honor of dancing a country dance with the lady of Mount Vernon, a partner I found most lively, pretty, and amiable. Yet, I must confess, my wife's glowing praise of her reception was met with a grim silence by my mother when Lady Warrington recounted the evening's events. Could not Sir George Warrington have danced with Lady Dunmore or her daughters, or anyone else but Mrs. Washington? Indeed, the Colonel seemed to consider himself and his wife the apex of society, forgetting that he had once been a road surveyor earning a guinea a day. One cannot measure the pride of these provincial upstarts, nor explain away Lord Dunmore's evident infatuation with the man. As for Mr. Washington, I remain unsure of the depth of his pride, but I know that my good mother harbored no affection for him, nor anything connected to him.

Nor did my mother improve her temper toward him for attending the ball, nor for his conduct three days later, when the day of fasting and humiliation fell. He attended the service performed by our new clergyman, while Madam Esmond, having invited Mr. Belman and sundry colonial authorities to dinner, received an excuse from the clergyman. Undeterred, Madam Esmond tossed her head and declared he might do as he pleased. She staged a magnificent dinner, lighting her house brightly while the rest of the city succumbed to darkness and gloom. She requested Mr. Hardy, one of His Excellency's aides-de-camp, to sing "God save the King," believing the passersby to be listening to a religious observance. Instead, when the simple young gentleman, fresh from Europe, began "Britons, strike home!", a deafening yell erupted from the street. A massive stone, hurled by a rebellious hand, struck the punch-bowl before me, scattering its contents across our dining room.

My mother, undaunted, stepped to the window. I can still see her rigid, tiny figure standing tall, arms outstretched in frills, the twinkling stars her backdrop, singing in chorus, "Britons, strike home! Strike home!" The crowd before the pales roared, "Silence! For shame! Go back!" yet she refused to retreat. "Fling more stones, if you dare!" she challenged the brave little lady. More might have followed, but gentlemen emerging from the Raley Tavern intervened. "You must not insult a lady," a voice I recognized intoned. "Hurrah, Colonel! Hurrah, Captain! God bless your honor!" shouted the people, and thus the enemies were pacified.

Though my mother protested that the disturbance was merely a blip, she insisted on another performance by Mr. Hardy. He offered a sickly grin, claiming he disliked singing under such accompaniments, and the concert ended. Yet, scoundrels returned that night, frightening my poor wife nearly to madness and shattering every window in the front of our tenement. "Britons, strike home!" was simply too much; Madam should have contented herself with "God save the King." Militias were drilled, bullets cast, ammunition stockpiled, and cunning schemes devised to frustrate the royal ordinances. Still, "God save the King" remained the universal cry. When I objected to the gentlemen-patriots, accusing them of plotting separation and inviting the wrath of the greatest power on earth, the retort was always the same: "We mean no separation! We yield to no men in loyalty! We glory in the name of Britons!" Powder barrels were heaped in the cellars, trains laid, yet Mr. Fawkes persisted in his dutiful petitions to King and Parliament, feigning innocence. True, when I spoke of the Empire's power, I naively believed it would be exercised with restraint, avoiding the humiliation of deploying Hessian mercenaries and Indian warriors against thirteen colonies. Did we not wish to subdue them without insulting them further? Why bring in these hordes? Afterward, when the war was lost, it became easy to scrutinize the map and declare how we ought not to have failed. Long Island should have annihilated Washington's army; he should never have emerged from Valley Forge as anything other than a prisoner. The South was ours after Camden, undone only by the inexplicable meddling of the Commander-in-Chief at New York, who paralyzed the efforts of the only capable British general and sent him to a cul-de-sac at York Town, leading to his eventual capture as a prisoner. Oh, for a few more hours of darkness or light! Reading over our American campaigns from their ill-fated beginning to their ignoble end, now that we can see the enemy's movements as clearly as our own, I believe we can discern how a timely advance might have placed enemies in our power, altering the entire issue of the struggle.

Yet, it was ordained by Heaven—for the good, as we now realize, of both empires—that the great Western Republic should separate. The gallant soldiers who fought on her side, led by their indomitable and heroic chief, earned glory by facing not only veteran troops accustomed to war but also the scourges of wretchedness, cold, hunger, dissension, and internal treason. Where everything else would have collapsed, it survived solely because of the pure, unquenchable flame of patriotism burning in the bosom of that heroic leader. What constancy, what magnanimity, what persistence against fortune! Washington, before the enemy, was no greater or braver than the hundreds who fought beside or against him—who had not heard the repeated sneers at "Fabius"? But Washington, the Chief of a nation in arms, battling distracted factions, remained calm amidst conspiracy, serene before the open foe and the darker enemies at his back. He inspired order and spirit into troops hungry and ragged, stung by ingratitude yet showing no anger, ever ready to forgive. Invincible in defeat, magnanimous in conquest, he was never so sublime as on that day when he laid down his victorious sword to seek his noble retirement. Here indeed is a character worthy of admiration and reverence; a life without stain, a fame without flaw. *Quando invenies parem?* In that larger work I have planned and partly written concerning this great war, I hope I have done justice to the character of its greatest leader. [And I trust that in the opinions I have recorded regarding him, I have demonstrated that I, too, can be just and magnanimous

In the era of our British dominion, we employed America as a sanctuary for our moral failings. Alongside the convicts and indentured servants we shipped across the Atlantic, we released upon its shores the reckless, the dispossessed, and the younger sons for whom the constraints of England had become unbearable. Much like Captain Cook, who gifted newly discovered islands with samples of European fauna, we sent over our "black sheep" to roam free and multiply their peculiar lineage in the colonies.

I confess, however, a certain guilt in this arrangement regarding the Reverend Hagan, husband of my kinswoman. Since we could find no suitable employment for him in England, we gladly dispatched him to Virginia, granting him a colonial pulpit. He conducted himself as a valiant, honorable gentleman, diligently serving both his flock and his monarch, thereby reflecting credit upon my modest patronage. Madame Theo, when I voiced my scruples, invariably reminded me that my actions, in this instance as in all others, sprang from the highest principles of morality and honor. Yet, if I had truly sought to offer Hagan a living at home, selecting him and his wife to minister to our parish, I am certain I would have done so. Though I never doubted Cousin Maria's sincerity in repenting, I admit to a secret relief when she departed for the wilderness. And I speak this openly, acknowledging both my pride and my error: twice, when I needed her sympathy and friendship most, this virtuous woman stood by me. She endured her own trials with fortitude and soothed the sorrows of others with admirable devotion. Alas, I and some of my circle (save for Theo) looked down upon her. Oh, the shame of such arrogance!

Lady Maria was hardly alone among our family in being exiled to the American hinterlands. The Honourable William Esmond, having exhausted his capacity to borrow, steal, and cheat within the bounds of propriety, was accommodated with a post in New York. His noble brother and royal master expressed a hearty desire to see naught further of him. When the troubles commenced, news of his exploits reached us. Deceit and malice were his vanguard wherever he traveled. Lord Dunmore informed me that Mr. William had publicly declared that our estate of Castlewood existed solely at the pleasure of his brother; that his father, moved by consideration for Madam Esmond, Lord Dunmore's half-sister, had granted her the lands for life; and that William himself was currently negotiating with Lord Castlewood to purchase the reversion of the estate! We possessed the deed of gift in our strongroom at Castlewood, and it was duly registered at Williamsburg, rendering us secure on that count. Nevertheless, intent was everything. Hal and I vowed, upon our next encounter with Mr. William, to extract from him confirmation of this charming fabrication. The impact of these words on Madam Esmond need not be detailed. "What! Is my father, the Marquis of Esmond, a liar, and am I a cheat?" she cried. "Will he seize my son's property upon my death?" She was prepared to write not only to Lord Castlewood in England but to His Majesty at St. James's herself, only prevented by my assurance that Mr. William's duplicity was notorious among all his acquaintances and that we could not reasonably expect consistency from him. Soon thereafter, we heard of him as a leading voice among the Loyalists in New York, holding commissions as Captain and later Major of volunteer corps sending addresses to the well-disposed throughout the colonies, announcing their perfect readiness to lay down their lives for the Mother Country.

It became impossible to remain in a house lacking a whole window. After shutting the shutters of our ill-fated rented mansion at Williamsburg, Madam Esmond left our little capital, and my family returned to Richmond, which had likewise been abandoned by the members of the dissolved Assembly. Captain Hal and his wife returned promptly to their plantation. I, however, somewhat annoyed by the trajectory of events, divided my time between my own household and that of our Governor, who professed an eager desire for my counsel and companionship. Despite profound political divergences, no actual quarrel had yet broken out between us. Even following the dissolution of our House of Assembly—whose members adjourned to a tavern, where, I believe, the concept of a continental congress was first conceived—the gentlemen staunchest in opposition remained friends with His Excellency, sharing his hospitality and joining him in recreational excursions. Once the session concluded, the gentry retired to their respective counties to hold local meetings. With Assemblies in most other provinces abruptly dissolved as well, it was universally agreed that a general congress should convene. Philadelphia, as the continent's largest and most significant city, was chosen as the venue. These celebrated conferences began, serving merely as the angry prelude to war. We still sang "God Save the King"; we still presented humble petitions to the throne. Yet, when I visited my brother Harry at Fanny's Mount one afternoon, he rode out on business that morning, and I, following the same path, was told by a groom that Master Harry had proceeded toward Willis's Ordinary. Thinking no harm, I pursued him. Upon reaching a clearing near Willis's, as I emerged from the wood, I encountered Captain Hal on horseback, surrounded by thirty or forty countrymen armed with every conceivable weapon—pikes, scythes, fowling-pieces, and muskets. The Captain, flanked by two or three capable young men acting as officers, was drilling the men. As I drew nearer, a peculiar expression crossed Hal's face. "Present arms!" he commanded, and the makeshift army endeavored to render the salute as competently as possible. "Captain Cade, this is my brother, Sir George Warrington."

"As a relation of yours, Colonel," the man addressed as Captain replied, "the gentleman is welcome," extending his hand accordingly.

"And—a true friend to Virginia," Hal stammered, his face flushing crimson.

"Yes, please God! Gentlemen," I interjected, whereupon the regiment erupted in a hearty hurrah for the Colonel and his brother. Once the drill concluded, the officers and men alike wished to adjourn to Willis's for refreshments. However, Colonel Hal declined to drink with them that afternoon, and we trotted homeward together.

"So, Hal, the cat is out of the bag!" I remarked.

He fixed me with a stern gaze. "I suspect there are wilder cats within the sack, brother. It must come to this. Say, you must not tell Madam."

"Good heavens!" I exclaimed. "Do you mean that with fellows such as those I saw yonder, you and your friends intend to make fight against the greatest nation and the finest army in the world?"

"I fancy we shall receive an awful whipping," Hal admitted, "and that is the fact. But then, George," he added, flashing his sweet, kind smile, "we are young, and a few whippings may do us good. Won't it do us good, Dolly, you old slut?" He playfully touched the whip of an old mongrel of all trades trotting beside him.

I refrained from urging him again (having done so in vain on numerous previous occasions) regarding our British allegiance, the side which appeared to me the superior one. He was accustomed to deflecting my arguments with the refrain, "All mighty well, brother, you speak as an Englishman, and have cast in your lot with your country, as I have with mine." To this plea, I acknowledge, there is no reply, and all that remains for the disputants is to fight it out when the stronger party proves right. In the wars of the last century, which side held the right? The King or Parliament? The side that prevailed was deemed the right, and on the whole, their victory proved far more humane than the Cavaliers would have achieved had they won. Indeed, suppose we Tories had secured the day in America; how frightful and bloody that triumph would have been! One imagines countless ropes and scaffolds, countless noble heads laid low. A strange sensation, I confess; I stood on the Loyalist side, yet I desired the Whigs to win. On the other hand, my brother Hal, who distinguished himself greatly with his regiment, never uttered a word of disrespect against the enemy he opposed. "The officers of the British army," he would say, "are gentlemen: at least, I have not heard that they have altered significantly since my time. There may be scoundrels and ruffians among the enemy's troops; I dare say we could find similar ones among our own. Our business is to beat His Majesty's forces, not to call them names—any rascal can do that." Consequently, owing to a title bestowed by Mr. Lee upon my brother, which many of his rough horsemen failed to comprehend, Harry was frequently dubbed "Chevalier Baird" within the Continental Army. He was, indeed, a knight without fear and without reproach.

Regarding the argument, "What could such people as those you were drilling accomplish against the British army?" Hal offered a confident response.

"They can beat them," he asserted, "Mr. George, that is precisely what they can do."

"Great heavens!" I cried, "do you mean that with your company of Wolfe's, you would hesitate to attack five hundred such?"

"With my company of the 67th, I would go anywhere. And, agreed with you, that at this present moment I know more of soldiering than they... but place me on that open ground where you found us, armed as you please, and surround me with half a dozen of

Upon concluding my visit to my brother, and seeing my wife and young child safely return to our maternal home at Richmond, I set out for Governor Dunmore's country residence near Williamsburg. There, amidst the gathering storm clouds of war that darkened not only Virginia but every colony within hearing, I sought counsel. Delegates were already being named for the General Congress in Philadelphia. While Massachusetts faced open hostility between the populace and royal troops, and the Carolinas and Georgia remained staunchly loyal, Virginia prided itself on its ancient Cavalier heritage. Yet, surrounding the Governor was a swarm of loud, confident Loyalists, eager to draw swords and scourge the "rascally rebels" before them.

In these heated councils, I was forced to endure a barrage of vitriol aimed at my brother, Harry. Everyone agreed, with some justification, that his wife had steered him toward these extreme anti-British views. He was accused of being infatuated with the gentleman of Mount Vernon—a man who, day by day, committed himself deeper into a course of desperate resistance. To the ears of these eager supporters, my friendship for Harry and the trust I placed in him read as treachery. They saw it not as a final, exhausted attempt at reconciliation, but as a betrayal of the Crown. They toasted His Majesty's health with fervor, declaring they would die to defend his rights and see a Prince of his Royal house rule once more. They pleaded for my intervention: *Was it not time to secure Harry with a good command? Did I not hold sufficient influence?* Thus, our councils swung wildly between exultant triumph and furious despair.

I promised to plead with my brother. I wrote to him, though with scant hope, reiterating arguments I had used for years. My mother joined me, wielding her customary authority. She assailed him with Scripture, threatening him with divine punishment for disobedience and pointing to his elder brother's example. She hinted, I feared, at his subservience to his wife—the weakest possible argument. Though she hid her own letter from me, likely fearing its harsh tone, she revealed Harry's reply. To my astonishment, my brother had countered with scores of scriptural texts, penned in a neat, temperate, and elegantly composed style that surpassed my wife's own capabilities. He had even enlisted the services of Mr. Belman, a clergyman of the New Richmond who had adopted strong Whig opinions and preached sermons against Hagan. In this intellectual skirmish, Belman seemed to hold the upper hand.

Yet, my exhortations proved just as futile as my mother's. Harry ignored my letters entirely. Pressed further by the Government's friends, I acted imprudently, writing that I would visit him at Fanny's Mount within the week. Upon arrival, however, I found only my sister. She greeted me with perfect cordiality but informed me that Harry had ridden far into the countryside, toward the Blue Mountains, ostensibly to view horses, and would be away for an indefinite period.

Realizing hope was lost, I confessed to Fanny, "Harry is riding away. God knows to what end."
"The Lord prosper the righteous cause, Sir George," she replied.
"Amen, with all my heart," I answered, noting the irony that she spoke as an American while I remained an Englishman. "Tell him from me that if anything happens to our mother, I have ample provision in England for myself and mine, and I shall resign all our lands here in Virginia to him."
"You don't mean that, George?" she cried, her eyes brightening. "Well, it is but right and fair!" she conceded moments later. "Why should you, the eldest by mere hours, possess everything—a palace and estates in England, this plantation, the title, and children—while my poor Harry has nothing?" She praised my generosity, noting I did not share my mother's stubbornness, and kissed my cheek before I departed. With a woman like Fanny guiding him, how could I ever hope to turn my brother back?

Disappointed, I returned to the Governor. We agreed it was time to arm earnestly for the inevitable shock. The Court of Officials was agitated, savage in their abuse of the "wicked Whigs," and loud in their chants for Old England. They craved the day when they might ride down their enemies hand-to-hand. Having received thanks from His Excellency and pledged to raise a body of men for the defense of the Crown, I departed. Thus began the corps known as the Westmoreland Defenders, for which I was appointed Colonel.

News of a gentleman of my station taking command spread swiftly through the Government Gazette. Officers filled up readily, yet recruits were slow to arrive and quick to vanish. Nevertheless, friend Hagan eagerly offered to serve as chaplain. Madam Esmond distributed our colors and traveled the countryside recruiting volunteers. Most diligent of all was my old tutor, little Mr. Dempster, who, having fought on the Jacobite side in Scotland as a youth, ventured into the Carolinas to recruit among the descendants of his banished comrades, many of whom remained fiercely loyal to the Crown.

Meanwhile, Harry's expedition for horses carried him far beyond the Blue Mountains, eventually leading him to Annapolis, Baltimore, and finally Philadelphia, where a second General Congress sat.
