History records what transpired next: Lexington had fallen, and the first shots of a war destined to birth an independent nation rang out. Though we continued to protest our loyalty to the King, our resolve hardened—"to die or be free." Twenty thousand loyal petitioners assembled around Boston with arms and cannons seized from government stores. Benedict Arnold began his infamous career with the daring capture of two forts. Generals from London marched to reinforce General Gage at Boston. Soon, the armies clashed. Our British generals commenced their campaign of conquest with the "glorious blunder" of Breed's Hill. Fortifications were raised, and the two armies watched one another while Congress debated at Philadelphia who should command the confederated colonial forces.

All eyes turned to the Virginian regiment marching to join the new General-in-Chief. One wing was commanded by Henry Esmond Warrington, Esq., formerly a Captain in His Majesty's service, riding beside his brave little wife. I felt relief that she had left Virginia behind; had she stayed, our mother would undoubtedly have crossed lines to fight alongside her. Fortunately, that terrifying spectacle of civil war was spared to our immediate family.

The rush of farmers and country folk flowed almost exclusively toward the new northern army. News of the blood spilled at Lexington ignited a frenzy of wrath. Men denounced the "bloody British invader," ignoring the reality that he was merely executing duty, while resisting parties sought to prevent him from seizing supplies. Anger clouded judgment; the colonists sided with the rebels, driven by a natural ardor to test their strength against the "braggart domineering mother country." Breed's Hill became a symbol, a flaming summit where Liberty, Victory, and Glory seemed to dance. If these dreaded troops could be withstood by farmers and ploughmen, surely these famous officers could be outgeneralled by doctors, lawyers, and civilians! Who could compare with Britons, save their children across the Atlantic?

For the most part, the people around us sided with the revolution. Consequently, Sir George Warrington found his regiment of Westmoreland Defenders woefully thin at first, diminishing rapidly thereafter. Not only did the news of battles from the North demoralize them, but the behavior of Lord Dunmore enraged his own partisans. It had been agreed in countless meetings that the Governor would plant the King's standard and summon all loyal gentlemen. Many a gentleman, now a smug officer of the United States, once prayed aloud to perish under the folds of his country's flag or roared challenges to the "bloody traitors." But let bygones be bygones. Public history records that His Lordship, the Sovereign's representative, who so loudly invited the lieges to rally, was the first to pocket the standard and flee to his ships to escape danger. He refused to leave unless he could act as a pirate at midnight, burning and destroying.

While the loyal gentry remained on shore, subject to greater peril due to their leader's weakness, the Governor's conduct reached a nadir. It was early June; orchards bloomed with summer plenty. A week prior, I had exchanged compliments with His Excellency in Williamsburg, devising plans to check rebellion, shaking hands with the promise of *vincere aut mori*—to conquer or die—as our final words. Back at Richmond, our family discussed the prospects in the North and the quarrels between the Assembly and the Governor, who had reconvened them. Then, our ghostly Hagan rushed into the parlor, asking, "Have we heard the news of the Governor?"

"Has he dissolved the Assembly again, and put that scoundrel Patrick Henry in irons?" asked Madam Esmond.
"No such thing!" Hagan replied. "His lordship, with his lady and family, left their palace secretly at night. They are aboard a man-of-war off York. My lord sent a despatch to the Assembly, begging them to continue sitting, and announced that he had quit Government House solely out of fear of the people's fury."

What was to become of the sheep when the shepherd ran away? The gentlemen of the House of Assembly made pathetic entreaties, guaranteeing security if he would land, imploring him to appear to pass bills. But no. The man-of-war became his seat of government. He demanded his House of Commons wait upon him there. This was planting the King's standard with a vengeance. Our Governor had abandoned us; the

What utility does a Colonel possess without a regiment to command? The Governor and Council, who had so pompously celebrated the endowment of my commission, were nowhere to be seen. They had fled to ships, engaging in sporadic piracy and arson from the deck, leaving the shores to chaos. Lord Dunmore's black auxiliaries had driven away those of his own blood, while rumors spread that envoys had been dispatched to Southern tribes, promising thousands of warriors to bring our people to "good behavior" via the tomahawk. "And these are to be our allies!" I murmured to my mother, exchanging a look of grim foreboding. My mind flashed back with ghastly clarity to the savage whose face had glared over mine, the knife pressed to my throat before Florac struck him down at Braddock's Field. We reinforced Castlewood with whatever defenses we could devise, yet our terror lay not in the rebels, but in the painted heroes and the freed slaves. I had never witnessed my mother lose her composure save once—when recounting her father's death in an Indian raid decades prior. Yet, when shadowy figures materialized before our house at night, she could not be persuaded otherwise; she knelt, weeping, "The Lord have mercy! The Indians—the Indians!"

Lord Dunmore's negro allies eventually scattered aboard his fleeing vessels or vanished into the fray for pay and plunder, while the promised Southern warriors never materialized. Still, I gazed upon my mother's grey hair, my wife's brown locks, and our child's golden curls with a terrible pang of doubt, wondering if these innocents would become victims of the very war we helped ignite. Had I not dared to interpret Providence, I might claim that employing these Indians and German mercenaries brought swift retribution. On the field, the mercenaries crumbled before the Provincials, fueling the Continental spirit through their defeat; meanwhile, the slaughter of Miss McCrea by a handful of drunkards inflicted far more damage to the Royal cause than the loss of battles or entire regiments.

Once the Indian panic subsided, Madam Esmond's courage returned, replaced by a serious unease regarding the dangers I courted. "What harm can they do me, a poor woman?" she argued. "If I have one son, a Colonel without a regiment, I have another leading two hundred Continentals in Mr. Washington's camp. If the Royalists come, they will spare me for your sake; if the rebels appear, Harry's passport will suffice. I cannot stand for your delicate wife and this baby to remain here, increasing our peril! We must flee to Boston or New York. Do not speak of defending me! Who would harm a harmless old crone? If the rebels arrive, I shall hide behind Mrs. Fanny's petticoats and feel safer outside the house than within." She spoke partly for reason, but mostly because she felt compelled to remain in the soil where her father slept and where she was born. She lived backward, blessfully sending off the new generation while clinging to the past, to old days and fading memories.

As we debated an escape to Boston, news arrived that the British had evacuated the ill-fated city of Boston entirely. They had never ventured to attack Washington at Cambridge, despite the Continental army lying there for months without powder, content to wait until he secured munitions and seized Dorchester Heights. From there, the entire British force and colonial garrison were forced into a humiliating retreat. While the British victory at Bunker's Hill was undeniable, akin to Blenheim, their commanders thereafter seemed paralyzed by a fear of assaulting entrenched Continentals. Why the hesitation from July to March? Why the timidity at Long Island, when the Continental army lay in our grasp? Why the paralysis before Valley Forge, where starving, ragged remnants barely manned lines against a vast, victorious foe?

With the shifting tides of hope and fear, the tone of the partisans altered dramatically. Upon hearing of Boston's evacuation, every local Whig bowed to Madam Esmond, urging immediate submission. Though she no longer flaunted her loyalty openly, she never wavered. Each night and morning, the poor Hagan prayed for the Royal Family within our walls, and on Sundays, neighbors attended services where my mother, acting as a fervent clerk, delivered a stately prayer for the High Court of Parliament under our most religious and gracious King. Brave Hagan, a parson without a living, stood as I did, a Militia Colonel without a regiment. He had continued praying for King George in Williamsburg long after the Governor fled, but returning one Sunday, he found a corporal's guard blocking the church doors. Told that the Committee of Safety had installed another divine, he demanded to be "led before their chiefs." Thus, escorted by a chorus of white and black scoundrels, he was marched to the Capitol. Confronting the new magistrates, including Mr. Henry, Hagan intended to deliver an heroic speech, perhaps even accept martyrdom. But Mr. Henry, impatient, interrupted him. "I am too busy to listen to speeches. As for King George, he has no more authority here than King Nebuchadnezzar. Stick to King John and King Macbeth! Send around your tickets, and the Assembly shall hear you." Turning to General Lee, Washington's second-in-command, Henry dismissed the bewildered Hagan, who left reddening and near tears. Consequently, we withdrew the worthy pastor from the restless flock in the city, confining his ministrations to our rural sanctuary.

Though the selection of Virginians for high office in the new government bribed and flattered many leaders—who might otherwise have remained faithful to the Crown—the faithful Loyalists kept intelligences flowing underground. Even when gagged and muzzled, when the Capitol fell to the Whigs and our loyal levies proved as ineffective as Falstaff's regiments, we maintained a network of trust. Some ran away, others feigned republicanism before committees, vowing perdition to kings, yet beneath the surface, we knew one another. Depending on the shifting winds, we concealed or displayed our colors, sometimes apostatizing temporarily to cry "Down with King George!" Negroes carried messages and tokens from house to house, weaving endless plots in the shadows. When the battle turned, neutrals joined the winners, shouting as loudly as the patriots. The runaways were uncounted. Could anyone deny that the signers and ardent supporters of Independence were a minority, yet it was the minority that prevailed? Knowing that a fragment of the defeated Massachusetts army planned a crucial southern expedition, I sought to join forces with the former Governor Martin of North Carolina, bringing three or four Virginian gentlemen and officers to bolster his corps, lacking only privates.

We departed Castlewood with little secrecy; the affairs of Congress were not yet robust enough to enforce tyranny upon those of doubtful allegiance. Gentlemen's houses remained open, and in our southern tradition, we visited friends for months at a stretch. My wife, I, our infant, and a retinue of servants took leave of Madam Esmond, journeying first to a neighboring plantation, then to another friend's estate, until finally reaching Wilmington, North Carolina, where we awaited the succors destined for us. Before our arrival, our brother Royalists from Carolina had appeared in force, though their encounters with the Whigs had been disastrous. The poor Highlanders, no more fortunate in this contest for King George than when they drew swords against him in Scotland, met a similar fate. We did not reach Wilmington until late May, finding Admiral Parker's squadron there, carrying General Clinton and five British regiments, preparing for a descent upon Charleston.

General Clinton, to whom I introduced myself, initially greeted me with grim reserve, noting that my "regiment" consisted of Lady Warrington, our infant, and three negro servants. However, Captain Horner of the *Sphinx*, who had served kindly under Governor Dunmore in Jamaica, warmly embraced Lady Warrington upon learning she was General Lambert's daughter, offering her his finest cabin. Thus, like Lord Dunmore, we became refugees who had waved our flag, pocketed it, and slipped out the back door. Bearing swiftly toward Charleston, I grew intimate with Mr. Clinton during the voyage and our delay in the river. It was the King's birthday when we entered the harbor, a day we deemed glorious for the commencement of the expedition.

The operation did not commence for several days, and I omit the description of Penelope parting from Hector, for Hector remains sound (save for a slight gout), and no rascal Pyrrhus has claimed his widow. In times of war, such scenes of woe occur hourly. I can still see her gentle face over the bulwarks as we descended into the boats, the smile of the infant on her arm. Captain Miles, lacking poetic taste, likely forgot the verses in Pope's Homer describing such a parting, yet my mother often recited them to me as a boy and keeps the gorge I wore that day among her trunks.

My second venture at fighting proved no luckier than the first. We returned to our ships that evening thoroughly beaten. The reckless Lee, whom Clinton had faced at Boston, now met him at Charleston, delivering a brilliant and successful resistance. The fort on Sullivan's Island was a nut we could not crack. Our frigates' fire failed to breach its shell; the passage for our assault was unfordable, trapping Sir Henry Clinton, who was always the first to charge, as he waded into the deadly current. Death by shot, drowning, and exposure to the chill air—I had braved these perils before returning to my wife. Our frigate, grounded for a time, was cannonaded by the enemy until she managed to free herself.

A small incident during this unlucky struggle fostered

To a soldier, the entrenchments of Breed's Hill were perhaps the single most significant defensive feature of the entire Revolutionary War. The carnage inflicted upon us from behind those lines was so terrible, and the enemy's behavior so stubborn, that British commanders thereafter held the American barricades in profound, superstitious awe. Even if the defenders were merely hiding behind rows of blankets, certain of our generals hesitated to force them. At White Plains, when Washington's army stood literally at the mercy of victory, we later learned that our triumphant troops were halted before a makeshift barrier composed entirely of cornstalks and straw.

Another chance for decisive action presented itself, lasting through a long, bitter winter. During this time, the dwindling, disheartened forces of Congress starved and remained unarmed beneath our grasp, while the magnanimous Lord Howe allowed the infamous Camp at Valley Forge to remain untouched. Meanwhile, his own great, brave, and impeccably equipped army fiddled, gambled, and feasted in Philadelphia. Triumphal arches were raised by his countrymen; medieval-style tournaments were held in mocking celebration; and beautiful ladies presented wreaths and garlands to this "clement" chief, adorned with fantastical mottos declaring his laurels would be immortal. Yet, why have my ungrateful countrymen never erected a statue to this general? For within their entire army, there was no officer who fought their battles better, who retrieved their errors with such dexterity, or who ensured their defeats caused them so little harm. Above all, when fortune inevitably turned against them, who displayed such tireless tenderness, patience, and calm in lifting the battered opponent back onto his feet? One cannot help but recall the fiery young warrior England sent to fight across the American continent—a man forever pacing the defenses behind which the foe hid, sleepless and eager by day and night, consumed by a fierce wrath and longing that denied him rest. He wound his tracks with an unwearying scent, panting and hungry for blood and battle, prowling through midnight forests or climbing silent precipices before dawn. He watched until his great heart was nearly worn out, until finally the foe revealed himself, at which moment he sprang upon him, grappled, and, dying, slew him. Think of Wolfe at Quebec, and then listen to Howe's fiddles as he sat smiling among the dancers in Philadelphia!

A favored strategy among our ministers at home and some of our generals in America was to establish a communication between Canada and New York, thereby cutting off New England from the neighboring colonies, overwhelming them in detail, and forcing submission. Burgoyne was entrusted with this plan and departed from Quebec, confidently promising success. His march commenced in full military state; the trumpets of his proclamations blared ahead, summoning the colonists to remember the immense power of England and ordering the misguided rebels to lay down their arms. He brought with him a formidable English force, an army of German veterans of equal power, a terrifying band of Indian warriors, and a brilliant train of artillery. It was hoped the populace along his route would rally to the Royal cause. Initially, the Continental force opposing him was small, and Washington's army had been further weakened by the withdrawal of troops hurried forward to intercept this Canadian invasion. A British detachment from New York was to sweep up the Hudson, clearing the route, and join Burgoyne at Albany. This was the moment when Washington's weakened army should have been crushed; but a greater Power willed otherwise. Nor do I, for one, regret the termination of the war. Looking back now, how clear the blunders of the losing side appear! From start to finish, we were perpetually too late. Our supplies and reinforcements from home arrived too late. Our troops struggled, and aid reached them too late. Our fleet appeared off York Town just in time—only too late—to witness Cornwallis surrender. A way of escape was offered to Burgoyne, but he resolved upon retreat too late. I have heard discomfited officers in later years prove infallibly how a different wind would have saved America for us; how we must have destroyed the French fleet but for a tempest or two; how, once, twice, thrice, but for nightfall, Mr. Washington and his army would have fallen into our power. Who has not, in reading history, speculated upon the "Has Beens" and "Might Have Beens"? I take my tattered old map-book from the shelf and examine the board upon which the great contest was played. I marvel at the curious chances that lost it. Putting aside any idle talk about the relative bravery of the two nations, I cannot but see that we held the best cards, and yet we lost the game.

I confess the sport possessed a considerable fascination for me, stirring my languid blood. My brother Hal, settled on his plantation in Virginia, was perfectly content with the pleasures and occupations there. The company of country neighbors sufficed him; he never tired of tending his crops and people, catching fish, shooting ducks, hunting in the woods, or enjoying his rubber and supper. Happy Hal, in his great barn-like house, under his roomy porches, with his dogs lying at his feet; his friends, the Virginian Will Wimble, staying at free quarters in his mansion; his negroes fat, lazy, and ragged; his shrewd little wife ruling over them and her husband—who always obeyed her implicitly while she lived, and who was consoled with surprising speed when she died! I call him happy, though his lot would have been intolerable to me. My income was five times as great, my house in England larger and built of brick faced with freestone. My wife... would I have changed her for any other woman in the world? My children... well, I am content with Lady Warrington's opinion of them. But with all these plums, peaches, and rich fruits pouring from Plenty's horn into my lap, I fear I have been something of an ingrate. Hodge, my gatekeeper, who shares his bread and scrap of bacon with a family as large as my own, seems to me to enjoy his meal as much as I do, despite Mrs. Molly's best dishes and sweetmeats, and Mr. Gumbo's finest wines. Ah, me! Sweetmeats have lost their savor for me, however much they may delight my young ones in the nursery, and the perfume of claret grows pallid upon old noses!

Our parson has preached many a sermon to me, and perhaps I did not care for them much when he first began. Dost thou remember, honest friend? (Sure he does, for he has repeated the story over the bottle as often as his sermons, and my Lady Warrington pretends she has never heard it)—I say, Joe Blake, thou rememberest full well, with advantage, that October evening when we scrambled up an embrasure at Fort Clinton, and a clubbed musket would have dashed these valuable brains out, had not Joe's sword whipped my rebellious countryman through the gizzard. Joe wore a red coat in those days—the uniform of the brave Sixty-Third, whose leader, the bold Colonel Sill, fell pierced with many wounds beside him. He exchanged his red coat for black and my pulpit. His doctrines are sound, and his sermons short. We read the newspapers together over our wine. Not two months ago, we read of our old friend Howe's glorious deeds of the first of June. We were told how the noble Rawdon, who fought with us at Fort Clinton, had joined the Duke of York; and today, that Royal Highness is in full retreat before Pichegru, while he and my son Miles have taken Valenciennes for nothing! Ah, parson! Would you not like to don your old Sixty-Third coat? (Though I doubt Mrs. Blake could ever make the buttons and buttonholes meet again over your broad frame). The boys were acting a play with my militia sword. Oh, that I were young again, Mr. Blake! That I had not the gout in my toe! And I would saddle Rocinante and ride back into the world, feeling the pulses beat again, and play a little of life's glorious game!

The last "hit" I witnessed was gallantly won by our side, though truth be told, even in this party, the Americans won the rubber. Our people gained only the ground they stood upon, capturing and destroying the guns, stores, and ships, while our efforts at rescue proved too late to prevent the catastrophe impending over Burgoyne's unfortunate army. Following one of those inevitable delays that retarded our plans and weakened the blows our chiefs intended to deliver, an expedition was launched from New York at the close of September, 1777. Had it advanced but a fortnight earlier, it might have saved the doomed force of Burgoyne. *Sed Dis aliter visum.* This delay was not Sir Henry Clinton's fault, who could not leave his city unprotected, but rather the winds and weather that delayed the arrival of reinforcements we had long awaited from England. The fleet bringing them also delivered long, fond letters from home, containing the very latest news of the children under the care of their good Aunt Hetty and their grandfather. The mother's heart yearned toward the absent young ones. She offered me no reproaches, but I could read her importunities in her anxious eyes, her terror for me, and her longing for her children. "Why stay longer?" she seemed to say. "You who have no calling to this war, or to draw the sword against your countrymen—why continue to imperil your life and my happiness?" I understood her appeal. We were to engage in no immediate service of danger.

Must I recount, dear friends, the simple joys of gathering beneath the same roof, the mother's quiet pride as her flock returns to her shelter? Our home, spacious and warm, welcomed us all. Yet Aunt Hetty, though our kin, could not abide to remain within these walls. With a frankness that cut to the quick, she declared that yielding up her eldest children—hers for nearly three years—to live among strangers, while accepting a secondary role, tore at her heart too deeply. Thus, she and Father departed for a dwelling at Bury St. Edmunds, a short journey hence, where they lived apart. There, in privacy, Aunt Hetty doted upon her oldest nephew and niece, indulging them fully.

It was the season following our return home when the Jamaican planter, Mr. B, passed away. He left Aunt Hetty half his fortune, a legacy born of a long-standing infatuation; for in Jamaica, despite her refusal, he had proven his steadfast devotion. Yet, Heaven knows how much of Aunt Hetty's inheritance Monsieur Miles has already swallowed! The cost of his commission, the flamboyance of his uniforms, the debts contracted in gaming houses, the frivolous entertainments at the Cocoa-Tree—all these drain the purse. Do you suppose, sir, I am ignorant of human nature? Of the price of Pall Mall revelry and modest pleasures? Can a gentleman sustain such a lifestyle on the five hundred pounds a year I grant him? Aunt Hetty, convinced by her trials, has resolved to spin an old maid. "I vowed," she told me, "never to wed until I found a man as virtuous as my dear father." "And I never did, Sir George," she replied to me later, adding with a shrug, "nor half as good as Sir George. Let him smoke that thought in his pipe."

Yet, when our beloved General breathed his last—calm, aged, and content to depart—I believe it was my wife who wept most profoundly. "I mourn because I fear I did not love him enough," the gentle soul confessed. In contrast, Hetty barely concealed her composure, outwardly at least. She speaks of him constantly, as if he walks beside her; she recalls his wit, his kindness to his offspring, and brightens like a girl again when mentioning them. She sits cheerfully before the slab in church that bears his name and virtues, telling no lies in her reverence.

I had often suspected that my brother Hal, harboring a youthful passion for Hetty, retained a hold on her heart. When he visited us ten years prior, I mentioned this childhood romance, hoping he might persuade her to replace Mrs. Fanny—a loss my wife, prone to self-reproach, lamented endlessly for failing to grieve sufficiently. Hal arrived consumed by sorrow for Fanny, vowing no woman equal existed. Our General, then alive, took him in, housing him as he did in happier, earlier days. The women played melodies he heard as a boy at Oakhurst; hearts softened by old memories. Harry poured out his griefs and praises of his late wife to Hetty, and spoke fondly of his dear Aunt Lambert, whose virtues were music to the faithful old husband, whose thoughts of his wife seemed absent for no three waking minutes.

General Hal ventured to Paris as an American General Officer, clad in blue and yellow, a fashion adopted by Mr. Fox and others here. Much honored at Versailles, though presented by the Marquis de Lafayette to a King and Queen who held neither in high esteem, a Marquise reportedly took a liking to the Virginian General, offering immediate marriage. Had he not resisted and fled back to England, Warrington, and ultimately Bury, where locals listened with endless patience and sympathy to his tales of his late wife? As for us, who knew the paragon, we were civil, though not entirely enthusiastic regarding her, occasionally puzzled to answer our children's queries about Uncle Hal's angelic spouse.

The two Generals, myself, Captain Miles, and Parson Blake—who was routed at Monmouth the year after I left America, returning to exchange coats and accept my living—often fought the battles of the Revolution over a bottle. The parson would exclaim, "By Jupiter, General!" (compounding for Jupiter when divested of his military habit), "You are the Tory, and Sir George the Whig! He faults our leaders, while you stand forever by them; last Sunday, when I prayed for the King, I heard you following me loudly."

"And so I do, Blake, with all my heart," Hal replied. "I cannot forget I wore his coat."

"Ah, if Wolfe had lived twenty years longer!" cried Lambert.

"Aye, sir!" shouted Hal. "You must hear the General talk of him!"

"What General?" I asked, seeking to vex him.

"My General," Hal answered, rising to fill a bumper. "His Excellency, General George Washington!"

"With all my heart," I cried, though the parson looked ill-disposed toward the toast or the claret.

Hal never tires of speaking of his General. It was on such an evening of friendly discourse that he recounted how he had fallen into disgrace with the man he cherished so dearly. Their rift stemmed from the aforementioned Monsieur le Marquis de Lafayette, a figure who played a pivotal role in recent history before vanishing mysteriously. Hal's prior rank and acknowledged gallantry ought to have secured his promotion in the Continental army, where a "whipper-snapper" like M. de Lafayette was swiftly complimented by Congress with the rank of Major-General. With the freedom of an old soldier, Hal expressed contempt for certain congressional appointments, alleging that unscrupulous officers, greedy for promotion, orchestrated intrigues and cabals. Mr. Warrington, perhaps emulating his now-illustrious friend of Mount Vernon, affected the air of *gentilhomme*, paying his wages but spending them on comforts and clothing for his men. Regarding rank, he deemed it of no earthly consequence, preferring to serve as colonel over any higher grade. He certainly added contemptuous remarks concerning certain General Officers of the Congress army, their origins, and the causes of their advancement. Notably, he was furious about the sudden promotion of the young French lad—the Marquis, as they affectionately called him—in the Republican army, who, incidentally, was a prodigious favorite of the Chief himself. There were not three officers in the entire Continental force (after poor madcap Lee was captured and disgraced) who could speak the Marquis's language, allowing Hal to judge the young Major-General more closely and familiarly than other gentlemen, including the Commander-in-Cheif. Mr. Washington, good-naturedly, chided friend Hal for his jealousy of the bearded commander of Auvergne; he himself was pleased by the filial regard and profound veneration the enthusiastic young nobleman always displayed toward him. Moreover, he possessed the very best political reasons for treating the Marquis with friendship and favor.

Subsequently, it transpired that the Commander-in-Chief was most urgently pressing Colonel Warrington's promotion upon Congress. As if his difficulties before the enemy were not sufficient—he was entrenched at Valley Forge during a hard winter, commanding five or six thousand men, almost without fire, blankets, food, or ammunition, facing Sir William Howe's army, which was perfectly equipped and thrice as numerous—as if this trial were not enough, he faced further the cowardly distrust of Congress and insubordination and conspiracy among officers in his own camp. During the terrible winter of '77, when a single blow from the sluggish head of the British forces might have ended the war, and all was doubt, confusion, and despair in the opposing camp (save in one indomitable breast), my brother had an interview with the Chief. He subsequently described it to me, and Hal could never speak of it without succumbing to the deepest emotion. Mr. Washington had won no such triumph as that procured for Gates and the northern army by the daredevil courage of Arnold and the elegant imbecility of Burgoyne. Save in one or two minor encounters proving his daring bravery and unceasing watchfulness, General Washington met defeat after defeat from an enemy superior in every point. Congress mistrusted him. Many an officer in his own camp hated him. Those disappointed in ambition, those detected in peculation, those whose selfishness or incapacity his honest eyes had exposed—all were, more or less, in league against him. Gates was the chief toward whom the malcontents turned. Mr. Gates was the only genius fit to conduct the war; with a vaingloriousness he later generously acknowledged, he did not refuse the homage paid him.

To illustrate the dreadful troubles and anxieties with which General Washington contended, I may mention what was then termed the "Conway Cabal." A certain Irishman—a Chevalier of St. Louis, an officer in the French service—arrived in America early in '77 seeking military employment. Speedily appointed brigadier, he could not be contented without immediate promotion to major-general. Mr. C. had friends at Congress who, as the General-in-Chief learned, had promised him speedy promotion. General Washington remonstrated, representing the injustice of promoting to the highest rank the youngest brigadier in the service. While the matter was pending, he was put in possession of a letter from Conway to General Gates, whom Conway complimented, saying, "Heaven had been determined to save America, or a weak general and bad councillors would have ruined it." The General enclosed the note to Mr. Conway without a word of comment; Conway offered his resignation, which was refused by Congress, who appointed him Inspector-General of the
